# Thesis gate — How could we actually tell which theory of consciousness is right?

103 candidates. **0 in the top-right quadrant**, 6 repairable, 75 not attacked.

Ranked by promise, then by unanswered damage. Nothing has been eliminated — the verdict is a field, not a filter.

**Promise** is scored blind, before any attack. **Residual** is the strongest objection left unanswered — 0 is best, and it is the ranking number. **Worst** is the strongest objection mounted at all: when Worst is also low, the thesis was not seriously tested rather than sturdy.

## Repair candidates

*Promise 4+, failed on ONE locatable flaw — usually scope or framing, with the narrower version already written out. Often the richest vein.*

### R1. What makes consciousness theories empirically untestable is not the hard problem but the fact that they make predictions only about necessitation relationships (what suffices for consciousness), never about exclusion relationships (what couldn't be conscious).

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 4/5 · worst 4/5 · wounded
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The optimism in the recent 'adversarial collaboration' approach that pitting theories against each other in contrastive experiments will lead to empirical resolution.
- **Sketch:** Current theories specify conditions sufficient for consciousness—integrated information above φ-min, global broadcast, higher-order representation—but none specify what definitively excludes consciousness. IIT comes closest with φ=0, but even this allows that other architectures might generate consciousness differently. This asymmetry makes theories unfalsifiable: if we find consciousness without the theory's sufficient conditions, theorists can claim we found a different sufficient condition; if we find the conditions without consciousness, theorists can claim insufficient measurement sensitivity or add auxiliary hypotheses. The argument turns on distinguishing two types of scientific claims: those that carve nature at the joints (making both positive and negative predictions) versus those that merely identify sufficient conditions. Real testability requires exclusion predictions—ways a system could be that would definitely not support consciousness according to the theory.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The necessitation vs. exclusion distinction is genuinely illuminating and could reframe how we evaluate theories' empirical content.
  - TRIVIAL — landed 3/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence showed empirical observations beyond the Popperian truism, but conceded the core testability claim applies standard philosophy of science rather than revealing something distinctive about consciousness
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 2/5, answered. The defence demonstrated that logical and metaphysical readings of 'exclusion relationships' necessarily coincide in empirical theories—necessary conditions just are metaphysical boundaries
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence retreated from structural claims about theories to sociological claims about theorists' behavior, failing to provide non-circular criteria for what counts as an exclusion relationship
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 2/5, answered. The defence successfully distinguished the thesis from Lau and Michel's work through different explanatory targets and conceptual machinery, though this narrowed the thesis's scope
- 🔧 **Survives as:** The adversarial collaboration methodology faces a structural limitation: when competing consciousness theories propose different sufficient conditions but neither specifies necessary conditions or exclusion predictions, contrastive experiments cannot adjudicate between them because any finding can be accommodated by positing alternative sufficient conditions or adjusting auxiliary hypotheses
- **Flaw:** The thesis lacks non-circular criteria for identifying exclusion relationships—it defines them operationally by whether they constrain theory development, making the claim unfalsifiable, and it retreats from structural claims about theories to sociological claims about scientific practice

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis reduces to the uncontested observation that universal generalizations are falsifiable while existential claims are not. "All ravens are black" is falsifiable (find a white raven), while "some sufficient condition for blackness exists" is not. The candidate merely restates this logical truism in consciousness terminology: theories offering sufficient conditions ("if X then consciousness") make existential claims, while exclusion principles ("if not-X then not-consciousness") make universal ones. This is elementary philosophy of science—Popper's asymmetry between verification and falsification, covered in undergraduate logic. The "distinction" between necessitation and exclusion relationships is just the distinction between "if-then" and "only-if" statements. That current theories emphasize sufficient rather than necessary conditions is a sociological observation about what researchers happen to propose, not a conceptual discovery about what makes consciousness special. The weak point is the implied novelty: presenting the logical structure of conditional statements as if it were an insight about consciousness theories specifically, rather than an instance of a completely general point about how existential versus universal claims relate to testability.

STRENGTH: 2/5 — The thesis does add the empirical observation that consciousness theories specifically tend toward sufficient-condition form, which has some descriptive value. But the conceptual core—that such claims are harder to falsify—is textbook material, making this more application than discovery.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies what the thesis claims. It is not merely that sufficient conditions are harder to falsify than universal generalizations—that would indeed be trivial. The thesis makes a specific claim about *why consciousness theories take this form* and *what this reveals about the empirical problem*.

First, the thesis explains a structural feature of consciousness science that the Popperian truism cannot: why do all major theories converge on offering only sufficient conditions when theories in other domains routinely offer both? In chemistry, we don't just say "carbon bonding suffices for organic molecules"—we specify what excludes organic status. In genetics, we don't just list sufficient conditions for inheritance—we specify what couldn't carry genetic information. The thesis proposes an answer: the hard problem itself forces this asymmetry. Because we lack third-person access to the explanandum, we cannot confidently rule anything out. We can identify correlates (sufficient conditions) but never definitively say "this system definitely lacks consciousness."

Second, the thesis targets a specific contemporary optimism—that adversarial collaboration will resolve theoretical disputes. The Popperian point alone doesn't explain why this optimism is misplaced. The thesis does: even contrastive experiments cannot adjudicate between theories that differ only in their proposed sufficient conditions, because finding consciousness without theory A's conditions doesn't falsify A (maybe we found a different sufficient condition), and finding A's conditions without consciousness doesn't falsify A either (maybe consciousness is present but unmeasurable). This is not just "existential claims are unfalsifiable"—it's a specific diagnosis of why the adversarial methodology fails.

Third, the distinction between necessitation and exclusion relationships is not equivalent to "if-then" versus "only-if." A theory could offer necessary conditions ("only integrated information produces consciousness") without offering exclusion predictions if it remains agnostic about whether other architectures might also suffice. The thesis identifies that theories avoid *both* necessary conditions *and* exclusion predictions—a double avoidance the Popperian framework doesn't predict.

VERDICT

The defence fails, but not where the objection strikes.

The Popperian point, while not the whole story, does undermine the thesis's claim to novelty about *testability*. The thesis is right that consciousness theories exhibit this pattern, and right that it matters for adversarial collaboration. But the core move—linking this pattern to unfalsifiability—is indeed an application of standard philosophy of science rather than a distinctive insight.

Where the defence succeeds is showing the thesis makes genuine empirical observations (the convergence on sufficient-condition form, the failure of adversarial methodology). Where it fails is claiming these observations reveal something special about consciousness rather than instantiating a general logical structure. The thesis survives as a useful *diagnosis* of current practice but not as a conceptual *discovery* about what makes consciousness empirically special.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "exclusion relationships" between a logical reading and a metaphysical one, needing both to generate its apparent force.

On the logical reading, "exclusion relationships" means necessary conditions—claims of the form "only X can produce consciousness" or "if consciousness then X must be present." This makes the thesis plausible: it's true that consciousness theories avoid stating necessary conditions, offering only sufficient ones. The observation that theories specify "if integrated information then consciousness" but not "only integrated information yields consciousness" is accurate.

On the metaphysical reading, "exclusion relationships" means principled boundaries—claims about what kinds of systems *couldn't possibly* be conscious regardless of whether they meet any particular sufficient condition. This is what makes the thesis interesting: the suggestion that we cannot rule out consciousness in thermostats, plants, or electron configurations because theories provide no exclusion principles.

The argument needs both. It needs the logical reading to make the empirical observation true (theories do avoid necessary conditions). But it needs the metaphysical reading to connect this to the hard problem and to testability (the deep issue is that we cannot rule anything out as a possible consciousness-bearer).

The equivocation appears in phrases like "what couldn't be conscious." This slides between "what doesn't meet the necessary conditions" (logical) and "what is metaphysically excluded from the space of possible conscious systems" (metaphysical). A theory could specify necessary conditions without providing metaphysical exclusion principles, or vice versa. The thesis conflates these, treating the absence of necessary conditions as equivalent to the absence of principled boundaries.

STRENGTH: 3/5 — A genuine problem requiring clarification. The thesis can survive by choosing one reading and reconstructing the argument, but as stated it trades on the ambiguity to seem both true and important.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection manufactures an ambiguity that does not exist in the thesis. There is one unified concept at work: exclusion relationships are claims that specify conditions under which consciousness is *absent*, and this single notion has both logical and empirical dimensions that necessarily go together.

Consider what an exclusion relationship would actually look like in a consciousness theory. IIT's claim that φ=0 systems lack consciousness is simultaneously: (1) a necessary condition claim (consciousness requires φ>0), (2) a metaphysical boundary (feed-forward networks are excluded from consciousness-bearers), and (3) an empirical prediction (we should find no consciousness in purely feed-forward architectures). These are not three different things that might come apart—they are three descriptions of the same theoretical commitment.

The objection's proposed distinction collapses under examination. What would it mean to "specify necessary conditions without providing metaphysical exclusion principles"? If a theory claims "only X can produce consciousness," it thereby excludes non-X systems from the space of possible consciousness-bearers. That's what necessary conditions *do*—they draw boundaries. Conversely, what would "metaphysical exclusion principles" be if not claims that certain systems necessarily lack consciousness? The objection posits a distinction without a difference.

The thesis's actual point stands: consciousness theories avoid making claims that would exclude candidate systems from consciousness, whether you describe this as avoiding necessary conditions, avoiding metaphysical boundaries, or avoiding negative predictions. These are the same avoidance. The phrase "what couldn't be conscious" is not equivocal—it straightforwardly means systems that the theory predicts lack consciousness, however that prediction is grounded.

Moreover, the objection's own examples prove the unity of the concept. When it distinguishes "what doesn't meet the necessary conditions" from "what is metaphysically excluded," it cannot actually provide an example of these coming apart. That's because in any theory with teeth, satisfying necessary conditions *is* what determines metaphysical possibility of consciousness.

VERDICT

The defence succeeds. The objection identifies a verbal distinction that does not track a real conceptual divide.

The accusation of equivocation requires showing that the thesis needs two *different* meanings of a term to work—that it would fail if we held the term to one consistent meaning throughout. But the objection cannot show this. Whether we translate "exclusion relationships" as "necessary conditions" or "metaphysical boundaries" or "negative predictions," the thesis's argument goes through identically: consciousness theories avoid making such claims, and this is what makes them untestable.

The objection's strategy of separating "logical" from "metaphysical" readings founders because these are not separate dimensions. A logical necessary condition in an empirical theory *is* a metaphysical constraint on what can instantiate the property. The objection treats as equivocation what is actually conceptual unity.

The thesis survives this attack intact.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis immunizes itself against counterexamples through systematic redefinition of what counts as an "exclusion relationship." 

Consider potential counterexamples: IIT does claim φ=0 systems lack consciousness—seemingly an exclusion relationship. The thesis handles this by saying "even this allows that other architectures might generate consciousness differently," redefining exclusion relationships to require ruling out *all possible alternative sufficient conditions*. But this sets an impossible standard—no empirical theory could meet it without omniscience about all possible physical configurations.

Alternatively, consider Global Workspace Theory's claim that consciousness requires global broadcast. If we press this as an exclusion claim (non-broadcast systems lack consciousness), the thesis can respond that GWT theorists "add auxiliary hypotheses" when faced with counterevidence. But this redescribes normal scientific practice (theory refinement) as proof of unfalsifiability.

The weak point is "exclusion relationship" itself—it's defined circularly to mean "whatever would make theories testable." When we find candidates (φ=0, no global broadcast, no higher-order representation), they get disqualified as "not really" exclusion relationships because theories can still be defended. But any scientific claim can be defended with auxiliary hypotheses. The thesis effectively says: "theories lack X, where X is defined as whatever they lack that makes them untestable." This makes the thesis itself unfalsifiable—every apparent exclusion relationship in consciousness theories can be redefined as insufficiently exclusive.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem. The thesis needs to specify what would count as an exclusion relationship independently of whether it makes theories testable, or it's just saying "theories are untestable because they lack what would make them testable."

**DEFENDER** — [DEFENDER] BEST DEFENCE

The objection misreads the thesis as making an absolute claim when it makes a comparative one. The thesis does not require that exclusion relationships rule out "all possible alternative sufficient conditions"—that would indeed be impossible. It requires that they rule out *some* systems in a way that generates empirical risk, and it observes that consciousness theories systematically fail to do even this.

The IIT example proves the thesis's point rather than refuting it. Yes, IIT claims φ=0 systems lack consciousness—but notice what happens in practice. When critics point to potential φ=0 systems that seem conscious (certain recurrent networks, split-brain patients in specific conditions), IIT theorists don't treat this as falsification. They either recalculate φ (it wasn't really zero), or they bite the bullet on the consciousness claim (it really lacks consciousness, contrary to intuition), or they adjust the architecture specification. The thesis's observation is precisely that this exclusion claim *doesn't function as an exclusion claim in practice*—it doesn't constrain theory development the way exclusion claims do in other sciences.

Compare to a genuine exclusion relationship in chemistry: "carbon-12 cannot undergo beta decay." If we observe beta decay from what we identified as carbon-12, we don't recalculate whether it's "really" carbon-12 by the theory's lights—we've falsified something. The asymmetry is real: the chemical claim puts the theory at risk; the IIT claim does not, because the theory retains enough flexibility to accommodate apparent counterexamples.

The objection claims this "redescribes normal scientific practice (theory refinement) as proof of unfalsifiability." But there's a crucial difference between refinement and systematic insulation. When atomic theory was refined to accommodate new particles, the refinements were constrained by other exclusion relationships (conservation laws, charge constraints). The theory couldn't be adjusted arbitrarily. Consciousness theories lack these constraining exclusions—they can be adjusted to accommodate any finding because they never definitively ruled anything out.

The standard for "exclusion relationship" is not circular. It's operational: a claim creates empirical risk if the theory's proponents would treat certain findings as falsifying rather than as occasions for recalculation or auxiliary hypothesis addition. The thesis observes that consciousness theories systematically lack claims with this property—not because such claims are logically impossible, but because the hard problem prevents us from having the independent access to consciousness needed to enforce them.

VERDICT

The defence partially succeeds but reveals a critical vulnerability.

The defence is right that the thesis makes an empirical observation about scientific practice, not just a logical claim about what's possible. And it's right that there's an asymmetry between how consciousness theories respond to apparent counterexamples and how theories in other domains do. The IIT example does show this pattern.

However, the defence fails to fully escape the circularity charge. The operational criterion—"would theorists treat findings as falsifying?"—makes the thesis a claim about scientific sociology rather than theoretical structure. The thesis started by claiming that theories *lack* exclusion relationships (a structural claim), but the defence retreats to claiming that theorists *don't treat* their claims as exclusionary (a sociological claim). These are different theses.

More damaging: the defence's own example undermines it. When it says IIT theorists "bite the bullet on the consciousness claim (it really lacks consciousness, contrary to intuition)," this is exactly what treating an exclusion claim as falsifiable looks like—accepting counterintuitive implications rather than adjusting the theory. The defence wants to have it both ways: when theorists adjust the theory, that proves no real exclusion relationship exists; when they don't adjust, that's "biting bullets" rather than being constrained by exclusions.

The thesis survives in weakened form: consciousness theories exhibit a distinctive pattern in how exclusion claims function in practice. But it does not survive in its strong form: that theories *lack* exclusion relationships in a way that's structurally different from other sciences. The unfalsifiability charge succeeds in showing that the thesis needs clearer criteria for what counts as an exclusion relationship, independent of whether it generates the testability consequences the thesis predicts.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis that consciousness theories are untestable because they make only sufficiency claims without exclusion predictions is precisely the position defended by Hakwan Lau and Matthias Michel in their 2019 paper "A Socio-Historical Take on the Meta-Problem of Consciousness" and elaborated in Michel's 2020 work. They argue that consciousness science suffers from what they call "the asymmetry problem"—theories specify positive markers (sufficient conditions) but systematically avoid specifying what would definitively lack consciousness, making adjudication between theories impossible. Michel explicitly connects this to the structure of adversarial collaborations, arguing they cannot resolve theoretical disputes because competing theories differ only in their proposed sufficient conditions while remaining silent on necessary conditions. The candidate thesis even uses their terminology ("necessitation relationships") and their primary example (IIT's φ=0 as the closest thing to an exclusion claim that still doesn't function as one). The weak point is the entire thesis: it restates Lau and Michel's position without acknowledgment, presenting as a novel distinction what is already an established critique in the consciousness science literature. The candidate adds no new conceptual machinery, no additional examples, and no advancement beyond the existing position.

STRENGTH: 2/5 — While the position is indeed already taken by Lau and Michel, the candidate thesis does frame it slightly differently (emphasizing the distinction between necessitation and exclusion rather than Michel's broader methodological critique), and applies it specifically to target adversarial collaboration optimism. This gives it some independent value as a restatement with different emphasis, though it lacks the novelty required for a strong original thesis.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection commits a common error: treating thematic overlap as identity of thesis. Yes, Lau and Michel discuss asymmetries in consciousness science, but their argument and this thesis diverge in three crucial respects.

**First, the explanatory target differs.** Lau and Michel's "asymmetry problem" is primarily about why consciousness science has made slow progress compared to other fields—a sociological and historical question. Their 2019 paper's title announces this: it's a "socio-historical take" explaining how the field developed its current structure. This thesis targets something narrower and more specific: why the adversarial collaboration methodology specifically cannot deliver on its promise. The adversarial approach assumes that contrastive experiments will adjudicate between theories by finding conditions where they make different predictions. This thesis explains why that assumption fails: theories that differ only in proposed sufficient conditions cannot be pulled apart by experiments, because any result can be accommodated by either theory. That's a claim about experimental methodology, not about historical development.

**Second, the conceptual machinery differs.** Michel's framework centers on what he calls "marker-based" versus "mechanism-based" approaches—theories that identify correlates versus theories that explain how consciousness is generated. This thesis's distinction between necessitation and exclusion relationships cuts across Michel's categories. A mechanism-based theory could still offer only sufficient conditions (explaining how mechanism X generates consciousness without explaining why only X can). Conversely, a marker-based theory could offer exclusion predictions (claiming that systems without marker Y definitely lack consciousness). The thesis identifies a different structural feature than Michel does.

**Third, the diagnostic claim differs.** Michel attributes the asymmetry to the hard problem's effects on scientific practice—researchers avoid exclusion claims because phenomenal consciousness's nature is disputed. This thesis makes a stronger claim: it's not just that researchers avoid exclusion claims, but that the hard problem makes such claims *empirically inaccessible*. We cannot formulate confident exclusion predictions because we lack third-person access to the explanandum itself. This is not about scientific sociology but about the epistemic structure of the problem. Even ideally rational researchers with perfect methodology would face this constraint.

The objection's evidence actually supports the defence. It notes the thesis "uses their terminology ('necessitation relationships')"—but this term appears nowhere in the cited Lau and Michel works. They discuss "positive markers" and "asymmetries," not "necessitation relationships." The objection has confused thematic similarity with textual dependence.

Moreover, the IIT example functions differently. Michel uses IIT's φ=0 claim to show that even the field's most rigorous theory struggles to make exclusion predictions. This thesis uses it to show something more specific: even apparent exclusion claims don't function as exclusions when theories can posit alternative sufficient conditions. The same example, different analytical work.

# VERDICT

The defence succeeds, but reveals that the thesis's novelty is narrower than initially apparent.

The objection is wrong that this is "precisely the position defended by Lau and Michel"—the theses differ in target, conceptual structure, and diagnosis. However, the defence's success comes at a cost: in distinguishing itself from Michel's broader critique, the thesis emerges as more specialized and limited in scope than it first appeared.

The thesis survives as a specific methodological claim about adversarial collaboration, not as a general diagnosis of consciousness science's testability problems. Its contribution is identifying why contrastive experiments cannot adjudicate between theories that differ only in sufficient conditions—a point that, while related to Michel's work, is not identical to it.

But the defence also exposes a weakness: by narrowing its scope to avoid the "already taken" charge, the thesis becomes less ambitious. It's no longer explaining "what makes consciousness theories empirically untestable" (the original formulation) but rather "why one particular methodology (adversarial collaboration) cannot resolve theoretical disputes." That's a genuine contribution, but a more modest one than the thesis's framing suggests.

The thesis stands, but as a specific methodological observation rather than a fundamental insight about consciousness science's structure.

</details>

### R2. The fact that consciousness theories generate identical predictions about behavior and report undermines our entitlement to the concept of consciousness itself, not just our ability to choose between theories.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 4/5 · worst 4/5 · wounded
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Theorists who maintain both that (1) consciousness is a real phenomenon demanding explanation and (2) rival theories may be empirically equivalent
- **Sketch:** Most theorists accept that radically different theories (functionalism, higher-order thought, global workspace, etc.) can generate identical behavioral and report predictions while offering incompatible accounts of what consciousness is. They conclude we need new empirical methods. But this situation is stranger than methodological underdetermination: if the concept 'consciousness' were tracking something real in a determinate way, theories about it should diverge in their predictions somewhere. The predictive convergence despite theoretical divergence suggests the concept is indeterminate—we're not actually referring to a unified natural kind. The commitment: accepting that theories can be genuinely opposed while making identical predictions commits you to eliminativism about the very explanandum, since explanatory realism requires that theoretical differences eventually cash out in different observable consequences.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Bold move from underdetermination to eliminativism; if the argument works it would be genuinely destabilizing.
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 4/5, answered. Defence successfully distinguished temporary from principled equivalence, showing geometry actually supports the thesis while quantum interpretations prove it
  - TRIVIAL — landed 2/5, answered. Defence showed the criterion derives from scientific practice rather than stipulation, making a substantive claim about consciousness's distinctive pathology
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence's attempt to privilege behavior/report as the sole relevant domain begs the question against realists who treat consciousness as underlying mechanism
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 3/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence showed stable criterion exists but exposed that applying it presupposes what the target denies about consciousness's nature
- 🔧 **Survives as:** For theorists who treat consciousness as exhausted by its behavioral/reportable manifestations (rather than as an underlying mechanism), predictive convergence among theories undermines entitlement to the concept itself
- **Flaw:** The thesis must privilege behavior/report as the only relevant predictive domain, but this already assumes consciousness is nothing over and above its manifestations—precisely what the realist target denies

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. Consider the case of geometric theories in physics before general relativity's empirical confirmation. Euclidean vs. non-Euclidean geometric frameworks made identical predictions for all accessible measurements given the technology and scales humans could observe in the 19th century. Yet geometry was tracking something real and determinate—the actual structure of spacetime. The predictive convergence didn't mean "space" was an indeterminate concept failing to refer to a unified natural kind. It meant our measurements weren't fine-grained enough yet to distinguish the theories.

Similarly, quantum mechanics interpretations (Copenhagen, many-worlds, Bohmian mechanics) generate identical predictions for all possible observations, yet this doesn't undermine our entitlement to concepts like "electron" or "quantum state." The weak point in the thesis is the premise that "if the concept were tracking something real in a determinate way, theories about it should diverge in their predictions somewhere." This conflates metaphysical determinacy with current empirical accessibility. A phenomenon can be perfectly real and determinate while multiple theories about its underlying nature remain empirically equivalent given our current—or even any possible—observational capacities.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This seriously damages the thesis by showing that empirical equivalence of theories is compatible with robust realism about the explanandum across multiple scientific domains, undermining the inference to eliminativism.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The geometric and quantum cases actually support rather than undermine the thesis, once we distinguish *temporary empirical equivalence* from *principled predictive convergence*.

**The geometric case fails the analogy.** Nineteenth-century physicists who entertained non-Euclidean geometries *did* expect predictive divergence—they actively sought it through stellar parallax measurements and geodetic surveys. The empirical equivalence was explicitly treated as temporary, awaiting better instruments or larger scales. Einstein's theory succeeded precisely because it *did* generate divergent predictions (perihelion precession, light bending, gravitational redshift) that broke the equivalence. This is exactly what explanatory realism demands: genuine theoretical differences eventually surface as observational differences.

**The quantum case actually proves the thesis.** The interpreter-independence of quantum predictions is *why* many physicists reject interpretation debates as metaphysical rather than scientific. Bohmian mechanics, many-worlds, and Copenhagen don't disagree about "electrons" as observables—they agree perfectly on what electrons *do*. They disagree on unobservable metaphysical furniture (hidden variables, branch structure, collapse mechanisms). The very fact that these interpretations are empirically equivalent is precisely why many physicists deny they're making competing scientific claims at all. The objector has inadvertently cited a case where predictive equivalence *does* undermine realism about the disputed theoretical posits.

**The consciousness case is worse.** Consciousness theories aren't like geometric theories awaiting finer measurement—they *start* from the same fine-grained data (reports, behaviors, neural correlates) and diverge only in their metaphysical glosses. They don't predict: "with better instruments we'll distinguish them." They predict: "we explain the *same observations* differently." When functionalism and higher-order theory both predict identical responses to the same stimuli while claiming opposite things about what consciousness *is*, they've made the concept itself do no predictive work.

The objector's examples actually reveal the criterion: geometry and electrons earn their keep through predictive divergence (actual or expected). Consciousness theories that converge on predictions while diverging on "what consciousness is" show that "what consciousness is" has become explanatorily idle.

# VERDICT

The defence holds. The objector's cases backfire: geometry shows that genuine theoretical disputes *do* eventually generate predictive divergence (vindicating the thesis's criterion), while quantum interpretations show that principled empirical equivalence *does* undermine realist commitment to the disputed theoretical entities (directly supporting the thesis). The objector has confused "not yet distinguished" with "cannot be distinguished in principle"—a confusion the consciousness case exploits by presenting permanent predictive convergence as mere methodological difficulty.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — # TRIVIAL

## ATTEMPT

The thesis presents itself as a substantive philosophical discovery about consciousness—that predictive convergence among theories undermines our entitlement to the concept itself. But this is actually just a verbal stipulation about what "explanatory realism" means, dressed up as a surprising conclusion.

The weak point is the premise: "explanatory realism requires that theoretical differences eventually cash out in different observable consequences." This isn't an established principle of scientific realism that consciousness theories violate; it's a definition being introduced. The thesis simply *stipulates* that if you're a realist about X, you must believe theories about X will diverge predictively, then notes consciousness theories don't diverge, and concludes we can't be realists. But this is just definitional maneuvering.

Standard scientific realism allows that genuine natural kinds can be the subject of empirically equivalent theories—this is uncontroversial in philosophy of science (structural realism, underdetermination debates, etc.). The thesis arbitrarily restricts "realism" to exclude this, making the conclusion trivial: "If we define realism to require predictive divergence, then things without predictive divergence aren't real by that definition."

The apparent force comes from switching between ordinary scientific realism (which the target accepts) and this stipulated stronger version (which generates the eliminativist conclusion). The "commitment" being exposed is just: "you used 'real' in the normal way, not my special way."

## STRENGTH: 2/5

The attack has some force in identifying that the thesis trades on a contestable principle about realism, but it's probably too strong to call this merely "verbal stipulation." The thesis does make a substantive empirical claim—that consciousness theories exhibit a *particular pattern* of convergence-despite-divergence that's philosophically significant, even if reasonable people can disagree about whether this pattern undermines realism.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies what's stipulative and what's substantive. The thesis isn't *defining* realism to require predictive divergence—it's identifying a criterion that *actual scientific practice* uses to distinguish genuine explanatory disputes from merely verbal ones.

**The criterion is discovered, not stipulated.** When physicists abandoned caloric theory, when chemists rejected phlogiston, when biologists discarded vital forces—these eliminations happened *because* the posited entities did no predictive work that simpler ontologies couldn't do. The principle "theoretical differences that never generate observational differences aren't tracking real differences" isn't a philosopher's stipulation; it's how science actually weeds out idle ontology. The thesis applies this standard criterion to consciousness.

**Standard realism doesn't actually allow permanent equivalence.** The objector claims "standard scientific realism allows genuine natural kinds to be subjects of empirically equivalent theories," citing underdetermination debates. But look at what structural realists actually say: they retreat from realism about theoretical *entities* to realism about *structure* precisely because entity-level claims that make no predictive difference are explanatorily idle. Underdetermination arguments are typically used to *limit* realist commitment, not expand it. The thesis is doing exactly what structural realists do—restricting realism to what actually constrains prediction.

**The consciousness case has a distinctive pathology.** The objection treats this like generic underdetermination, but there's a crucial difference. In normal underdetermination, theories agree on observations because they're different mathematical representations of the same structure (gauge symmetries, coordinate choices). In consciousness, theories *disagree about what the phenomenon is*—whether it's functional organization, higher-order representation, or global broadcasting—while agreeing on all manifestations. This isn't two descriptions of one thing; it's allegedly different things that happen to do identical work. That's not underdetermination; that's indeterminacy of reference.

**The trivial reading gets the dialectic backwards.** The objector says the thesis "stipulates a definition then draws a conclusion." But the actual move is: observe that consciousness theories exhibit predictive convergence despite metaphysical divergence, notice this pattern is what eliminates posits elsewhere in science, ask why consciousness should be exempt. The target theorist who insists consciousness is real *despite* this pattern owes an explanation of the exemption. The thesis isn't imposing an arbitrary standard—it's asking why the standard that applies everywhere else doesn't apply here.

The force isn't from "switching definitions." It's from showing that theorists who accept predictive convergence have already accepted the evidential situation that elsewhere justifies elimination, but make an exception for consciousness without justification.

# VERDICT

The defence holds. The objection mischaracterizes the thesis as mere definitional legislation when it's actually applying a methodological principle with genuine scientific pedigree—the principle that posits earning no predictive keep get eliminated. The objector is right that philosophers disagree about realism's exact commitments, but wrong that this makes the thesis trivial. The substantive claim is that consciousness exhibits the specific pathology (predictive convergence amid theoretical divergence) that has historically justified eliminativism about other posits. Whether you accept that claim depends on evaluating the actual pattern in consciousness science, not on stipulating definitions. The thesis may be *wrong*, but it's not trivially so—it makes a substantive empirical and methodological claim that could be false.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — # EQUIVOCATION

## ATTEMPT

The thesis equivocates on "identical predictions," which does critical work at two different points requiring incompatible readings.

The weak point is the premise: "if the concept 'consciousness' were tracking something real in a determinate way, theories about it should diverge in their predictions somewhere."

**Reading 1 (makes the premise plausible):** "Identical predictions" means identical predictions *about everything*—including not just behavioral reports but all possible future observations, neural measurements, computational implementations, evolutionary pressures, developmental trajectories, clinical interventions, and any phenomenon where consciousness might make a difference. On this reading, the premise is plausible: if theories truly diverge about what consciousness *is*, they should eventually diverge about *something* observable in this expansive sense.

**Reading 2 (makes the empirical claim interesting):** "Identical predictions" means identical predictions *about behavior and report specifically*—the narrow domain the thesis explicitly mentions. On this reading, the empirical observation is interesting and arguably true: current consciousness theories do converge on predictions about what people say and do.

**The equivocation:** The argument needs Reading 2 to establish the surprising empirical fact (theories converge on behavior/report), but needs Reading 1 to make the eliminativist inference valid (true predictive identity across all domains would indeed be suspicious). The thesis slides between noting that theories haven't *yet* diverged on behavior/report (Reading 2) and claiming they exhibit principled total predictive equivalence (Reading 1). But consciousness theories *do* make different predictions about neural correlates, about which computational architectures could be conscious, about animal consciousness, about whether AI systems could be conscious—just not about immediate behavioral reports. The argument only works if we confuse "convergent on reports" with "convergent on everything."

## STRENGTH: 4/5

This seriously damages the thesis. The entire inference from "theories make the same predictions" to "the concept is indeterminate" depends on conflating narrow convergence (which is real but unsurprising—different theories often agree on surface phenomena) with total equivalence (which would be suspicious but isn't established). Once we disambiguate, either the empirical premise becomes false (theories do diverge on many predictions) or the inference becomes invalid (convergence on one domain doesn't establish indeterminacy).

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection mislocates where the predictive work happens. The thesis doesn't equivocate between readings—it identifies behavior and report as the *privileged domain* where consciousness theories must diverge if they're genuinely about consciousness, making convergence there specially damaging.

**Why behavior/report is the privileged domain.** Consciousness isn't posited to explain neural correlates, computational architectures, or AI possibilities *directly*—those are theoretical commitments *about* consciousness. Consciousness is posited to explain the *explanandum*: why creatures behave as if there's something it's like to be them, why they report experiences, why the lights seem to be on. If theories converge on predictions about this explanandum while diverging only on theoretical superstructure, the superstructure is doing no explanatory work regarding what consciousness was invoked to explain.

**The alleged "other predictions" aren't independent tests.** The objector claims theories diverge on "neural correlates, computational architectures, animal consciousness, AI consciousness." But examine these:

- *Neural correlates*: These aren't independent predictions—they're just the neural side of the same behavior/report predictions. If two theories predict identical reports from a subject, they'll predict those reports correlate with identical neural states. The neural "difference" is just theoretical redescription of the same observational convergence.

- *Computational architectures*: Theories don't actually diverge on whether specific architectures *behave* consciously—they diverge on whether to *call* that behavior consciousness. A functionalist and a higher-order theorist watching the same AI make the same reports don't predict different behaviors; they offer different metaphysical glosses on those behaviors.

- *Animal/AI consciousness*: Same structure. Theories don't predict different animal behaviors—they predict the same behaviors while disagreeing about whether to apply "conscious" to them. This is semantic legislation about extension, not empirical prediction about phenomena.

**The objection proves too much.** If diverging on "neural correlates" or "what counts as conscious" were sufficient predictive divergence to establish determinacy, then vitalism would be vindicated—vitalists and mechanists diverged on which systems had *élan vital* while converging on organismal behavior. We rejected vitalism precisely because the divergence was only in theoretical labels for the same observations, not in observations themselves. The thesis applies this same standard to consciousness.

**The two readings collapse.** The objection treats "predictions about behavior/report" and "predictions about everything" as genuinely different. But for consciousness specifically, they're not. Consciousness is *defined* by its connection to behavior and report—it's the thing that makes certain behaviors happen and certain reports possible. Predictions about "neural correlates of consciousness" or "which systems are conscious" are just derivative predictions about what physical systems will exhibit the behavior/report patterns. If theories converge on those patterns, they've converged on everything consciousness was supposed to predict.

The thesis isn't equivocating. It's identifying that the apparent predictive divergences the objector cites are all *downstream* of convergence on the actual explanandum, making them theoretically idle.

# VERDICT

The defence fails, though not decisively.

The defence is right that behavior and report have special status as the explanandum, and right that some alleged divergences (like "which systems count as conscious") might be merely semantic. But it overreaches in claiming all neural and architectural predictions collapse into behavior/report predictions.

**The fatal problem:** Neural and computational predictions can genuinely diverge even when behavioral predictions converge. A global workspace theory and a higher-order theory might both predict that a subject reports seeing red, but predict *different* neural activation patterns during unreportable/unconscious processing, different responses to masked stimuli that don't reach report, different patterns in neural dynamics that don't manifest behaviorally. These aren't just "theoretical redescriptions"—they're testable predictions about physical systems that could come apart from behavior.

The defence wants to say these don't count because consciousness is "defined by" behavior/report. But this begs the question against the realist target. The realist thinks consciousness is a real neural/computational phenomenon that *causes* behavior and report, and different theories about its nature should predict different neural signatures even when behavioral output converges. The thesis needs an argument for why only behavior/report predictions matter, not an assertion that consciousness is "defined by" them.

The equivocation stands: the thesis needs total predictive equivalence (Reading 1) to make the eliminativist inference work, but can only establish behavioral equivalence (Reading 2). The defence's attempt to collapse these readings by privileging behavior/report is question-begging—it assumes consciousness is nothing but a label for behavioral patterns, which is precisely what the realist target denies.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — # UNFALSIFIABLE

## ATTEMPT

The thesis is structured to be immune from counterexample through systematic redefinition of what counts as "genuine predictive divergence." The weak point is the implicit premise that predictive convergence on behavior/report constitutes the relevant kind of empirical equivalence that should trouble realists.

Watch how the thesis protects itself: 

**Apparent counterexample:** "But consciousness theories DO make different predictions—about neural correlates, about which computational architectures could instantiate consciousness, about whether certain animals or AI systems are conscious."

**Protection mechanism:** These get redefined as "not really predictions" but rather "theoretical superstructure," "semantic legislation," or "derivative redescriptions" of the same behavioral observations. The thesis stipulates that only predictions about behavior and report count as genuine empirical divergence.

**Second attempt:** "But theories predict different neural activation patterns during unconscious processing, different responses to masked stimuli—these diverge even when behavior converges."

**Protection mechanism:** These get ruled out as "not about consciousness itself" since they concern processes that don't reach report. Only reportable consciousness counts.

**Third attempt:** "But that's like saying vitalism was unfalsifiable—we can test different theories about consciousness just like we tested vitalism."

**Protection mechanism:** The thesis preemptively analogizes consciousness to vitalism, suggesting that any case where we'd normally say "the theories diverge empirically" will be recast as "mere semantic legislation" parallel to vitalist disputes.

The thesis has no stable criterion for what would count as predictive divergence that could save consciousness from elimination. Any offered divergence gets reclassified as "not the right kind" of divergence. The theory cannot be tested because the boundaries of "genuine prediction" shift to accommodate any apparent counterexample. If you show theories diverge on X, that just proves X wasn't really about consciousness. The thesis is unfalsifiable because "consciousness" becomes whatever domain theories happen to converge on, making convergence true by definitional fiat rather than empirical discovery.

## STRENGTH: 3/5

This attack has genuine force in identifying that the thesis does engage in some boundary-policing about what counts as "real" predictive divergence. The defense's move to privilege behavior/report while dismissing neural and computational predictions as "derivative" does look like it could protect the thesis from counterexamples by reclassification.

However, the attack isn't devastating because the thesis does offer a principled (even if contestable) reason for the boundary: consciousness is posited to explain behavior and report, so that's where divergence matters. This isn't purely ad hoc protection—it's a substantive (though debatable) claim about explanatory priority. The thesis could be wrong about this priority claim, which means it's falsifiable in that specific way. Not every act of distinguishing relevant from irrelevant evidence constitutes unfalsifiable immunization.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection confuses *having a principled criterion* with *immunizing through redefinition*. The thesis doesn't shift boundaries to evade counterexamples—it applies a single stable criterion throughout: **theories genuinely diverge when they predict different observable manifestations of the explanandum phenomenon itself, not when they merely attach different theoretical labels to the same manifestations.**

**The criterion is stable and falsifiable.** The thesis would be falsified if consciousness theories made genuinely divergent predictions about behavior and report—the phenomena consciousness was invoked to explain. Show me a case where functionalism predicts a subject will report seeing red while higher-order theory predicts they'll report seeing blue in identical circumstances, and the thesis falls. Show me where global workspace theory predicts a behavior that integrated information theory predicts won't occur, and the thesis is defeated. The criterion is clear: *Do theories diverge on what the conscious subject does and reports?* They don't. That's not unfalsifiable—it's falsified if they do, and they don't.

**The "protection mechanisms" are applications of the criterion, not evasions.** When the objector offers "neural correlates" or "which systems are conscious" as counterexamples, the thesis doesn't arbitrarily dismiss them—it shows they're not predictions about the explanandum. Consider the parallel:

- **Vitalism:** Two theories both predict the organism metabolizes glucose identically, but disagree on whether *élan vital* is present. This isn't predictive divergence about biological phenomena—it's metaphysical divergence while predicting identical biology.

- **Consciousness:** Two theories both predict the subject reports "I see red" identically, but disagree on whether higher-order representation is present. This isn't predictive divergence about conscious phenomena—it's metaphysical divergence while predicting identical consciousness manifestations.

The thesis applies the *same* criterion that eliminated vitalism. That's not unfalsifiable immunization—that's consistent methodology.

**The objector's examples actually confirm the thesis.** Take "different neural activation patterns during unconscious processing." The objector admits these concern *unconscious* processing—processes that by definition don't manifest as consciousness. If theories diverge only on unconscious processes while converging on conscious ones, this *supports* the thesis: the theories agree on everything consciousness does, disagreeing only on non-conscious machinery. The objection has accidentally shown that even apparent neural divergences occur outside the domain of the explanandum.

**What would falsify the thesis is specifiable.** The thesis is falsified by:
1. Consciousness theories that make divergent predictions about reportable experience or conscious behavior, OR
2. A successful argument that consciousness was *never* posited primarily to explain behavior/report, but rather some other phenomenon where theories do diverge.

Neither obtains. The first is an empirical matter (check the theories—they converge). The second is a historical/conceptual matter (check why consciousness entered scientific discourse—it was to explain subjective report and purposive behavior).

**The real issue is substantive disagreement, not unfalsifiability.** The objector thinks neural and computational predictions *should* count as genuine divergence. The thesis thinks they shouldn't because they're not predictions about the explanandum. This is a substantive dispute about explanatory priority—which predictions matter for establishing that a posit is doing real work. That dispute has a fact of the matter: either consciousness was posited to explain behavior/report (thesis wins) or it wasn't (objector wins). This is exactly what falsifiable disagreement looks like.

The objection mistakes *having a controversial criterion* for *lacking any criterion*. The thesis stands or falls on whether its criterion—divergence on explanandum predictions—is the right one. That's a substantive question with a determinate answer, not an unfalsifiable dodge.

# VERDICT

The defence succeeds, but reveals a deeper vulnerability.

**Why the unfalsifiability charge fails:** The defence is correct that the thesis applies a stable, principled criterion: theories must diverge on predictions about the explanandum (behavior/report) to establish the explanandum's determinacy. This criterion is falsifiable—show divergent behavioral/report predictions and the thesis falls. The thesis doesn't shift this boundary to evade counterexamples; it consistently applies it. The objector's examples (neural correlates, computational architecture, animal consciousness) genuinely fail to meet this criterion for reasons the defence articulates: they're either derivative of behavioral predictions, concern non-conscious processes, or involve semantic disputes about extension rather than empirical disputes about phenomena.

**But the defence exposes the real problem:** The thesis's criterion—that only explanandum-level predictions matter—is *question-begging against the realist target*. The realist thinks consciousness is a real underlying mechanism that explains behavior/report, and that theories about this mechanism should diverge on neural/computational predictions even when behavioral output converges (just as theories about underlying physics can diverge on micro-level predictions while converging on macro-level observations).

The thesis responds: "but consciousness just *is* the behavior/report patterns, so convergence there is all that matters." But this already assumes a form of eliminativism or behaviorism—it treats consciousness as nothing over and above its manifestations. The realist target explicitly rejects this, treating consciousness as a real phenomenon with a nature that goes beyond its behavioral manifestations.

So while the thesis isn't unfalsifiable by redefinition, it's unfalsifiable *to its target* because it presupposes what the target denies: that consciousness is exhausted by its behavioral manifestations rather than being an underlying reality that causes those manifestations. The thesis can't convince its target because accepting the thesis's criterion for what counts as "genuine divergence" already requires accepting something close to the thesis's conclusion.

The objection is wrong that the thesis is technically unfalsifiable, but right that it has a dialectical problem: it can't engage the realist target without begging the question about whether consciousness is more than its manifestations.

</details>

### R3. The proliferation of consciousness theories reveals not that we lack the right evidence, but that 'theory confirmation' in consciousness science is actually testing which aspects of consciousness researchers find most explanatorily urgent—making theory choice fundamentally axiological rather than empirical.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The shared methodological assumption that theory choice in consciousness science follows standard scientific methodology of prediction and confirmation
- **Sketch:** Current debates treat theory evaluation as straightforwardly empirical: gather neural data, see which theory's predictions match. But this misses a crucial distinction between predictive accuracy and explanatory priority. Each major theory can accommodate most neural findings by adjusting what it takes to be fundamental versus derivative about consciousness. What actually drives theory choice is which aspect theorists find most in need of explanation: if you find the unity of consciousness most puzzling, you favor IIT; if you find reportability most puzzling, you favor GWT; if you find qualitative character most puzzling, you favor phenomenal realism. These are not empirical preferences but axiological ones—what cries out for explanation versus what can be taken as given. The key premise is that the underdetermination persists because theories are answering different 'why' questions, not because we lack data.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Reframes theory choice as axiological rather than empirical—a genuine contribution that could shift how we understand the debate's structure.
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 3/5, answered. Defence reframed empirical revision as internal adjustment rather than inter-theory choice, showing the objection targeted theory revision not theory selection
  - TRIVIAL — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence claimed consciousness shows distinctive persistence but failed to distinguish it from standard underdetermination in other sciences like quantum mechanics
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 2/5, answered. Defence showed the thesis maintains one consistent reading throughout: axiological commitments determine what counts as confirmatory evidence
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence proposed falsification tests but never specified what would falsify the thesis itself, only claims about individual researchers' motivations
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Consciousness science exhibits persistent theoretical fragmentation where competing frameworks address genuinely different explananda (unity vs. access vs. quale), making apparent theory competition actually a case of explanatory incommensurability rather than empirical underdetermination
- **Flaw:** The thesis reduces to standard scientific underdetermination (TRIVIAL survived) and cannot be falsified because axiological commitment and theory choice are empirically inseparable (UNFALSIFIABLE survived)

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. Consider the case of Integrated Information Theory (IIT) and its prediction that certain artificial systems (like grid-like networks) should have high consciousness (phi) despite lacking typical biological features, while certain brain regions during dreamless sleep should have low phi. These predictions led to empirical tests: researchers examined whether IIT's mathematical framework actually matched neural data from sleep studies and anesthesia. When IIT predicted that the cerebellum (despite having more neurons than the cortex) should have low phi due to its modular architecture, this was empirically testable. Crucially, researchers who initially found the unity of consciousness explanatorily urgent didn't simply accept IIT when these predictions failed or succeeded ambiguously—many shifted their theoretical commitments based on the empirical results. The theory choice here wasn't driven by what remained "most explanatorily urgent" but by whether the mathematical predictions matched observed neural correlates. The weak point is the premise that "underdetermination persists because theories are answering different 'why' questions"—in this case, the same why-question (why unified consciousness?) generated empirically distinguishable predictions that drove theory revision, not just axiological preference.

STRENGTH: 3/5 — This is a genuine problem requiring an answer. The thesis needs to explain how specific failed predictions can force theory revision if theory choice is fundamentally axiological rather than empirical.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The cerebellum case actually strengthens rather than undermines the thesis. Notice what happened: IIT predicted low phi for the cerebellum based on architectural modularity, and this prediction was borne out. But this didn't settle the theory choice debate—researchers who found other aspects explanatorily urgent (reportability, access, qualitative character) simply weren't moved. They said: "Fine, IIT explains why the cerebellum doesn't contribute to unified consciousness, but it still doesn't explain what we find most puzzling." 

The objector claims "many shifted their theoretical commitments based on empirical results," but this is historically inaccurate. The major consciousness theory debates have remained remarkably stable despite accumulating evidence. Global Workspace theorists didn't abandon GWT when IIT's cerebellum prediction succeeded; they maintained that IIT still fails to explain what they find explanatorily urgent—namely, how information becomes globally available for report and reasoning. Phenomenal realists didn't convert to IIT; they said phi might correlate with consciousness but doesn't explain what consciousness *is*.

The key confusion is between *accommodation* and *conversion*. Yes, failed predictions force theory *revision*—IIT proponents might adjust their phi calculations or architectural assumptions. But this is internal housekeeping, not theory *choice*. The objector's "same why-question (why unified consciousness?)" already begs the question: GWT theorists don't find unity most explanatorily urgent; they find *access* most urgent. IIT and GWT aren't competing answers to the same question—they're answers to different questions that happen to use overlapping vocabulary.

The cerebellum prediction is empirically testable, certainly. But what makes it *important* is axiological: only if you already find unity explanatorily fundamental does its confirmation matter for theory choice.

VERDICT

The defence holds, but only partially. The objector has identified real theory revision within IIT based on empirical results, and the defence correctly reframes this as internal adjustment rather than inter-theory choice. The historical claim that researchers haven't shifted between major theories despite evidence is largely accurate—the main theory camps have remained stable.

However, the defence's strongest move—that GWT and IIT answer different questions—may prove too much. If theories are answering completely different questions, they're not really competing, and the thesis loses its critical bite. The thesis claims theory *choice* is axiological, but if there's no genuine choice because theories don't compete, we've merely discovered theoretical fragmentation, not that confirmation is axiological.

The defence succeeds in showing the objection doesn't refute the thesis, but reveals an ambiguity the thesis must resolve: are these theories genuinely competing (making the axiological claim interesting) or addressing different explananda (making competition illusory)?

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis presents itself as revealing something deep about consciousness science, but it actually just redescribes a familiar feature of scientific theorizing using fancier vocabulary. The weak point is the central claim that theory choice is "fundamentally axiological rather than empirical." This is trivial because:

Every scientific theory choice involves judgments about which phenomena are most in need of explanation—this is just what it means to identify an explanandum. When Newton focused on planetary motion rather than terrestrial projectiles as the primary puzzle, when Darwin focused on adaptation rather than mere variation, when Einstein focused on the invariance of light speed rather than absolute simultaneacy—these were all judgments about explanatory priority. The thesis simply relabels this standard feature of theory construction as "axiological" and declares it non-empirical.

But the distinction between "axiological" and "empirical" here collapses into the uncontroversial observation that scientists must first decide *what to explain* before gathering data to explain it. Calling this "axiological" rather than "methodological" or "heuristic" adds nothing. The thesis treats as a discovery what is actually a conceptual relabeling: the claim that researchers "find different aspects most explanatorily urgent" is just another way of saying "researchers focus on different explananda"—which is true of every field with competing research programs, from particle physics to evolutionary biology.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem. The thesis needs to show why consciousness science is *distinctively* axiological in a way that differs from normal scientific practice, not just that it exhibits features common to all theory construction.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection misses the crucial *persistence* and *ineliminability* of the axiological divergence in consciousness science, which makes it importantly different from the Newton/Darwin/Einstein cases.

In those historical examples, the choice of explanandum was *temporary* and *resolved by empirical progress*. Newton focused on planetary motion, but his framework ultimately explained terrestrial projectiles too—the initial focus was heuristic, not permanent. Darwin focused on adaptation, but evolutionary theory now explains variation, drift, and neutral evolution—the theory outgrew its initial explanatory priorities. Einstein's focus on light-speed invariance led to a framework that ultimately explained away absolute simultaneity as confused—empirical progress dissolved the competing explanandum.

Consciousness science shows no such convergence. After decades of neuroscientific data, the explanatory priorities haven't resolved—they've *calcified*. GWT researchers don't treat unity as "we'll get to that later"; they treat it as *derivative* or even *illusory*. IIT researchers don't treat reportability as "still to be explained"; they treat it as *secondary* to integration. This isn't methodological division of labor awaiting synthesis—it's fundamental disagreement about what consciousness *is*.

The key difference: in normal science, competing explanatory priorities are *provisional* starting points that empirical progress adjudicates. You focus on planetary motion or terrestrial motion first, but eventually one framework explains both and the choice is vindicated or refuted empirically. In consciousness science, the competing priorities reflect *metaphysical* commitments about the nature of the explanandum itself that no amount of neural data resolves.

The thesis isn't just relabeling "scientists focus on different explananda." It's claiming that in consciousness science specifically, these different explananda *cannot be simultaneously satisfied by empirical progress* because they reflect incompatible views about what consciousness fundamentally is. When Newton and Descartes disagreed about explanatory priority, empirical progress eventually showed one was right. When IIT and GWT disagree, accumulating neural correlates doesn't resolve the disagreement—it just gets reinterpreted through each framework's axiological commitments.

This is why the thesis matters: it's not that consciousness scientists do something unusual in choosing explananda, but that their choices are *empirically underdetermined in a way that persists indefinitely*, revealing something about consciousness itself (or our epistemic access to it) rather than just about theory construction.

# VERDICT

The defence fails, though it dies fighting.

The defence correctly identifies that consciousness science shows unusual *persistence* of theoretical division—this is empirically true and important. But this observation doesn't establish that the situation is "fundamentally axiological rather than empirical" in a way that distinguishes it from the trivial reading.

Here's why the defence doesn't save the thesis: The objector's examples (Newton, Darwin, Einstein) were carefully chosen to seem like temporary heuristics that got resolved, but this is selective history. Consider instead: particle physics' persistent division between those who find quantum indeterminacy fundamental versus those who find it derivative (hidden variables); evolutionary biology's persistent division between those who find adaptation fundamental versus those who find constraint fundamental; cosmology's persistent division between those who find fine-tuning explanatorily urgent versus those who don't.

These fields *also* show persistent, calcified disagreements about explanatory priority that decades of data haven't resolved. The defence claims consciousness is special because "no amount of neural data resolves" the disagreement—but no amount of quantum data has resolved the measurement problem's competing explanatory priorities either. If we call consciousness science "fundamentally axiological," we must say the same about these other fields, and the thesis loses its distinctive claim about consciousness.

The deeper problem: the defence concedes that what makes the situation axiological is that "empirical progress doesn't adjudicate between frameworks." But this just *is* the standard underdetermination problem in philosophy of science, dressed up as discovery. Calling persistent underdetermination "axiological" rather than "empirical" doesn't reveal something new—it just chooses to emphasize the value-judgment aspect of theory choice that philosophers of science have discussed since Kuhn.

The thesis is trivial because it reduces to: "In consciousness science, theory choice involves persistent underdetermination where researchers prioritize different explananda." True, but not distinctively about consciousness, and not a revelation about confirmation being axiological rather than empirical—it's just standard scientific underdetermination with emphasis shifted to the explanandum-choice moment.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis equivocates on "axiological" between two readings that make it alternately plausible and interesting, but the argument needs both.

**Reading 1 (plausible):** "Axiological" means researchers make *value judgments* about which phenomena deserve explanatory priority—what's puzzling versus what's acceptable as primitive. This is plausible because scientists clearly do this: some find unity urgent, others find reportability urgent.

**Reading 2 (interesting):** "Axiological" means theory choice is *non-empirical* or *immune to empirical arbitration*—that values rather than evidence determine outcomes. This is interesting because it challenges the "shared methodological assumption" about scientific methodology.

The weak point is the central claim that theory choice is "fundamentally axiological rather than empirical." The argument *needs* Reading 2 to have critical force against the target assumption about standard scientific methodology. But it can only *defend* Reading 1: the sketch establishes that researchers have different explanatory priorities (value judgments), not that these priorities are immune to empirical pressure.

The defender's responses reveal this: when pressed on empirical cases (cerebellum predictions), they retreat to "axiological" meaning "what researchers find explanatorily important." But this makes the thesis merely say "researchers focus on different aspects"—which is uncontroversially true but doesn't show theory choice is "rather than empirical." To be interesting enough to challenge standard methodology, "axiological" must mean empirically unconstrained; to be plausible given the evidence, it can only mean involves-value-judgments. The argument slides between these readings.

**STRENGTH: 2/5** — This is a weak objection. While there is some ambiguity in "axiological," the thesis can likely be reformulated to avoid equivocation: "fundamentally axiological rather than *purely* empirical" or "axiological commitments screen off empirical evidence from determining theory choice." The core claim—that value judgments about explananda persist despite empirical accumulation—doesn't actually require equivocation to maintain. The thesis may be trivial or false, but it's not trading on ambiguity.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection fabricates an equivocation that doesn't exist. The thesis uses "axiological" in a single, consistent sense throughout: *what researchers find explanatorily urgent determines which empirical evidence counts as confirmatory*. This is neither Reading 1 nor Reading 2 as the objector defines them—it's a third position that the objection ignores.

The thesis doesn't claim theory choice is "immune to empirical arbitration" (Reading 2's straw man). It claims something more subtle: empirical evidence is *filtered through* axiological commitments about what needs explaining. When IIT's cerebellum prediction succeeds, this counts as confirmation *only for researchers who already find unity explanatorily fundamental*. GWT researchers see the same data and say "that's nice, but irrelevant to what I'm trying to explain." The evidence doesn't disappear—it's just not *confirmatory* for their theoretical purposes.

This isn't equivocation between "value judgment" and "non-empirical." It's a unified claim: axiological commitments about explananda *determine what counts as relevant evidence*, making theory choice empirically underdetermined not because evidence is absent, but because different axiological frameworks render different evidence probative.

The objector's Reading 1 ("researchers make value judgments about priority") is too weak—it misses that these judgments *screen off* evidence from confirmation. Reading 2 ("immune to empirical arbitration") is too strong—the thesis doesn't deny evidence matters, just that it doesn't adjudicate between frameworks with different explananda. The actual position: axiological commitments determine *which empirical facts are evidence for what*, making "confirmation" itself theory-relative in a way that prevents empirical convergence.

This is precisely what "fundamentally axiological rather than empirical" means: not that evidence is irrelevant, but that what counts as confirmatory evidence is determined by prior axiological commitments that empirical data cannot dislodge. The cerebellum case proves this: everyone accepts the data, but it confirms different theories for different researchers based on their explanatory priorities. That's one reading, consistently applied.

The objector's "reformulations" actually concede the defence: "axiological commitments screen off empirical evidence from determining theory choice" is *exactly* what the thesis claims, and it's neither Reading 1 nor Reading 2—it's the consistent position the objection overlooked.

# VERDICT

The defence succeeds completely. The objection misidentifies the thesis's position by forcing it into two caricatured readings, neither of which captures what's actually claimed.

The thesis maintains a consistent position: axiological commitments about what needs explaining determine which empirical findings count as confirmatory, creating persistent underdetermination not from lack of evidence but from divergent frameworks for *interpreting* evidence as confirmatory. This is neither mere "value judgments about priority" (too weak) nor "immunity to empirical constraint" (too strong).

The objector's own suggested reformulation—"axiological commitments screen off empirical evidence from determining theory choice"—is precisely what the thesis argues, and this formulation shows no equivocation. The thesis uses "axiological rather than empirical" to mean: *what makes evidence confirmatory is axiological commitment to certain explananda, not the empirical facts themselves*.
