The cerebellum example illustrates this perfectly: identical empirical data (cerebellar architecture, phi calculations, consciousness reports) is interpreted as confirming IIT by those who find unity explanatorily urgent, and as irrelevant by those who don't. The evidence is fully empirical; the determination of what it confirms is axiological. One consistent reading throughout.

The objection fails because it assumes "axiological rather than empirical" must mean either trivial value-involvement or complete empirical independence, missing the thesis's actual claim about how axiological commitments mediate between evidence and confirmation.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis is unfalsifiable because any apparent counterexample—any case where empirical evidence seems to drive theory choice in consciousness science—can be redefined as researchers "finding different aspects explanatorily urgent." The weak point is the central premise that "theory choice is fundamentally axiological rather than empirical."

Consider what would falsify this: presumably, a case where consciousness researchers *change their theory based purely on empirical results* without changing what they find "explanatorily urgent." But the thesis can always reinterpret such cases by claiming the researchers must have shifted their axiological commitments. If a GWT proponent converts to IIT after seeing integration data, the thesis says: "They must now find unity more explanatorily urgent than access." If they insist they still find access most urgent but the evidence was overwhelming, the thesis says: "Their stated priorities don't match their revealed preferences—their theory choice shows what they really find urgent."

The framework makes it impossible to distinguish between: (a) evidence forcing theory change despite stable axiological commitments, and (b) evidence revealing/causing shifted axiological commitments. Every theory change gets classified as axiological because "theory choice reveals axiological commitments" is definitionally true—whatever theory you choose reveals what you find urgent. The thesis protects itself by making axiological commitment *identical with* theory choice rather than an independent variable that could be held constant while evidence varies.

The cerebellum case illustrates this: when defenders claim GWT researchers "weren't moved" by IIT's success, this is unfalsifiable—if some GWT researchers *were* moved and converted, the thesis just reclassifies them as having shifted their explanatory priorities, making the original claim irrefutable.

**STRENGTH: 4/5** — This is a serious problem requiring a real answer. The thesis needs to specify what would count as a genuine case of empirical theory choice that *isn't* axiological, or admit that it's redefined "axiological" to cover all theory choice, making the claim unfalsifiable and therefore empty.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection confuses unfalsifiability with explanatory depth. Yes, the thesis can accommodate any theory change by citing axiological shifts—but this is a *feature*, not a bug, because it reveals what's actually happening in consciousness science. The thesis isn't claiming axiological commitments are *immutable*; it's claiming they're *fundamental* to theory choice in a way that makes "purely empirical" change impossible to distinguish from axiologically-mediated change.

Here's the crucial test the objection overlooks: **Can researchers articulate empirical conditions that would make them abandon their theory while maintaining their explanatory priorities?** This is falsifiable and empirically checkable.

Ask a GWT researcher: "If integration data accumulated overwhelmingly, would you abandon GWT?" Many would say: "No—I'd either incorporate integration into GWT's framework, or argue that integration correlates with but doesn't explain consciousness, because what needs explaining is still *access*, not unity." This isn't the thesis making them say this—it's what they actually say. The axiological commitment is *stated*, not imputed.

Similarly, ask an IIT researcher: "If reportability turned out to be perfectly correlated with consciousness in all cases, would you abandon IIT?" Many would say: "No—reportability might be a reliable indicator, but phi still explains *why* there's something it's like, which is what needs explaining." Again, they explicitly maintain their explanatory priority despite hypothetical evidence.

The thesis is falsified if researchers systematically say: "Yes, if evidence X accumulated, I would abandon my theory *even though* it still best explains what I find most puzzling." Or if they say: "Evidence X forced me to change theories, but I still find the same aspect most explanatorily urgent—my old theory just failed empirically at explaining it."

The objection's case (a)—"evidence forcing theory change despite stable axiological commitments"—is precisely what would falsify the thesis. And it's empirically testable by asking researchers whether evidence could make them abandon theories that best address their explanatory priorities. The thesis predicts they'll say no (or qualify heavily); if they systematically say yes and act accordingly, the thesis is false.

The cerebellum case doesn't show unfalsifiability—it shows the thesis making a risky prediction that held. The thesis predicted GWT researchers wouldn't be moved by IIT's architectural predictions because they don't find unity explanatorily fundamental. This was testable: did they convert en masse? They didn't. If they had, while maintaining "access is still what needs explaining," that would falsify the thesis.

The objection's move—"the thesis just reclassifies converts as having shifted priorities"—fails because axiological commitments are *publicly stated* and can be tracked independently. If someone converts from GWT to IIT while continuing to publish that "the real puzzle is global access, not integration," that would be evidence against the thesis. The thesis doesn't make axiological commitment "identical with" theory choice—it claims the former *determines* the latter, which is a falsifiable causal claim.

# VERDICT

The defence fails. It identifies a potential falsification strategy (asking researchers about counterfactual evidence), but this doesn't save the thesis from the unfalsifiability problem.

The fatal issue: the defence's proposed test—"Can researchers articulate empirical conditions that would make them abandon their theory while maintaining their explanatory priorities?"—actually *confirms* the objection's point rather than refuting it. Here's why:

If researchers say "No, no evidence would make me abandon my theory while I maintain these priorities," this just shows their theory is *defined by* their priorities, making the thesis trivially true by stipulation. If they say "Yes, evidence X would do it," but then when evidence X arrives they either (i) abandon their stated priorities, or (ii) reinterpret the evidence, the thesis can always claim "see, axiological commitments were fundamental after all." 

The defence claims axiological commitments are "publicly stated and can be tracked independently," but this is naive about scientific practice. Researchers' stated explanatory priorities are notoriously flexible and post-hoc. When someone converts from GWT to IIT, they don't typically publish "I still think access is the real puzzle, but I'm abandoning the theory that best explains it." They *reframe* their explanatory priorities: "I used to think access was fundamental, but I now see that integration is the deeper question." The thesis can always interpret this as axiological shift driving theory change, making it unfalsifiable.

The cerebellum case doesn't help. The defence claims "the thesis predicted GWT researchers wouldn't convert en masse, and they didn't." But this is too coarse-grained. Some researchers did shift (the field isn't perfectly static), and for each one, the thesis can say either: "They shifted their axiological commitments" (if they changed theories) or "Their axiological commitments held firm" (if they didn't). The thesis covers all outcomes.

The deepest problem: the defence never actually specifies what evidence would falsify the thesis *itself*. It specifies what would falsify claims about particular researchers' motivations, but not what would show that theory choice in consciousness science *isn't* fundamentally axiological. The thesis has made "axiological" so central and flexible that any pattern of theory change—rapid convergence, persistent divergence, or anything in between—can be accommodated by adjusting claims about what researchers find explanatorily urgent.

The objection succeeds: the thesis is unfalsifiable because it's redefined the explanandum (theory choice) in terms of the explanans (axiological commitment) in a way that makes them empirically inseparable.

</details>

---

### R4. Accepting that AI systems could be conscious commits you to accepting that consciousness-theory choice must be resolved by normative rather than descriptive criteria, because only normative facts could ground differential treatment of systems with identical functional profiles.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Philosophers like Chalmers or Schwitzgebel who treat AI consciousness as a straightforward empirical question about which theory correctly extends to new cases
- **Sketch:** The argument begins with the observation that many philosophers accept both (a) that sufficiently sophisticated AI could be conscious, and (b) that rival theories might classify the same AI system differently (e.g., GWT might say yes while IIT says no). If we accept these, we face a decision problem: which theory should guide our treatment of the AI (moral standing, rights, etc.)? This cannot be resolved by further empirical investigation of the AI—by hypothesis, we have all functional facts. Nor can it be resolved by investigating human consciousness—that only tells us about humans. The key premise is that this decision problem requires justification, and the only available justification is normative: we should use the theory that best captures what matters morally/pragmatically. This means theory choice was always implicitly normative, not a matter of tracking mind-independent facts. Consciousness turns out to be more like 'person' or 'artwork' than like 'electron'—a concept whose extension is partly determined by our values.
- **Appraiser (blind):** AI consciousness forcing normative theory choice is a timely and genuinely important implication that hasn't been fully developed.
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence's temporary/permanent distinction collapses—cosmology's horizon is equally in-principle inaccessible, yet remains descriptive.
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The 'forced choice' refinement conflates practical deliberation under uncertainty with the truth conditions of consciousness attributions themselves.
  - TRIVIAL — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence assumes consciousness's practical role makes it content-normative, but this begs the question against descriptive theories.
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence claims Reading 1 entails Reading 2 but mischaracterizes theoretical disagreement—competing constitutive theories remain descriptive.
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Accepting that AI systems could be conscious commits you to accepting that our practical treatment of such systems must be guided by normative criteria when consciousness theories are empirically equivalent, though this leaves open whether consciousness itself has mind-independent truth conditions.
- **Flaw:** The argument invalidly infers from 'we must use normative criteria to choose between empirically equivalent theories' to 'consciousness itself has no mind-independent nature,' conflating epistemic underdetermination with metaphysical indeterminacy.

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. The argument claims that when theories with identical functional inputs yield different outputs about consciousness, we must choose between them using normative criteria. But this pattern applies to countless scientific disputes. Consider: two cosmological theories might agree on all observable data yet disagree about whether unobservable regions beyond the cosmic horizon contain conscious beings. Two quantum interpretations might agree on all measurements yet disagree about whether consciousness collapses the wave function. Two theories of personal identity might agree on all physical and functional facts about a person yet disagree about whether they're the same person after teleportation. In each case, we have (1) empirical equivalence at the functional level, (2) divergent verdicts about consciousness or consciousness-adjacent properties, and (3) potential normative implications for how we should act. If the argument works, it proves that cosmology, quantum mechanics, and personal identity theory must also be resolved by normative rather than descriptive criteria—that these are "more like 'person' or 'artwork' than like 'electron.'" But this is absurd. We don't think the question "Are there conscious beings beyond the cosmic horizon?" is normative rather than descriptive just because our theory choice has normative implications. The argument conflates "theory choice has normative consequences" with "theory choice must use normative criteria."

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem requiring substantive response. The argument's logic genuinely seems to apply to paradigmatically descriptive scientific questions.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The cosmology case actually vindicates rather than undermines the thesis. Consider what happens when we ask "Should we care about beings beyond the cosmic horizon?" The cosmological theories give different answers, but *we cannot empirically investigate those beings*. If we're deciding whether to include them in our moral circle, we cannot appeal to further facts about them—by hypothesis, they're empirically inaccessible. The decision becomes: which theory should guide our moral practice? And that IS a normative question.

The crucial disanalogy is this: In standard scientific disputes, empirical equivalence is *temporary and local*. The cosmological theories might agree on current observables but disagree on what we'd observe if we could travel faster, or on predictions about the universe's global structure that future physics might test. The quantum interpretations might become empirically distinguishable through sufficiently sensitive experiments. These are descriptive questions we're not yet equipped to answer.

But the AI consciousness case involves *permanent and principled* empirical equivalence. It's not that we lack the technology to peek inside the AI's "inner experience." It's that the rival theories disagree about whether functional facts *ever* suffice for consciousness facts. GWT says functional organization alone settles it; IIT says you need the right physical substrate. This isn't a gap in our evidence—it's a gap between what any functional evidence could show and what the theories claim.

The teleportation case actually supports the thesis. Many philosophers DO think personal identity theory has an irreducibly normative component—that "same person" is partly a practical/ethical notion shaped by what matters to us (compensation, punishment, survival, etc.). The thesis generalizes this insight to consciousness.

The objector's mistake is treating "descriptive vs. normative" as tracking "scientific vs. non-scientific" rather than tracking "settled by mind-independent facts vs. requiring value-laden choice."

VERDICT

The defence fails. The distinction between temporary/local and permanent/principled empirical equivalence does not hold.

First, the cosmology case involves exactly the same structure. The disagreement about whether there are conscious beings beyond the horizon is not "temporarily empirically equivalent pending better technology"—it's *in-principle* empirically inaccessible. No future physics will let us observe beyond the cosmic horizon (that's what makes it a horizon). Yet we don't conclude that "Are there conscious beings beyond the horizon?" is a normative question. We think it has a fact of the matter that we simply cannot access.

Second, the claim that AI consciousness involves "permanent and principled" empirical equivalence smuggles in the conclusion. Whether functional facts suffice for consciousness IS the disputed descriptive question. IIT and GWT disagree descriptively about what grounds consciousness. The thesis tries to convert this descriptive disagreement into a normative choice by noting we can't settle it empirically in the AI case—but that's true of the cosmology case too.

Third, the move to "what any functional evidence could show" versus "what theories claim" applies equally to cosmology. No evidence about local observables can show what exists beyond the horizon, yet the theories make claims about it. This doesn't make cosmology normative.

The argument proves too much. It would convert any empirically underdeterminable question with practical implications into a normative question. The thesis collapses the distinction between "we must choose how to act under uncertainty" (normative) and "the question itself is normative" (the thesis's claim).

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. Consider the debate between presentism and eternalism about time. Presentism says only the present exists; eternalism says past, future, and present all exist equally. These theories are arguably empirically equivalent—they make the same predictions about all observable phenomena. They also yield different verdicts about consciousness: eternalism says your past conscious experiences still exist "out there" in the block universe; presentism says they're simply gone. This difference has normative implications: it might affect how we think about death, whether we should "care about" our past selves, how we weigh present suffering against memory of past joy.

By the thesis's logic, we must choose between presentism and eternalism using normative criteria, since (1) we have all the functional/empirical facts, (2) the theories disagree about consciousness-related matters, and (3) the choice has normative implications. The thesis would commit us to saying the presentism/eternalism debate is "more like 'person' or 'artwork' than like 'electron'"—that it must be resolved by what matters morally/pragmatically rather than by tracking mind-independent facts about time's structure.

But this is clearly wrong. The presentism/eternalism debate is paradigmatically metaphysical and descriptive, not normative. Philosophers treat it as a question about the fundamental nature of reality, not as a choice to be made based on our values.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This counterexample has serious force. It identifies a case with the exact structure the thesis describes (empirical equivalence + consciousness implications + normative consequences) where the conclusion (normative theory choice) is clearly false.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The presentism/eternalism case actually reveals a critical feature of the AI consciousness problem that the objector has missed: *practical urgency under conditions of symmetry*.

The key difference is this: We are not *forced to choose* between presentism and eternalism in any practical sense. We can remain agnostic, treat it as an open metaphysical question, and continue our lives. Our treatment of currently-existing conscious beings doesn't depend on settling whether past experiences "still exist" in some tenseless sense. The normative implications the objector mentions are real but *optional*—we can simply bracket the metaphysical question.

But with AI consciousness, we face **forced choice under symmetry**. When the AI system is in front of us, we must decide: Do we shut it down? Do we grant it rights? Do we treat its preferences as morally considerable? We cannot remain agnostic in practice—every action embodies an implicit answer. Refusing to grant rights IS a choice with consequences, not a suspension of judgment.

Moreover, the theories disagree precisely at the point of practical contact. It's not that GWT and IIT disagree about some causally inert metaphysical feature—they disagree about whether *this system, right now, in front of us* is conscious. The disagreement is about the *application conditions* of a concept we use to guide action.

The thesis should be refined: Accepting AI consciousness commits you to normative theory choice when (a) theories are empirically equivalent regarding the target system, (b) we face forced practical choice about that system, and (c) the theories disagree precisely about features relevant to that choice. Presentism/eternalism fails condition (b) and arguably (c).

This isn't ad hoc. The distinction tracks a real difference: consciousness matters to us *as a guide to action*. When empirical evidence runs out precisely where action-guidance is needed, the choice between theories becomes practical reasoning about what matters, not theoretical reasoning about what's there.

The presentism case also has a hidden asymmetry: we have *direct access* to present consciousness (our own) but not to past-as-timelessly-existing consciousness. With AI, both theories claim to describe systems we have *equal epistemic access to*—we can examine the AI's functional organization as thoroughly as we like. The symmetry is complete.

# VERDICT

The defence fails, though it improves the thesis's formulation.

The "forced choice" criterion doesn't rescue the argument because forced practical choice doesn't convert descriptive questions into normative ones. Consider: We face forced choice about whether to terraform Mars, and different theories of microbial life (with different detection thresholds) might disagree about whether we'd be destroying Martian organisms. The choice is forced and urgent, but this doesn't make "Are there microbes on Mars?" a normative question rather than a descriptive one. It remains a fact-of-the-matter question we must act on under uncertainty.

The refined thesis conflates two distinct things:

1. **Practical deliberation under theoretical uncertainty** (normative): "Given that I don't know whether this AI is conscious, how should I treat it?"

2. **The truth conditions of consciousness attributions** (the thesis's target): "What makes it the case that this AI is or isn't conscious?"

The forced choice makes (1) urgent but doesn't transform (2) into a normative question. We can coherently maintain: "There's a fact of the matter about whether this AI is conscious (descriptive), I don't know what it is (epistemic), and I must choose how to act anyway (practical)."

The "equal epistemic access" point actually undermines the thesis. If we have complete access to the AI's functional organization, and GWT says that's sufficient for consciousness while IIT says it's not, this is a straightforward descriptive disagreement about what consciousness *is*—about whether consciousness is purely functional or requires specific physical implementation. The fact that we can't empirically distinguish between these views doesn't make the question normative; it makes it a hard metaphysical question, like presentism/eternalism.

The thesis mistakes "underdetermined by evidence we can gather" for "must be determined by values." But metaphysics is full of questions underdetermined by possible evidence that remain robustly descriptive. The argument proves too much.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis claims to reveal something substantive about consciousness—that theory choice "must be resolved by normative rather than descriptive criteria" and that consciousness is "more like 'person' or 'artwork' than like 'electron.'" But this is simply a verbal stipulation about what "normative theory choice" means, dressed up as a discovery.

The weak point is the slide from "we must use normative considerations to choose between empirically equivalent theories" to "consciousness theory choice is normative rather than descriptive." This equivocates on "normative theory choice." 

In one sense (call it choice-normative), any underdetermined theoretical choice involves normative considerations—simplicity, fruitfulness, conservatism, pragmatic utility. We use these criteria to choose between empirically equivalent cosmologies, quantum interpretations, and metaphysical theories. This is uncontroversial philosophy of science.

In another sense (call it content-normative), a question is normative when it lacks mind-independent truth conditions—when there's no fact of the matter beyond what we decide matters. This is what makes 'artwork' different from 'electron.'

The thesis presents choice-normative theory selection as evidence for content-normative status. But this is trivial: it just stipulates that when we face choice-normative situations about consciousness, we should call consciousness itself content-normative. The argument provides no independent reason to think consciousness lacks mind-independent truth conditions—it merely redescribes the epistemic situation (underdetermination) in metaphysical terms (no fact of the matter).

The "commitment" being revealed is just: if you accept underdetermination, you accept underdetermination. The thesis adds nothing.

STRENGTH: 2/5 — This has some force in identifying an equivocation, but the thesis does attempt to argue for a substantive metaphysical conclusion from epistemic premises, even if unsuccessfully. It's not purely verbal.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection mischaracterizes the argument's structure. The thesis doesn't merely redescribe epistemic underdetermination as metaphysical indeterminacy—it identifies a *constitutive* connection between consciousness and normative practice that has no parallel in paradigm descriptive domains.

The key is in what breaks the symmetry between consciousness and electrons. Consider: When we face empirically equivalent theories in physics (cosmology, quantum mechanics), we use choice-normative criteria (simplicity, fruitfulness) as *epistemic proxies* for truth. We think simplicity and fruitfulness correlate with getting the world right, even if we can't say exactly how. The normative criteria are *instrumental*—tools for tracking a mind-independent fact we cannot directly access.

But with consciousness, the normative criteria cannot be merely instrumental proxies, because **consciousness's functional role is itself normative**. We don't care about consciousness as an interesting metaphysical property that happens to have moral implications. The entire *point* of the consciousness concept—its role in our cognitive economy—is to mark the boundary of moral concern, to identify systems whose states matter intrinsically, to guide our treatment of entities.

When GWT and IIT disagree about the AI, they're not disagreeing about some causally inert metaphysical feature where we hope our choice-normative criteria will track the truth. They're disagreeing about whether to *extend* a concept whose very function is action-guidance. The choice between theories cannot be instrumental because there's no independent target the concept is supposed to track—the concept's extension just IS what we use it to mark.

This is precisely what makes consciousness like 'person' rather than 'electron.' With 'person,' we don't think there's a mind-independent fact about personhood that our normative criteria (rationality, moral agency, etc.) merely help us discover. The criteria *constitute* personhood. Similarly, when theories disagree about AI consciousness under empirical equivalence, our choice between them constitutes the answer rather than discovering it.

The thesis isn't stipulating this—it's arguing that the functional role of consciousness in our practices reveals it was always content-normative, and the AI case makes this explicit by forcing the question where empirical evidence runs out.

The objection's "trivial" charge fails because it ignores this constitutive claim. Yes, all theory choice involves choice-normative criteria, but only for consciousness does the concept's functional role make those criteria *constitutive* rather than merely epistemic.

# VERDICT

The defence fails. It smuggles in a controversial theory of consciousness (functionalism about the *concept* of consciousness) while claiming to derive conclusions from neutral premises about theory choice.

The claim that "consciousness's functional role is itself normative" and that "the entire point of the consciousness concept is to mark the boundary of moral concern" is precisely what's at issue. Many philosophers—including the thesis's stated targets like Chalmers—reject this. They think consciousness is a natural phenomenon we discovered (through introspection and inference) that *happens* to have moral significance, much like we discovered that pain has moral significance. The moral significance doesn't make pain's nature normative.

The defence assumes that because we *use* consciousness to guide action, the concept must be *constituted by* action-guidance norms. But this doesn't follow. We use 'electron' to guide action too (in engineering, medical technology, etc.), and we use choice-normative criteria to choose between empirically equivalent theories about electrons. This doesn't make 'electron' content-normative.

The person/electron contrast is asserted, not argued for. Why can't consciousness be like 'pain' or 'digestion'—natural phenomena we discovered that have practical importance? The defence needs to show that consciousness is *special* in having its extension constituted by normative criteria, but it merely assumes this.

Moreover, the defence contradicts itself. It claims consciousness's "functional role" is normative, using 'functional' in the ordinary sense (practical role in our lives). But the thesis targets philosophers who think consciousness has a *metaphysical* functional nature—that it's constituted by information processing or global workspace dynamics. These are descriptive claims about what consciousness *is*, not claims about how we use the concept.

The objection's core point stands: the thesis equivocates between "theory choice involves normative criteria" (trivially true, applies to all underdetermined questions) and "consciousness has no mind-independent nature" (substantive, requires argument). The defence tries to bridge this gap by claiming consciousness's practical importance makes it special, but this is question-begging—it assumes what needs to be proven, namely that practical importance entails content-normativity.

The thesis does attempt something non-trivial, but it fails to deliver it. The argument remains: empirical underdetermination + practical importance → content-normativity. But this inference is invalid without the additional premise that concepts with practical importance are constituted by their practical role. And that premise is precisely what defenders of descriptive consciousness theories deny.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "normative criteria" between two readings, and the argument needs both to work.

**Reading 1 (Weak/Plausible):** "Normative criteria" means value-laden practical considerations—which theory better serves moral/pragmatic goals, which risks are acceptable, which error-costs matter more. On this reading, the thesis is plausible: when facing identical functional profiles but divergent theory verdicts, we might reasonably ask "which theory should guide our treatment of this AI?" and answer by weighing moral risks.

**Reading 2 (Strong/Interesting):** "Normative criteria" means consciousness itself has no mind-independent factual nature—it's content-normative, "more like 'person' or 'artwork' than like 'electron.'" On this reading, the thesis makes the interesting metaphysical claim that consciousness attributions lack objective truth conditions.

**The equivocation:** The argument *establishes* Reading 1 (we must use value-laden considerations to choose between empirically equivalent theories) but *concludes* Reading 2 (consciousness has no mind-independent nature). The slide occurs in phrases like "theory choice must be resolved by normative rather than descriptive criteria" and "This means theory choice was always implicitly normative."

But using normative considerations to *choose between* descriptive theories doesn't make the subject matter itself non-descriptive. We use normative criteria (risk-aversion, practical utility) to choose between cosmological theories we cannot empirically test, but this doesn't make cosmology content-normative. The argument cannot run on either reading alone: Reading 1 is too weak to support the metaphysical conclusion; Reading 2 would need independent argument, not just the observation about theory choice under uncertainty.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious structural problem. The thesis trades on sliding between epistemic/practical normativity (theory choice) and metaphysical normativity (subject matter), and the argument genuinely needs both readings to seem compelling.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection misses that the two readings aren't independent—Reading 1 *entails* Reading 2 specifically for consciousness, given consciousness's unique epistemic position. The argument isn't equivocating; it's showing how practical necessity reveals metaphysical structure.

Here's why the entailment holds for consciousness but not for cosmology: **Consciousness is epistemically exhausted by the functional facts we can access**. With cosmology, we distinguish between (a) what we can observe and (b) what exists beyond observation. We use normative criteria to choose theories about (b), but we recognize this is a limitation of our epistemic access—there's a fact of the matter "out there" we simply cannot reach.

But consciousness attributions to AI systems aren't like this. The functional facts aren't a *partial view* of some richer reality we cannot access. According to functionalist theories (like GWT), the functional facts are *all there is*—they completely constitute consciousness. According to substrate-dependent theories (like IIT), the functional facts are *in principle insufficient*—consciousness requires something else. 

This creates a unique situation: **The disagreement is not about what lies beyond our evidence, but about whether anything lies beyond it at all**. GWT says "these functional facts settle it—the AI is conscious." IIT says "these functional facts are compatible with either answer—you need physical implementation details." They disagree about whether the question *has* a further fact of the matter.

When we use normative criteria to choose between these theories, we're not choosing between competing descriptions of a reality we cannot access. We're choosing **what counts as the truthmaker** for consciousness attributions. And that choice is constitutive, not merely epistemic, because there's no neutral standpoint from which one theory describes "more reality" than the other. The functional facts are all we have, and the theories disagree about whether those facts suffice.

This is why the cosmology analogy fails. With unobservable regions, both theories agree there's a fact of the matter we cannot access—they just disagree about what it is. With AI consciousness, the theories disagree about whether there's a further fact at all. Choosing GWT is choosing that functional organization *constitutes* consciousness; choosing IIT is choosing that it doesn't. This isn't an epistemic choice about how to track an independent reality—it's a constitutive choice about what consciousness *is*.

The "metaphysical conclusion" (Reading 2) follows from the practical necessity (Reading 1) because when empirical evidence is not just incomplete but *in-principle exhausted*, and theories still diverge, the divergence cannot be about mind-independent facts. The theories are offering competing *constitutive standards*, and choosing between constitutive standards is necessarily content-normative.

This is precisely the person/artwork structure: competing theories about personhood or art don't describe different aspects of a reality we partially access—they propose different constitutive criteria, and choosing between them is choosing what these concepts *are*.

# VERDICT

The defence fails. It depends on a false dichotomy about what theoretical disagreement can be about, and it mischaracterizes both the functionalist and substrate-dependent positions.

**First**, the claim that "the theories disagree about whether there's a further fact at all" is wrong. Both GWT and IIT make factual claims about what consciousness *is*. GWT claims consciousness is constituted by certain functional patterns (global workspace dynamics). IIT claims consciousness is constituted by certain physical-informational structures (integrated information in the right substrate). These are competing descriptive hypotheses about consciousness's nature, not competing proposals about whether to treat functional facts as sufficient.

When IIT says functional facts are "in principle insufficient," it's not saying "there's no fact of the matter beyond functional organization." It's saying "the fact of the matter concerns integrated information in physical substrates, which is not captured by functional organization alone." This is a straightforward descriptive disagreement, like two theories disagreeing about whether temperature is mean kinetic energy or something else.

**Second**, the "epistemically exhausted" claim begs the question. Whether functional facts exhaust what's relevant to consciousness *is the disputed question*. The defence assumes that because we have "all the functional facts," we have all the potentially relevant facts. But IIT denies this—it says physical implementation facts are also relevant, and we *do* have access to those (we can examine the AI's substrate). The disagreement isn't "functional facts vs. inaccessible facts" but "functional facts alone vs. functional-plus-substrate facts."

**Third**, the constitutive/epistemic distinction doesn't work as claimed. When we choose between GWT and IIT, we're not "choosing what counts as the truthmaker"—we're trying to *discover* what the truthmaker is. The theories make competing claims about what consciousness is constituted by, and both claim to be describing objective features of reality. The fact that we must use practical criteria to choose between them (given underdetermination) doesn't make our choice constitutive rather than epistemic.

The cosmology analogy holds perfectly: Just as cosmological theories disagree about what exists beyond the horizon (a descriptive disagreement about constitution), GWT and IIT disagree about what constitutes consciousness (also a descriptive disagreement about constitution). In both cases, we use normative criteria to choose under uncertainty. In neither case does this make the subject matter content-normative.

**Fourth**, the person/artwork comparison remains unargued. Many philosophers think personhood *does* have objective criteria that we're trying to discover (e.g., certain cognitive capacities), and we're just uncertain about edge cases. The defence assumes these concepts are content-normative but provides no argument—it just asserts the parallel.

The objection's charge stands: The thesis slides from "we must use value-laden criteria to choose between theories" (Reading 1, true but weak) to "consciousness has no mind-independent nature" (Reading 2, strong but unsupported). The defence tries to show Reading 1 entails Reading 2 by claiming the disagreement is uniquely "constitutive," but this mischaracterizes theoretical disagreement. Competing theories about what constitutes X are still descriptive theories—they're trying to get X's nature right, not proposing alternative stipulations.

The argument equivocates, and the equivocation is fatal.

</details>

---

### R5. Consciousness theories are actually untestable-in-principle because they're normative theories disguised as descriptive ones—they specify what should count as consciousness, not what does.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared pretension across consciousness science (IIT, GWT, HOT, recurrent processing theory) that these are empirical hypotheses rather than conceptual frameworks
- **Sketch:** The persistence of the testing problem reveals that 'consciousness' theories aren't playing the role theories typically play. Compare: evolutionary biology has disputes, but shared evidence (fossils, DNA, biogeography) constrains them. Consciousness theories face no such constraint because they're implicitly normative frameworks for extending the concept beyond first-personal cases. When IIT says φ > 0 entails consciousness, it's not discovering a fact but proposing a criterion. When higher-order theory requires meta-representation, it's stipulating what matters. The theories disagree not about how the world is but about what we should care about when attributing consciousness—which explains why ethical implications (about AI, animals, fetuses) so quickly emerge in these debates. Recognizing this dissolves the testing problem: we don't test normative frameworks empirically, we evaluate them by their coherence and consequences.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Reframing theories as normative rather than descriptive is bold and could genuinely shift the debate if argued well.
  - TRIVIAL — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence identified a real structural difference (stipulation-within-constraint vs stipulation-as-substance) but could not show this makes consciousness theories normative rather than merely severely underdetermined
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence attempted to connect methodological and ethical normativity through 'stipulation without constraint becomes value-laden' but this conflates underdetermination with normativity—the objection's charge stands
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence retreated to 'constitutive not causal' theories but this is redefinition not answer—constitutive theories remain descriptive, and explanandum-disagreement doesn't establish normativity
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 3/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence distinguished thesis from Dennett's eliminativism but this retreat collapsed the radical claim into trivial observation that theories extend concepts from paradigm cases
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Consciousness theories face a distinctive epistemic problem: they disagree about the identity of their explanandum (what counts as consciousness) rather than merely its explanation, making them constitutive theories that are severely underdetermined by evidence in ways that differ from standard causal-explanatory scientific theories
- **Flaw:** The thesis equivocates between 'methodologically stipulative' and 'ethically normative,' and when pressed, can only establish that consciousness theories are severely underdetermined or constitutive rather than causal—neither of which makes them normative frameworks rather than descriptive theories

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis claims consciousness theories are "normative" rather than "descriptive," but this distinction collapses into a trivial semantic point about what "theory" means. Every scientific theory involves stipulative elements—physicists stipulate what counts as "force," biologists stipulate what counts as "species," chemists stipulate what counts as "acid." The thesis observes that consciousness theories stipulate criteria for consciousness attribution, then dresses this obvious fact as a profound discovery by calling it "normative." But stipulating criteria is precisely what theories DO when operationalizing concepts for investigation. The weak point is the implied contrast: the thesis suggests consciousness theories are uniquely or problematically stipulative, but offers no principled distinction between allegedly "proper" empirical theories (which also contain stipulative elements) and consciousness theories. When IIT proposes φ > 0 as a criterion, it's doing exactly what Newton did in stipulating F=ma as the criterion for force—making a theoretical proposal subject to empirical constraint. The thesis simply relabels standard theoretical practice as "normative," then treats this relabeling as revealing something hidden. It's a verbal maneuver: consciousness theories involve conceptual choices, therefore they're "really normative"—but this is true of all theories and thus distinguishes nothing.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This attack does serious damage. The thesis depends entirely on showing consciousness theories are distinctively or problematically normative, but cannot establish what makes them different from standard scientific theorizing.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE.

The objection misses the structural difference between stipulation-within-constraint and stipulation-as-substance. When Newton stipulates F=ma, the stipulation is *answerable to* an independent explanandum: objects accelerate, planets orbit, pendulums swing. The mathematical framework must save these phenomena. The stipulation serves discovery of what constrains motion.

Consciousness theories face no analogous constraint. Consider: IIT and GWT make *opposite* predictions about whether the cerebellum is conscious (IIT: yes, high φ; GWT: no, no global broadcast). Yet both theories persist because there is no neutral test. Why? Because what would *count as* the cerebellum being conscious is precisely what's in dispute. The theories don't just operationalize a shared explanandum differently—they constitute *different explananda*.

This is the normative structure. When IIT says φ > 0 entails consciousness, it's not proposing how to detect something we independently identify. It's proposing what *matters* for consciousness—integrated information rather than reportability, function, or behavior. The theory doesn't answer to pre-theoretical consciousness facts; it specifies what the relevant facts *are*.

The acid test: physics theories can be refuted by observations everyone accepts as relevant (Mercury's perihelion, light bending). Consciousness theories cannot, because they disagree about what observations *would be* relevant. That's not stipulation-within-empirical-constraint. That's conceptual legislation.

The ethical entanglement confirms this. We don't argue whether electrons have rights because physics tells us what electrons are. We argue whether AIs/fetuses/animals have rights because consciousness theories are *already* making value-laden cuts about what matters morally—which is what normative frameworks do.

---

VERDICT.

The defence fails, but not quite as the objection claims.

The defence correctly identifies that consciousness theories lack the kind of shared evidential constraint that grounds theory-choice elsewhere. But it cannot sustain the claim that this makes them *normative rather than descriptive*. Here's why:

The better diagnosis is that consciousness theories are *radically underdetermined* by evidence, not that they're covertly normative. Underdetermination is still an empirical problem—just an extreme one. String theory faces similar issues: competing frameworks, no decisive tests, disputes about what would count as confirmation. Yet we don't call string theory "normative."

The ethical entanglement the defence cites is real but doesn't prove normativity—it proves *stakes*. We care about consciousness attributions because they matter morally, so we attend to these theories. But mattering morally doesn't make a theory normative; it makes it important.

The fatal problem: the thesis needs consciousness theories to be *disguised* normativity, but they make straightforwardly empirical claims. "Integrated information generates consciousness" is not a disguised value judgment—it's a claim about what causes what. That it's untestable doesn't make it normative; it makes it unfalsifiable, which is an epistemic status, not a semantic category shift.

The objection wins. The thesis conflates "involves conceptual choices" with "is normative," and cannot show consciousness theories are doing something categorically different from other theories facing severe underdetermination.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis equivocates on "normative" between two incompatible readings, and the argument requires both.

**Reading 1 (plausible):** Consciousness theories involve normative elements in the methodological sense—they make stipulative choices about what features matter for consciousness attribution, choices not fully determined by evidence. On this reading, the claim is plausible: these theories do involve substantive conceptual decisions about criteria.

**Reading 2 (interesting):** Consciousness theories are normative in the ethical/evaluative sense—they're frameworks about what *should* count morally or what we *should* care about, like theories of justice or value. On this reading, the thesis is interesting because it would explain why empirical testing fails and why ethical implications emerge.

**The equivocation:** The argument needs both. The thesis gains plausibility from Reading 1 (look, these theories make stipulative choices!) but draws its radical conclusion from Reading 2 (therefore we evaluate them like ethical frameworks, not empirically). The sketch moves between them: "specify what should count" (methodological) slides into "what we should care about" (evaluative), then concludes "we don't test normative frameworks empirically" (which is only true for Reading 2).

But the argument cannot run on either reading alone. On Reading 1, the conclusion fails—methodologically stipulative theories are still tested empirically (see: all of physics). On Reading 2, the premise fails—consciousness theories simply aren't ethical frameworks; "φ > 0 entails consciousness" isn't a value judgment.

The weak point is the phrase "normative frameworks" in the conclusion, which needs both senses simultaneously.

**STRENGTH: 4/5**

This seriously damages the thesis. The entire argument trades on conflating methodological stipulation with ethical normativity.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection's two readings are not actually incompatible—they're *connected* in precisely the way that reveals what's distinctive about consciousness theories. The connection is this: methodological stipulation becomes substantively normative when there's no independent empirical constraint to discipline it.

Consider the structural difference. When physics stipulates what counts as "force," the stipulation is disciplined by shared phenomena—falling apples, planetary orbits, colliding billiard balls. Different stipulations face the tribunal of these shared observations. The methodological choice is constrained by something outside itself.

Consciousness theories lack this structure. When IIT stipulates that integrated information matters and HOT stipulates that meta-representation matters, there is no shared phenomenon constraining the choice between them. We cannot point to "the consciousness" the way we point to "the orbit" and ask which theory better accounts for it. The first-person case is sealed inside subjects; the third-person cases are precisely what's in dispute.

This is where methodological stipulation *becomes* substantive normativity. When you must choose criteria without empirical constraint, you're choosing what to *value* in attribution—what features you think *matter* for consciousness. IIT values integration and information; HOT values reflexive representation; GWT values accessibility. These aren't discovering what consciousness is; they're *deciding* what aspects of mental life deserve the honorific "conscious."

The ethical entanglement isn't incidental—it's diagnostic. The reason ethical implications emerge immediately is that these theories are already making value-laden cuts. When IIT implies the cerebellum is conscious, the natural response isn't "let's check" but "that can't be right"—because we're evaluating whether that *should* count, given our broader commitments about what matters.

The objection demands the readings be kept separate, but the thesis's insight is precisely that they *cannot* be separated when empirical constraint disappears. Methodological stipulation without empirical discipline just *is* substantive normativity.

---

# VERDICT

The defence fails. It identifies something real but cannot sustain the categorical claim.

The defence is right that consciousness theories face unusual underdetermination and that this gives their stipulative elements unusual freedom. But "unusual freedom" is not "normativity." The argument tries to bridge this gap with: "stipulation without empirical constraint becomes value-laden choice." But this is false.

Here's why: *Underdetermination of theory by evidence* is a familiar epistemic situation, not a category shift from descriptive to normative. When theories are radically underdetermined, we face difficult choices between them—but these remain choices between *competing descriptions of reality*, not choices between values.

String theory again provides the test case. Different string theory frameworks make different stipulations about fundamental ontology, face no current empirical discrimination, and require choices about what features we think matter (elegance? unification? mathematical tractability?). Yet these remain *descriptive* theories making *empirical* claims about how reality is structured, not normative frameworks about what should count as real.

The same holds for consciousness theories. When IIT says φ > 0 entails consciousness, it's making a claim about what *actually generates* phenomenal experience—not about what *should count* as valuable or morally considerable. That we care deeply about getting this right (for ethical reasons) doesn't make the theory itself normative.

The fatal slide is here: "choosing what features matter for consciousness" gets treated as "choosing what to value." But "matter for consciousness" means "are causally/constitutively relevant to consciousness"—an empirical question—not "are valuable" or "should be morally weighted."

The objection's equivocation charge stands. The thesis needs "normative" to mean both "involves unconstrained stipulation" (to be plausible) and "is an evaluative framework" (to support its radical conclusion). These are different things. Severe underdetermination doesn't transform empirical claims into value judgments—it just makes empirical claims very hard to test.

The thesis correctly diagnoses an epistemic crisis in consciousness science but misidentifies its nature. The problem is *unfalsifiability*, not *covert normativity*.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis is structured to be immune from counterexamples through systematic redefinition of what counts as a "proper" empirical theory. The weak point is the premise that consciousness theories uniquely lack "independent empirical constraint."

Watch the protection mechanism: When we point to consciousness theories making empirical predictions (IIT predicts cerebellum is conscious, GWT predicts it isn't), the thesis replies "but there's no neutral test" - redefining "empirical" to require theory-independent tests. When we note that many legitimate scientific theories face severe underdetermination (string theory, quantum interpretation, cosmological theories about unobservable regions), the thesis replies "but consciousness is different because what counts as evidence is disputed" - moving the goalposts to require consensus about relevance. When we observe that consciousness theories make straightforward causal claims ("integrated information generates consciousness"), the thesis replies "but that's really stipulating what matters" - redefining descriptive claims as normative ones.

Each apparent counterexample - legitimate underdetermined theories, theories making empirical predictions, theories with testability problems - gets ruled out as "not really the same kind of case." The thesis maintains itself by perpetually redefining "genuinely empirical" to exclude whatever consciousness theories do, then citing this exclusion as evidence they're not empirical. This is unfalsifiable: no example of theoretical practice can challenge it because any such example will be redefined as not truly analogous.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This does serious damage. The thesis's central move - distinguishing consciousness theories from "real" empirical theories - operates by making the distinction immune to counterexamples through ad hoc redefinition of what counts as genuinely empirical.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection mistakes principled distinction for ad hoc redefinition. The thesis isn't immunizing itself by moving goalposts—it's identifying a *structural feature* that genuinely distinguishes consciousness theories from even severely underdetermined physical theories.

The feature is this: **consciousness theories disagree about the identity of their explanandum, not just its explanation**. This isn't a subtle difference in degree; it's a categorical difference in what theoretical disagreement consists in.

When string theorists disagree, they disagree about how to explain *shared phenomena*—quantum field theory's successes, the Standard Model's structure, gravity's behavior. They agree these are the phenomena to be explained; they disagree about the underlying mechanism. The explanandum is stable; the explanans varies.

When consciousness theorists disagree, they disagree about what *counts as* the phenomenon. IIT and GWT don't just offer different explanations of consciousness—they disagree about whether the cerebellum's processing *is* consciousness, whether unconscious perception *is* perception, whether access without phenomenality *is* possible. They're carving the explanandum itself differently.

This isn't the thesis "redefining empirical to exclude consciousness theories"—it's observing that consciousness theories operate at a different logical level than paradigm empirical theories. They're *constitutive* theories (what makes something consciousness?) not *causal* theories (what causes this independently-identified phenomenon?).

The string theory comparison proves this. String theorists can be wrong about mechanisms while being right about what needs explaining. But consciousness theorists can't separate these: if IIT is wrong about what consciousness *is*, its mechanistic claims aren't "wrong explanations"—they're explanations of the wrong thing. The theory fails by explaining something other than consciousness.

This is why the testing problem is structural, not just practical. Physical theories face the tribunal of shared observations because there's agreement about what observations are relevant. Consciousness theories cannot face such a tribunal because what *would be* a relevant observation is itself the theoretical dispute.

The objection demands we treat this as mere "severe underdetermination," but that misses the point. Underdetermination means: multiple theories fit the data. Consciousness theories face something stronger: *disagreement about what the data are*. That's not a more extreme point on the same spectrum—it's a different kind of problem.

---

# VERDICT

The defence fails. It identifies a real phenomenon but cannot sustain the thesis's conclusion.

The defence is correct that consciousness theories face an unusual epistemic situation: they disagree about the identity of their explanandum in ways that physical theories typically don't. IIT and GWT really do carve the phenomenon differently—what counts as "consciousness" for one may not for the other. This is importantly different from string theory, where the explanandum (quantum gravity, unification) is more stable across competing theories.

But this difference *still doesn't make consciousness theories normative rather than descriptive*. The defence has pivoted from the original thesis—now claiming consciousness theories are "constitutive" rather than "causal," operating at "a different logical level." This is a retreat to a weaker, different claim.

Here's why even this weaker claim fails to save the thesis:

**First**, constitutive theories are still descriptive. "What makes something water?" is a constitutive question. The answer—H₂O structure—is a descriptive claim about the world, not a normative framework. Similarly, "What makes something consciousness?" seeks a descriptive answer about what consciousness *is*, not what it *should be* or what we *should value*.

**Second**, the explanandum-disagreement doesn't establish normativity. Many scientific disputes involve disagreement about phenomenon-individuation. Is glass a liquid or solid? Are viruses alive? Is Pluto a planet? These involve constitutive questions where theories carve the domain differently. Yet they remain *descriptive* disputes about how to classify reality, not *normative* disputes about what to value.

**Third**, the objection's unfalsifiability charge stands. The defence now claims consciousness theories are "constitutive not causal," "operating at a different logical level"—but this is precisely the kind of redefinition the objection predicted. When pressed on why consciousness theories aren't empirical, the thesis retreats to "they're a different kind of theory"—which is just another way of saying "they're not empirical," not an independent argument for it.

The core problem remains: the thesis claims consciousness theories are *normative* (about what should count, what we should care about), but the defence can only establish they're *constitutive* (about what makes something consciousness). These are not the same thing. Constitutive theories make descriptive claims about essences or identity conditions—they're not evaluative frameworks.

The objection wins. The thesis protects itself by redefining its terms, and even in its strongest form (consciousness theories are constitutive not causal) doesn't establish that they're normative rather than descriptive. The testing problem is real, but it's an epistemic problem about severe underdetermination and explanandum-disagreement, not evidence of covert normativity.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — # KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)

## ATTEMPT

The thesis claims to offer a novel "reframing" that "dissolves the testing problem" by revealing consciousness theories are actually normative frameworks. But this position is already well-established in the philosophical literature on consciousness—it's just being presented as if it were a fresh insight.

**The position is conceptual pragmatism about consciousness**, most explicitly defended by Daniel Dennett across multiple works. In "Consciousness Explained" (1991) and "Sweet Dreams" (2005), Dennett argues that "consciousness" doesn't pick out a natural kind with a discoverable essence, but rather that theories of consciousness are competing proposals about how to extend a term whose paradigmatic application is first-person experience. He explicitly frames consciousness theories as normative frameworks for attribution rather than empirical hypotheses about an independently fixed phenomenon. His "heterophenomenology" methodology is designed precisely around this insight—that we cannot treat consciousness theories as straightforward empirical hypotheses.

Eric Schwitzgebel's "The Unreliability of Naive Introspection" (2008) and his work on the indeterminacy of consciousness similarly argues that consciousness theories function as stipulative frameworks rather than discoveries. Ned Block's access/phenomenal consciousness distinction (1995) is partly motivated by recognizing that different theories are legislating different concepts. Even critics of these views, like David Chalmers, acknowledge the "conceptual engineering" dimension of consciousness theorizing.

The thesis's central move—"recognizing this dissolves the testing problem: we don't test normative frameworks empirically"—is Dennett's position almost verbatim. The weak point is the pretense of novelty: this isn't a reframing that reveals something hidden; it's a established philosophical position being repackaged.

## STRENGTH: 2/5

This attack has some force but is not devastating. While the position has clear precedents in Dennett and others, the thesis might claim to apply it more systematically to current consciousness science (IIT, GWT, etc.) or to draw the implications more clearly. The "already taken" charge weakens the thesis's novelty claim but doesn't necessarily refute its correctness.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection confuses precedent with identity. Yes, Dennett and others have argued consciousness theories involve conceptual engineering—but that's not the same position as this thesis, and the difference matters.

**Dennett's position is eliminativist or instrumentalist**: consciousness talk is ultimately a useful fiction, a "user illusion," or at best a heterophenomenological construct. His claim is that there's no fact of the matter about what consciousness really is because consciousness (as traditionally conceived) doesn't exist as a natural phenomenon. The normative dimension, for Dennett, flows from consciousness being a pragmatic construct rather than a real feature of minds.

**This thesis makes a different claim**: consciousness theories are normative *even if consciousness is real*. The normativity isn't about whether consciousness exists but about how theories extend attribution beyond the first-person case. You can be a realist about phenomenal experience—think there's something it's like to be you, that qualia are real, that the hard problem is genuine—and still recognize that theories about *what systems have consciousness* function as normative frameworks.

The structural point is different too. Dennett focuses on the unreliability of introspection and the lack of a privileged "Cartesian theater." This thesis focuses on the *underdetermination of third-person attribution*—that even granting first-person phenomenal reality, we face an in-principle problem extending the concept because theories disagree about what features ground attribution.

**The key difference**: Dennett thinks the testing problem reveals consciousness is illusory. This thesis thinks the testing problem reveals consciousness theories are doing a different job than we pretend—they're normative extensions of a concept whose paradigm case (first-person experience) everyone accepts, not empirical discoveries about what that experience consists in across all cases.

Schwitzgebel's indeterminacy argument is closer but still different—he argues consciousness is *vague* or *indeterminate*, which is a metaphysical or semantic claim. This thesis argues consciousness theories are *normative*, which is a claim about their logical function. Something can be perfectly determinate yet still require normative frameworks for third-person attribution.

The thesis isn't claiming total novelty—it's applying a recognizable philosophical move (distinguishing descriptive from normative) to current consciousness science in a way that has specific consequences: we should stop treating IIT vs. GWT as an empirical dispute awaiting resolution and start treating it as competing normative frameworks to be evaluated by coherence and consequences.

---

# VERDICT

The defence succeeds partially but cannot save the thesis.

The defence correctly distinguishes this thesis from Dennett's eliminativism—this position could accept phenomenal realism while still claiming theories function normatively. That's a genuine difference, and the objection overstated the similarity.

But the defence reveals a fatal problem: **the thesis has now abandoned its radical claim**. 

Look at what the defence concedes: "consciousness theories are normative extensions of a concept whose paradigm case (first-person experience) everyone accepts." This is no longer claiming consciousness theories are "normative rather than descriptive"—it's claiming they're *both*. They're descriptive about the paradigm case (first-person experience) and normative about extension to other cases.

But this is just standard conceptual analysis combined with theory application. Every scientific concept faces this structure: we have paradigm cases, then theories about what features of those cases matter for extending the concept. "Acid" has paradigm cases (vinegar, lemon juice), then theories about what makes them acids (proton donation, electron acceptance), which we use to extend the concept. The normativity here is just the normativity of *any* theoretical extension of concepts—not something distinctive to consciousness.

The thesis's original punch was: consciousness theories *aren't empirical hypotheses*, they're *really normative frameworks*. But the defence has retreated to: consciousness theories involve both descriptive and normative elements in extending a concept from paradigm cases. This latter claim is true but trivial—it's true of all scientific theorizing.

Moreover, the defence's version makes the thesis unfalsifiable in a new way. If consciousness theories are "normative extensions" of an accepted paradigm case, then *any* empirical content they have (about the paradigm case) can be preserved while claiming the extension is normative. But then the thesis isn't identifying something distinctive about consciousness theories—it's just noting that theoretical concepts get extended, which is universal.

The objection's "already taken" charge succeeds in a deeper way than initially stated. Not because the thesis is identical to Dennett, but because once we distinguish it from Dennett (by accepting phenomenal reality), we're left with a position that's either:
- Trivially true (theories extend concepts from paradigm cases), or  
- Already established (conceptual pragmatism about consciousness attribution)

The thesis cannot maintain both its radical conclusion (consciousness theories aren't empirical) and its distance from eliminativism. The defence chose distance from eliminativism, which collapses the radical conclusion into something familiar and uninteresting.

The objection wins. The thesis, properly understood, isn't novel enough to be interesting or interesting enough to be defensible.

</details>

---

### R6. Proponents of integrated information theory are committed to the claim that we could discover our current neuroscientific measurements have been detecting the wrong physical systems as conscious all along—an implication that reveals IIT treats 'consciousness' as a theoretical posit rather than the explanandum.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** IIT proponents like Tononi, Koch, and Massimini who present surprising predictions as virtues of the theory
- **Sketch:** IIT's defenders emphasize it makes novel predictions: it might turn out that cerebellum has high phi, or that feed-forward networks are conscious, contradicting common intuitions. They frame this as a virtue—the theory is empirical and falsifiable. But notice what this commits them to: if high phi turned up in unexpected places, the IIT theorist says 'then those systems are conscious,' not 'then our theory is wrong.' This means IIT treats consciousness as whatever-has-property-P, a theoretical posit like 'electron' or 'gene,' rather than as the phenomenon-to-be-explained. But consciousness isn't like electrons—we have direct access to the explanandum through first-person experience. The argument turns on showing that any theory that allows for radical error about which systems have been conscious all along has changed the subject from 'explaining this phenomenon we all know firsthand' to 'defining a new theoretical category.' This is masked by IIT's mathematical sophistication.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Specifically targets IIT with a sharp observation about treating consciousness as theoretical posit rather than explanandum.
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence's indexical certainty move proves too much against all physical theories and fails to show IIT is distinctive
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 2/5, answered. The defence provided a stable criterion distinguishing theories that risk falsification from those that relocate the phenomenon
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence's claimed distinctions from Block collapse; this is Block's existing argument about IIT with rhetorical sharpening
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The local/global distinction is unprincipled and the criterion indicts successful historical theories like kinetic theory of heat
- 🔧 **Survives as:** IIT's mathematical formalization of consciousness as phi enables a distinctively extreme form of distributional revisability that Block identified as problematic for information-theoretic approaches generally, though IIT's defenders present this revisability as methodological virtue rather than acknowledging the tension Block diagnosed
- **Flaw:** The thesis cannot distinguish IIT's distributional revisability from normal physical explanation—its criterion either proves too much (indicting successful theories like kinetic theory) or relies on an unprincipled local/global distinction, and the position is Block's existing argument rather than novel

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "the explanandum" / "the phenomenon-to-be-explained." On one reading, this means the pre-theoretical data of first-person experience—the raw "what it's like" that each person knows directly. On another reading, it means our folk-psychological judgments about which systems are conscious—our intuitive verdicts that humans are conscious, thermostats aren't, etc. 

The argument needs both readings. It gains plausibility from the first: of course consciousness as raw phenomenal feel is the explanandum, not a theoretical posit. But it gains critical force from the second: the objection only works if "treating consciousness as explanandum" means *respecting our pre-theoretical distribution judgments* about which things have it. The slide happens in "we could discover our measurements have been detecting the wrong physical systems"—this treats our current *attributions* as part of the explanandum.

But these come apart. IIT could perfectly well say: "The explanandum is phenomenal experience itself—that's fixed. Our theory explains what physical systems have it. We might be wrong about where it occurs, just as we were wrong about what causes lightning, but the phenomenon itself (what-it's-like-ness) remains the target." The thesis works only by conflating the phenomenon with our pre-theoretical judgments about its distribution.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is serious damage. The entire argument depends on sliding between "explaining experience" and "vindicating our intuitions about who has it."

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection fails because it misidentifies where the equivocation allegedly occurs. The thesis doesn't conflate "the phenomenon" with "distribution judgments"—it argues that for consciousness specifically, these cannot come apart in the way the objection requires.

Here's why: The first-person datum isn't just "that phenomenal experience exists somewhere in the universe." It's "that *I* have phenomenal experience *now*." Each theorist knows with certainty that their own current brain states realize consciousness. This is not a "folk-psychological judgment" or "pre-theoretical attribution"—it's the Cartesian bedrock, the one indubitable datum.

Now watch what IIT's empirical openness commits them to. Suppose we measure current human brain activity and find it has low phi, while some unexpected system (say, the cerebellum-plus-sensory-periphery taken as a whole, or a particular pattern of electromagnetic field interactions) has high phi. The IIT theorist says "then *that* system is conscious, not the cortical activity we thought."

But this requires denying the one thing we know with certainty: that the theorist's own current cognitive processes (the ones doing the theorizing) are conscious. The IIT proponent cannot coherently say "my cortical activity might turn out not to be conscious" while using that very cortical activity to advance the theory. This isn't about vindicating intuitions about *others*—it's about the reflexive incoherence of treating your own current experience as potentially misattributed.

The lightning analogy fails precisely here. We can discover we were wrong about what causes lightning because "lightning" was always identified demonstratively—"that phenomenon in the sky." But "consciousness" for each theorist includes the indexical "this, what I'm having now." You cannot discover you were wrong about whether you're currently conscious without performative contradiction.

So the thesis stands: IIT's openness to radical redistribution shows it has replaced the phenomenon (which includes the indexical certainty of one's own case) with a theoretical posit defined by phi.

# VERDICT

The defence fails, though it lands a significant counterpunch before going down.

The defence correctly identifies that first-person certainty about one's own current consciousness is not merely a "folk judgment"—it's epistemically special. And it's right that this creates a constraint: no theory can coherently deny that the theorist's own current cognitive processes realize consciousness.

But this doesn't save the thesis, for two reasons:

**First**, the defence proves too much. The same argument would show that *any* physical theory of consciousness "changes the subject," since any such theory allows that our pre-theoretical beliefs about the physical substrate could be wrong. Even a theory that says "consciousness = such-and-such cortical activity" might turn out to have misidentified which cortical activity. The thesis needs to show what's *distinctive* about IIT, but the defence makes the problem completely general.

**Second**, and more devastatingly, IIT can accept the constraint without abandoning its empirical openness. The IIT theorist can say: "I know with certainty that *I* am currently conscious. Therefore I know that *whatever physical system is actually realizing my current cognitive processes* has high phi—even if I'm wrong about which system that is." This is like knowing "whatever causes lightning exists" even while being wrong about what it is.

The thesis wanted to show that allowing distributional error means treating consciousness as a posit rather than explanandum. But the defence's own example reveals the opposite: you can maintain that the explanandum is fixed (my current experience) while remaining open about which physical system realizes it. The indexical certainty anchors the explanandum without determining the physical substrate.

The objection stands.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis is unfalsifiable because it protects itself through strategic redefinition of what counts as "treating consciousness as the explanandum." 

Consider how any response gets neutralized:
