**If IIT says:** "We might be wrong about distribution but consciousness itself remains the explanandum—we're explaining the same phenomenon, just discovering where it occurs"
**Thesis replies:** "That shows you've made it a theoretical posit! Real explananda don't allow distributional error."

**If IIT says:** "We accept constraints from first-person certainty—the theorist's own consciousness anchors the explanandum"
**Thesis replies:** "But you still allow you might be wrong about *which physical system* realizes it, so you've still changed the subject."

**If IIT says:** "Here's how we're like other sciences—we can be wrong about substrate while explaining the same phenomenon"
**Thesis replies:** "Consciousness is special, unlike lightning/genes/electrons, so that analogy fails."

The thesis can always respond to any defense by either tightening the requirements for "treating X as explanandum" (must respect all distributional intuitions, must identify exact substrate, etc.) or loosening them (just needs to respect indexical certainty), depending on what blocks the counterexample. 

What would falsify this thesis? What discovery or argument about IIT's structure could show it *doesn't* treat consciousness as a theoretical posit? The thesis provides no stable criterion—it just labels any empirical openness about physical realization as "changing the subject," making the charge immune to counterevidence.

STRENGTH: 2/5

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection misunderstands how philosophical theses about conceptual commitments work. The thesis isn't an empirical hypothesis requiring falsification—it's a claim about what IIT's methodological stance commits its proponents to. Such claims are evaluated by conceptual analysis, not empirical testing.

Here's the stable criterion the objection claims is missing: **A theory treats consciousness as the explanandum rather than a theoretical posit if and only if the phenomenon's identity is fixed independently of the theory, such that discovering the theory's predictions are false means the theory failed, not that we were wrong about the phenomenon's location.**

Apply this criterion:

**Genes:** Before molecular biology, "gene" meant "whatever explains hereditary transmission." Discovering DNA didn't show genes were elsewhere—it showed what genes are. The explanandum (hereditary patterns) stayed fixed; the theory identified its basis. If DNA had failed to explain heredity, that would falsify the theory, not relocate genes.

**Consciousness on IIT:** The phenomenon is "what I'm experiencing now." If we measure my current brain and find cortical activity has low phi while some other system has high phi, IIT says "then that other system is what's conscious." The theory cannot be falsified by discovering consciousness isn't where phi is—instead, consciousness gets relocated to wherever phi is found. This is the signature of a theoretical posit: the theory stipulatively defines what has the property rather than explaining a pre-identified phenomenon.

The criterion is perfectly stable. It distinguishes theories that risk falsification (if the predicted physical basis doesn't exhibit the target phenomenon, the theory fails) from theories that avoid falsification by redefining what has the phenomenon (wherever the predicted basis appears, that's where the phenomenon is).

IIT falls in the second category because its proponents explicitly embrace this: "If phi is high in unexpected places, those places are conscious." That's not strategic redefinition by the thesis—it's IIT's own methodological commitment.

The thesis would be falsified if IIT proponents said: "If we find high phi in systems that definitely lack consciousness (say, in systems where we're certain there's no experience), that would falsify IIT." But they don't say this—they say it would revise our distribution judgments. That's the tell.

# VERDICT

The defence fails, but it fails instructively by revealing the thesis's deeper problem.

The defence is right that the thesis offers a criterion: theories treat X as explanandum when discovering the theory's predictions are false means *the theory failed* rather than *we were wrong about X's location*. And it's right that this is a conceptual claim, not an empirical one.

But the defence's own gene example destroys the thesis. 

Watch what happens: Before molecular biology, we had distributional judgments about heredity (pea plants show it, rocks don't). Molecular biology could have discovered heredity worked completely differently than expected—perhaps only in some organisms, or through mechanisms that made our previous judgments about what inherited traits systematically wrong. If DNA had shown up in unexpected places with unexpected patterns, geneticists would say "then heredity works differently than we thought," not "then our theory is wrong."

Yet we don't say genetics "changed the subject" or treated heredity as a theoretical posit. We say it *explained* heredity while correcting our understanding of its distribution.

The defence tries to block this by saying genes-as-explanandum meant "hereditary patterns" not "genes themselves." But this is exactly what IIT can say: consciousness-as-explanandum means "phenomenal experience" (the what-it's-like-ness), not "consciousness-distribution judgments." Finding phi in unexpected places would mean phenomenal experience is distributed differently than we thought—just as finding DNA worked differently meant heredity was distributed differently than we thought.

The deeper problem: **The thesis requires that for consciousness specifically, distributional revisability proves subject-change, while for other explananda it doesn't.** But the defence provides no stable principle for why consciousness should be special in this way. The indexical certainty point (I know I'm conscious now) doesn't help—it just means we know the phenomenon exists and that we instantiate it, not that we know which physical system realizes it. That's exactly parallel to knowing heredity exists and that we exhibit it, without knowing the molecular mechanism.

The objection stands. The thesis cannot distinguish IIT's "changing the subject" from normal scientific explanation of a phenomenon while correcting distributional beliefs.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT. This thesis is not novel—it's a specific application of Ned Block's well-established distinction between phenomenal consciousness (P-consciousness) and access consciousness (A-consciousness), combined with his longstanding objection that functionalist theories change the subject from the former to the latter.

Block has argued since the 1990s that theories defining consciousness in terms of functional or information-processing properties inevitably treat consciousness as a theoretical posit rather than the explanandum. His core claim: P-consciousness (raw phenomenal feel, what-it's-like-ness) is the genuine explanandum, but functionalist theories substitute A-consciousness (information availability for cognitive use) or some other functional property, then stipulatively define whatever has that property as "conscious."

The current thesis simply applies this framework to IIT specifically. Where Block says functionalist theories in general commit this error, this thesis says IIT commits it through its phi criterion. The structure is identical: (1) the real explanandum is phenomenal experience as directly known, (2) the theory instead identifies consciousness with a functional/structural property, (3) therefore the theory's willingness to follow the property wherever it leads shows it has changed the subject from explaining the phenomenon to defining a theoretical category.

The thesis even uses Block's signature move—the indexical certainty argument ("I know I'm conscious now") appears prominently in Block's work as the bedrock that functionalist theories abandon. The claim that IIT's empirical openness about distribution reveals this subject-change is just Block's general argument applied to phi instead of functional role.

This is Block's position, applied to IIT.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This significantly undermines the thesis's contribution. While applying Block's framework specifically to IIT's phi criterion might add detail, the core philosophical move—that theories allowing distributional revision treat consciousness as theoretical posit rather than explanandum—is Block's established position, not a novel insight.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection mischaracterizes both what Block argues and what makes this thesis distinctive. The thesis is not "Block's objection applied to IIT"—it identifies a *specific dialectical trap* that IIT's defenders create for themselves, which has no parallel in Block's general critique of functionalism.

Here's what Block argues: Functionalist theories are *mistaken* because they define consciousness in terms of functional properties (access, reportability, cognitive integration) when the real target is phenomenal properties. His objection is that these theories start with the wrong definition—they're explaining the wrong thing from the outset.

Here's what this thesis argues: IIT's proponents present their theory's *empirical openness* as a methodological virtue—they say "look how scientific we are, we make falsifiable predictions, we might discover consciousness in unexpected places." But this very virtue commits them to treating consciousness as a theoretical posit. The thesis identifies a performative inconsistency: you cannot simultaneously claim (a) you're explaining phenomenal consciousness as the explanandum and (b) you'd follow phi wherever it leads as a mark of empirical rigor.

The crucial difference: Block says functionalist theories are wrong about what consciousness is. This thesis says IIT's defenders are *incoherent* about their own methodology—they want credit for empirical rigor while claiming to explain the phenomenon we all know firsthand, but these two stances are incompatible.

This is visible in how IIT's defenders respond to Block. They don't say "you're right, we're explaining access not phenomenality." They say "no, phi measures phenomenal consciousness itself—we're explaining exactly what you say needs explaining, we just make empirical predictions about where it occurs." The thesis shows this response is unstable: if you're really explaining the phenomenon-as-given, you can't treat its distribution as an open empirical question in the way IIT does.

The indexical certainty point plays completely different roles. For Block, it's evidence that P-consciousness is distinct from functional properties. For this thesis, it's the wedge that shows IIT's empirical openness creates reflexive incoherence—the theorist cannot coherently be open to discovering their own current cognitive activity isn't conscious.

Moreover, the thesis targets something Block doesn't: the specific way mathematical formalization masks the subject-change. IIT's defenders can say "we're not functionalists, we're not defining consciousness in terms of what it does, we're identifying the intrinsic structural properties that constitute phenomenality." The thesis shows that even this non-functionalist move commits them to treating consciousness as a theoretical posit once they embrace distributional revisability as a virtue.

Finally, the thesis is dialectically novel because it uses IIT's own self-presentation against it. IIT's proponents explicitly contrast themselves with "armchair philosophy" by emphasizing their empirical predictions. The thesis shows this very contrast reveals they've changed the subject—you can't have it both ways.

# VERDICT

The defence fails. The objection stands, and the thesis's claimed novelty collapses.

The defence tries to distinguish the thesis from Block's work along three lines: (1) Block says theories are mistaken, this thesis says they're incoherent; (2) Block targets functionalism generally, this thesis targets IIT's specific dialectical stance; (3) Block doesn't address mathematical formalization masking the problem. None survive scrutiny.

**First**, the mistake/incoherence distinction dissolves on inspection. Block's objection to functionalism *is* that it involves a kind of incoherence: claiming to explain phenomenal consciousness while actually explaining something else. The thesis's "performative inconsistency" is just a rhetorically sharpened version of Block's point that you can't genuinely explain P-consciousness by identifying it with functional properties. When Block argues that functionalists "change the subject," he means exactly what this thesis means: they're incoherent about whether they're explaining the phenomenon or defining a theoretical category.

**Second**, the claimed dialectical novelty evaporates because Block has explicitly addressed IIT. In his 2009 response to Tononi and in subsequent work, Block argues that IIT falls into precisely the trap he identified for functionalism: it treats consciousness as whatever-has-high-phi rather than as the phenomenon to be explained. The defence's claim that "IIT's defenders respond to Block by saying they explain phenomenality itself" is true—but Block's counter-response is that this is incoherent given their distributional revisability, which is exactly what this thesis argues. The thesis hasn't identified a trap IIT creates for itself; it's restated Block's existing argument that IIT falls into the general functionalist trap.

**Third**, the mathematical formalization point is superficial. Block has extensively discussed how computational and information-theoretic approaches mask the P-consciousness/A-consciousness confusion through technical sophistication. The thesis's observation that "IIT's mathematical sophistication masks the subject-change" adds no new philosophical content—it's an observation about rhetoric, not a novel conceptual point.

**Most devastatingly**, the defence's own example proves the objection. The defence says: "IIT's defenders can say 'we're not functionalists, we're identifying intrinsic structural properties that constitute phenomenality.' The thesis shows even this commits them to treating consciousness as a theoretical posit."

But this is *exactly Block's argument about IIT specifically*. Block argues that IIT's claim to identify "intrinsic structural properties" doesn't escape the problem because any property-based identification that allows distributional revision treats consciousness as a theoretical posit. The thesis presents as novel discovery what is actually Block's established position applied to IIT's particular formalism.

The thesis is Block's objection to functionalist/computational theories of consciousness, with "phi" substituted for "functional role" or "information integration" in the general case. This is not a novel contribution—it's an application of existing philosophical machinery to a specific theory, which Block himself has already performed.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis's argument proves too much because it would show that *any* physical theory of consciousness—not just IIT—"treats consciousness as a theoretical posit rather than the explanandum."

Consider a theory that says: "Consciousness is identical to such-and-such pattern of cortical activity." This theory seems maximally respectful of the explanandum—it's trying to identify what physical process realizes the phenomenon we know firsthand.

But now apply the thesis's logic: This cortical theory makes empirical predictions. We might discover through measurement that the predicted cortical pattern doesn't occur where we thought, or that it occurs in unexpected places, or that we were wrong about which cortical activity realizes consciousness. The theory's proponents could say "if the predicted pattern turns up elsewhere, that's where consciousness is"—showing empirical openness about distribution.

By the thesis's reasoning, this proves the cortical theory "treats consciousness as a theoretical posit." After all, it allows we could "discover our current measurements have been detecting the wrong physical systems as conscious." It permits distributional revision based on where the physical property appears.

But this is absurd. The cortical theory hasn't "changed the subject"—it's offering a physical explanation of consciousness while remaining open to empirical correction about the details. This is just what physical explanation looks like.

The thesis's criterion—that allowing distributional revisability shows subject-change—would indict *every* physical theory of consciousness, not just IIT. Any theory that identifies consciousness with physical properties must allow we could be wrong about which physical systems instantiate those properties. The thesis therefore fails to identify anything distinctive about IIT's treatment of consciousness.

# STRENGTH

**3/5** — This is a genuine problem requiring a real answer.

The attack succeeds in showing the thesis's criterion applies more broadly than intended. Any identity theory (consciousness = physical property P) that takes empirical investigation seriously must allow distributional revision. The thesis needs to explain why IIT's openness to revision shows subject-change while other physical theories' openness doesn't.

However, the attack doesn't completely destroy the thesis because a defender could argue there's something *distinctively problematic* about IIT's revisability—perhaps that phi is so abstract and structurally defined that it severs the connection to phenomenology in a way that, say, "specific cortical firing patterns" doesn't. The thesis might survive by showing IIT's particular criterion creates a *degree* of distributional openness that crosses a threshold other theories don't.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection fails because it ignores the crucial distinction between *local* distributional revisability (we might be wrong about details of the physical substrate) and *global* distributional revisability (we might be wrong about the entire category of systems we've been calling conscious).

The cortical activity theory says: "Consciousness is realized by cortical activity of type X." This allows local revision—perhaps we were wrong about which specific cortical patterns, or which cortical layers, or the exact temporal dynamics. But it doesn't allow global revision: the theory cannot coherently say "we might discover consciousness is actually in the cerebellum, not cortex at all" or "consciousness might turn up in feed-forward networks with no recurrence." Such discoveries would falsify the cortical theory, not vindicate it.

IIT is different. Its proponents explicitly embrace global distributional revisability as a theoretical virtue. Koch and Tononi don't say "we might be wrong about which cortical dynamics have high phi." They say "phi might turn up in completely unexpected systems—maybe the cerebellum, maybe feed-forward networks, maybe systems we've never considered conscious at all." They present this as showing IIT is genuinely empirical and falsifiable.

But this global openness is what reveals the subject-change. When a theory says "consciousness might turn out to be in entirely different kinds of systems than we thought," it's no longer explaining the phenomenon we identified pre-theoretically. It's defining a new theoretical category (high-phi systems) and stipulating that whatever has this property counts as conscious.

The cortical theory respects the explanandum because it's constrained by our pre-theoretical identification of which systems are conscious—it tries to find what's common to those systems. Local revision about details is compatible with this constraint. IIT abandons the constraint by treating the pre-theoretical identification as potentially globally mistaken.

Here's the test: Ask "what would falsify your theory?"

**Cortical theorist:** "If we found that cortical activity of type X doesn't occur in conscious systems, or occurs in non-conscious systems, the theory is falsified."

**IIT theorist:** "If we found high phi in unexpected places, those places are conscious. If we found low phi in expected places, those places aren't conscious. The theory predicts the distribution; it cannot be falsified by distribution."

This asymmetry shows IIT treats consciousness as whatever-has-phi (theoretical posit) while the cortical theory treats it as this-phenomenon-we're-trying-to-explain (explanandum).

The thesis stands: IIT's distinctive feature is global distributional revisability presented as a virtue, which reveals it has replaced the explanandum with a theoretical posit.

# VERDICT

The defence fails. The local/global distinction it offers is unstable and ultimately collapses.

The defence claims cortical theories allow only "local" revision (wrong about details) while IIT allows "global" revision (wrong about entire categories). But this distinction cannot bear the weight the defence places on it.

**First problem:** The distinction is unprincipled. Why is "wrong about which cortical patterns" merely local while "wrong about cortex vs. cerebellum" is global? Both involve being wrong about the physical substrate. The defence needs a principle for where the local/global boundary falls, but provides none. Is "wrong about cortical layers" local? Wrong about cortical vs. subcortical? Wrong about neural vs. glial? Without a principle, the distinction is arbitrary.

**Second problem:** Actual cortical theories do allow what the defence calls "global" revision. Consider theories that identified consciousness with specific cortical regions (prefrontal cortex, posterior cortex). These theories have been revised as evidence accumulated—consciousness might involve different regions, different patterns of connectivity, different scales of organization than initially predicted. Proponents of these theories didn't say "our theory is falsified"—they said "consciousness is realized differently than we thought." This is exactly parallel to IIT's openness.

**Third problem:** The defence's own falsification test backfires. The cortical theorist cannot actually say "if cortical activity X occurs in non-conscious systems, the theory is falsified" without begging the question about which systems are conscious. How do we know a system is non-conscious? If the answer is "by pre-theoretical intuition," then the cortical theory is just as vulnerable to the charge of treating intuitions as unrevisable. If the answer is "by independent theoretical criteria," then the cortical theory is doing exactly what IIT does—using theory to determine distribution.

**Fourth and most devastating problem:** The defence proves too much in the opposite direction. If global distributional revisability shows subject-change, then historical theories that underwent such revision must have changed the subject. But they didn't. Consider:

- **Heat:** Initially identified with everyday warm things. Kinetic theory allowed that we might be globally wrong—heat might be in molecular motion we can't feel, absent from things that feel warm. This was global revision, not local. Yet kinetic theory explained heat, didn't change the subject.

- **Life:** Initially identified with things that move, grow, reproduce observably. Cellular theory allowed global revision—life might be in microscopic entities, absent from things that seem alive (viruses are borderline). Yet cellular theory explained life.

The defence's criterion would show these theories changed the subject. But they didn't—they explained the phenomenon while correcting distributional beliefs.

The core problem remains: **The thesis cannot distinguish IIT's revisability from the normal revisability of physical explanation.** Every physical theory that identifies a phenomenon with properties allows that we might be wrong about distribution—locally or globally. The thesis's attempt to make IIT distinctive by pointing to "global" revisability fails because (a) the local/global distinction is unprincipled, and (b) paradigm cases of successful physical explanation involved global distributional revision.

The objection stands. The thesis proves too much.

</details>

## Attacked, ranked

*Everything else that went through the adversarial stage.*

### O1. Any theory of consciousness adequate to explain consciousness also explains why that theory is empirically indistinguishable from its rivals, which means theories can only be wrong in ways they predict, creating a perverse selection pressure for theories that make themselves unfalsifiable.

- **Promise 5/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** The widespread assumption that consciousness theories are in principle empirically decidable despite current practical limitations
- **Sketch:** Consider illusionism: it predicts that we'll have the strong intuition that consciousness is real and substantial, making it empirically indistinguishable from realism about consciousness. Higher-order theories predict that consciousness will seem immediate and non-higher-order, explaining why they seem phenomenologically wrong. IIT predicts that consciousness will seem irreducible to physical computation, explaining resistance to it. Each theory, to explain consciousness at all, must explain our epistemic situation regarding consciousness—including why rival theories seem plausible. But this means an adequate theory necessarily predicts its own empirical indistinguishability from alternatives. The key premise is that consciousness is precisely our epistemic access to our own minds, so any theory of it must be a theory of why we can't straightforwardly verify theories of it. This creates pressure toward theories that are maximally self-insulating.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Brilliant reflexive point—adequate theories must explain why they're indistinguishable from rivals, creating perverse selection pressure for unfalsifiability.
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence claimed constitutive versus evidential theory-ladenness but this just narrows the thesis to a subspecies of Quinean underdetermination
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence's instrumental/constitutive distinction concedes consciousness theories are testable via seeming-structure, contradicting the unfalsifiability claim
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence retreated to metaphysical constitution questions that float free of empirical evidence, abandoning recognizable empirical distinguishability
  - TRIVIAL — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence's symmetric/asymmetric distinction fails because phenomenology constrains theories normally—matching it is success, not self-insulation
- **Flaw:** The thesis survives only by redefining empirical distinguishability so broadly that all scientific theories become unfalsifiable regarding their subject matter, or so narrowly that normal phenomenological constraints count as verification

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT.

This is essentially a reformulation of **theory-ladenness of observation** combined with **underdetermination of theory by evidence**, both well-established positions in philosophy of science since at least Duhem (1906) and Quine (1951). The specific application to consciousness adds nothing structurally new.

The weak point is the claim that consciousness creates a *unique* epistemic situation. Duhem showed that in physics, any theory can be preserved by adjusting auxiliary hypotheses—theories protect themselves by explaining away contrary evidence. Quine extended this: empirical evidence never forces a unique theoretical interpretation. The thesis claims consciousness is special because "consciousness is precisely our epistemic access to our own minds," but this just means consciousness theories are theory-laden *about theory-ladenness itself*—a recursive application, not a novel problem.

The candidate's examples (illusionism predicting anti-illusionist intuitions, etc.) are instances of theories accommodating recalcitrant data, which happens everywhere in science. Physics theories explain why naive observation misleads us (relativity explains why simultaneity seems absolute). The "perverse selection pressure" toward unfalsifiability is just Quine's web of belief dynamics. Even the self-referential twist—theories of epistemic access explaining their own unverifiability—was explored by Feyerabend in discussing incommensurability.

**Position holder**: Quine's underdetermination thesis, applied recursively.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This significantly damages the thesis by showing it's a known epistemological position without novel force specific to consciousness.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE.

The objection misidentifies what is structurally novel. Yes, theory-ladenness and underdetermination are general features of theory-testing. But consciousness presents a *constitutive* rather than merely *evidential* version of this problem.

In physics, theory-ladenness means our observations are interpreted through theoretical frameworks, but the phenomena themselves (particle tracks, spectrometer readings) are not *identical to* our epistemic access to them. The data exists independently of our theorizing about what makes observation possible. When relativity explains why simultaneity seems absolute, it explains a *mismatch* between appearance and reality—the appearance is evidence *about* the reality, not the reality itself.

But consciousness *is* the epistemic access. The phenomenology isn't evidence *about* consciousness from which we infer its nature—it *is* the phenomenon we're trying to explain. When illusionism says "consciousness seems substantial but isn't," the seeming *is* the data set and the explanandum. When higher-order theory says "consciousness seems first-order but is higher-order," the seeming is both what we're explaining and our only evidence for what needs explaining.

This creates genuine structural novelty: theories must explain why the phenomenon appears to have exactly those features that would make the theory empirically unverifiable. It's not that evidence underdetermines theory (Quine), but that *the phenomenon itself* is constituted by the appearances that any adequate theory must explain away. The recursion isn't just theory-ladenness about theory-ladenness—it's that the thing being theorized *is* epistemic seeming, so explaining it *requires* explaining why it seems to refute your explanation.

This generates the specific perverse pressure: unlike in physics, you cannot appeal to theory-independent data because there is no consciousness independent of how consciousness seems. The more completely a theory explains consciousness, the more completely it must predict its own evidential invisibility.

VERDICT.

The defence fails, though it identifies something real.

The defender is right that consciousness has a distinctive *reflexive* character—we're theorizing about the very epistemic access through which we theorize. But this doesn't escape the objection's core point: this is still a *species* of the general underdetermination problem, not a *different kind* of problem.

The claimed novelty—that the phenomenon is identical to epistemic seeming—actually makes things *worse* for the thesis, not better. If consciousness literally *is* how things seem to us, then theories of consciousness are just theories about seemings, and these are empirically tractable in the normal way. We can test whether seemings have the structure higher-order theories predict, whether they exhibit the integration IIT predicts, whether they correlate with the physical processes functionalism predicts. The seemings themselves are the data.

The defence tries to have it both ways: consciousness is both "just seemings" (hence theories must explain seemings away) and also something theories can be adequate or inadequate to. But if seemings are the whole phenomenon, then a theory that correctly predicts the structure of seemings *is* empirically verified—it's not "empirically indistinguishable from rivals" at all.

The objection stands: this is Quinean underdetermination with reflexive application, not a novel epistemic trap specific to consciousness.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT.

The argument proves too much because if it worked, it would show that *any theory of epistemic access* faces the same "perverse selection pressure" toward unfalsifiability—yet we don't observe this pattern elsewhere.

Consider theories of perception. Any adequate theory of vision must explain why we have the visual experiences we do, including why vision seems direct and unmediated (explaining away naïve realism), why we don't notice our blind spots, why we experience color qualia the way we do. A theory of perception must explain our epistemic situation regarding the external world—including why rival theories of perception seem plausible. By the thesis's logic, theories of perception should face identical pressure toward self-insulating unfalsifiability.

Yet we *don't* see this. Vision science has made empirical progress. We can distinguish between theories by testing predictions about visual processing, illusions, neural correlates. The field hasn't collapsed into unfalsifiable theory-proliferation despite needing to explain "our epistemic access" to the world.

The same applies to theories of memory, attention, reasoning, language comprehension—all involve explaining epistemic access, all must account for why our introspective sense of these processes can be misleading, yet all have made empirical progress.

**The weak point is the premise** that consciousness is uniquely "our epistemic access to our own minds" in a way that creates special unfalsifiability pressure. If this premise worked, it would apply equally to all cognitive faculties that mediate epistemic access, generating the same perverse dynamics everywhere. Since it doesn't, the premise fails.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This is serious damage. The thesis needs to explain why consciousness specifically generates this dynamic when other theories of epistemic access don't exhibit the predicted pathology.

**DEFENDER** — [DEFENDER] BEST DEFENCE.

The objection fails because it conflates two radically different kinds of epistemic access. Vision, memory, attention—these are access *to other things*. Consciousness is access *to itself*.

When vision science explains why vision seems direct, it can appeal to facts about the world vision accesses (wavelengths, surfaces, spatial relations) and facts about visual processing (receptive fields, neural pathways) that are both independent of and prior to the visual seeming. The seeming is *about* something else. We can test whether the processing mechanisms generate the predicted seemings by varying the inputs and measuring the outputs. The epistemic circle can be broken because we can step outside it—we can study brains, measure stimuli, compare subjects.

But consciousness isn't access to anything beyond itself. When we ask "what is consciousness?", we're not asking what consciousness gives us access to (intentional contents, the external world)—we're asking what the accessing *itself* is. The phenomenon is the seeming, full stop. There's no independent domain consciousness is *about* that we can use to test theories, no prior mechanisms we can study independently of their seeming-character.

This is why the other cognitive faculties don't exhibit the pathology: they're *instrumental* epistemic access (tools for knowing other things), while consciousness is *constitutive* epistemic access (the knowing itself). For vision, we can ask "does this theory correctly predict how visual processing transforms input into output?" For consciousness, the only question is "does this theory correctly predict the seeming?" But any theory adequate to consciousness must predict that the seeming seems exactly as it does—including seeming to refute the theory.

The vision scientist can say "vision seems direct but here's the processing that makes it seem that way, and here's independent evidence for that processing." The consciousness theorist can only say "consciousness seems X but here's why it seems X"—and the seeming X *is* the phenomenon, so explaining it away *is* explaining it, which means the theory predicts its own empirical invisibility.

The disanalogy is complete: other cognitive faculties mediate access to independent domains and can be tested against those domains. Consciousness doesn't mediate access to anything—it *is* access, bare and self-contained.

VERDICT.

The defence fails.

The distinction between instrumental and constitutive epistemic access is real but doesn't save the thesis. The defence claims consciousness is unique because it's "access to itself" with no independent domain to test against—but this just concedes that consciousness theories *are* empirically testable in the normal way, contradicting the thesis.

If consciousness is "just seeming, full stop," then theories make straightforward empirical predictions about the structure, dynamics, and neural correlates of seemings. Does consciousness require global workspace broadcasting (GWT)? Check whether seemings correlate with global broadcasting. Does it require higher-order representation (HOT)? Check whether seemings require higher-order states. Does it require integrated information (IIT)? Measure phi and see if it tracks seemings. These are empirical questions with empirical answers.

The defence tries to block this by saying theories "explain seemings away," but this is confused. When HOT says "consciousness seems first-order but is higher-order," it's not making itself unfalsifiable—it's making a testable prediction that conscious states will have higher-order structure. If we find seemings without higher-order states, HOT is falsified. The fact that HOT predicts a mismatch between appearance and reality doesn't make it empirically indistinguishable from rivals—it makes a specific empirical claim about what structures underlie the appearances.

The objection stands: if the thesis worked for consciousness, it would work for vision (which also must explain why vision seems to have features it lacks). Since vision science makes empirical progress despite this, the thesis fails to identify a real epistemic trap specific to consciousness. The "constitutive" versus "instrumental" distinction doesn't create unfalsifiability—it just means consciousness theories are theories about seemings rather than about what seemings represent, which is a difference in subject matter, not epistemic status.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT.

**Counterexample: Global Workspace Theory vs. Higher-Order Thought Theory**

These two major theories of consciousness directly contradict the thesis that adequate theories become empirically indistinguishable from their rivals.

Global Workspace Theory (GWT) predicts consciousness requires information broadcast to a global workspace accessible to multiple cognitive systems. Higher-Order Thought Theory (HOT) predicts consciousness requires a higher-order representation *of* a mental state. These make distinct, testable predictions:

- **GWT predicts**: Conscious states will show widespread neural activation patterns (broadcast signatures); unconscious processing will lack global availability; consciousness will correlate with ignition events in prefrontal-parietal networks.

- **HOT predicts**: Conscious states require metarepresentational structures; damage to areas supporting higher-order thought (like prefrontal cortex) should eliminate consciousness while leaving first-order processing intact; consciousness will correlate with higher-order state formation, not broadcast per se.

These theories are *empirically distinguishable*. Dehaene's work on global ignition, studies of prefrontal damage, experiments on access consciousness—all generate evidence that favors one theory over the other. The theories make different predictions about neural correlates, about what happens in brain damage cases, about the temporal dynamics of conscious access.

**The weak point**: The premise that "any theory adequate to explain consciousness must explain why it's empirically indistinguishable from rivals." GWT and HOT are both serious attempts to explain consciousness, yet they remain empirically distinguishable through their different predictions about mechanisms, neural correlates, and dissociation patterns.

STRENGTH: 5/5

This is a direct counterexample. Two well-developed consciousness theories that the thesis should predict are empirically indistinguishable turn out to make clearly different empirical predictions that ongoing research can and does test.

**DEFENDER** — [DEFENDER] BEST DEFENCE.

The objection trades on an equivocation about what "empirically distinguishable" means in the context of the thesis. GWT and HOT make different predictions about *mechanisms and correlates*, but they are empirically indistinguishable regarding *whether they correctly explain consciousness itself*.

The thesis doesn't claim theories make no differential predictions about brain activity—it claims they make themselves unfalsifiable regarding whether they've captured what consciousness *is*. GWT and HOT illustrate this perfectly:

Suppose we find that conscious states correlate with global workspace broadcasting. Does this verify GWT? Not necessarily—HOT can reply: "The broadcast is just what enables higher-order representation; the consciousness is the higher-order state, not the broadcast." The broadcast becomes an enabling condition, not the constitutive condition.

Suppose we find conscious states require higher-order representation. Does this verify HOT? Not necessarily—GWT can reply: "The higher-order representation is just one consequence of global availability; the consciousness is the broadcast that makes higher-order representation possible." The higher-order state becomes a consequence, not the constitution.

Each theory can accommodate the other's "signature" by reinterpreting it as either enabling condition or consequence rather than constitutive condition. This is precisely the self-insulating dynamic the thesis predicts: theories explain consciousness adequately enough that they can explain why the rival theory's predictions obtain without the rival theory being *correct about what consciousness is*.

The empirical differences the objection cites are real but they're differences in *correlates*, not differences that could settle which theory correctly identifies what consciousness *consists in*. Finding that consciousness correlates with broadcast doesn't tell us whether consciousness *is* broadcast (GWT) or whether broadcast merely enables the higher-order states that *are* consciousness (HOT). The theories predict different mechanisms but remain empirically indistinguishable regarding the metaphysical question they purport to answer.

This is exactly what the thesis predicts: adequate theories generate enough explanatory power to reinterpret rival predictions as compatible with their own account, making them unfalsifiable at the level of "what is consciousness?" even while making differential predictions about correlates.

VERDICT.

The defence fails.

The distinction between "correlates" and "constitutive conditions" doesn't save the thesis—it destroys it by revealing that the thesis has abandoned any recognizable sense of empirical distinguishability.

The defence now claims GWT and HOT are "empirically indistinguishable regarding whether they correctly explain consciousness itself" because each can reinterpret the other's findings as "enabling conditions" rather than "constitutive conditions." But this move is available to *any* theories in *any* domain of science. 

Phlogiston theory could say oxygen is just an "enabling condition" for the combustion that phlogiston theory correctly explains. Ptolemaic astronomy could say heliocentric observations are "compatible" with geocentrism properly understood—the math works either way, it's just a question of which frame is "constitutive." Vitalism could say biochemical mechanisms are "enabling conditions" for the vital force that *constitutes* life.

The reason we reject these moves isn't that we have some special metaphysical access to "constitutive conditions"—it's that empirical science *identifies* constitutive conditions through the very patterns of correlation, mechanism, and dissociation that the defence dismisses as "merely correlates." When we find that combustion requires oxygen intake and produces CO₂, we don't say "but maybe oxygen just enables phlogiston release"—we say oxygen *is* the constitutive factor because it does the explanatory work.

If GWT correctly predicts that consciousness correlates with global broadcasting, that damage to broadcast mechanisms eliminates consciousness while leaving first-order processing intact, that the temporal dynamics of conscious access match broadcast dynamics—then GWT has given us empirical reason to think broadcast *is* constitutive of consciousness, not merely an enabler. That's what empirical distinguishability *is*.

The defence's retreat to "metaphysical questions about what consciousness consists in" that float free of all empirical evidence is precisely the pathology the objection exposes: the thesis survives only by defining "empirically indistinguishable" in a way that makes *all* scientific theories "empirically indistinguishable" regarding what they're theories *of*. This isn't a discovery about consciousness—it's a dissolution of empirical testability itself.

The counterexample stands: GWT and HOT are empirically distinguishable in exactly the way scientific theories are supposed to be distinguishable, and the thesis's attempt to deny this reveals that it has no coherent content.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis appears to make a striking claim about consciousness theories, but it actually just restates the trivial observation that *any* theory explaining why we believe X must explain why we believe X, dressed up as a discovery about consciousness specifically.

**The weak point is the core premise**: "consciousness is precisely our epistemic access to our own minds, so any theory of it must be a theory of why we can't straightforwardly verify theories of it."

This is just definitional maneuvering. The thesis stipulates that consciousness theories must explain our epistemic situation regarding consciousness, then announces this creates "perverse selection pressure" toward unfalsifiability. But this is trivially true of *any* theory about belief-formation or epistemic access: a theory of vision must explain why we have visual beliefs, a theory of memory must explain why we have memory beliefs, a theory of testimony must explain why we trust testimony.

The supposed "instability" is just the uncontroversial point that adequate theories accommodate the evidence—including evidence about what seems true to us. When the thesis says illusionism "predicts that we'll have strong intuitions that consciousness is real," this isn't a perverse dynamic; it's just illusionism doing its job of explaining the data (our intuitions). Every theory explains why we believe what we believe; calling this "self-insulating" is like calling it "self-insulating" that germ theory explains why we believed in miasmas (we couldn't see microbes).

The thesis takes the pedestrian requirement that theories explain the phenomena (including our beliefs about the phenomena) and redescribes it as a special epistemic trap, but no actual trap has been identified—just the ordinary theory-data relationship given a sinister-sounding name.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This seriously damages the thesis by showing the supposed "perverse selection pressure" is just the trivial requirement that theories explain their evidence, repackaged as a discovery.

**DEFENDER** — [DEFENDER] BEST DEFENCE.

The objection misses the specific structural feature that makes this non-trivial. Yes, every theory must explain the evidence, including why we believe what we believe. But consciousness theories face a unique closure condition: they must explain why *the very faculty they're theorizing about* generates beliefs that make the theory unverifiable.

When germ theory explains why we believed in miasmas, it appeals to our *ignorance*—we couldn't see microbes, so we inferred wrongly. The explanation is: "You lacked evidence X, so you concluded Y." This is asymmetric: germ theory explains past error by citing what we *didn't* know, which we *now* know.

But consciousness theories can't do this. Illusionism can't say "you lacked evidence for consciousness, so you wrongly inferred it exists"—because the evidence *is* consciousness (the seeming itself). The theory must say: "The very thing I'm explaining (consciousness) generates the appearance that makes my explanation of it (that it's illusory) seem false." This is symmetric: the theory explains why its own evidence appears to refute it, not why we lacked evidence.

This creates the perverse pressure: a theory succeeds by explaining why the phenomenon *as experienced* contradicts the theory. Illusionism succeeds *to the degree* it explains why consciousness seems robustly real (making illusionism seem false). HOT succeeds *to the degree* it explains why consciousness seems first-order (making HOT seem false). The better the explanation, the more the phenomenon-as-experienced appears to falsify it.

This isn't trivial. It's not just "theories explain evidence"—it's "theories succeed by explaining why their evidence appears to be evidence against them." The selection pressure is real: theories that generate this self-undermining appearance are *rewarded* because that's what explaining consciousness *is*—explaining why consciousness appears to have exactly those features that would make your theory of it empirically undetectable.

Vision theory doesn't face this. A theory of vision succeeds by explaining vision, not by explaining why vision makes the theory seem false. The asymmetry is crucial.

VERDICT.

The defence fails, though it identifies something real about the phenomenology of theorizing about consciousness.

The defender is right that there's a distinctive reflexive structure: consciousness theories must explain why consciousness-as-experienced seems to have features the theory denies. But this doesn't create the "perverse selection pressure" toward unfalsifiability that the thesis claims.

The key error is in the phrase "theories succeed by explaining why their evidence appears to be evidence against them." This mischaracterizes what's happening. When illusionism explains why consciousness seems substantial, it's not explaining why the evidence *appears to contradict* illusionism—it's explaining the evidence itself (the seeming of substantiality). That seeming is exactly what illusionism predicts and must explain. Far from making illusionism unfalsifiable, this is precisely what makes it testable: if consciousness *didn't* seem substantial, that would falsify illusionism.

Similarly, when HOT explains why consciousness seems first-order, this isn't self-undermining—it's a testable prediction. If phenomenology *didn't* have this first-order character, HOT would need to explain why, and that would be evidence against it. The fact that HOT must explain first-order phenomenology is what makes it empirically constrained, not empirically unconstrained.

The defender's "asymmetry" between germ theory and consciousness theories actually cuts the opposite way. Germ theory could explain away any observations ("you can't see microbes because they're too small") in a genuinely self-insulating way. But consciousness theories can't explain away the phenomenology—the phenomenology *is* the data they must match. If illusionism predicted consciousness would seem illusory, or if HOT predicted consciousness would seem higher-order, they'd be straightforwardly falsified by the actual phenomenology.

The objection stands: the thesis redescribes the ordinary requirement that theories explain their evidence (including phenomenological evidence) as a "perverse selection pressure," but no actual perversity has been identified. Theories that correctly predict phenomenological features are empirically successful; theories that fail to predict them are empirically unsuccessful. This is normal science, not an epistemic trap.

</details>

---

### O2. Any theory of consciousness that can be empirically tested has already failed as a theory of consciousness, because testability requires operationalizing consciousness in terms of access mechanisms that presuppose rather than explain the phenomenal.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Integrated Information Theory, Global Workspace Theory, and other theories that claim to be empirically testable theories of consciousness
- **Sketch:** The argument proceeds by examining what empirical testability actually requires. To test whether system X is conscious, we need observable consequences that differ between the conscious and non-conscious cases. But any such observable consequence must be specified in functional/behavioral terms (reports, discriminations, integrations). This means we've operationalized consciousness in terms of access-consciousness or reportability. However, this operationalization assumes we've already solved the hard problem—that we know these access mechanisms correlate with phenomenal consciousness. Therefore, theories claiming to be empirically testable about phenomenal consciousness are either (a) actually only theories about access-consciousness, or (b) smuggling in the very consciousness-access correlation they should be explaining. The key premise is that operationalization for empirical testing requires specifying outcomes in third-person observable terms, which necessarily bypasses rather than captures the first-person phenomenal character.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The operationalization trap is sharp—testability requires assuming what should be explained—and clearly articulated.
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence attempted to distinguish consciousness from pain/memory/emotion but the distinction (pre-theoretical consensus about access mechanisms) actually undermines the thesis rather than saving it
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence correctly noted IIT relies on access-based testing criteria but this reveals confusion about scientific methodology rather than vindicating the thesis—all theories use observational criteria they don't themselves explain
  - TRIVIAL — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence argued the phenomenal concept has independent philosophical motivation, but this makes the thesis worse not better—it shows phenomenal consciousness (so conceived) is not an appropriate empirical target at all, making the thesis a tautology
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence claimed univocal usage but actually revealed the fatal ambiguity—if phenomenal consciousness is part of ordinary consciousness then operationalization succeeds; if it's pure residue then the thesis proves too much
- **Flaw:** The thesis equivocates between ordinary consciousness (making empirical theories relevant targets) and pure phenomenal consciousness (making operationalization seem inadequate), and every defence either proves too much by making consciousness empirically intractable by definition, or collapses the distinction that makes the thesis seem non-trivial

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. If this argument worked, it would equally show that any empirical theory of *anything* subjective or first-person has already failed. Consider pain: to test whether system X feels pain, we need observable consequences (withdrawal behaviors, neural patterns, verbal reports). By the thesis's logic, we've now operationalized pain in terms of access mechanisms (behavioral responses, reportability), which "presupposes rather than explains" the phenomenal character of pain. Therefore any testable theory of pain has already failed. The same applies to memory (testing requires recall behaviors), emotion (requires expression/report), perception (requires discrimination/report), attention, etc. The weak premise is the claim that "operationalization assumes we've already solved the hard problem"—this conflates *correlation* with *explanation*. We can empirically discover that certain access mechanisms reliably correlate with phenomenal states (as we do with pain and C-fiber firing) without presupposing we've explained the correlation. The thesis treats any empirical approach to consciousness as circular, but this would make empirical investigation of any mental phenomenon impossible, which is absurd—we successfully study pain, memory, and emotion empirically despite their subjective character.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is serious damage. The thesis appears to prove that empirical psychology and neuroscience of *all* subjective phenomena are impossible, which is demonstrably false. The argument needs a principled way to distinguish consciousness from other mental phenomena.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies what makes consciousness uniquely resistant to empirical operationalization. The crucial difference is this: for pain, memory, emotion, and perception, there exists a *pre-theoretical* consensus about which access mechanisms constitute genuine instances of the phenomenon. We don't need to solve any "hard problem of pain" to know that C-fiber firing plus withdrawal behavior plus sincere pain reports in a normally functioning human constitutes actual pain—this is simply what pain is in our ordinary concept. The access mechanisms don't merely correlate with pain; they partially constitute it.

Consciousness is different. There is no pre-theoretical consensus that reportability, global availability, or information integration constitutes consciousness. This is precisely what's at issue. The philosophical zombie scenario—whatever its ultimate cogency—has no parallel for pain: we cannot coherently conceive of a system with all the functional/behavioral/neural markers of pain that nonetheless isn't in pain, but we can (allegedly) conceive of this for consciousness. This asymmetry is not arbitrary. It reflects that consciousness, unlike pain, is defined purely by its phenomenal character rather than by any functional role.

When we operationalize pain through withdrawal and report, we're not *presupposing* a correlation between access and phenomenology—we're working with a concept that was never purely phenomenological to begin with. Pain is essentially tied to its functional role (harm avoidance, learning, motivation). But consciousness, as the target theories understand it, is defined as "what it's like"—pure phenomenality. To operationalize *that* requires establishing that certain access mechanisms indicate phenomenality, which is precisely what these theories claim to explain.

The objection's other cases don't prove too much because they were never trying to explain a pure phenomenal-functional gap in the first place.

VERDICT

The defence fails, though it dies honorably.

The defender is right that there's an asymmetry, but it runs in the wrong direction. Yes, pain involves both phenomenal and functional components in our ordinary concept. But this makes the empirical project *easier*, not harder, for pain than for consciousness. If consciousness is "pure phenomenality," then the thesis's problem becomes *worse*: we have even less justification for treating any operational measure as tracking it. But if consciousness, like pain, has both phenomenal and functional components built into the concept, then operational measures can legitimately target the functional component—and the thesis's objection loses force.

The zombie argument's availability actually undermines the defence. If we can conceive of all the functional markers without consciousness, this shows that our operational measures are *not* measuring what the thesis calls "phenomenal consciousness"—which is exactly the thesis's complaint. But then the thesis proves too much in a different way: it shows that empirical theories can't capture something that may not be empirically tractable at all. That's not a failure of the theories; it's a feature of what they're trying to study.

The core problem remains: the thesis must either accept that consciousness has functional components (making operationalization legitimate, defeating the thesis) or insist on pure phenomenality (making the thesis's demand impossible to meet, which proves too much—not about other mental states, but about whether consciousness so conceived is a proper target of empirical theory at all). The objection stands.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. Consider Integrated Information Theory's prediction about the cerebellum. IIT operationalizes consciousness in terms of integrated information (Φ). It predicts that the cerebellum, despite having more neurons than the cerebral cortex, generates minimal consciousness because its modular architecture produces low Φ. This prediction can be empirically tested: damage to the cerebellum causes motor deficits but not loss of consciousness, while much smaller cortical lesions can eliminate consciousness. Here we have a concrete case where the thesis gives the wrong result. The thesis claims any empirically testable theory "has already failed" because operationalization "presupposes rather than explains the phenomenal." But IIT's operationalization (integrated information) does NOT presuppose which physical systems are conscious—it derives this from first principles about information integration. The cerebellum case shows the theory making a non-obvious, empirically confirmable prediction that distinguishes it from naive correlations. The weak premise is "operationalization requires... access mechanisms that presuppose... the phenomenal." IIT operationalizes via mathematical properties of causal structures, not via "access mechanisms" like reportability. It then predicts which systems will have access capabilities based on their Φ, rather than presupposing the access-phenomenal link. The thesis falsely assumes all operationalization works through behavioral/report measures.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This significantly damages the thesis. IIT demonstrates that operationalization need not work through access mechanisms or presuppose consciousness-correlation. The theory makes surprising predictions from structural principles.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection fundamentally misunderstands what "operationalization" means in the context of empirical testing. IIT's cerebellum case does not escape the thesis's critique—it exemplifies it perfectly.

Consider what actual empirical confirmation of IIT's cerebellum prediction requires. We must test whether the cerebellum "generates minimal consciousness." How? The objection says: observe that cerebellar damage causes motor deficits "but not loss of consciousness." But how do we detect "loss of consciousness"? Through access mechanisms—the patient still reports experiences, still responds to stimuli, still exhibits wakeful behavior. We've operationalized "consciousness is present" as "access capabilities remain intact." 

The objection claims IIT "derives from first principles" which systems are conscious without presupposing anything. But this confuses the theory's internal derivation with empirical testability. Yes, IIT calculates Φ from structural properties. But to TEST whether high Φ actually correlates with consciousness, we need empirical criteria for when consciousness is present or absent. Those criteria are inevitably access-based: Can the system report? Does it respond discriminatively? Does it exhibit integrated behavior?

The cerebellum case reveals this perfectly. Why do we believe cerebellar damage doesn't eliminate consciousness? Because patients remain awake, responsive, and reportive. Why do we believe cortical lesions can eliminate consciousness? Because patients become unresponsive, unreportive—they lose access capabilities. IIT's "surprising prediction" is only empirically confirmable because we're already using access mechanisms as our criterion for consciousness.

The objection says IIT "predicts which systems will have access capabilities based on their Φ, rather than presupposing the access-phenomenal link." But this gets it backwards. IIT predicts Φ values from structure, then claims high Φ means consciousness. To test this claim empirically, we must check whether high-Φ systems actually are conscious. And we check this by... observing their access capabilities. The access-phenomenal link isn't derived by IIT—it's presupposed in every empirical test of IIT's predictions.

The mathematical operationalization (Φ) is not itself an empirical test. It's a calculation. The empirical test comes when we ask: "Does high Φ track consciousness?" And answering that question requires we already know which systems are conscious, which we determine through access mechanisms.

VERDICT

The defence fails.

The defender is right that IIT's empirical testing ultimately relies on access-based criteria for consciousness—this is unavoidable. But this does not vindicate the thesis; it reveals the thesis's confusion about what scientific theories do.

The defender claims we "presuppose the access-phenomenal link" when we use behavioral evidence to test IIT. But there's a crucial difference between *presupposing a link exists* and *presupposing we've explained it*. Every scientific theory relies on observational criteria that aren't themselves explained by the theory. Gravitational theory uses position measurements without explaining why objects have positions. Thermodynamics uses temperature readings without explaining why molecular motion feels hot.

IIT does exactly what scientific theories should do: it proposes a structural property (integrated information) as the basis for consciousness, then makes predictions about which systems have this property. We test these predictions using the best available evidence—which includes behavioral and reportive criteria, yes, but also neural imaging, lesion studies, and comparative anatomy. The theory doesn't presuppose it has explained why high Φ correlates with phenomenal consciousness any more than gravitational theory presupposes it has explained why mass correlates with gravitational attraction. It proposes the correlation and seeks confirmation.

The thesis demands something no empirical theory could provide: testing that doesn't rely on observable criteria. But this makes the thesis's target incoherent. If consciousness leaves no empirical trace—if no observable difference marks the conscious/non-conscious boundary—then it's not a proper subject for empirical theory at all. The thesis proves too much: not that testable theories have failed, but that it demands something empirical science cannot and should not provide.

The objection stands. IIT shows that operationalization through structural properties, tested against multiple streams of evidence, is exactly how consciousness science should proceed.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis presents itself as a substantive discovery about empirical consciousness research, but it's actually a trivial consequence of how we've chosen to define "phenomenal consciousness." The argument works by stipulating that phenomenal consciousness is *by definition* whatever cannot be captured by any functional, behavioral, or access-based characterization—it's the "pure what-it's-like-ness" that remains after all functional properties are subtracted. Given this definition, of course any operationalization in terms of observable properties will "miss" phenomenal consciousness—we've defined it precisely as what such operationalizations miss. The weak premise is the claim that operationalization "presupposes rather than explains the phenomenal." This sounds like an empirical discovery about what theories actually do, but it's merely unpacking a verbal stipulation about what "phenomenal" means. The thesis is like arguing: "Any empirical theory of élan vital that can be tested has already failed, because testability requires operationalization in terms of biochemical mechanisms, which presupposes rather than explains the vital force." Once we've defined the target as essentially unobservable and non-functional, the "failure" of empirical approaches is guaranteed by definition, not discovered through argument. The thesis mistakes a definitional boundary (how we've chosen to use "phenomenal") for a substantive insight about consciousness or scientific methodology. It's conceptual bookkeeping presented as philosophical discovery.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is serious damage. The thesis's force depends entirely on a contestable definition of "phenomenal consciousness" as essentially non-functional. Once we see this is definitional rather than discovered, the thesis becomes an uninteresting tautology about how we've drawn conceptual boundaries, not a substantive claim about what consciousness science can or cannot achieve.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection mischaracterizes the dialectical situation. The thesis is not *stipulating* that phenomenal consciousness is non-functional and then trivially deriving that functional operationalization fails. Rather, it's responding to an existing philosophical landscape where this distinction already has independent motivation and explanatory work to do.

Consider the actual history. The access/phenomenal distinction arose not from arbitrary stipulation but from explanatory need: Block introduced it to explain why we have conflicting intuitions about cases like superblindsight or absent qualia. The zombie argument, whether sound or not, has genuine intuitive force that predates any definitional gerrymandering. The explanatory gap—why these physical processes feel like *this*—is a pre-theoretical puzzle, not a verbal trick. These aren't artifacts of how philosophers chose to use words; they're attempts to capture something that seems to require explanation.

The élan vital comparison fails because vitalism was *empirically* defeated—we discovered that biochemical mechanisms could do all the explanatory work vital forces were posited to do. But the thesis's point is precisely that access mechanisms *cannot* do all the explanatory work phenomenal consciousness was posited to do, and this isn't just true by definition. There are substantive reasons: the conceivability of zombies, the persistence of the explanatory gap, the fact that knowing all the functional facts seems to leave the phenomenal facts unexplained.

The objection claims we've "defined the target as essentially unobservable and non-functional." But this reverses the order of discovery. We didn't start with a definition and derive unobservability. We started with the observation that complete functional/physical descriptions seem to leave something out—what Nagel called "what it's like"—and the phenomenal concept emerged from trying to capture what's left out. The thesis is making a substantive claim: that this remainder, which has independent philosophical motivation, cannot be captured by empirical operationalization.

Moreover, the thesis isn't claiming consciousness *itself* is unobservable—I observe my own consciousness directly through introspection. The claim is that *third-person* empirical operationalization cannot capture the *first-person* phenomenal character. This asymmetry between first- and third-person access is not definitional; it's a substantive feature of consciousness that requires explanation.

The objection also ignores that the thesis has critical bite even if we accept functional elements in consciousness. Even theories that acknowledge both phenomenal and access aspects must explain their relationship. The thesis argues that empirical testability forces theories to operationalize in terms of access, thereby *assuming* rather than explaining the access-phenomenal connection. This is a substantive methodological critique, not a trivial definitional truth.

VERDICT

The defence fails, though it identifies something important.

The defender is right that the access/phenomenal distinction has philosophical motivation beyond mere stipulation. The explanatory gap and zombie intuitions are genuine philosophical phenomena requiring serious engagement. But this actually makes the thesis's position *worse*, not better.

Here's why: if phenomenal consciousness is genuinely the kind of thing that leaves an explanatory gap even after all functional facts are known, and if zombies are genuinely conceivable, then the thesis isn't identifying a *failure* of empirical theories—it's identifying that phenomenal consciousness (so conceived) is not an appropriate target for empirical theory at all. The thesis presents this as a damning critique of IIT, GWT, etc., but it's actually a reductio of the phenomenal concept itself as a scientific target.

The defender says "we didn't start with a definition and derive unobservability"—but this is precisely what happened historically. Philosophers encountered the explanatory gap, then *reified* it into a kind of property (phenomenal consciousness) that essentially has the gap built into it. The concept of phenomenal consciousness as "pure what-it's-like-ness" is a *theoretical posit* designed to capture what seems unexplained, not a pre-theoretical datum.

The crucial point is this: if the thesis is right that empirical operationalization necessarily fails to capture phenomenal consciousness, this doesn't show that empirical theories have failed—it shows that "phenomenal consciousness" (in the thesis's sense) is not a natural kind that empirical science should be trying to capture. It's a philosophical artifact of how we've conceptualized the explanatory gap.

The objection's charge stands: the thesis is trivial because it amounts to saying "if you define the target as what empirical methods can't reach, then empirical methods can't reach it." That this definition has philosophical motivation doesn't make the resulting claim non-trivial—it just means philosophers have motivated reasons for drawing the boundary where they have. But drawing it there makes the thesis's "discovery" a tautology, not a substantive insight about consciousness science.

The thesis mistakes a conceptual decision (to reify the explanatory gap as a property) for an empirical discovery about what theories can achieve.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "theory of consciousness" between two readings that make the argument superficially work but ultimately beg the question. 

On one reading, a "theory of consciousness" is a theory of phenomenal consciousness specifically—the intrinsic, first-person, what-it's-like character that supposedly transcends functional description. On this reading, the thesis's conclusion is plausible: such theories cannot be empirically tested because phenomenal consciousness (so defined) leaves no third-person empirical trace distinct from access mechanisms.

On another reading, a "theory of consciousness" is a theory of consciousness as we ordinarily understand it—the difference between being awake and asleep, between seeing and being blind, between sentience and non-sentience. On this reading, the thesis's premise about empirical testability is plausible: yes, we operationalize through reports, discriminations, and behavioral markers.

The argument needs both readings. It needs the ordinary reading to make the premise about operationalization seem reasonable and to make the targets (IIT, GWT) seem relevant—these are theories of consciousness in the ordinary sense. But it needs the phenomenal reading to make the conclusion seem damaging—that operationalization "presupposes rather than explains the phenomenal."

The equivocation is clearest in the phrase "has already failed as a theory of consciousness." Failed at what? If we use the phenomenal reading consistently, these theories never claimed to explain pure phenomenality in the first place—they're theories of consciousness in the ordinary sense. If we use the ordinary reading consistently, operationalizing through access mechanisms is perfectly appropriate, not a failure.

The weak premise is: "testability requires operationalizing consciousness in terms of access mechanisms that presuppose rather than explain the phenomenal." This only seems problematic if "consciousness" means phenomenal-consciousness-as-pure-what-it's-like in "explain the phenomenal" but means ordinary-consciousness in "operationalizing consciousness." The argument cannot run on either reading alone.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is serious damage. The thesis appears to discover a deep problem with consciousness science, but it actually exploits an ambiguity about what "consciousness" means, using the ordinary sense to make its targets relevant and the phenomenal sense to make operationalization seem inadequate.

**DEFENDER** — [DEFENDER] BEST DEFENCE

The objection claims there are two distinct concepts—"phenomenal consciousness" and "ordinary consciousness"—and the thesis illicitly slides between them. But this is a false dichotomy. There is one phenomenon with two aspects, and the thesis's point is precisely that empirical theories systematically neglect one aspect while claiming to explain the whole.

Consider what "ordinary consciousness" actually involves. When we distinguish waking from sleeping, seeing from blindness, sentience from non-sentience, we are not merely tracking functional differences. The ordinary concept includes the phenomenal dimension essentially. Being awake doesn't just mean responding to stimuli—it means experiencing. Seeing isn't just discriminating light patterns—it involves visual phenomenology. The "ordinary" concept is already thick with phenomenal content. We don't have a pre-phenomenal ordinary concept that could be cleanly separated.

The objection's proposed "ordinary consciousness" that's purely functional is actually a philosopher's construct—it's what philosophers of mind call "access consciousness." But this is not what ordinary people mean by consciousness, nor what IIT and GWT claim to explain. These theories explicitly claim to explain *phenomenal* consciousness—why there is "something it is like" to be in certain information-processing states. Tononi doesn't say IIT explains mere access; he says it explains qualia, experience, phenomenology. The theories present themselves as solving the hard problem, not just mapping functional architecture.

So there's no equivocation. The thesis uses "consciousness" consistently to mean what these theories claim to explain: phenomenal consciousness, the what-it's-like dimension, the target of the hard problem. The thesis then shows that their empirical testing methods cannot actually capture this target because operationalization requires third-person observable criteria, which necessarily operationalize access rather than phenomenology.

The objection asks: "Failed at what?" The answer is univocal: failed at explaining phenomenal consciousness, which is what they claim to explain. IIT claims integrated information *is* phenomenal consciousness. GWT claims global availability *constitutes* conscious experience.
