These are not theories that modestly restrict themselves to "ordinary consciousness" while disclaiming phenomenal ambitions. They claim to explain why certain physical processes feel like something. The thesis argues their empirical methods cannot deliver on this claim.

The objection's mistake is assuming that because phenomenal consciousness is philosophically problematic, it must be a technical concept separate from ordinary consciousness. But the reverse is true: phenomenal consciousness is what makes consciousness ordinarily interesting. Strip away the phenomenal and you have mere information processing, which nobody would call consciousness. The reason we care about the difference between waking and sleeping, seeing and blindness, is precisely because these involve phenomenal differences—differences in what it's like.

Therefore, no equivocation. One concept (phenomenal consciousness), which is what both ordinary usage and scientific theories target, which empirical operationalization systematically fails to capture.

VERDICT

The defence fails completely.

The defender claims "there is one phenomenon with two aspects" and that ordinary consciousness essentially includes phenomenal content. But this response actually *strengthens* the objection by revealing how the thesis needs to have things both ways.

If ordinary consciousness essentially includes phenomenal content, then when IIT and GWT operationalize consciousness through access mechanisms and successfully predict which systems are conscious in the ordinary sense, they *have* captured phenomenal consciousness—because phenomenal consciousness just is (part of) ordinary consciousness. The thesis cannot simultaneously claim that (a) phenomenal consciousness is essentially included in ordinary consciousness, and (b) theories that successfully track ordinary consciousness have failed to capture phenomenal consciousness.

The defender says these theories "claim to explain phenomenal consciousness" as if this settles the matter. But the objection's point is precisely that "phenomenal consciousness" is ambiguous between:
- The ordinary phenomenal aspect (what it's like to see red, feel pain, etc.)—which is empirically tractable
- The pure phenomenal residue after all functional facts are known—which may be a philosopher's fiction

IIT and GWT claim to explain the former. The thesis accuses them of failing at the latter. This is the equivocation.

The defender's own examples reveal this. When discussing "being awake doesn't just mean responding to stimuli—it means experiencing," the defender uses "experiencing" to do all the work. But what is experiencing? If it's something empirically detectable (people report experiences, behave as if experiencing, show neural signatures of experiencing), then operationalizing it is legitimate. If it's something that could be absent despite all these markers (the zombie scenario), then we're in the pure-phenomenal reading where the thesis proves too much—it shows not that theories have failed but that the target is empirically intractable by definition.

The defender claims no pre-phenomenal ordinary concept exists, but this is false. We successfully identify consciousness in infants, animals, and patients who cannot report, using behavioral and neural criteria. We do this without first solving the hard problem. This shows we have workable empirical criteria for consciousness that don't presuppose we've explained the explanatory gap.

Most devastatingly, the defender says: "Strip away the phenomenal and you have mere information processing, which nobody would call consciousness." But IIT's whole point is that certain kinds of information processing (high Φ) *constitute* phenomenal consciousness—not that they merely correlate with some separate phenomenal property. If the defender is right that this reduces consciousness to "mere" information processing, then the defender has conceded that IIT fails—but for reasons having nothing to do with empirical testability. The thesis's argument about operationalization becomes irrelevant.

The equivocation stands. The thesis needs "consciousness" to mean ordinary-consciousness (to make IIT and GWT relevant targets) and pure-phenomenal-consciousness (to make operationalization seem inadequate) simultaneously. The defence's attempt to unify these actually reveals they come apart: either phenomenal consciousness is empirically tractable (making operationalization legitimate) or it's not (making the thesis trivial). No middle ground survives scrutiny.

</details>

---

### O3. The apparent inability to test consciousness theories reveals that they are actually competing analyses of our concept of consciousness rather than empirical theories, but this doesn't make them unverifiable—it makes them answerable to conceptual engineering criteria like fruitfulness and reflective equilibrium instead.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** Both scientific realists about consciousness (Chalmers, Koch) and eliminativists (Frankish) who agree that consciousness theories are empirical theories that happen to be hard to test
- **Sketch:** Philosophers treat the testability problem as a crisis revealing something wrong with consciousness science. But actually, we should recognize that theories like IIT, HOT, and GWT are doing conceptual work—they're competing proposals for what we should mean by 'consciousness' given our various theoretical and practical purposes. The key premise is that the persistent inability to find decisive empirical tests, despite decades of effort, is not a bug but a feature: it reveals these are fundamentally proposals about how to precisify a folk concept, not discoveries about a pre-existing natural kind. This doesn't make them arbitrary—they can be evaluated on whether they serve our purposes (clinical diagnosis, AI rights, understanding ourselves), cohere with related concepts, and survive reflective scrutiny. The debate should shift from 'which theory is true?' to 'which precisification is optimal for which purposes?'
- **Appraiser (blind):** Reframing as conceptual engineering rather than empirical discovery could genuinely redirect the field's self-understanding.
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 2/5, answered. The defender showed the thesis uses 'empirical theory' consistently in the narrow sense and that the diagnostic criterion does the work, not definition-shifting.
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defender's falsification conditions either require indefinite waiting or depend on criteria that presuppose the thesis's own framework, leaving it practically unfalsifiable.
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 3/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defender showed the thesis differs from eliminativism but failed to establish substantial novelty beyond incremental synthesis of existing positions.
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defender's refined criterion (explanandum disagreement plus folk origins) still applies to developing empirical sciences like astrobiology, failing to make consciousness distinctively conceptual.
- **Flaw:** The diagnostic criterion for distinguishing conceptual proposals from empirical theories either proves too much (reclassifying immature empirical sciences as conceptual engineering) or relies on unfalsifiable standards that presuppose the thesis's own interpretive framework.

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "empirical theory" in a way that makes the argument work but undermines its force. 

The weak premise is the claim that "persistent inability to find decisive empirical tests...reveals these are fundamentally proposals about how to precisify a folk concept, not discoveries about a pre-existing natural kind."

This argument trades on two readings of "empirical theory":

Reading 1 (narrow): An empirical theory is one where we currently possess decisive experimental tests that can adjudicate between competing views. On this reading, consciousness theories aren't empirical theories, making the thesis plausible—yes, if they're not empirical in this sense, perhaps they're conceptual proposals instead.

Reading 2 (broad): An empirical theory is one that makes claims about features of the actual world, regardless of current testability. On this reading, the thesis becomes interesting—it would be surprising and important to say that theories making claims about neural correlates, information integration, and reportability aren't really about the world but about concepts.

The argument needs both: it needs the narrow reading to establish that these aren't empirical theories (the premise), but needs the broad reading to make the conclusion significant (that theories seemingly about consciousness in the world are actually conceptual). On the broad reading alone, the premise fails; on the narrow reading alone, the conclusion is trivial.

STRENGTH: 2/5

This has some force but isn't devastating. The author could respond that they're consistently using "empirical theory" in the narrow sense throughout, and their point is precisely that what *seemed* to be theories about the world (broad sense) are better understood as conceptual proposals. The equivocation exists but the thesis can likely survive by clarifying that the shift from "empirical theory" to "conceptual proposal" is the entire point, not a confusion.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies what's doing the work in the argument. There is no equivocation because the thesis doesn't rest on shifting between two senses of "empirical theory"—it rests on a *diagnostic criterion* for distinguishing empirical theories from conceptual proposals.

The key move is methodological: when a community of competent researchers produces multiple frameworks that (1) each accommodate the available data, (2) generate predictions that consistently fail to decisively adjudicate between them, and (3) persist in this state despite sustained effort and conceptual refinement, this is evidence that they are not converging on a natural kind but negotiating how to carve up a phenomenon.

Consider the parallel with "species" in biology. Pre-Darwin, naturalists debated what species *really are*. Post-Darwin, we recognize multiple legitimate species concepts (biological, morphological, phylogenetic) serving different purposes. The persistent inability to find *the* correct species concept—despite mountains of empirical data—revealed that "species" doesn't pick out a unique natural kind but rather admits of multiple precisifications.

The consciousness case exhibits the same structure. IIT, GWT, and HOT all make claims about neural correlates and information processing—they're not *a priori* armchair philosophy. But their persistent empirical non-decidability, despite decades of neuroscientific progress, suggests they're like competing species concepts: empirically informed but fundamentally conceptual proposals.

So there's no equivocation. The thesis uses "empirical theory" consistently in the narrow sense (currently testable), and argues that theories failing this criterion *despite empirical engagement* should be reconceived as conceptual engineering projects. The significance doesn't come from shifting definitions but from recognizing a category mistake: treating conceptual proposals as if they were natural kind discoveries.

# VERDICT

The defence fails, but not quite for the reasons the objection gives.

The species analogy actually exposes the problem rather than solving it. Species concepts *are* empirically decidable—we can test whether the biological species concept or phylogenetic species concept better serves evolutionary biology, conservation, or other purposes by seeing which generates more predictive success, explanatory power, and practical utility *in empirical contexts*. The pluralism about species doesn't mean species concepts escape empirical constraint; it means different empirical purposes warrant different concepts.

But this undercuts the thesis's central move. If consciousness theories are like species concepts—empirically informed conceptual proposals—then they should *still* be empirically testable relative to purposes. We should be able to ask: does IIT or GWT better predict recovery from disorders of consciousness? Which framework generates more fruitful research programs in neuroscience? These are empirical questions.

The thesis wants to move consciousness theories from empirical testing to "conceptual engineering criteria like fruitfulness and reflective equilibrium." But fruitfulness *is* empirically assessable, and reflective equilibrium without empirical constraint is just coherentism about intuitions.

The real problem: the defence shows these theories remain empirically engaged even as conceptual proposals, which means the thesis hasn't actually escaped the testability problem—it's just relabeled it as purpose-relative empirical assessment. The reframing doesn't avoid the original difficulty; it reproduces it.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis is unfalsifiable because it has immunized itself against the most natural counterexample: successful empirical adjudication between consciousness theories.

The weak premise is: "persistent inability to find decisive empirical tests, despite decades of effort...reveals these are fundamentally proposals about how to precisify a folk concept, not discoveries about a pre-existing natural kind."

Suppose tomorrow neuroscientists develop a decisive test that falsifies IIT and confirms GWT—say, a case where GWT predicts consciousness and IIT predicts its absence, and the prediction is cleanly verified. This should refute the thesis: it would show these *were* empirical theories all along, just hard ones.

But the thesis can deflect this: "Ah, but that just shows GWT happened to be the better conceptual precisification for predictive purposes—it doesn't show there was a pre-existing fact about what consciousness *really is*. We're still doing conceptual engineering; GWT just turned out more fruitful." 

The problem deepens with the species analogy in the defence. Any apparent empirical success gets reinterpreted as "purpose-relative fruitfulness" rather than discovery. The thesis has built in a redescription mechanism: empirical success becomes "this precisification serves predictive purposes well," and empirical failure remains "these are just competing precisifications."

The theory cannot specify what evidence would show consciousness theories are *not* conceptual proposals but genuine empirical discoveries about natural kinds. Every possible empirical outcome—decisive tests, continued underdetermination, partial progress—gets absorbed into the "conceptual engineering" framework.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This is a serious problem requiring a real answer. The thesis needs to specify what would constitute evidence against the "conceptual proposal" interpretation, but its core move—reinterpreting empirical engagement as purpose-relative assessment—seems to prevent this.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection misunderstands what makes a philosophical thesis falsifiable. The thesis *is* falsifiable—by the very evidence that motivated it failing to obtain.

Here's what would refute it: **the testability problem dissolving under normal scientific progress**. Specifically, if consciousness theories converged through standard empirical methodology the way theories about, say, DNA replication or synaptic transmission did, that would falsify the thesis. The claim is precisely that the *persistence and character* of underdetermination reveals something about what kind of intellectual work these theories are doing.

Consider the contrast case. In the 1950s, multiple theories of DNA replication competed (conservative, semi-conservative, dispersive). The Meselson-Stahl experiment decisively resolved this. That's what normal empirical convergence looks like. If consciousness theories exhibited this pattern—competing frameworks narrowing down through experimental refinement until one clearly wins—the thesis would be false.

The objection's imagined scenario ("tomorrow a decisive test") actually *would* refute the thesis, but not in the way the objection claims. The thesis doesn't say "reinterpret any empirical success as conceptual engineering." It says: look at the *actual historical pattern* of persistent underdetermination despite empirical engagement, and infer from this pattern that we're not tracking a natural kind.

If that pattern broke—if we got Meselson-Stahl-style resolution—the inference fails. The thesis can't "deflect" this by saying "oh, that's just fruitfulness." That would be changing the subject. The thesis rests on a specific empirical claim about the sociology and history of consciousness science: that it exhibits persistent underdetermination of a particular kind. Falsify that claim, falsify the thesis.

Moreover, the thesis makes a further prediction: that even apparent "decisive tests" will, on closer examination, leave room for theoretical maneuvering in ways that tests in mature sciences don't. This is testable. Take any proposed crucial experiment and examine whether the losing theory can be preserved through auxiliary adjustments that would be considered *ad hoc* in normal science but remain *theoretically principled* in consciousness science. The thesis predicts yes; if the answer is systematically no, the thesis fails.

The species analogy helps here. We know species concepts are conceptual proposals *because* attempts at empirical unification failed in a characteristic way—not through lack of data, but through principled disagreement about what explanatory work "species" should do. If biology had converged on one species concept the way chemistry converged on atomic theory, that would have refuted the pluralist view.

So the thesis is falsifiable by: (1) normal scientific convergence occurring, (2) the historical pattern of underdetermination being mischaracterized, or (3) proposed decisive tests functioning like they do in mature natural sciences rather than remaining negotiable.

# VERDICT

The defence fails.

The problem is that the falsification conditions offered are either too weak or reproduce the original difficulty.

**On normal convergence**: The defence says if consciousness theories converged "the way theories about DNA replication did," that would falsify the thesis. But this sets up a waiting game that can extend indefinitely. How long must underdetermination persist before we conclude it's "characteristic" rather than just "hard science moving slowly"? Fifty years? A hundred? The thesis needs to specify a timeframe or degree of effort that would be diagnostic, but any such specification will be arbitrary. And if it doesn't specify, then the thesis remains unfalsifiable *in practice*—we can always say "give it more time."

**On the Meselson-Stahl comparison**: This actually backfires. The objector's imagined scenario—a clean decisive test tomorrow—is *exactly* analogous to Meselson-Stahl. The defence claims this would refute the thesis, but then immediately hedges: such tests will "on closer examination, leave room for theoretical maneuvering." This is the immunization the objection identified. If a seemingly decisive test can always be reinterpreted as leaving "principled" room for adjustment, we're back to unfalsifiability.

**The deepest problem**: The defence's prediction that losing theories can be preserved through "theoretically principled" rather than "*ad hoc*" adjustments gives away the game. Who decides what counts as "principled" vs. "*ad hoc*"? If consciousness theories are conceptual proposals, then adjustments that preserve theoretical coherence will *always* look principled from within that framework. The thesis has built in its own standard of evidence—one that makes apparent empirical refutation reinterpretable as conceptual negotiation.

The defence needed to specify *independent* criteria for when underdetermination is diagnostic of conceptual rather than empirical work. Instead, it offers criteria that themselves depend on already accepting the conceptual-proposal interpretation. That's circular, and it leaves the thesis unfalsifiable.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis is already taken—this is **conceptual pragmatism** or **Carnapian deflationism** about scientific ontology, applied to consciousness. The weak point is the claim that this represents a novel "reframing" rather than a well-established position.

Carnap argued in "Empiricism, Semantics, and Ontology" that apparent ontological disputes are often really proposals for adopting different linguistic frameworks, assessable by pragmatic criteria rather than truth. Quine's "ontological relativity" and more recently Haslanger's work on ameliorative analysis and Chalmers' own work on conceptual engineering all occupy this space. The thesis that consciousness theories are competing precisifications of a folk concept, answerable to purpose-relative criteria rather than correspondence to natural kinds, is precisely the Carnapian move applied to this domain.

The species analogy the defender uses isn't original either—it's standard fare in philosophy of biology (Ereshefsky, Dupré) for illustrating pragmatic pluralism about scientific categories. Even the specific application to consciousness has precedents: Dennett's heterophenomenology explicitly treats consciousness attributions as answerable to pragmatic, instrumentalist criteria rather than tracking a determinate natural kind. Frankish's illusionism involves similar deflationary moves.

The thesis presents itself as resolving a crisis through reframing, but it's actually just applying an existing philosophical position (conceptual pragmatism about scientific disputes) to a particular case. That's not worthless, but it's not the novel intervention the "reframing" designation suggests.

# STRENGTH: 3/5

This is a genuine problem requiring an answer. The thesis needs to establish what's novel about its application beyond "Carnap/Dennett/Haslanger, but for the current consciousness debates." However, it's answerable: the author could argue that even if the general philosophical move is familiar, its application here is distinctive because it explains the *specific* pattern of persistent underdetermination in consciousness science in a way previous applications didn't, or that synthesizing conceptual engineering with the empirical details of IIT/GWT/HOT debates constitutes original work. The thesis survives but must justify its novelty claim.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection conflates *using established philosophical resources* with *lacking novelty*. Yes, the thesis deploys conceptual pragmatism—but its contribution lies in diagnosing *why this particular scientific domain* requires this treatment when superficially similar domains don't.

**The diagnostic novelty**: Carnap and Quine offered *general* frameworks for understanding ontological disputes. The thesis makes a *specific empirical claim* about consciousness science: that it exhibits a distinctive pattern—theories that are richly empirically engaged (neuroimaging, lesion studies, clinical applications) yet persistently underdetermined in ways that resist resolution through more data. This isn't true of all "hard" sciences. Quantum gravity is hard, but physicists don't have multiple complete frameworks that each accommodate all current data while making systematically untestable divergent predictions. Consciousness science does.

**The pattern is distinctive**: The thesis identifies something specific about how IIT, GWT, and HOT relate to evidence. They don't fail empirical engagement (unlike pure armchair philosophy), but they don't converge through it either (unlike mature natural sciences). Each can accommodate surprising findings through principled theoretical adjustments. Each generates research programs. Yet they remain fundamentally at odds about what consciousness *is*. This pattern—sustained empirical engagement without convergence—is the diagnostic sign that conceptual work is primary.

**Dennett and Frankish aren't doing this**: The objection misreads these predecessors. Dennett's heterophenomenology is *eliminativist*—it denies that consciousness as traditionally conceived exists. Frankish's illusionism is similar. The thesis is *not* eliminativist. It says consciousness exists, but "consciousness" admits of multiple legitimate precisifications, like "species" does. That's pluralism, not elimination. The thesis explains why IIT, GWT, and HOT can *all* be onto something—they're carving the phenomenon differently for different purposes. Dennett and Frankish say the losing theories are just *wrong*.

**The novelty is in the synthesis**: Previous work either applied conceptual pragmatism generally (Carnap, Quine) or took eliminativist stances on consciousness specifically (Dennett, Frankish). The thesis uniquely combines: (1) close attention to the *empirical* structure of consciousness science, (2) a *pluralist* rather than eliminativist conclusion, and (3) the shift from "which theory is true?" to "which precisification for which purpose?" This synthesis hasn't been articulated before, even if its components have precedents.

**On Haslanger and conceptual engineering**: Haslanger's ameliorative analysis focuses on *normatively loaded* concepts like race and gender, where the engineering is explicitly political. The thesis extends this to a seemingly descriptive scientific domain, arguing that even here, purposes and values shape which precisification we should adopt. That extension is non-trivial—it suggests conceptual engineering applies more broadly than Haslanger's paradigm cases suggest.

The thesis isn't just "apply known framework to new case." It's "diagnose why this case requires this framework through attention to its specific empirical-yet-underdetermined structure, and show how pluralism differs from elimination."

# VERDICT

The defence succeeds partially but reveals the thesis is weaker than claimed.

**What works**: The defence correctly identifies that the thesis isn't straightforwardly eliminativist like Dennett/Frankish, and that diagnosing the *specific pattern* in consciousness science adds something beyond generic Carnapian deflationism. The observation that consciousness theories are empirically engaged yet persistently underdetermined in a distinctive way *is* a substantive empirical claim about this scientific domain.

**What fails**: The defence overstates the novelty in two ways.

First, **the pluralist position is already occupied**. Tye, Rosenthal, and others in consciousness studies have explicitly argued for pluralism about consciousness—that there are multiple legitimate ways to precisify the concept (access consciousness vs. phenomenal consciousness, creature consciousness vs. state consciousness, etc.). The thesis's move from "which theory is true?" to "which precisification for which purpose?" isn't new; it's been the default position for philosophers who reject both reductive unity and eliminativism. The thesis needed to show it's doing something beyond this existing pluralism, and the defence doesn't establish that.

Second, **the Haslanger extension isn't as novel as claimed**. Philosophers of science have long recognized that even "descriptive" scientific concepts involve pragmatic choices—this is standard in discussions of natural kinds (Boyd, Griffiths) and scientific realism debates. The idea that scientific categorization serves purposes and that multiple frameworks might serve different purposes legitimately isn't Haslanger's invention, and applying it to consciousness isn't a surprising extension.

**The real problem**: The defence succeeds in showing the thesis isn't *identical* to previous positions, but fails to show it's *substantially novel* rather than a minor variant. It's Carnapian pragmatism + scientific pluralism + attention to consciousness's specific empirical pattern. Each component exists; the combination is new but incremental. For a "reframing" move—which promises to *transform* how we see the debate—this is disappointingly modest. The thesis survives as a competent synthesis but doesn't deliver the revolutionary reframing it advertises.

The objection succeeds in deflating the thesis's ambitions, even if it doesn't kill it entirely.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis proves too much because the diagnostic criterion it uses—persistent empirical underdetermination despite sustained effort—would force us to reclassify many genuine empirical theories as mere "conceptual proposals."

The weak premise is: "persistent inability to find decisive empirical tests, despite decades of effort...reveals these are fundamentally proposals about how to precisify a folk concept, not discoveries about a pre-existing natural kind."

Consider string theory in physics. Multiple versions (Type I, Type IIA, Type IIB, heterotic, M-theory) have coexisted for decades. Each accommodates available data, generates predictions that consistently fail to decisively adjudicate between them, and persists despite sustained effort and conceptual refinement. By the thesis's own diagnostic criterion, we should conclude string theory frameworks are "competing proposals for what we should mean by 'fundamental physics'" rather than empirical theories—that physicists are doing conceptual engineering, not discovering natural kinds.

Or consider dark matter versus modified gravity (MOND). Decades of sophisticated empirical work, yet the theories remain empirically underdetermined in precisely the way the thesis describes for consciousness. Should we conclude "dark matter" is just a conceptual proposal we're engineering rather than a potential empirical discovery?

The thesis's criterion—sustained underdetermination reveals conceptual rather than empirical work—would transform vast swaths of frontier science into conceptual engineering projects. But this is absurd. String theorists and dark matter researchers are clearly trying to discover what the universe is actually like, not negotiating how to precisify folk concepts. If the diagnostic criterion licenses reclassifying these as conceptual proposals, it's the criterion that's broken, not our understanding of these sciences.

# STRENGTH: 4/5

This is serious damage requiring a real answer. The thesis needs principled grounds for why consciousness science crosses the threshold into "conceptual proposal" territory while string theory, dark matter, quantum interpretation disputes, and other persistently underdetermined research programs don't. Without such grounds, the argument licenses an unacceptable conclusion: that most of frontier physics, cosmology, and theoretical biology are doing conceptual engineering rather than empirical science.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection misses what's distinctive about the consciousness case: it's not *just* underdetermination, but underdetermination *about the explanandum itself*.

String theory and dark matter debates presuppose agreement on what they're trying to explain. Physicists agree on what counts as "fundamental physical interactions" or "gravitational effects requiring explanation"—they disagree about the underlying mechanisms. The explanandum is stable; the explanans is contested. That's normal empirical underdetermination.

Consciousness theories differ fundamentally. IIT, GWT, and HOT don't just disagree about the mechanisms underlying a shared explanandum—they disagree about *what counts as consciousness in the first place*. 

- IIT says a system is conscious if it has high integrated information (Φ), even if it can't report or access that information. A hypothetical inactive grid of logic gates with the right causal structure would be conscious.
- GWT says consciousness requires global broadcasting and accessibility. The inactive grid isn't conscious because nothing is being broadcast.
- HOT says consciousness requires higher-order representation of mental states. The grid lacks this, so it's not conscious.

These aren't competing explanations of the same phenomenon—they're competing *specifications* of which systems count as conscious. When IIT and GWT make different predictions, they're not disagreeing about what explains consciousness; they're disagreing about what consciousness *is*.

This is why the species analogy works for consciousness but not for string theory. Biologists eventually recognized that "species" doesn't pick out a unique natural kind because different biological processes (reproductive isolation, morphological similarity, phylogenetic descent) come apart—there's no fact about which is *the* right explanandum. But physicists haven't concluded that "fundamental forces" or "gravitational effects" are similarly plural. They assume a unified explanandum exists and seek the right explanation.

The diagnostic criterion should be refined: **sustained underdetermination plus disagreement about the explanandum's boundaries** reveals conceptual work. Consciousness exhibits both; string theory exhibits only the first.

Moreover, consciousness uniquely involves folk concepts in a way frontier physics doesn't. "Consciousness" originates in first-person experience and everyday psychology—we're trying to precisify something we already talk about. "Strings" and "dark matter" are theoretical posits with no folk antecedents. When scientists propose them, they're stipulating new entities, not refining pre-existing concepts. This difference matters: conceptual engineering is the right framework when we're precisifying folk concepts for scientific purposes, not when we're positing wholly novel theoretical entities.

The thesis doesn't prove too much because it includes an implicit restriction: it applies to domains where (1) theories disagree about the explanandum's boundaries, and (2) the target concept has folk origins requiring precisification. Consciousness meets both conditions; string theory and dark matter meet neither.

# VERDICT

The defence fails, though it identifies something real.

**What's right**: The defence correctly observes that consciousness theories disagree about extension—which systems count as conscious—in ways string theory variants don't disagree about which phenomena count as "fundamental physics." This is a genuine difference.

**Where it fails**: The defence doesn't save the thesis because the distinction it draws doesn't support the conclusion.

**First problem: Explanandum disputes are common in science**. Many empirical sciences feature disagreements about what counts as an instance of the phenomenon:
- Is a virus alive? Biologists disagree, and this affects which theories of life they accept.
- Is Pluto a planet? Astronomers disagreed about the explanandum's boundaries.
- Are electromagnetic waves a form of light? This was contested before Maxwell.

These disputes got resolved *empirically*—through discovering what natural kinds actually exist and which categorizations prove scientifically fruitful. They weren't "conceptual engineering projects" exempt from empirical testing. The fact that consciousness theories disagree about extension doesn't show they're not empirical theories; it shows they're empirical theories at an early stage where the natural kind's boundaries are still being discovered.

**Second problem: The folk concept criterion backfires**. Many scientific concepts originate in folk concepts requiring precisification—"fish," "heat," "force," "memory," "learning," "disease." Science didn't respond by treating these as conceptual engineering projects answerable to reflective equilibrium. It discovered what natural kinds (if any) the folk concepts approximately tracked, often revising the concepts radically (whales aren't fish) or eliminating them (phlogiston). The folk origin doesn't make a concept unsuitable for natural kind discovery; it makes empirical investigation *necessary* to determine whether the folk concept tracks anything real.

**Third problem: The restriction doesn't save the thesis from proving too much**. Consider "life" in astrobiology. Scientists disagree about the explanandum's boundaries (do prions count? self-replicating RNA?), and "life" has clear folk origins. By the refined criterion, theories of life should be conceptual proposals, not empirical discoveries. But that's wrong—astrobiologists are trying to discover what life actually is, not engineer a concept for pragmatic purposes.

**The deepest failure**: The defence tries to make consciousness special by citing explanandum disagreement plus folk origins. But these features don't distinguish conceptual engineering from immature empirical science. They distinguish *mature* science (stable explanandum, theoretical posits) from *developing* science (contested explanandum, folk concept refinement). The thesis has mistaken the characteristics of young empirical sciences for characteristics of non-empirical conceptual work.

The objection succeeds. The thesis's diagnostic criterion, even refined, would reclassify too much genuine empirical science as conceptual engineering. The thesis cannot specify what makes consciousness different without either proving too much or making ad hoc restrictions that beg the question.

</details>

---

### O4. Testing consciousness theories requires we can reidentify 'the same' phenomenal state across different measurement contexts, but for relational theories like HOT and AIR, phenomenal states are individuated by their relations to other mental states—which means these theories make the evidential standard for confirming them impossible to meet.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Proponents of Higher-Order Thought theory and Attention-Based theories who present these as empirically testable frameworks
- **Sketch:** The argument targets higher-order and self-representational theories specifically. To test whether a higher-order thought makes a state conscious, we need to check whether subject S has 'this pain' without the HOT versus 'this same pain' with the HOT. But HOT theory says phenomenal character is constituted by the higher-order representation—there is no 'same pain' independent of the relational context. Similarly, AIR theory (attention is what makes states conscious) individuates experiences by attentional structure. The key premise is that these theories face a methodological catch-22: they need to track phenomenal sameness across conditions that they themselves claim cannot preserve phenomenal identity. This isn't just an epistemic problem about access—it's that the theories' own individuation conditions undermine the coherence of the experimental question. Either they must grant that phenomenal states have an intrinsic character independent of higher-order/attentional relations (contradicting the theory), or they cannot be empirically tested using standard contrastive methods.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Targets specific theories with a genuine methodological catch-22 about phenomenal reidentification—this is sharp and original.
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 2/5, answered. Defence showed the thesis identifies a genuine structural asymmetry, not mere equivocation on standards
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence admitted Block made the core argument; claimed only reframing and extension, which is insufficient
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence's distinction collapsed—HOT theorists can use functional markers exactly as water/H₂O uses macro properties
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence's attempt to exclude IIT by definitional restriction failed; IIT is relationally individuated yet testable
- **Flaw:** The thesis is unoriginal (Block 2011 made the core argument) and overgeneralizes (IIT counterexample shows relationally-individuated consciousness theories can be tested; the proves-too-much objection shows the reidentification demand would doom all constitutive theories).

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis protects itself through equivocation on "reidentification." When confronted with actual experimental protocols that HOT/AIR theorists accept as valid tests—like comparing reports before and after attentional manipulation, or neural correlates with and without higher-order activation—the thesis can retreat to demanding "strict numerical identity" of phenomenal states, which no theory of consciousness (not even first-order theories) guarantees across different causal contexts. When HOT theorists say "we can test whether adding a HOT changes experience," they mean something like "same stimulus, same early processing, different higher-order state"—a perfectly coherent contrast class. But the thesis redefines "reidentification" to require theory-neutral phenomenal identity that survives all relational changes, then complains this standard cannot be met. Any working experimental paradigm gets dismissed as "not really tracking the same state," while no specification of what would count as adequate reidentification is provided that wouldn't also doom first-order theories. The weak point is the undefended assumption that empirical testing requires reidentifying phenomenal states in a sense stronger than "controlled comparison with isolated variables"—the standard that actual science uses.

STRENGTH: 2/5 — The attack has some force because the thesis does seem to demand an unusually strong reidentification standard, but HOT/AIR theorists might genuinely struggle to specify their contrast classes without circularity.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection misunderstands the structural problem. We are not demanding "strict numerical identity" as some gratuitous philosophical standard—we are pointing out that HOT/AIR theories cannot even specify their contrast classes without presupposing what they deny.

Consider the objector's own formulation: "same stimulus, same early processing, different higher-order state." This already concedes our point. What makes it the "same early processing"? If HOT theory is right that phenomenal character is constituted by higher-order representation, then first-order processing without a HOT has no phenomenal character at all—it is not an "experience" in any sense that can be compared to the HOT-accompanied case. The theorist cannot say "we're comparing the same pain without versus with higher-order awareness" because on their view, there is no pain without the HOT—just non-conscious neural activity.

The objector says first-order theories face the same problem, but this is false. A first-order theorist can say: "This V4 activation pattern constitutes red-experience; we track whether it occurs with or without prefrontal activity, but the phenomenal state is the V4 pattern either way." The relational factor (prefrontal activity) is external to what individuates the experience. HOT/AIR theories cannot do this—the relational factor is internal to phenomenal individuation.

When HOT theorists present "actual experimental protocols" comparing reports before and after attentional manipulation, they are either: (a) tacitly treating phenomenal states as having first-order identity conditions (contradicting their theory), or (b) only tracking reports and neural correlates while remaining silent on whether phenomenal character changed—which means they are not testing the constitutive claim at all, only correlations.

The objector demands we specify "what would count as adequate reidentification." Here it is: the ability to say "state S occurred in context C1 and context C2" where S's identity conditions are specifiable without reference to whether C1 or C2 obtains. First-order theories can do this. HOT/AIR theories cannot, because their identity conditions are "S-in-C1" versus "S-in-C2" are different states by definition.


VERDICT

The defence holds against the charge of equivocation, but only partially rescues the thesis.

The defence succeeds in showing there is a genuine structural asymmetry: relational theories that make the relation constitutive of phenomenal character face a contrast-class specification problem that non-constitutive theories do not face. The objector's proposed experimental paradigm does presuppose first-order identity conditions when it speaks of "same early processing"—if that processing has no phenomenal character without the HOT, it cannot serve as one term in a phenomenal comparison.

However, the defence does not establish that this makes the theories untestable, only that it makes them untestable by a particular method (contrastive reidentification). The thesis overstates its case by claiming these theories "cannot be empirically tested using standard contrastive methods"—but perhaps non-standard methods suffice. HOT theorists might test their theory by predicting when reports of consciousness occur, when they are absent, and what neural patterns accompany each, without ever claiming to track "the same phenomenal state" across conditions. This would be testing the theory's predictive adequacy rather than directly confirming the constitutive claim, but that may be all empirical testing can do for any consciousness theory.

The thesis survives as a methodological constraint but not as a demonstration of unfalsifiability. The verdict: these theories face a distinctive reidentification problem that limits certain experimental approaches, but this is a feature of testing constitutive claims about consciousness generally, not a special defect making HOT/AIR uniquely untestable.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT. This structural problem about relational individuation undermining experimental contrast classes was identified by Ned Block in his 2011 critique of higher-order theories, specifically in "The higher order approach to consciousness is defunct" (Analysis). Block argued that higher-order theories face what he called the "methodological problem": to test whether a HOT makes a state conscious, you need to compare the state with and without the HOT, but if the HOT constitutes phenomenal character, there's no phenomenal state to track across the comparison. He used this to argue HOT theories cannot account for the phenomenology of overflow cases (where subjects seem to experience more than they can report), because the theory cannot distinguish "experienced but unreportable" from "unexperienced"—the very distinction requires reidentifying phenomenal states independently of the higher-order relation. Block's formulation was specifically about the evidential inaccessibility this creates: the theory makes its own confirmation conditions incoherent. The current thesis extends this to AIR theory and frames it more generally as an instability about testing standards, but the core insight—that constitutive relational theories cannot specify the contrast classes their own empirical testing requires—is Block's methodological objection from 2011.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This significantly damages the thesis's claim to originality. Block's methodological objection is well-known in the consciousness literature and makes essentially this point about reidentification across relational contexts. The thesis adds some refinement (extending to AIR, framing as "instability" about evidential standards rather than just a testing problem), but the central logical structure is already established.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies what is novel and conflates the dialectical target. Block's 2011 argument was a *first-order objection*—an attempt to show HOT theory gets the phenomenology wrong in overflow cases. Our thesis is a *second-order methodological claim*—that these theories create an instability between their content and their epistemic conditions.

Three differences matter:

**First, scope and generalization.** Block targeted HOT theory specifically in the context of overflow experiments. We identify a structural feature common to all constitutively relational theories (HOT, AIR, and by extension any view where phenomenal character is individuated by relations to other mental states). Block's point was about one theory's trouble with one experimental paradigm. Ours is about a class of theories facing a general methodological constraint. Extending an insight is not repeating it—if it were, every application of the problem of induction after Hume would be "already taken."

**Second, the instability diagnosis.** Block said HOT theory cannot handle certain evidence (overflow). We say something stronger: relational theories cannot coherently specify what would count as confirming evidence *for their central claim* without either (a) contradicting that claim by presupposing non-relational identity conditions, or (b) retreating to mere correlational predictions that leave the constitutive thesis untested. This is not "the theory gets this case wrong" but "the theory makes its own confirmation conditions incoherent"—a claim about rational instability, not empirical adequacy.

**Third, the dialectical context.** Block was arguing HOT theory is false. We are arguing that proponents who present these theories as empirically testable frameworks face a methodological catch-22 they have not acknowledged. Our target is not "HOT theory is wrong" but "HOT/AIR theorists cannot consistently maintain both their constitutive claims and their empirical credentials without addressing this reidentification problem." That is a different thesis with a different burden.

The objector points to Block's 2011 paper, but notice what Block himself concluded: that HOT theory is "defunct" because it gets the phenomenology wrong. He did not conclude that it faces a general problem about testability that extends to other relational theories. If he had made our argument, the literature would look different—there would be a recognized "Block's reidentification problem" that AIR theorists explicitly address. There is not.

Moreover, even if Block identified the reidentification issue, identifying a problem is not the same as diagnosing it as an *instability* between theoretical commitments and evidential standards. Plenty of theories face testing difficulties. We claim this difficulty is not merely practical but structural—arising from the theory's own individuation conditions—and that proponents have not reckoned with this when presenting their views as empirically superior to competitors.

# VERDICT

The defence fails. The thesis does not survive as original.

While the defence correctly identifies that our framing differs from Block's—he argued HOT theory gets phenomenology wrong in overflow cases; we argue relational theories face a general methodological instability—this distinction does not save the thesis from the "already taken" charge.

The core logical structure is indeed Block's: constitutively relational theories cannot specify contrast classes for empirical testing because they cannot reidentify phenomenal states independently of the very relations they claim constitute those states. Block explicitly made this point about reidentification and used it to argue the theory creates "methodological problems" for its own testing. That he deployed this insight as one move in a larger argument against HOT theory, rather than as a freestanding methodological critique, does not mean the insight is available for re-use.

The defence's appeal to "scope and generalization" is weak. Extending Block's point from HOT to AIR is straightforward—anyone who understood Block's argument could immediately see it applies to any constitutively relational theory. That is application, not innovation. The defence's claim that "if it were, every application of the problem of induction after Hume would be 'already taken'" actually undermines its case: we do not credit philosophers with original theses for applying Hume's problem to new domains unless they add genuine theoretical content.

The "instability diagnosis" distinction is more defensible but ultimately insufficient. Block did frame this as a problem about what could count as confirming evidence—that is what "methodological problem" means in his paper. Calling it an "instability" rather than a "methodological problem" is re-labeling, not reconceptualizing.

Most damaging: the defence admits that "if Block had made our argument, the literature would look different" and notes there is no recognized "Block's reidentification problem." But there is—it is discussed in the HOT literature, and AIR theorists like Prinz have responded to similar worries about whether attention-based theories can distinguish experienced-but-unattended states from unexperienced states without presupposing non-attentional identity conditions. The absence of a catchy name does not mean the problem is unrecognized.

The thesis is not original enough to stand as a contribution. It is a competent restatement and modest extension of Block's 2011 methodological objection, but does not add sufficient theoretical content to constitute a distinct thesis.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — # ATTEMPT

The argument proves too much because it would render *any* constitutive theory untestable by the same logic. Consider: "Testing whether neural activity constitutes consciousness requires we reidentify 'the same' phenomenal state across contexts with and without the neural correlate—but constitutive neural theories say phenomenal states are individuated by their neural properties, which means these theories make their own evidential standard impossible to meet." 

The weak point is the implicit premise that **constitutive claims require tracking identity across contexts where the constituting factor varies**. But this is precisely what constitutive theories deny is possible—and that's not a bug, it's the point. A constitutive theory says X doesn't exist without Y. Testing it doesn't require finding "the same X" without Y (incoherent on the theory's own terms), but rather predicting when X-and-Y complexes occur versus when neither does.

If the thesis worked, it would show that claiming "water is H₂O" is untestable because we cannot reidentify "the same water" in contexts lacking H₂O molecules—the very thing the identity claim denies exists. Every constitutive identity in science would face this "methodological catch-22." Yet we test such claims successfully by predicting co-occurrence patterns, not by tracking entities across contexts where their constitutive conditions fail to obtain.

**STRENGTH: 4/5** — This seriously damages the thesis. The reidentification demand, if genuinely required for testing, would make all constitutive theories untestable, including uncontroversially scientific ones. The thesis needs principled grounds for why consciousness theories specifically cannot use the standard method for testing constitutive claims (predicting co-occurrence rather than tracking identity across absence/presence of constituting factors), but provides none.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection trades on a false analogy between consciousness and other constitutive claims. The water/H₂O case is disanalogous in precisely the way that saves our thesis from overgeneralization.

**The crucial asymmetry:** When we test "water is H₂O," we have *independent access* to both relata. We can identify water samples by their macro properties (clear, potable liquid, freezes at 0°C, etc.) and then investigate their molecular composition. We can identify H₂O by its molecular structure and then check whether it exhibits water's macro properties. The constitutive claim links two independently specifiable things. We never need to "reidentify the same water across contexts lacking H₂O" because we identify water by its functional/phenomenal profile, not by tracking numerical identity of samples.

**Consciousness is different.** For phenomenal states, we have no such independent access. We cannot identify "pain" by anything other than what it's like—its phenomenal character. If HOT theory says phenomenal character is constituted by higher-order representation, we cannot independently specify "pain" and "higher-order representation of pain" and then test whether they co-occur. There is only one thing to access—the phenomenal state—and the theory says that very thing is individuated by the relation in question.

The objection's neural constitution example actually helps us. When testing whether C-fiber firing constitutes pain, we *can* identify C-fiber firing independently (via neuroimaging) and pain independently (via reports/behavior), then check co-occurrence. That is exactly what HOT/AIR theories cannot do, because they make the relational factor internal to phenomenal individuation itself.

**The principled distinction:** Constitutive theories are testable when the constituted phenomenon has identity conditions specifiable independently of the constituting factor. Water has macro-functional identity conditions independent of molecular structure. Pain (on first-order neural theories) has neural identity conditions independent of prefrontal activity. But pain on HOT theory has no identity conditions independent of higher-order representation—that is what makes it a *phenomenal* constitution claim rather than merely a neural one.

The objection demands we explain "why consciousness theories specifically cannot use the standard method for testing constitutive claims (predicting co-occurrence)." Here is why: because phenomenal states, unlike water or neural states, are individuated solely by phenomenal character, and relational theories make that very character constitutively dependent on the relation. We cannot predict "when X-and-Y complexes occur versus when neither does" if we have no way to identify X except as already-in-relation-to-Y.

**The methodological point:** Testing water/H₂O works by predicting: "Wherever you find these macro properties, you'll find this molecular structure." Testing neural constitution works by predicting: "Wherever you find these neural patterns, you'll find these behavioral/report patterns." What would testing HOT theory by co-occurrence look like? "Wherever you find phenomenal character, you'll find higher-order representation"? But we identify phenomenal character by first-person reports, which HOT theory says require higher-order representation. The co-occurrence is guaranteed by the theory's own access conditions, not discovered empirically.

This is why the thesis does not prove too much. It identifies a specific problem for theories that make phenomenal character itself relationally constituted, not a general problem for all constitutive claims.

# VERDICT

The defence fails. The thesis does not survive the "proves too much" objection.

The defence attempts to draw a principled distinction based on "independent access to both relata," but this distinction collapses under scrutiny. The claim that "we identify water by its macro properties independently of knowing its molecular composition" is true, but the parallel claim for consciousness is equally available to HOT/AIR theorists: we identify conscious states by their functional role (reportability, availability for reasoning, behavioral guidance) independently of knowing their relational structure. HOT theorists can say: "We identify conscious states by reports and behavioral markers, then investigate whether they correlate with higher-order representation"—exactly parallel to identifying water by macro properties and investigating molecular structure.

The defence insists "pain on HOT theory has no identity conditions independent of higher-order representation," but this is question-begging. HOT theorists explicitly provide functional identity conditions: conscious states are those available for report, verbal reasoning, and rational control. These are specifiable without reference to higher-order representation, even if the theory claims higher-order representation explains why states have these functional properties. This is precisely parallel to how we specify water by its functional properties (potability, freezing point) even though H₂O structure explains why it has those properties.

The defence's claim that "co-occurrence is guaranteed by the theory's own access conditions, not discovered empirically" actually undermines the thesis rather than supporting it. If true, this would make HOT theory *unfalsifiable*, not *untestable due to reidentification problems*. These are different charges. Moreover, the claim is false: HOT theory predicts specific dissociations (unconscious states lack HOTs, conscious states have them) that are empirically testable via neural imaging combined with reports, exactly as neural theories are tested.

Most fatally, the defence's own example defeats it. The defence says neural constitution theories can "identify C-fiber firing independently (via neuroimaging) and pain independently (via reports/behavior), then check co-occurrence." But HOT theorists can do exactly this: identify higher-order representation independently (via prefrontal imaging) and consciousness independently (via reports/behavior), then check co-occurrence. The defence tries to block this by saying reports already presuppose higher-order representation, but this is no different from saying water's macro properties already presuppose H₂O structure—both are constitutive claims where the constituted phenomenon's observable features are explained by the constituting factor.

The "proves too much" objection stands. The thesis's reidentification demand, if genuinely required, would equally apply to testing "C-fiber firing constitutes pain" (cannot reidentify "the same pain" in contexts without C-fibers) or "H₂O constitutes water" (cannot reidentify "the same water" without H₂O). The thesis provides no principled reason why phenomenal relational theories face a unique testing problem that other constitutive theories escape.

The thesis is defeated. It overgeneralizes a testing constraint to all constitutive theories while failing to identify what makes phenomenal relational theories distinctively problematic.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — # ATTEMPT

Consider testing Integrated Information Theory (IIT), which makes phenomenal character constitutively dependent on a relational property (integrated information, φ). IIT says: "This experience of red has the phenomenal character it does because of its position in a high-φ integrated network." The phenomenal state is individuated by its relational role in the informational structure.

Yet IIT is widely regarded as empirically testable—indeed, as one of the more scientifically tractable consciousness theories. Researchers test it by:
1. Computing φ for neural structures independently (via connectivity analysis)
2. Identifying conscious states independently (via reports, behavior)  
3. Checking whether high-φ systems correlate with consciousness

IIT faces exactly the "reidentification problem" the thesis describes: you cannot track "the same phenomenal red" across contexts with different φ values, because IIT says phenomenal character is constituted by the integrated information structure. A red experience in a high-φ network versus low-φ network are *different experiences* by IIT's own individuation conditions—just as the thesis says about HOT/AIR theories.

Yet IIT avoids the "methodological catch-22" by doing exactly what the thesis claims is impossible: it tests the constitutive claim through co-occurrence predictions without requiring reidentification of phenomenal states across varying relational contexts. The theory predicts when consciousness occurs (high φ) and when it doesn't (low φ), and tests these predictions empirically.

If the thesis were correct, IIT should face the same instability—unable to meet its own evidential standards. But IIT is tested successfully using standard contrastive methods. This counterexample shows that constitutively relational theories of consciousness CAN be empirically tested without the reidentification the thesis demands.

**Weak point:** The premise that relational individuation of phenomenal states makes "the evidential standard for confirming them impossible to meet."

# STRENGTH: 4/5

This counterexample seriously damages the thesis. IIT is a clear case of a constitutively relational theory of phenomenal character that is widely regarded as empirically testable and is actually being tested using exactly the contrastive methods the thesis claims are unavailable. The thesis must either explain why IIT escapes the problem (undermining the generality of the claimed instability) or accept that the reidentification demand is not actually required for testing consciousness theories.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection mischaracterizes IIT and conflates two different kinds of relational dependence. IIT does not face our reidentification problem because it does not make phenomenal character constitutively dependent on relations to *other mental states*—it makes it dependent on *intrinsic structural properties* of the system itself.

**The crucial distinction:** HOT and AIR theories make phenomenal character depend on relations to distinct mental states (a higher-order thought *about* the first-order state, or attention *directed at* the sensory representation). These are genuine relational properties where the relata are separable mental items. IIT makes phenomenal character depend on the internal informational structure of a single integrated system—φ is not a relation between the experience and something else, but a measure of the system's own integration.

**Why this matters for reidentification:** When testing IIT, we can hold the first-order sensory processing constant (same retinal input, same V4 activation pattern) and vary the broader network integration, asking whether phenomenal character changes with φ. The sensory state has an identity independent of the network's φ value—it's the same V4 pattern in high-φ and low-φ conditions. IIT predicts that this pattern will be conscious in one case and not in the other, but the pattern itself is reidentifiable across conditions.

HOT/AIR theories cannot do this. They cannot say "same first-order state, different higher-order relation" because on their view, there is no phenomenal state in the first-order processing at all without the higher-order relation. The first-order state without a HOT is not an "unconscious version of the same experience"—it is not an experience at all.

**The objection's own description reveals the asymmetry:** It says IIT researchers "compute φ for neural structures independently" and "identify conscious states independently." But notice: they identify the *neural structures* independently, not the phenomenal states. They then check whether high-φ structures correlate with reports of consciousness. This is exactly what we said relational theories can do—test correlations between neural/functional markers and consciousness reports. What they cannot do is test the constitutive claim about phenomenal character itself.

When the objection says "IIT faces exactly the reidentification problem," it equivocates on what needs reidentifying. Yes, "red in high-φ" and "red in low-φ" are different experiences by IIT's lights. But IIT can still specify the first-order sensory state (the V4 pattern) that occurs in both conditions, and predict that this state will be phenomenally conscious in one case and not in the other. The reidentifiable element is the sensory processing, not the phenomenal character.

HOT theory cannot do this because it denies there is any reidentifiable first-order element that is the "same" across conscious and unconscious conditions. The first-order processing without a HOT is not "unconscious pain"—it is non-phenomenal nociceptive processing. There is no pain to reidentify.

**The methodological point:** IIT's testability comes from treating φ as a property of neural systems that can be measured independently of phenomenology, then checking whether high-φ systems exhibit behavioral/report markers of consciousness. This works because φ is a structural property of physical systems. HOT/AIR theories make phenomenal character depend on mental relations that cannot be measured independently of the phenomenology they supposedly constitute—we identify higher-order thoughts and attention by their phenomenal effects, not independently.

The thesis does not claim that all relational properties make theories untestable. It claims that theories making phenomenal character constitutively dependent on relations to other mental states face a reidentification problem. IIT makes phenomenal character dependent on intrinsic structural properties, not relations to other mental states. It escapes the problem by not being the kind of theory the thesis targets.

# VERDICT

The defence fails. The thesis does not survive the counterexample.

The defence attempts to distinguish IIT from HOT/AIR theories by claiming IIT involves "intrinsic structural properties" rather than "relations to other mental states," but this distinction does not hold up. Integrated information is explicitly a *relational* property—it measures how much a system's parts are informationally interdependent. A neuron's contribution to φ depends entirely on its relations to other neurons in the network. This is relational individuation in exactly the relevant sense.

The defence's claim that "φ is not a relation between the experience and something else, but a measure of the system's own integration" actually concedes the point against the thesis. If IIT can make phenomenal character depend on relational properties *within* the system (integration relations among neural elements) and still be testable, then the thesis's general claim about relational individuation creating untestability fails. The thesis cannot restrict itself to "relations to other mental states" without becoming so narrow it loses force—attention and higher-order representation are also aspects of the system's own processing, not relations to external entities.

More damaging: the defence's attempt to save the thesis by saying "IIT can hold first-order processing constant while varying φ" actually describes exactly what HOT theorists claim to do. HOT theorists say: hold the first-order nociceptive processing constant, vary whether a HOT is present, and check whether consciousness reports occur. The defence insists this doesn't work because "there is no phenomenal state in first-order processing without the HOT," but IIT says exactly the same thing—there is no phenomenal state in the V4 pattern without sufficient φ. Yet the objection shows IIT is tested successfully using precisely this method.

The defence's claim that "we identify higher-order thoughts by their phenomenal effects, not independently" is empirically false. Researchers identify prefrontal activity (the neural correlate of HOTs) via fMRI independently of phenomenal reports, exactly as they identify φ independently. The defence conflates conceptual constitution claims with epistemic access—HOT theory claims HOTs constitute phenomenal character, but this doesn't prevent independent neural measurement of HOT presence.

Most fatally, the defence ends by saying IIT "escapes the problem by not being the kind of theory the thesis targets." But IIT was explicitly presented as a theory that "makes phenomenal character constitutively dependent on a relational property." If the thesis must now exclude IIT by definitional fiat (claiming it's not "really" relational in the relevant sense), this shows the thesis has no stable target. Any relational theory that proves testable can be redefined as "not the kind of relation we meant."

The counterexample stands. IIT demonstrates that theories making phenomenal character constitutively dependent on relational properties—even complex integration relations that individuate experiences by their position in informational structures—can be empirically tested using standard contrastive methods. The thesis's claimed instability does not manifest in actual scientific practice.

The thesis is defeated. It claims a general problem about testing relationally-individuated theories of consciousness, but the counterexample of IIT shows such theories can be tested successfully. The thesis provides no principled account of why HOT/AIR face unique obstacles that IIT escapes.

</details>

---

### O5. Any empirical test that could distinguish consciousness theories must exploit a double-dissociation between consciousness and its functional effects, but the possibility of such dissociations is exactly what divides functionalist from non-functionalist theories, creating an untestable deadlock.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Philosophers like Doerig et al. who propose 'consciousness adversarial collaboration' frameworks assuming empirical decidability
- **Sketch:** The argument identifies a dilemma: To empirically test whether theory T correctly identifies consciousness, we need cases where T's proposed substrate comes apart from consciousness itself—otherwise we're just confirming functional correlations. But functionalist theories (GWT, HOT, AST) hold that consciousness just IS certain functional patterns, so they deny such dissociations are possible even in principle. Non-functionalist theories (IIT, certain qualia realism views) allow for them but then face the problem that any behavioral/neural evidence we could gather only tracks functional effects, not consciousness directly. The key insight is that one's view on whether consciousness-function dissociations are possible determines what counts as legitimate evidence, and this meta-level disagreement replicates the object-level theoretical divide. The position that 'we can empirically test theories' thus collapses—it requires assuming either functionalism (in which case non-functionalist theories are ruled out by methodological fiat) or non-functionalism (in which case empirical evidence becomes irrelevant).
- **Appraiser (blind):** Identifies a genuine structural dilemma about how functionalist/non-functionalist commitments determine what counts as evidence.
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence showed the thesis is more specific than general theory-ladenness but could not escape substantial anticipation by Block and Chalmers on the testability problem itself
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence's own logic made non-functionalism unfalsifiable by definitional fiat; vitalism parallel proved fatal as it was empirically defeated despite claiming immaterial forces beyond physical investigation
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. PCI studies showed functionalist and non-functionalist theories making differential predictions about shared observables; defence's demand for ground truth without functional markers created an incoherent standard
  - TRIVIAL — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence could not escape that the thesis reduces to the definitional truth that non-functionalists reject functional evidence while functionalists accept it; the 'deadlock' is rhetorical inflation of this truism
- **Flaw:** The thesis presents as a novel methodological deadlock what is actually either the trivial truth that theories with different ontologies validate different evidence, or the observation that non-functionalism makes itself empirically unfalsifiable—which is non-functionalism's vice, not a symmetric deadlock in consciousness science

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT. This thesis articulates what philosophers of mind have long called the "theory-ladenness of observation" problem as it applies specifically to consciousness science. Thomas Kuhn's analysis of paradigm incommensurability in *The Structure of Scientific Revolutions* (1962) established that competing theoretical frameworks can determine what counts as evidence, making neutral empirical adjudication impossible. More directly, Ned Block's access/phenomenal consciousness distinction (1995) already identifies the exact structure described here: whether reportability and other functional measures can dissociate from consciousness itself is precisely what divides theories, and this disagreement infects the evidential status of any proposed test. Joseph Levine's work on the explanatory gap (1983) similarly shows that the epistemic gulf between functional/physical descriptions and phenomenal properties means empirical evidence systematically underdetermines consciousness theories. The "untestable deadlock" is simply theory-ladenness applied to consciousness: what the thesis calls a dilemma about double-dissociations is the standard observation that functionalists and non-functionalists operate within incommensurable frameworks where evidence itself is interpreted through incompatible theoretical lenses.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious challenge. The thesis presents itself as revealing a novel structural problem, but it appears to be a specific instance of well-established positions about theory-ladenness and incommensurability in consciousness studies. The core insight isn't new; it's an application of Block's and Kuhn's frameworks.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE.

The thesis is not merely restating theory-ladenness or Block's distinction—it identifies a *specific structural feature* that makes consciousness science uniquely vulnerable in a way that distinguishes it from standard Kuhnian cases.

First, against the Kuhn comparison: Kuhnian incommensurability concerns interpretive frameworks, but competing paradigms in normal science can still agree on *what would count as a decisive experiment* even while disagreeing on predictions. Newtonian and relativistic mechanics disagreed about planetary orbits, but both accepted that measuring Mercury's perihelion precession was a legitimate test. The thesis claims consciousness theories face something worse: disagreement about whether *any* empirical configuration could possibly constitute a test, because the theories disagree on whether the target phenomenon (consciousness) can come apart from what we measure (functional effects) even in principle.

Second, against Block: The access/phenomenal distinction identifies a conceptual gap, but Block himself thought careful experimentation might reveal which theory is correct—he proposed overflow experiments, change blindness studies, and other empirical approaches. The thesis makes a stronger claim: that the very *possibility* of such experiments working presupposes settling the functionalism question first. Block's distinction is about two types of consciousness; the thesis is about whether empirical methods can adjudicate theories at all.

Third, the thesis identifies a *reflexive* problem: the methodological assumptions required to treat something as evidence (e.g., "behavioral reports track consciousness") already embed theoretical commitments that determine which theories survive. This isn't evidence being theory-laden in interpretation—it's the *evidential relevance* itself being theory-dependent. When Doerig et al. propose adversarial collaboration, they assume both sides can agree on what constitutes a test.
