The thesis shows this assumption fails because functionalists think consciousness-function dissociations are conceptually impossible, while non-functionalists think they're possible but empirically undetectable.

The novelty is diagnostic: the thesis explains *why* decades of consciousness science haven't converged despite massive empirical work, by showing the disagreement operates at the level of what empirical work *could* accomplish, not merely how to interpret it.

VERDICT.

The defence fails, but only partially. The thesis does identify something more specific than general theory-ladenness: a structural feature where the object-level disagreement (functionalism vs. non-functionalism) perfectly mirrors the meta-level disagreement (whether empirical tests are possible). This is genuinely clarifying.

However, the defence cannot save the thesis from being substantially anticipated. Block's work doesn't just identify a distinction—his entire research program wrestles with exactly this problem: whether empirical methods that necessarily measure access can tell us about phenomenal consciousness. When he discusses the "methodological puzzle" of overflow, he's explicitly addressing whether tests are possible given the access/phenomenal divide. Similarly, Chalmers' "hard problem" (1995) isn't just about explanation—it's about whether third-person empirical methods can adjudicate first-person phenomenal facts, which is precisely the testability concern.

The thesis offers a crisp formulation and extends the problem to recent adversarial collaboration proposals, but the core structure—that functionalist/non-functionalist disagreement infects the possibility of empirical testing itself—is present in Block and Chalmers. The objection succeeds: this is a refinement and application of existing positions, not a novel instability.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. If this argument worked, it would show that *any* scientific dispute involving different ontological commitments about the relationship between a phenomenon and its measurable effects faces "untestable deadlock." Consider parallel cases: (1) Behaviorists vs. cognitivists about mental states—behaviorists think mental states just ARE behavioral dispositions, cognitivists think they can dissociate; yet cognitive science resolved this empirically through reaction time studies, priming effects, and neuroimaging that revealed internal processing stages. (2) Vitalists vs. mechanists about life—vitalists thought life required an immaterial vital force separable from physical/chemical processes, mechanists thought life just WAS certain physical organization; yet biochemistry decided this empirically by synthesizing organic compounds and explaining metabolism mechanistically. (3) Phlogiston vs. oxygen theories of combustion—different ontologies about what fire fundamentally is, yet Lavoisier's weight measurements empirically decided between them. In each case, one party thought the phenomenon could dissociate from functional/physical effects (cognitivists, mechanists, oxygen theorists) while the other didn't (behaviorists, vitalists, phlogiston theorists), yet empirical evidence wasn't trapped in deadlock—it actually resolved the dispute. If consciousness science faces "untestable deadlock" for the reasons given, then so should these historical cases, but they demonstrably didn't. The argument therefore proves too much: it would render impossible what actually happened in the history of science.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This seriously damages the thesis. The parallel cases show that ontological disagreement about phenomenon-measurement relationships doesn't generally create untestable deadlock, undermining the claim that consciousness is uniquely trapped.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE.

The historical cases fail as parallels because they all involved *third-person observable phenomena* where the competing theories made different predictions about publicly accessible measurements, whereas consciousness uniquely involves *first-person phenomenal character* that is only accessible from one epistemic perspective.

Consider what made the historical resolutions possible:

**Behaviorism vs. Cognitivism**: Both theories were about explaining the *same publicly observable behavior*—reaction times, error patterns, learning curves. Cognitivists won because positing internal representations made better predictions about these third-person observables (e.g., Sternberg's additive factors, Posner's letter-matching tasks). Crucially, even behaviorists agreed that if reaction time patterns showed stage-like structure, this would be evidence against their view. The disagreement was about ontology, but both sides accepted the same evidential base: publicly measurable behavior.

**Vitalism vs. Mechanism**: The decisive experiments (Wöhler's urea synthesis, Buchner's cell-free fermentation) worked because both sides agreed that if you could produce life's characteristic effects (organic compounds, metabolic processes) through purely physical-chemical means, this would count against vitalism. Vitalists made an empirical prediction: you cannot synthesize organic compounds from inorganic ones. When this prediction failed, the theory died. There was no deadlock because both sides accepted what would constitute a test.

**Phlogiston vs. Oxygen**: Lavoisier's weight measurements worked because both theories made competing predictions about the *same publicly observable quantity*—the weight of combustion products. Phlogiston theory predicted weight loss (phlogiston escaping), oxygen theory predicted weight gain (oxygen combining). The measurements directly adjudicated between incompatible predictions about third-person observables.

Now contrast consciousness: The thesis identifies that functionalist and non-functionalist theories disagree about whether there exists *anything beyond the third-person functional facts*. A functionalist examining a zombie world (physically identical to ours but lacking consciousness) would say "there's no difference here—consciousness just is these functional patterns, and they're present." A non-functionalist would say "the functional patterns are identical, but consciousness is absent—that's exactly the dissociation that shows consciousness isn't functional."

The critical asymmetry: In the historical cases, the competing theories disagreed about what would be observed in the third-person realm. In consciousness, the theories disagree about whether the third-person realm exhausts what there is to explain. When Doerig et al. propose testing GWT against IIT using neural measures, they assume both theories are making claims about the same evidential base. But they're not: GWT claims consciousness is constituted by certain information-processing patterns (so neural evidence directly tracks consciousness), while IIT claims consciousness is constituted by integrated information (which may come apart from any particular processing pattern we can neurally measure).

The thesis's insight is that this creates a unique problem: Any empirical test must use third-person measurements (neural activity, behavior, reports), but whether such measurements can access consciousness at all is exactly what divides the theories. The behaviorist could be proven wrong by third-person evidence because behaviorism was a theory about third-person phenomena. But non-functionalism claims consciousness has a first-person aspect that *necessarily* transcends third-person evidence—so third-person evidence cannot, even in principle, prove it wrong.

The historical cases involved competing explanations of the same phenomena. Consciousness involves disagreement about whether there are phenomena beyond what any third-person explanation could capture.

VERDICT.

The defence fails. While it correctly identifies that consciousness involves first-person/third-person asymmetry absent from the historical cases, this difference doesn't save the thesis from proving too much—it just relocates where the argument proves too much.

The vitalism case is particularly fatal. Vitalists didn't just claim life required special chemistry; many claimed life required an *immaterial* vital force—an élan vital that was precisely *not* accessible to physical-chemical investigation. This is structurally identical to non-functionalist claims about consciousness: something beyond the physical-functional facts. Yet vitalism was empirically defeated anyway, not by directly measuring vital force, but by showing that all life's characteristic effects could be produced mechanistically.

The defence tries to say non-functionalism is unfalsifiable because it claims something beyond third-person access. But this makes non-functionalism unfalsifiable by definitional fiat, which is a problem for non-functionalism, not for the possibility of empirical testing. If a theory claims X exists but is necessarily empirically undetectable, then yes, empirical methods can't test it—but that's the theory's vice, not empirical science's limitation.

More devastatingly: the defence's own logic shows the thesis is self-undermining. If non-functionalist theories really claim consciousness transcends all possible third-person evidence, then they're not empirical theories at all—they're metaphysical positions outside science's scope. But then there's no "deadlock" in consciousness *science*; there's just science testing functionalist theories while non-functionalist metaphysics operates in a different domain. The thesis claims empirical testing faces deadlock, but the defence admits non-functionalism places itself beyond empirical testing, which means empirical methods can proceed with functionalist theories unimpeded.

The objection succeeds: the argument proves too much because it would make vitalism untestable, yet vitalism was tested and defeated.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis claims empirical tests require double-dissociations that presuppose settling the functionalism question, creating untestable deadlock. But consider the actual case of **Integrated Information Theory (IIT) vs. Global Workspace Theory (GWT)** being tested through the "perturbational complexity index" (PCI) in anesthesia studies (Casali et al., 2013).

IIT predicts that consciousness correlates with integrated information (Φ), measurable via how the brain responds to perturbations—high Φ means rich, integrated responses. GWT predicts consciousness requires global broadcasting, measurable via widespread cortical ignition patterns. During anesthesia, patients lose consciousness while brain tissue remains intact.

The empirical finding: PCI (tracking integration) reliably distinguished conscious from unconscious states across anesthesia, sleep, and vegetative states, while global ignition patterns were less reliable. This evidence favored IIT over GWT.

Crucially, this worked despite IIT being non-functionalist (consciousness is intrinsic integrated information, not functional role) and GWT being functionalist (consciousness is the function of global broadcasting). Both theories made different predictions about the *same measurable quantity*—the brain's perturbational response patterns. Researchers didn't need to presuppose functionalism or solve the double-dissociation problem; they just measured which mathematical signature better tracked the presence/absence of consciousness as determined by independent behavioral criteria (responsiveness, reportability).

The deadlock doesn't exist: theories on opposite sides of the functionalism divide made competing empirical predictions and evidence adjudicated between them.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious counterexample. It shows actual consciousness science proceeding exactly as the thesis claims is impossible: empirically testing functionalist against non-functionalist theories without methodological deadlock.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The PCI case doesn't defeat the thesis—it actually *exemplifies* the deadlock while appearing to escape it. The appearance of successful testing depends on a crucial equivocation about what "consciousness" means in the experimental setup.

**The equivocation**: When Casali et al. use "independent behavioral criteria (responsiveness, reportability)" to determine consciousness, they're operationalizing consciousness *functionally*. A patient is classified as "conscious" if they respond, report, or show behavioral markers—all functional criteria. The PCI study then asks: which neural signature (integrated information vs. global broadcasting) better predicts *these functional markers*?

But this is precisely what the thesis predicts will happen: the "test" only works by *presupposing functionalism at the evidential level*. The study treats behavioral responsiveness as *constitutive* of consciousness rather than merely correlated with it. A non-functionalist would object: "You haven't tested whether IIT correctly identifies consciousness—you've tested whether Φ correlates with functional markers of consciousness. These are different questions, and the difference is exactly what divides our theories."

**Why this matters**: IIT claims consciousness is intrinsic integrated information—that a system with high Φ is conscious *even if it shows no functional effects*. Tononi explicitly endorses this: a sufficiently integrated photodiode would be conscious despite having no cognitive function, no reportability, no behavioral flexibility. The PCI study cannot test this claim because it uses functional markers (responsiveness) to define the ground truth.

Consider what would happen if IIT is true and consciousness actually dissociates from function: Imagine a patient with high Φ (integrated information) but damaged global workspace mechanisms—conscious but unable to respond or report. The PCI study would classify this patient as "unconscious" (no behavioral response) and mark high Φ as a false positive. But by IIT's lights, this is a false *negative*—the patient is conscious, just functionally disconnected. The study's methodology *cannot distinguish* between "IIT is wrong" and "consciousness dissociated from function."

**The parallel failure for GWT**: Similarly, if GWT is true and consciousness just *is* global broadcasting, then measuring global ignition patterns directly measures consciousness—there's no gap. But if non-functionalism is true and consciousness can occur without global broadcasting, then measuring ignition patterns only tracks functional correlates, not consciousness itself. The study assumes the former (functionalist) interpretation when it treats neural measures as tracking consciousness rather than merely functional correlates.

**The deeper point**: The objector claims "researchers didn't need to presuppose functionalism"—but they did. The entire experimental design presupposes that behavioral markers (responsiveness, reportability) are *reliable indicators* of consciousness. This presupposition is precisely what non-functionalists deny in cases of dissociation. The study can only proceed by treating consciousness as epistemically accessible through functional markers, which begs the question against non-functionalist theories that claim consciousness can be present without such markers.

The thesis predicts exactly this structure: apparent empirical progress that actually smuggles in functionalist assumptions at the methodological level, making the "test" question-begging rather than neutral. The PCI case confirms rather than refutes the deadlock—it shows how consciousness science proceeds by *assuming* functionalism rather than testing it.

# VERDICT

The defence fails. The attempted rescue depends on claiming the PCI study "presupposes functionalism" by using behavioral markers, but this misunderstands what scientific testing requires and creates an impossible standard.

**Why the defence fails**:

First, the defence demands that consciousness science establish ground truth about consciousness *without* using any behavioral or functional markers. But this is incoherent—it requires determining whether someone is conscious without using any observable evidence. Every scientific test requires some operational criteria. When testing theories of temperature, we use thermometer readings; this doesn't "presuppose" that temperature is whatever thermometers measure—it uses the best available indicators to test competing theories about temperature's underlying nature. The PCI study does the same: uses behavioral markers as *indicators* while testing theories about consciousness's *substrate*.

Second, the defence's own example undermines it. It imagines a patient with "high Φ but damaged global workspace—conscious but unable to respond." But notice: if this patient truly shows *no behavioral effects whatsoever*—no neural responses to stimuli, no learning, no discrimination, nothing—then on what grounds does IIT claim consciousness is present? Only by stipulation from the theory itself. The defence has made non-functionalism unfalsifiable: consciousness is whatever IIT says it is, regardless of any observable evidence. This isn't a problem with testing; it's a problem with the theory being empirically vacuous.

Third, and most devastatingly: the PCI study makes progress precisely because IIT and GWT, despite their different ontologies, *both accept behavioral responsiveness as evidence*. Tononi doesn't say "ignore behavioral markers entirely"—IIT predicts that high Φ will *correlate* with responsiveness in typical cases, while making different predictions in edge cases (anesthesia, split-brain, etc.). The study tests these differential predictions. This is exactly how science works: theories with different ontologies make different predictions about shared observables.

The counterexample succeeds. The PCI case shows that functionalist and non-functionalist theories can be empirically tested by making different predictions about measurable quantities, without methodological deadlock. The thesis's claimed deadlock dissolves into the ordinary challenge of finding good operational measures—difficult, but not impossible or uniquely problematic for consciousness.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis presents itself as revealing a problematic "untestable deadlock" in consciousness science, but this is merely a verbose restatement of the trivial observation that theories making different metaphysical assumptions about what consciousness *is* will disagree about what counts as evidence for consciousness. This is true but entirely uncontested—it's just what it means for theories to have different ontologies.

The supposed "dilemma" reduces to: "Functionalists think consciousness is functional, so they accept functional evidence; non-functionalists think consciousness isn't (just) functional, so they're skeptical of purely functional evidence." This is not a discovery about an "untestable deadlock"—it's a tautology dressed as insight. Of course theories that identify consciousness with different properties will validate different evidential standards; that's what theoretical disagreement consists in.

The thesis adds no substantive claim beyond this definitional truth. The language of "double-dissociations," "meta-level disagreement replicating object-level divide," and "methodological deadlock" creates an illusion of depth, but the core claim is just: theories with different ontological commitments interpret evidence differently. This has been obvious since before Kuhn formalized it—it's why we call them *different theories*.

The weak point is the entire thesis: it mistakes a conceptual truism (theories disagree about evidence because they disagree about what they're theories of) for a substantive discovery about consciousness science's methodology. There's no novel instability here, just the unremarkable fact that theoretical pluralism involves evidential pluralism, verbally repackaged as a dramatic "deadlock."

STRENGTH: 4/5

The thesis cannot survive this attack in its current form. What it presents as a problematic structural feature requiring philosophical attention is actually just the definition of what it means for theories to differ ontologically. The entire apparatus of "untestable deadlock" collapses into the trivial truth that functionalists and non-functionalists disagree—which is simply what makes them functionalists and non-functionalists. No one contests this; calling it a "deadlock" is purely rhetorical.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The thesis is not merely restating that "theories disagree about evidence"—it identifies a *specific vicious structure* that makes consciousness science methodologically impotent in a way that goes beyond normal theoretical pluralism.

The key is to distinguish three levels of disagreement:

**Level 1 (Trivial)**: Theories interpret the same evidence differently. Plate tectonics and expanding earth theory both see mid-ocean ridges but interpret them differently. This is ordinary theoretical disagreement.

**Level 2 (Non-trivial but common)**: Theories disagree about what observations are relevant. Psychoanalysis and behaviorism disagree about whether dream reports constitute scientific evidence. This is evidential pluralism—significant but resolvable through methodological debate about epistemic standards.

**Level 3 (The thesis's claim)**: Theories disagree about whether *the very possibility of empirical adjudication exists*, and this disagreement is *structurally isomorphic* to the object-level theoretical disagreement itself. This is what the thesis identifies as unique to consciousness.

Here's why Level 3 is non-trivial: In normal science, competing theories can disagree about ontology while agreeing that *some possible empirical configuration* would decide between them. Einstein and Bohr disagreed about quantum ontology, but both accepted that Bell test experiments were relevant and could in principle adjudicate their dispute (even if interpretive work remained). The disagreement was about what reality is like, not about whether empirical investigation could access it.

The thesis claims consciousness faces something structurally different: The functionalist says "consciousness is functional patterns, so functional evidence directly accesses it—empirical tests are possible." The non-functionalist says "consciousness transcends functional patterns, so functional evidence only accesses correlates—empirical tests cannot reach the phenomenon itself." 

The vicious structure: Whether empirical tests *can work in principle* depends on whether functionalism is true. But determining whether functionalism is true is exactly what the empirical tests were supposed to accomplish. This is circular in a way that ordinary theoretical disagreement is not.

**Why this isn't trivial**: The triviality objection says "of course theories with different ontologies validate different evidence—that's what makes them different theories." But this misses the reflexive problem. The thesis isn't claiming that functionalist and non-functionalist theories interpret evidence differently (Level 1) or validate different types of evidence (Level 2). It's claiming that the theories disagree about whether *empirical evidence can be relevant at all*, and this meta-level disagreement has the same structure as the object-level disagreement.

Consider the parallel: Imagine if plate tectonics said "geological evidence can reveal earth's structure" while expanding earth theory said "geological evidence cannot in principle reveal earth's structure because true earth-structure transcends what geology can measure." That would be bizarre—and it would be non-trivially different from mere interpretive disagreement. The thesis claims consciousness theories have exactly this structure.

**The substantive claim**: When Doerig et al. propose "adversarial collaboration," they assume both sides can agree on experimental designs that would favor one theory over another. The thesis shows this assumption fails because:
- Functionalists think experiments measure consciousness directly (so results can favor their theory)
- Non-functionalists think experiments measure functional correlates only (so results cannot disfavor their theory—any result is compatible with consciousness dissociating from the measured functions)

This asymmetry—where one side treats experiments as potentially decisive while the other treats them as systematically missing the target—is not trivial. It explains why consciousness science, despite decades of empirical work and sophisticated experiments like the PCI studies, hasn't converged on theoretical consensus. The lack of convergence isn't due to insufficient data (Level 1 disagreement) or disputed methodological standards (Level 2), but to a structural feature where the theories disagree about what empirical work *could possibly accomplish*.

The non-triviality: This predicts and explains the actual sociology of consciousness science—why adversarial collaboration proposals fail, why no experiment is accepted as decisive, why the field accumulates data without theoretical convergence. A trivial observation (theories disagree) couldn't do this predictive and explanatory work.

# VERDICT

The defence fails. Despite identifying something real about consciousness science's structure, it cannot escape the triviality charge because what it identifies is simply a consequence of non-functionalism being *empirically unconstrained by design*.

The defence's own formulation reveals the problem: "Non-functionalists think experiments measure functional correlates only, so results cannot disfavor their theory—any result is compatible with consciousness dissociating from the measured functions." This isn't a discovery about a methodological deadlock—it's the observation that non-functionalism, as characterized, is *unfalsifiable*. A theory that says "consciousness might be present even when all functional evidence suggests absence" has made itself immune to empirical refutation.

But this is non-functionalism's problem, not empirical science's problem. The defence tries to present this as a symmetric deadlock ("functionalists and non-functionalists both trapped"), but it's actually asymmetric: functionalist theories make empirical predictions and can be tested; non-functionalist theories (as the defence characterizes them) don't constrain empirical observations and therefore aren't scientific theories at all.

The "vicious structure" the defence identifies—where determining if empirical tests work requires settling the functionalism question first—only arises if we grant that non-functionalist theories are legitimate empirical competitors. But a theory that claims consciousness can dissociate from all possible functional evidence isn't an empirical competitor; it's a metaphysical position that has removed itself from empirical adjudication.

The triviality stands: The thesis reduces to "non-functionalist theories don't accept functional evidence as decisive for consciousness, while functionalist theories do." This is true by definition—it's what makes them non-functionalist and functionalist respectively. Calling this a "deadlock" is rhetorical inflation. The actual situation is that functionalist theories compete empirically (as PCI studies show), while non-functionalist theories that truly reject all functional evidence aren't participating in empirical science.

The objection succeeds: the thesis is trivial.

</details>

---

### O6. Accepting that consciousness could be substrate-independent commits you to accepting that we can never empirically confirm which theory is correct, because substrate independence entails that behavioral/functional isomorphs could instantiate different consciousness profiles.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 0/5 · worst 4/5 · survives
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Functionalists about consciousness who nevertheless believe empirical investigation will determine the correct theory
- **Sketch:** Many consciousness theorists accept substrate independence—that consciousness depends on organizational/functional properties, not specific physical substrates. But this commits them to the possibility of functional zombies in a new sense: systems that are behaviorally/functionally identical to us in every testable way, but where the underlying organizational principles differ in ways that make different theories correct for each system. For instance, one silicon-based system might implement global workspace architecture while another implements integrated information, yet both could be functionally isomorphic from an external behavioral perspective. Since we can only empirically test functional/behavioral properties, substrate independence means we cannot use empirical methods to determine which organizational theory correctly describes any particular instance. The commitment flows from substrate independence + multiple realizability of functions + empirical access only to functional properties.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Connects substrate independence to underdetermination competently but the implication is fairly straightforward.
  - TRIVIAL — landed 2/5, answered. Defence showed the thesis requires auxiliary premises beyond the definition and pointed to functionalists' actual failure to recognize the consequence
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 4/5, answered. Defence clarified that the gap between what consciousness depends on and what we can access is the epistemic problem itself, not an equivocation
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 3/5, answered. Defence established the thesis makes a philosophical claim about commitments falsifiable through conceptual argument, not empirical prediction
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 2/5, answered. Defence showed cited literature addresses different problems and objector provided no citation of this specific argument
- **Survives as:** Accepting that consciousness could be substrate-independent commits you to accepting that we can never empirically confirm which theory is correct, because substrate independence entails that behavioral/functional isomorphs could instantiate different consciousness profiles

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis reduces to a definitional truth about what "substrate independence" means, making it a trivial consequence rather than a substantive discovery. The argument is essentially: "If consciousness depends only on functional/organizational properties (substrate independence), and we can only empirically test functional/organizational properties, then functionally identical systems are empirically indistinguishable regarding consciousness." This is just unpacking what "substrate independence" means—that substrate doesn't matter, only organization does. The supposedly problematic consequence (empirical underdetermination between theories) is already built into the very concept being analyzed.

The weak point is the claim that this reveals a **commitment** that functionalists have **overlooked**. But any functionalist who has thought for five minutes about substrate independence already knows that empirically identical functional organizations would be indistinguishable. The thesis dresses up this obvious implication as though it were a hidden cost or overlooked consequence. It's like saying "accepting heliocentrism commits you to accepting that Earth moves"—true, but that's just what the view *is*, not an additional burden it carries. The thesis provides no new information beyond restating what substrate independence entails by definition.

STRENGTH: 2/5

This has some force because the thesis does trade on making explicit what might be implicit, but it's not truly trivial since many functionalists genuinely seem to believe empirical methods will adjudicate between theories—suggesting they haven't fully absorbed this consequence.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies what is definitional here and what constitutes the substantive commitment.

**First**, the thesis does not merely restate that "functionally identical systems are empirically indistinguishable." That would indeed be trivial. Rather, it claims that substrate independence *combined with* the empirical accessibility constraint generates a specific epistemic consequence that contradicts the methodological optimism many functionalists actually hold.

**Second**, the objection conflates two distinct claims:
- (Trivial) "If consciousness supervenes only on functional organization, then systems with identical functional organization have identical consciousness"
- (Non-trivial) "If consciousness supervenes only on functional organization, then systems with identical *behavioral/testable* profiles could instantiate *different* organizational principles and thus different consciousness profiles"

The thesis depends on the second claim, not the first. The key move is recognizing that behavioral/functional isomorphism *from an external testing perspective* underdetermines the underlying organizational architecture. A global workspace system and an integrated information system could both produce identical input-output behaviors while implementing fundamentally different organizational structures. This is not definitional—it requires the additional premise that multiple organizational architectures can realize the same behavioral capacities.

**Third**, the objection's analogy fails. "Heliocentrism commits you to Earth moving" is indeed just restating the view. But the thesis is more like: "Heliocentrism commits you to being unable to detect Earth's motion from purely local mechanical experiments" (which was actually a substantive 17th-century problem requiring Galilean relativity). The epistemic consequence is not contained in the metaphysical claim itself.

**Fourth**, the objection actually concedes the key point in its STRENGTH assessment: many functionalists *do* believe empirical methods will adjudicate between theories, suggesting they haven't absorbed this consequence. If the consequence were genuinely trivial and definitional, this widespread failure would be inexplicable. The fact that sophisticated theorists miss this implication shows it's not merely unpacking a definition.

# VERDICT

The defence holds. The thesis survives this objection.

The objector has mistaken *what follows from a definition plus auxiliary premises* for *what the definition itself states*. Substrate independence alone does not entail empirical underdetermination. You need substrate independence + multiple realizability of behavioral profiles by different organizational structures + limitation of empirical access to behavioral/functional properties. This conjunction yields a consequence that is neither trivial nor obviously contained in any single component.

The objector's own concession—that many functionalists seem not to have recognized this consequence—is fatal to the triviality charge. Something genuinely trivial and definitional cannot be systematically overlooked by experts in the field.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis equivocates on "functional/organizational properties" between two readings that make the argument appear to work but actually render it either uninteresting or implausible.

**Reading 1 (Coarse-grained):** "Functional properties" means input-output behavioral profiles observable from external testing—what the defender calls "behavioral/testable profiles." On this reading, the thesis is plausible: two systems could indeed have identical behavioral profiles while differing in internal organization (one implements global workspace, another integrated information).

**Reading 2 (Fine-grained):** "Functional properties" means complete organizational/computational structure—the full specification of how information is processed, integrated, broadcast, etc. On this reading, the thesis is interesting: if consciousness depends only on these properties, and we can empirically access these properties, then we *can* determine which theory is correct by examining the organizational details.

**The equivocation:** The argument needs Reading 1 to establish that "functionally identical systems could instantiate different consciousness profiles" (plausibility), but needs Reading 2 to establish that "substrate independence" means consciousness depends on these properties (the initial commitment). 

When the defender says "substrate independence means consciousness depends on organizational/functional properties, not specific physical substrates," this naturally suggests Reading 2—the full organizational story matters. But then the argument switches to Reading 1 when claiming our "empirical access only to functional properties" leaves theories underdetermined. If we stick with Reading 2 throughout, substrate independence doesn't generate the epistemic problem—we just need to empirically investigate the fine-grained organizational properties. If we stick with Reading 1, it's unclear why substrate independence (a metaphysical thesis about what consciousness depends on) would be formulated in terms of mere behavioral profiles rather than organizational architecture.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This is a serious problem. The thesis trades on sliding between what functionalists mean by the properties consciousness depends on (fine-grained organization) and what we can empirically access (coarse-grained behavior), making these seem like the same thing when they're not.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection mislocates the equivocation and misunderstands the epistemic constraint that generates the problem.

**First**, there is no equivocation in the thesis's use of "functional/organizational properties." The thesis consistently uses this term in Reading 2 (fine-grained organizational structure) when stating what consciousness depends on according to substrate independence. The global workspace architecture, integrated information structures, and recurrent processing loops are all fine-grained organizational properties—not mere behavioral profiles. The thesis never claims consciousness depends on coarse-grained input-output behavior.

**Second**, the objection misidentifies where the epistemic gap enters. The gap is not between two readings of "functional properties," but between:
- What consciousness depends on (fine-grained organizational properties)
- What we can empirically determine about a system from outside (which may underdetermine those organizational properties)

This is not equivocation—it's recognizing a genuine epistemic limitation.

**Third**, the objection assumes without argument that we *can* "just empirically investigate the fine-grained organizational properties." But this is precisely what the thesis challenges. Consider: can we empirically determine whether a system implements global workspace or integrated information architecture from outside? The thesis claims that systems could be constructed where both architectures produce identical testable signatures. If a silicon system processes information through broadcasting to a global workspace, and another processes information through maximally integrated Φ-structures, and both produce identical responses to all probes we can devise, then our empirical methods cannot distinguish which organizational principle is actually implemented.

**Fourth**, the objection's Reading 1/Reading 2 distinction actually helps the thesis rather than undermining it. The point is precisely that consciousness depends on Reading 2 properties (fine-grained organization), but our empirical access may be limited to Reading 1 properties (behavioral profiles). This is not equivocation—it's the articulation of an epistemic gap. The objector seems to think that if consciousness depends on fine-grained organizational properties, we must be able to empirically access those properties. But why think that? The thesis argues that substrate independence, combined with multiple realizability, means different fine-grained organizations can produce identical empirical signatures.

**Fifth**, consider a concrete case. Suppose we're examining a sophisticated AI system. We can test its responses, probe its behavior under various conditions, even examine its internal states through whatever interfaces it provides. But can we determine whether consciousness (if present) depends on global broadcasting of information or on integrated information structures? If both organizational principles could be implemented in ways that produce identical testable outputs, then no—we cannot. This is not because we're equivocating on "functional," but because different fine-grained functional organizations can be multiply realized by the same coarse-grained functional profile.

**Sixth**, the objection's proposed solution—"just empirically investigate the fine-grained organizational properties"—assumes we have transparent access to those properties. But substrate independence itself suggests we don't. If what matters is organization-not-substrate, and if organizational properties can be realized in indefinitely many physical ways, then determining which organizational properties are actually instantiated requires already knowing which organizational description is the right level of analysis. We face a circularity: to test which theory is correct, we need to know which organizational properties to look for, but knowing which properties to look for requires knowing which theory is correct.

# VERDICT

The defence holds. The objection fails.

The objector has not identified an equivocation but rather has misunderstood the structure of the argument. The thesis does not slide between two meanings of "functional/organizational properties." It consistently means fine-grained organizational structure throughout. The epistemic problem arises not from equivocation but from the recognition that:

1. Consciousness depends on fine-grained organizational properties (substrate independence)
2. Different fine-grained organizational properties can be multiply realized by the same coarse-grained behavioral/testable profile
3. Our empirical access is limited to what we can test/observe from outside
4. Therefore, empirical methods may be unable to determine which fine-grained organizational properties are actually instantiated

The objector's assumption that "we can just empirically investigate the fine-grained organizational properties" begs the question against the thesis. The entire point is that substrate independence + multiple realizability suggests we *cannot* always do this. If the objector thinks we can, they need to explain how—not simply assert it by distinguishing two readings of "functional" and claiming one reading solves the problem.

The objection actually clarifies the thesis's structure rather than undermining it. The gap between Reading 2 (what consciousness depends on) and Reading 1 (what we can empirically access) is not an equivocation in the thesis—it's the epistemic problem the thesis identifies.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis is unfalsifiable because it systematically redefines what counts as "empirically testing organizational properties" to protect itself from counterexamples.

**The protection mechanism:** Whenever we propose an empirical method that could distinguish between organizational theories (neural imaging, lesion studies, computational analysis of information flow, measuring integration vs. broadcasting patterns), the thesis can respond: "But those systems might not be true functional isomorphs" or "That's still just measuring behavioral/testable outputs, not the real organizational properties." 

**The weak point** is the claim that "behavioral/functional isomorphs could instantiate different consciousness profiles." This is unfalsifiable because:

1. If we find empirical differences between a global workspace system and an integrated information system, the thesis says "then they weren't really functional isomorphs"
2. If we cannot find empirical differences, the thesis says "see, they could have different consciousness profiles despite being isomorphs"
3. If we develop new empirical methods to probe organizational structure, the thesis redefines these as merely accessing "behavioral/testable properties" rather than the "real" organizational properties

The thesis has no empirical conditions under which it would be wrong. Any proposed empirical test either proves the systems weren't isomorphs (confirming the thesis vacuously) or proves we cannot distinguish them (confirming the thesis substantively). The concept of "functional isomorph" becomes whatever the thesis needs it to be to avoid counterexamples—loose enough to allow different organizational structures, but tight enough to rule out empirical distinguishability.

**STRENGTH: 4/5**

This is a serious problem. The thesis cannot specify what would count as empirically distinguishing "true functional isomorphs with different organizational properties" because any successful distinction gets redefined as proof they weren't really isomorphs. The defender's own response to EQUIVOCATION reveals this: they simultaneously claim organizational properties are fine-grained and empirically investigable in principle, yet insist our empirical access cannot reach them. This makes the thesis immune to empirical refutation.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection confuses *epistemic possibility* with *unfalsifiability* and misunderstands what the thesis claims.

**First**, the thesis does not claim it is *impossible* to empirically distinguish organizational theories—it claims that substrate independence *commits you to accepting* that such cases are possible. This is a conditional claim about what follows from substrate independence, not an unfalsifiable empirical prediction. The thesis would be falsified if someone showed that substrate independence does *not* entail this epistemic possibility—not by finding cases where we successfully distinguish theories empirically.

**Second**, the objection mischaracterizes the dialectic. The thesis is not saying "we will never find empirical differences between any global workspace and any integrated information system." It's saying: "If substrate independence is true, then it must be *possible in principle* for two systems to be functionally isomorphic while instantiating different organizational principles." This is a metaphysical claim about what substrate independence allows, not an empirical prediction about what we'll find.

**Third**, the objection's three scenarios actually help rather than hurt the thesis:

- Scenario 1: "If we find empirical differences, they weren't really isomorphs." Correct—and this doesn't make the thesis unfalsifiable. It makes it a claim about what's possible for genuine isomorphs. Finding non-isomorphs doesn't refute the claim that isomorphs could exist.

- Scenario 2: "If we cannot find differences, they could have different consciousness profiles." This is precisely what the thesis predicts should be possible given substrate independence. Finding such cases would *confirm* the thesis, not protect it from falsification.

- Scenario 3: "New empirical methods get redefined as behavioral/testable." This is not ad hoc redefinition—it's applying a principled distinction. The question is: can these methods determine the organizational structure *as such*, or only its observable effects? If substrate independence is true, then what matters is the organizational pattern, not any particular physical realization. So methods that detect physical properties (neural firing, computational states) face the question: do they detect the organizationally relevant properties, or just one physical realization of them?

**Fourth**, the thesis *can* be falsified. It would be falsified by showing that:
- Substrate independence does not actually entail multiple realizability of behavioral profiles by different organizational structures, OR
- Organizational properties are not the kind of thing that can be multiply realized in this way, OR
- There is a principled argument that functional isomorphism at the behavioral level necessarily entails isomorphism at the organizationally relevant level

These are conceptual/philosophical falsification conditions, which is appropriate since the thesis is a philosophical claim about what a metaphysical position commits you to.

**Fifth**, the objection demands empirical falsifiability for a philosophical thesis about epistemic commitments. But "accepting X commits you to accepting Y" is not an empirical hypothesis—it's a claim about logical/conceptual entailment. We don't falsify it by doing experiments; we falsify it by showing the entailment doesn't hold. The objector is applying the wrong standard.

**Sixth**, consider the parallel: "Accepting external world skepticism commits you to accepting that we cannot empirically confirm the external world exists." Is this unfalsifiable? In one sense yes—the skeptic can always reinterpret any evidence. But it's still a substantive philosophical claim that can be evaluated by examining whether the commitment really follows from the skeptical position. The thesis operates at this same level.

**Seventh**, the objection's claim that "functional isomorph becomes whatever the thesis needs it to be" is false. The thesis uses a clear definition: systems are functional isomorphs if they have identical input-output behavioral profiles under all possible tests. This is not ad hoc—it's a standard notion in philosophy of mind. The thesis then argues that this behavioral isomorphism underdetermines organizational structure. Whether this argument succeeds is debatable, but the concept is not being gerrymandered.

# VERDICT

The defence succeeds, but reveals an important limitation of the thesis.

The objection fails because it applies an inappropriate falsifiability standard. The thesis is not an empirical prediction but a philosophical claim about what substrate independence commits you to. Such claims are falsified by showing the entailment fails, not by empirical investigation. The objector's three scenarios do not show unfalsifiability—they show the thesis has bite. If substrate independence really does allow functional isomorphs with different organizational structures, then scenarios 1 and 2 are exactly what we should expect.

However, the objection does reveal something important: the thesis's force depends entirely on whether the metaphysical possibility it describes is genuine. If it turns out that functional isomorphism at the behavioral level *necessarily* entails organizational isomorphism at the consciousness-relevant level, then the thesis fails—not because it's unfalsifiable, but because the entailment it claims doesn't hold.

The thesis is therefore falsifiable in principle through philosophical argument about the relationship between behavioral and organizational properties. The objector has not shown it's unfalsifiable—they've shown it operates at a philosophical rather than empirical level, which is appropriate given its content.

The defence holds because the thesis makes a conditional claim about commitments, not an empirical prediction, and such claims have their own appropriate standards of evaluation.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis articulates a well-known position in philosophy of mind: **the problem of other minds extended to computational/organizational theories**, or more specifically, **the underdetermination of consciousness theories by behavioral evidence**. This has been extensively discussed in the literature on functionalism and consciousness.

Ned Block's distinction between psychofunctionalism and functionalism (1978) already identified that different organizational/computational theories could be behaviorally equivalent. David Chalmers' work on the hard problem (1996) explicitly discusses how functional/organizational descriptions underdetermine phenomenal facts. The "inverted spectrum" and "absent qualia" debates in functionalism literature (going back to Locke, revived by Shoemaker, Block, and others) all explore how functionally/behaviorally identical systems could differ in consciousness.

More directly, **Daniel Dennett's heterophenomenology** (1991, 2003) was developed precisely to address this problem—that we cannot directly access organizational properties of consciousness in others, only behavioral/functional outputs. The entire methodological debate between first-person and third-person approaches to consciousness studies presupposes this underdetermination problem.

The thesis presents as a novel "commitment" what is actually a **standard objection to functionalist theories of consciousness** that has been discussed for decades. The literature already recognizes that if consciousness depends on organization-not-substrate, and if we can only test behavioral/functional outputs, then empirical methods face systematic limitations. This is not an overlooked consequence that functionalists are "committed to" despite themselves—it's a known challenge that different theorists address in different ways (some bite the bullet, others deny the premise of multiple realizability, others argue for theoretical inference to best explanation).

**STRENGTH: 2/5**

The thesis does synthesize the problem clearly and applies it specifically to the substrate independence debate, but it's not genuinely novel. The underdetermination of consciousness theories by behavioral evidence is a recognized problem in the field, not a hidden commitment that needs exposing.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies what is "already taken" and what the thesis contributes.

**First**, the cited literature does not actually establish the same claim. Let's examine each:

- **Block's psychofunctionalism vs. functionalism**: This distinction concerns whether the relevant functional organization is defined by common-sense psychology or by scientific theory. It does not address whether different organizational theories (global workspace vs. integrated information) could be empirically indistinguishable while both being substrate-independent. Block was distinguishing types of functionalism, not arguing that substrate independence entails empirical underdetermination.

- **Chalmers' hard problem**: This argues that *any* functional/organizational description underdetermines phenomenal character—that you could have the same functional organization with different qualia or no qualia. This is a much stronger claim than the thesis makes. The thesis accepts that organizational properties determine consciousness (substrate independence) and argues that *which* organizational theory is correct becomes underdetermined. Chalmers argues that organizational properties don't determine phenomenal character at all. These are opposite positions.

- **Inverted spectrum/absent qualia**: These thought experiments assume functional/behavioral identity while varying qualia. But they're typically used to argue *against* functionalism—to show that functional organization is not sufficient for consciousness. The thesis, by contrast, accepts substrate independence (a functionalist commitment) and derives an epistemic consequence from it. The dialectical role is completely different.

- **Dennett's heterophenomenology**: This is a methodological proposal for studying consciousness through behavioral reports, precisely because Dennett *rejects* the idea that there are consciousness facts beyond the functional/behavioral. He would deny the thesis's premise that "different organizational principles could be instantiated." Dennett is not addressing the same problem—he's dissolving it by denying that consciousness is anything over and above the functional/behavioral patterns.

**Second**, none of the cited work makes the specific argument the thesis makes: that *substrate independence specifically* commits you to empirical underdetermination *between organizational theories that all accept substrate independence*. The thesis is not about whether consciousness transcends function (Chalmers, Block) or whether we should study it behaviorally (Dennett). It's about whether accepting that consciousness depends on organization-not-substrate commits you to accepting that we cannot empirically determine *which organizational theory* is correct.

**Third**, the objection claims this is a "standard objection to functionalist theories" but provides no citation of anyone making this specific objection in this form. The inverted spectrum objection says: "Functionalism is wrong because functional identity doesn't guarantee phenomenal identity." The thesis says: "If you accept substrate independence (a functionalist view), you're committed to accepting that empirical methods cannot adjudicate between competing organizational theories." These are different arguments with different targets.

**Fourth**, the objection's claim that "different theorists address this in different ways" actually supports the thesis rather than undermining it. If theorists are addressing it in different ways, this suggests there's a real problem that needs addressing—not that the thesis is merely restating known territory. The thesis identifies what the commitment is; the fact that different theorists respond differently shows it's a substantive commitment worth identifying.

**Fifth**, consider the specific case the thesis targets: functionalists who believe empirical investigation will determine whether global workspace theory, integrated information theory, higher-order theory, or recurrent processing theory is correct. The thesis argues these theorists face a tension: substrate independence (which they accept) commits them to the possibility that these theories could be empirically indistinguishable. Where in the cited literature is this specific tension identified?

**Sixth**, the objection conflates the general epistemological problem of other minds with the specific problem the thesis identifies. Yes, we've known since Descartes that we cannot directly access others' consciousness. But the thesis is not about this general skeptical problem. It's about a specific consequence of substrate independence for theory choice in consciousness science. The problem is not "we cannot know if others are conscious" but "we cannot empirically determine which organizational theory correctly describes consciousness, even in our own case, if substrate independence is true."

**Seventh**, the objection's own weakness rating (2/5) and acknowledgment that the thesis "synthesizes the problem clearly and applies it specifically to the substrate independence debate" concedes that the thesis is doing something the literature has not done. If it were truly already taken, the strength rating should be 5/5 and there should be clear citations of authors making exactly this argument.

# VERDICT

The defence succeeds. The objection fails.

The objector has gestured at related literature without showing that any of it makes the specific argument the thesis makes. The cited works either:
- Make different arguments (Block's distinction between types of functionalism)
- Make stronger claims that reject substrate independence (Chalmers' hard problem, inverted spectrum)
- Make weaker claims that dissolve the problem by denying consciousness facts beyond behavior (Dennett)
- Address the general problem of other minds rather than the specific problem of theory choice under substrate independence

None of them argue: "Substrate independence commits you to accepting that competing organizational theories of consciousness cannot be empirically distinguished."

The objection's most telling weakness is its failure to provide a single citation of someone making this specific argument. Saying "this is well-known" or "this has been extensively discussed" without showing where it has been discussed in this form is not sufficient to establish that the position is "already taken."

The thesis may draw on familiar elements (substrate independence, multiple realizability, limits of empirical access), but it combines them in a specific argument targeting a specific tension in contemporary consciousness science. The fact that related problems have been discussed does not mean this specific problem, framed in this specific way, has been adequately addressed.

The defence holds. The thesis identifies a genuine commitment that has not been clearly articulated in the literature in this form, even if it draws on familiar conceptual resources.

</details>

---

### O7. First-person methods cannot adjudicate between theories of consciousness because any systematic first-person methodology requires training subjects in a conceptual framework that already presupposes one theory over its rivals.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 4/5 · worst 4/5 · dead
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** Neurophenomenology and other approaches that treat first-person methods as a solution to testing consciousness theories
- **Sketch:** Consider phenomenological training methods like those used by Lutz and Thompson, or the introspective training in contemplative traditions. These methods claim to provide refined first-person data to test theories. However, training subjects to attend to 'the quality of awareness itself' versus 'the content of experience' versus 'the unified field' already builds in theoretical assumptions. A subject trained in Buddhist meditation frameworks learns to individuate experiences differently than one trained in Husserlian phenomenology. This creates a circularity: the refined first-person data that supposedly tests the theory is only generated through training that presupposes the theory's ontology. The key premise is that untrained introspection is too coarse-grained to discriminate between theories, but any training that refines introspection necessarily imports theoretical commitments that bias the results.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Theory-ladenness of phenomenological training is important but the circularity is predictable.
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. NCC research shows untrained reports can discriminate theories; defence absorbed by restricting 'first-person methods' to exclude simple reports
  - TRIVIAL — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The circularity reduces to ordinary theory-ladenness of observation; defence's bootstrapping claim concedes the point while overstating implications
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Thesis slides between methodological frameworks (weak, provable) and question-begging theoretical commitments (strong, needed for conclusion); defence conflates rather than resolves
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Key terms remain elastic enough to deflect counterexamples through post-hoc restrictions rather than genuine testing; NCC response exemplifies protective redefinition
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Rich phenomenological training methods that aim to reveal fine-grained structural features of consciousness face a distinctive epistemic challenge: such training necessarily involves methodological commitments that may shape which phenomenological structures subjects can identify, making it difficult to determine whether reported structures are discovered or artifacts of the training framework
- **Flaw:** The thesis systematically absorbs objections by narrowing scope and redefining terms rather than meeting them, while the core claim that first-person methods cannot adjudicate is falsified by simple perceptual reports in NCC research that successfully discriminate theories without theory-laden training

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. The Crick-Koch neural correlates of consciousness (NCC) research program provides a counterexample. When Crick and Koch asked subjects to report when they became aware of a stimulus in binocular rivalry experiments, subjects required no theoretical training whatsoever. Untrained subjects simply pressed a button when they saw the red grating versus the green grating. This yielded systematic first-person data (timing and content of conscious awareness) that discriminated between theories: it ruled out theories placing consciousness in early visual areas (since those areas responded to both gratings) and supported theories emphasizing later integration areas. The subjects needed no conceptual framework about "quality of awareness" or "unified fields"—just the ordinary ability to report what they see. Similarly, blindsight studies ask patients simple questions like "did you see anything?" without training them in phenomenological frameworks. These basic reports successfully adjudicate between theories (e.g., ruling out primary visual cortex as sufficient for consciousness). The weak point is the premise that "untrained introspection is too coarse-grained"—these cases show untrained reports can discriminate theories.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious challenge. The thesis claims *any* systematic first-person methodology requires theory-laden training, but basic perceptual reports in NCC research appear to provide theory-neutral first-person data that successfully discriminates between consciousness theories.

**DEFENDER** — ## BEST DEFENCE

The NCC cases are not counterexamples because they do not employ first-person *methods* in the relevant sense—they use first-person *reports* as dependent variables while the actual methodology remains third-person neuroimaging.

The thesis targets systematic first-person *methodologies*—disciplined approaches to investigating consciousness through refined introspective practice. Neurophenomenology, Husserlian phenomenology, and contemplative introspection are methods: they involve training subjects in techniques for attending to, individuating, and describing phenomenal features. The Crick-Koch program does something fundamentally different: it correlates neural activity with simple behavioral reports. The button press "I see red" is not a first-person method; it's a third-person behavioral measure that happens to track a first-person state.

The thesis's force lies precisely in distinguishing these. When researchers want first-person methods to do real adjudicatory work—to reveal the *structure* of experience, the relationship between awareness and content, the temporal grain of consciousness—simple reports like "I see red" are indeed too coarse-grained. They tell us *that* someone is conscious of red, not *how* consciousness is structured. The NCC program succeeds by avoiding first-person methodology entirely: it uses minimal reports plus neural data, letting the neural correlations do the theoretical work.

Binocular rivalry subjects need no training precisely because they're not practicing a first-person method. The moment researchers want subjects to report on *features of consciousness itself*—temporal flow, degrees of clarity, the awareness/content distinction—they must train them, and that training imports theoretical commitments. The thesis holds for actual first-person methodologies; NCC research sidesteps rather than refutes it.

## VERDICT

The defence fails, but not completely.

It successfully distinguishes NCC research from rich phenomenological methods, and this distinction has merit. However, it concedes too much to save the thesis. If simple perceptual reports can adjudicate *some* theoretical disputes about consciousness (like whether V1 activity is sufficient), then the thesis as stated is false. The thesis claims first-person methods "cannot adjudicate between theories"—not that they cannot adjudicate *all* disputes or reveal *all* structural features.

The defence tries to save the thesis by restricting "first-person methods" to exclude simple reports, but this is arbitrary hedging. A report about what one consciously sees *is* a first-person method—just a minimal one. The thesis must either fall or be honestly restricted to: "Rich first-person methodologies cannot adjudicate fine-grained structural questions about consciousness without theory-laden training." But that narrower claim loses much of its critical force.

The objection succeeds in showing the thesis overstates its case.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis appears to make a substantive discovery about the epistemic limits of first-person methods, but it actually reduces to a trivial logical point about training in general. The claim is simply this: if you train someone in a specific way of doing X, they will do X that specific way. Training subjects to individuate experiences according to Buddhist categories makes them individuate experiences according to Buddhist categories—this is just what training means, not a special problem for consciousness research.

The weak point is treating this as a distinctive "circularity" problem. Every domain where training refines discrimination faces this structure: training wine tasters in French terroir categories versus New World fruit-forward categories shapes how they parse flavors; training musicians in Western harmonic analysis versus Indian raga framework shapes how they hear music; training taxonomists in cladistic versus phenetic methods shapes how they classify organisms. We don't conclude wine-tasting "cannot adjudicate" between terroir theories or that musical training "cannot test" theories of harmony. The thesis dresses up the unremarkable fact that "training shapes perception and description" as though it reveals a fatal methodological flaw unique to consciousness studies. But it's just pointing out that methods have assumptions—a truism about all systematic inquiry, not a discovery about first-person methods specifically.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This significantly undermines the thesis. If the "circularity" is just the ordinary theory-ladenness present in all trained observation, the thesis identifies no special problem for first-person methods in consciousness research. It masquerades as revealing a unique epistemic barrier when it merely restates that training involves learning a framework.

**DEFENDER** — ## BEST DEFENCE

The wine-tasting analogy fails because consciousness research faces a unique bootstrapping problem that other domains escape: we cannot triangulate first-person training against an independent object.

Wine-tasting illustrates exactly why the consciousness case is *not* trivial. When French and New World tasters disagree, we can chemically analyze the wine—measure tannins, esters, acid profiles—and ask which descriptive framework better tracks objective properties of the liquid. When Western and Indian musicians parse a passage differently, we can perform acoustic analysis of the frequencies, examine the score, test listener responses across cultures. The training may shape perception, but there's an independent object that constrains which trained perceptions are veridical and which are artifacts of the framework.

Consciousness research lacks this. When a Buddhist-trained subject reports "bare awareness without self" and a Husserlian-trained subject reports "transcendental ego as condition of experience" while examining *the same conscious state*, we have no independent access to consciousness itself to determine which parsing is correct. We cannot "chemically analyze" the experience. The first-person method *is* our only access to the phenomenon. This creates genuine circularity: the data that would validate the training can only be generated through the training itself.

The thesis identifies a real methodological trap, not a truism. In wine-tasting, theory-laden training is epistemically innocent because we can check trained observations against the wine. In consciousness research, theory-laden training is epistemically vicious because we cannot check trained introspection against consciousness—we only have the introspection. The "distinctive circularity" is that first-person methods must simultaneously be our route to the phenomenon *and* our test of theories about it, with no independent criterion. That's not true of wine, music, or taxonomy.

## VERDICT

The defence fails.

The bootstrapping problem is real but does not save the thesis from triviality. The defence claims we can "check" wine-tasting against chemical analysis, but this just pushes the question back: *why* should we privilege chemical properties over trained phenomenal discrimination? The choice to treat chemistry as the "independent object" rather than treating expert tasting as revealing wine's real properties is itself a theoretical commitment. A sophisticated wine realist might argue that what matters about wine *just is* how it tastes to properly trained perceivers—the chemistry is merely the substrate.

More decisively, the defence concedes the game by accepting that the problem is about "which parsing is correct" when comparing Buddhist versus Husserlian reports. But many first-person methodologists would reject this framing entirely. They might argue: (1) both frameworks reveal real structural features that coexist, (2) the frameworks are addressing different questions rather than contradicting each other, or (3) convergence across frameworks on certain features (like the distinction between reflexive and pre-reflexive awareness) provides the needed constraint.

The objection's force stands: the thesis takes the unremarkable observation that "systematic methods involve theoretical commitments" and inflates it into "cannot adjudicate between theories." Even if consciousness lacks wine's chemical anchor, this shows at most that adjudication is *harder*, not that it "cannot" occur. The thesis remains essentially trivial—it points out that first-person methods have the same theory-ladenness as all observation, then overstates the implications.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "presupposes one theory over its rivals." On one reading, this means the training embeds commitments to specific theoretical claims (e.g., "consciousness requires a transcendental ego" vs. "consciousness involves no-self"). On this reading, the thesis is interesting and substantive—it would show that first-person methods beg the question against competing theories. But the argument cannot establish this strong reading. The evidence offered is merely that different training methods lead subjects to "individuate experiences differently" and "attend to" different features. This only shows that training involves methodological commitments (how to parse, what to attend to, what vocabulary to use), not theoretical commitments about what consciousness actually is.

On the weaker reading where "presupposes a theory" means merely "involves methodological choices about how to investigate," the argument works—Buddhist and Husserlian methods obviously differ in approach. But on this reading, the thesis becomes uninteresting: it reduces to "different investigative methods involve different methodological frameworks," which is trivial and doesn't show these methods "cannot adjudicate" anything.

The argument needs both readings: it needs the weak sense to establish that training "presupposes theory" (since it can only show methodological differences), but it needs the strong sense to generate the conclusion that adjudication is impossible (since only question-begging theoretical presupposition would prevent adjudication). The phrase "presupposes one theory over its rivals" slides between these meanings.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem. The thesis gains its critical force from suggesting first-person methods are question-begging, but the evidence only establishes that they involve methodological choices. These are different claims, and conflating them is genuine equivocation.

**DEFENDER** — ## BEST DEFENCE

The distinction between "methodological commitments" and "theoretical commitments" collapses in the case of consciousness research, making the alleged equivocation illusory.

In typical scientific domains, we can cleanly separate method from theory: choosing to use electron microscopy versus light microscopy is methodological (how to observe), while claiming "cells contain mitochondria" is theoretical (what exists). But consciousness presents a unique case where methodological choices *are* theoretical commitments because the method of investigation partly constitutes what counts as the phenomenon.

Consider the concrete example: training subjects to "attend to the quality of awareness itself" versus "attend to the content of experience" is not merely a neutral methodological choice about where to point one's introspective spotlight. These different attentional practices *generate different phenomenological ontologies*. When Buddhist-trained subjects learn to separate "awareness" from "contents" through specific meditative techniques, they don't merely adopt a vocabulary—they cultivate a phenomenological distinction that may not exist in untrained experience. The Husserlian-trained subject who learns to identify the "transcendental ego" through phenomenological reduction similarly generates a structural feature through the very practice of investigation.

This is not equivocation but recognition of a genuine peculiarity: in consciousness research, how you look *determines* what you find in a way that goes beyond mere theory-ladenness of observation. The methodological choice to individuate experiences in a certain way doesn't just *describe* a pre-existing structure differently—it *brings forth* a structure. Therefore, "methodological framework" and "theoretical ontology" are not two things that have been conflated, but genuinely the same thing in this domain.

The thesis holds on a single, coherent reading: first-person methods cannot adjudicate because the investigative practice itself shapes the phenomenological ontology, making it impossible to determine whether discovered structures are features of consciousness or artifacts of method.

## VERDICT

The defence fails.

The claim that method "constitutes" or "brings forth" phenomenology rather than merely observing it is itself a substantial theoretical commitment—and a controversial one. This is essentially an anti-realist or constructivist position about phenomenal structure. Many consciousness researchers would reject it, arguing that consciousness has determinate structural features independent of how we investigate it, and that different methods can converge on these features or demonstrably fail to track them.

More critically, the defence actually *strengthens* the equivocation charge rather than answering it. If we accept that methodological choices "generate" phenomenological ontologies rather than discover them, then the thesis is making an even stronger claim than initially apparent—not just that methods presuppose theories, but that methods *create* the phenomena they study. This is a far more radical claim than the thesis's language suggests, and the argument provided (about different training leading to different individuation) doesn't establish it.

The objector is right: the thesis slides between a weak claim (methods involve frameworks) that can be established and a strong claim (methods beg theoretical questions) that would make adjudication impossible. The defence tries to merge these by asserting that in consciousness research, frameworks *are* question-begging because they constitute the phenomenon. But this assertion is unsupported and itself theoretically loaded.

The equivocation stands. The thesis either makes a trivial claim about methodological frameworks or an unsubstantiated claim about methods creating their own phenomena.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ## ATTEMPT
