The thesis is structured to be immune from counterexamples through systematic redefinition of what counts as a "first-person method." 

When presented with simple perceptual reports in NCC research that successfully discriminate theories without theory-laden training, the thesis retreats: "those aren't really first-person *methods*, just first-person *reports*." When pressed on what distinguishes a method from a report, the answer becomes circular: methods are the ones that require theory-laden training (the very thing the thesis claims to have discovered about them).

Consider any potential counterexample: contemplative practitioners from different traditions converging on similar phenomenological structures; subjects trained in one framework successfully reporting phenomena predicted by a rival framework; or development of training protocols that explicitly bracket theoretical commitments. Each can be dismissed by redefining terms: "that's not *real* convergence, just superficial similarity in vocabulary," "they weren't *really* trained in the first framework if they can report the other," or "bracketing is itself a theoretical commitment from Husserlian phenomenology."

The weak point is the lack of specified criteria for what would count as a first-person method that *doesn't* presuppose theory. Without such criteria, every case of successful adjudication gets reclassified as "not a real first-person method," while every case of theory-laden training confirms the thesis. The thesis becomes unfalsifiable: heads I win (theory-laden training proves my point), tails you lose (successful adjudication means it wasn't really a first-person method after all).

## STRENGTH: 4/5

This is a serious problem. The thesis's core terms ("first-person method," "presupposes theory," "adjudicate") remain sufficiently elastic that apparent counterexamples can be deflected through redefinition rather than genuine engagement. The defence against the NCC objection exemplifies this: rather than accepting it as a counterexample or explaining why those particular features make it irrelevant, it simply reclassifies NCC research as not involving "real" first-person methods. This protective maneuver suggests the thesis is structured to be immune from empirical challenge.

**DEFENDER** — ## BEST DEFENCE

The thesis provides clear, principled criteria that distinguish genuine counterexamples from non-cases—the objection mistakes theoretical precision for unfalsifiability.

The distinction between "first-person methods" and "mere reports" is not ad hoc but tracks a real methodological divide in consciousness research. First-person methods are *systematic investigative practices* for refining introspective access—phenomenological reduction, meditative training, introspective protocols that teach subjects to attend to previously unnoticed features. Simple perceptual reports are *not* investigative practices; they're behavioral responses that track conscious states without investigating consciousness's structure. This distinction is independently motivated and widely recognized in the field (see Varela's neurophenomenology vs. standard cognitive neuroscience).

The thesis makes a specific, falsifiable empirical prediction: whenever researchers develop training protocols that successfully refine introspective discrimination beyond naive reports, those protocols will embed theoretical commitments that bias results toward the training framework's ontology. This is testable. A genuine counterexample would be: a refined first-person method that (1) trains subjects beyond naive reporting capacity, (2) enables discrimination between competing theories, yet (3) demonstrably does not embed commitments favoring one theory.

The objection's hypothetical cases fail these criteria for identifiable reasons. "Convergence across traditions" could be genuine convergence on theory-neutral structures (a real counterexample) or superficial verbal agreement masking different phenomenological ontologies—we'd need to examine specific cases. "Subjects trained in framework A reporting framework B's phenomena" might show either that A's training is theory-neutral (counterexample) or that frameworks partially overlap (no counterexample). These aren't dismissals through redefinition; they're applications of clear criteria.

The thesis is falsifiable: show me a refined introspective training protocol that discriminates theories while remaining demonstrably neutral between them. The NCC case doesn't qualify because button-pressing "I see red" isn't refined introspection—it's the *starting point* the thesis says is too coarse-grained. That's not moving goalposts; it's maintaining the original target.

## VERDICT

The defence fails, though it identifies real distinctions.

The problem is that the "clear criteria" offered still leave too much interpretive flexibility at crucial junctures. Consider criterion (3): "demonstrably does not embed commitments favoring one theory." What would demonstrate this? The thesis's logic suggests that *any* systematic training in how to attend, parse, or describe experience involves choices that could be characterized as "embedding commitments." 

The defence says convergence across traditions "could be" genuine or superficial—but provides no criteria for determining which. It says subjects reporting across frameworks might show neutrality "or" overlap—but no way to distinguish these. These aren't applications of clear criteria; they're acknowledgments that the criteria remain unclear at precisely the points where counterexamples would bite.

Most tellingly, the defence's own example reveals the problem: it claims button-pressing isn't "refined introspection" but rather "the starting point the thesis says is too coarse-grained." But the thesis *doesn't* initially say this—it says "untrained introspection is too coarse-grained to discriminate between theories." The NCC case shows this is false: untrained reports *do* discriminate some theories. The defence responds by restricting "discriminate between theories" to mean something like "reveal fine-grained structural features"—but this restriction isn't in the original thesis, it's added to protect against counterexamples.

The objection succeeds. The thesis's key terms have enough elasticity that apparent counterexamples trigger clarifications and restrictions rather than genuine testing. A truly falsifiable thesis would specify *in advance* what counts as refined enough to be a "method," what counts as successfully "adjudicating," and what counts as "presupposing theory"—not discover these boundaries through defensive maneuvers against objections.

</details>

---

### O8. The only consciousness theories we can test are those that predict consciousness comes apart from what we pre-theoretically think is conscious, making our most confident intuitions (that normal humans are conscious) empirically useless.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 4/5 · worst 4/5 · dead
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** The widespread assumption that we can use both intuitive judgments about clear cases AND empirical testability as joint constraints on theories
- **Sketch:** Testing requires the possibility of disconfirmation. To test whether theory T correctly identifies what's conscious, we need cases where T and our pre-theoretical judgments diverge—otherwise T merely systematizes what we already believe and isn't genuinely tested. The key premise: the only testable predictions are surprising ones (insects are conscious per IIT but not per GWT; split-brain patients have two consciousnesses per some views but one per others). But this creates instability: if a theory says normal adult humans aren't conscious, we reject it as absurd; yet if it only confirms our existing judgments about humans, we haven't tested it. Therefore, the testable theories are the ones we reject on intuitive grounds, while the intuitively acceptable theories are empirically unfalsifiable. Consciousness theory is caught between triviality and incredibility.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The triviality-incredibility dilemma is real but this formulation doesn't add much to existing discussions of theory testing.
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 2/5, **UNANSWERED**. The precision objection has force but the defender absorbed it by conceding mechanistic testing while claiming this doesn't test 'what consciousness is'
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The natural kind parallel seriously damages the thesis; defender tried to distinguish consciousness as response-dependent but the verdict shows this distinction fails
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. GWT demonstrates theories can preserve paradigm intuitions while making testable mechanistic predictions; defender's 'only tests mechanisms not consciousness-itself' is question-begging absorption
  - TRIVIAL — landed 3/5, **UNANSWERED**. The thesis does overstate a methodological truism; defender's attempt to show consciousness is distinctive fails per the verdict
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Consciousness theories face a distinctive epistemic challenge: we cannot test theories that radically revise our paradigm-case judgments (that normal humans are conscious) because such revisions would undermine the anchor points needed for empirical investigation, even though theories preserving those judgments can still be tested through mechanistic predictions
- **Flaw:** The thesis creates a false dichotomy by defining 'genuine testing' so narrowly that it excludes paradigmatic cases of successful theory-testing through mechanistic predictions and convergent evidence at boundaries

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT. This is a version of the "theory-ladenness of observation" problem applied to consciousness, specifically echoing themes from Kuhn and Feyerabend about how paradigms determine what counts as evidence. More directly, it resembles Dennett's heterophenomenology concern that first-person reports cannot simultaneously serve as data and as what-needs-explaining. The weak premise is the claim that "only surprising predictions are testable." This ignores that theories can be tested through *precision* rather than surprise—a theory that predicts humans are conscious can still be tested by predicting *which aspects* of human neural activity correlate with *which features* of consciousness (access vs. phenomenal, temporal binding windows, reportability thresholds). Newton's theory wasn't untestable merely because it confirmed that apples fall down; it made precise quantitative predictions about trajectories. Similarly, a consciousness theory can agree with intuition that humans are conscious while making novel, testable predictions about the *mechanisms, correlates, and fine-grained structure* of that consciousness. The thesis conflates "confirms our broad category judgments" with "makes no testable predictions whatsoever."

STRENGTH: 2/5 — The thesis identifies a real tension but overstates it. Theories can be genuinely tested through precision and mechanism even when they agree with our basic ontology.

**DEFENDER** — ## BEST DEFENCE

The precision reply misses the deeper instability. Yes, theories can make precise mechanistic predictions about *how* consciousness works in humans—temporal binding windows, reportability thresholds, specific neural correlates. But this concedes the game. Those predictions don't test *whether the theory correctly identifies what consciousness is*. They test whether the theory correctly describes certain mechanisms in systems we've already assumed are conscious.

Consider the parallel: Newton's theory wasn't tested by apples falling down, but by *precise trajectory predictions*. Crucially, though, we had independent access to trajectories—we could measure them without presupposing Newton's theory. But for consciousness, we have no such independent access. The "trajectories" we're measuring (reportability thresholds, neural correlates) only count as consciousness-trajectories if we've already decided these systems are conscious.

The precision response actually reveals the problem. When IIT predicts insects are conscious or that cerebellums lack consciousness despite their computational complexity, we face a choice: (a) treat these as surprising empirical predictions that test the theory, or (b) reject them as reductios because they violate our confident intuitions. If we do (b)—as most philosophers do with the cerebellum case—we're using intuition to trump the theory's predictions. If we do (a), we're admitting that our most confident intuitions (about what is and isn't conscious) don't constrain theory choice.

The mechanistic predictions don't escape this bind. They presuppose we've already correctly identified the conscious systems. But *that identification* is what needs testing, and we can only test it where theory and intuition diverge—precisely where we're most tempted to reject the theory as absurd.

## VERDICT

The defence fails, but not completely.

It fails because the precision objection does genuinely break the instability for theories operating within the same ontological scope. If two theories both agree humans are conscious but disagree about mechanisms, they can be empirically distinguished through precise predictions about timing, reportability, and neural correlates. This is real testing, not mere systematization.

However, the defence points to a residual problem the objection doesn't fully dissolve: we still cannot empirically test *whether we've correctly identified the boundaries of consciousness itself*. The mechanistic predictions only work once we've fixed the extension of "conscious systems," but fixing that extension is itself not empirically testable when theory and intuition align. The instability re-emerges at the meta-level: theories that expand or contract the boundaries (insects, cerebellums, split-brains) are empirically testable but intuitively rejectable, while theories that preserve our intuitive boundaries make testable mechanistic predictions but cannot test whether those boundaries are correct.

The thesis overstates the problem but captures something real: empirical testability and intuitive constraint come apart specifically at the boundaries of consciousness, even if they can work together within those boundaries.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. If this argument worked, it would equally show that we cannot test theories about anything whose instances we identify through pre-theoretical judgment. Consider pain: we pre-theoretically judge that humans in certain conditions feel pain. A pain theory that merely confirmed these judgments would be "untestable" by this argument, while theories with surprising predictions (plants feel pain, or humans in clear agony don't) would be "testable but intuitively rejectable." Yet we clearly *can* test pain theories—through convergent evidence about mechanisms, behavioral correlates, and pharmaceutical interventions that track our pre-theoretical categories while refining them. 

More devastatingly: the argument would prove we cannot test theories of *any natural kind* where we start with paradigm cases. We pre-theoretically identify water, gold, mammals. Theories that preserve these identifications aren't thereby untestable—they're tested through mechanistic predictions, as the KILLER notes. Theories that radically revise them (XYZ is water, whales aren't mammals) face intuitive resistance, but this doesn't make the conservative theories unfalsifiable. The weak premise is that "agreement with pre-theoretical judgment in paradigm cases" entails "empirically useless." This conflates having fixed anchor points with having no testable content. Every successful scientific theory uses paradigm cases as anchors while making testable predictions about mechanisms, boundaries, and non-paradigm cases.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This seriously damages the thesis. The argument, if sound, would incorrectly render all natural kind theories with paradigm cases either trivial or incredible, which is absurd.

**DEFENDER** — ## BEST DEFENCE

The pain and natural kind cases actually *support* rather than undermine the thesis—they show the problem is general, not that it's spurious.

Start with pain. The objection claims we "clearly can" test pain theories through convergent evidence. But look what happens: When we test whether C-fiber firing correlates with pain, we identify pain through... self-reports and behavior in paradigm cases (humans saying "that hurts"). When theories make surprising predictions—that fish feel pain, or that people under certain anesthetics don't despite appearing to—we face exactly the instability the thesis describes. We either reject the surprising prediction as absurd (fish can't *really* feel pain, they just respond mechanically) or we accept it and thereby admit our paradigm-case judgments weren't constraining the theory after all. The "pharmaceutical interventions" only work as tests if we've already correctly identified what pain is, which is precisely what's in question.

The natural kind comparison actually strengthens the case. Water and gold are *disanalogous* because we have independent physical access to them. We can gather samples, measure properties, and test compositional claims without first settling whether something is "really" water. The H₂O discovery didn't depend on prior certainty about what water is—we could test whether this clear liquid on Earth and that clear liquid on Twin Earth have the same composition.

But consciousness (like pain) has no such independent access route. We cannot gather "samples" of consciousness and test their properties except by first judging what is conscious. The whale/mammal case proves the point: we *could* test whether whales are mammals because we had independent criteria (morphological, reproductive, genetic) that didn't presuppose the answer. We measured whale physiology and found it matched mammalian patterns. But for consciousness, the "physiology" we measure (neural correlates, reportability) only counts as consciousness-physiology if we've already decided these are conscious systems.

The objection says theories use "paradigm cases as anchors while making testable predictions about mechanisms, boundaries, and non-paradigm cases." Exactly right—but this reveals the instability. The *boundaries* are where testing happens (is the fetus conscious? the insect? the cerebellum?), but these are precisely where intuition and theory diverge, forcing the choice between empirical prediction and intuitive constraint. The *mechanisms* can be tested only within boundaries we've fixed by intuition, not by prior empirical testing.

The "proves too much" charge backfires: if the argument shows that pain, consciousness, and other phenomenal states face a distinctive epistemic problem that water and gold don't, that's not a reductio—it's a discovery about the structure of phenomenal concepts. They're response-dependent in a way that natural kind terms for publicly accessible substances aren't.

## VERDICT

The defence fails. The disanalogy it draws between consciousness and natural kinds is not sustainable.

The critical error is the claim that we have "independent physical access" to water and gold but not to consciousness. This is false. We had independent access to *the stuff people called water*—we could point to it, collect it, observe its behavior—before knowing its composition. Similarly, we have independent access to *the systems people judge conscious*—we can point to them, measure their neural activity, observe their behavior—before knowing what consciousness is. The access isn't to consciousness-as-such any more than pre-H₂O access was to water-as-such; it's to the phenomena we're theorizing about.

More fatally: the defence's own example of boundaries (fetus, insect, cerebellum) actually defeats the thesis. These *are* cases where theory and intuition diverge, and they *are* testable—but not in the way the thesis claims makes theories "intuitively rejectable." When IIT predicts cerebellums lack consciousness despite computational complexity, we can test this through: (1) lesion studies showing cerebellar damage doesn't affect reportable experience the way cortical damage does, (2) stimulation studies showing cerebellar activation doesn't produce qualia reports, (3) architectural analysis showing cerebellums lack the feedback structures other theories predict consciousness requires.

These tests don't require already knowing whether cerebellums are conscious. They test *convergent predictions* from the theory. If multiple independent lines of evidence (lesion, stimulation, architecture, comparison with paradigm cases) align, the theory is confirmed even at the boundaries. This is exactly how natural kind theories work: whales were classified as mammals not because we intuited it, but because multiple independent anatomical and physiological criteria converged.

The thesis mistakes "uses paradigm cases as anchors" for "cannot test boundary cases." But paradigm cases constrain without determining—they rule out theories that deny humans are conscious while leaving open substantial empirical questions about mechanisms and boundaries. The instability is illusory: theories can be both intuitively acceptable (about paradigms) and empirically testable (about mechanisms and boundaries) simultaneously.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis claims testable consciousness theories must diverge from pre-theoretical judgments, making our confident intuitions empirically useless. But consider Global Workspace Theory (GWT) as a counterexample. GWT agrees with pre-theoretical judgment that normal adult humans are conscious and that (say) thermostats are not—preserving our most confident intuitions. Yet GWT makes genuinely testable predictions that don't merely systematize existing beliefs: it predicts that (1) conscious contents must be globally broadcast to multiple brain systems, not just locally processed, (2) masking paradigms will prevent consciousness when they disrupt broadcast even if early processing is intact, (3) prefrontal patients with broadcast disruption will show specific reportability deficits, (4) binocular rivalry resolution requires global ignition measurable in P3 waves. These predictions have been empirically tested and could have been falsified—Dehaene's lab has run hundreds of experiments distinguishing GWT from local-processing theories. The weak premise is that preserving paradigm-case intuitions makes theories "empirically useless." GWT preserves the intuition that humans are conscious while making falsifiable predictions about *when* and *how* consciousness occurs in humans, and these predictions distinguish it from rival theories (like higher-order theories or recurrent processing theories) that agree about the paradigm cases but differ on mechanisms. Our confident intuition that humans are conscious remains empirically useful as an anchor point while the theory generates testable content about consciousness's signature.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem. GWT appears to be both intuitively acceptable about paradigm cases and genuinely testable through mechanistic predictions that go beyond mere systematization of existing beliefs.

**DEFENDER** — ## BEST DEFENCE

The GWT example actually demonstrates the instability rather than refuting it. Look carefully at what's doing the epistemic work.

GWT's "testable predictions" about global broadcast, masking paradigms, P3 waves, and prefrontal deficits are only tests of consciousness *if we've independently established that these manipulations affect consciousness*. But how do we establish that? Through the very pre-theoretical judgments the objection claims we've transcended.

Take the masking paradigm: We "test" whether disrupted broadcast prevents consciousness by asking subjects whether they saw the stimulus. When they report not seeing it, we conclude consciousness was prevented. But this just *is* our pre-theoretical judgment about when humans are conscious—when they can report experience. The "test" presupposes that reportability tracks consciousness in the paradigm case, which is precisely the intuitive anchor point, not an empirical discovery.

The same structure appears in all the objection's examples. Prefrontal patients show "reportability deficits"—but we only count these as consciousness deficits because we pre-theoretically judge that reportability indicates consciousness. P3 waves "measure global ignition"—but we only treat P3 as a consciousness signature because it correlates with conditions where subjects report being conscious. We're not testing whether GWT correctly identifies consciousness; we're testing whether GWT correctly predicts the neural correlates of *what we've already judged to be consciousness* through pre-theoretical means.

Here's the crucial point: The objection says GWT makes "falsifiable predictions about *when* and *how* consciousness occurs in humans." But "when" and "how" are parasitic on an independent judgment of "whether." We determine *when* consciousness occurs (during global broadcast vs. local processing) by checking when subjects report experiences—a pre-theoretical judgment. The theory doesn't predict "consciousness occurs here"; it predicts "the neural signature occurs here," and we only interpret that signature as consciousness because of our prior intuitive judgments about the paradigm cases.

The objection claims GWT is distinguished from "rival theories (like higher-order theories or recurrent processing theories) that agree about the paradigm cases but differ on mechanisms." Exactly—they *agree about paradigm cases*. This means all these theories are equally anchored to pre-theoretical judgment. The "testing" that distinguishes them is testing which mechanism best predicts the neural correlates of states we've *already identified as conscious* through intuition. That's not testing whether the theory correctly identifies consciousness; it's testing which mechanism underlies consciousness-as-we've-already-identified-it.

The real test would be: Does GWT predict something is conscious that we're confident isn't, or vice versa? When it does—say, if GWT implied that global broadcast in a thermostat would be conscious, or that local processing in humans couldn't be—we face the instability. Either we reject GWT as absurd (privileging intuition over theory), or we accept the surprising prediction (admitting intuition doesn't constrain the theory). The objection's examples carefully avoid this by staying within the paradigm cases where theory and intuition align.

## VERDICT

The defence fails, and the GWT counterexample seriously damages the thesis.

The defence makes a sophisticated move—arguing that mechanistic predictions only count as consciousness-tests if we've already identified consciousness through pre-theoretical judgment—but this move proves too much and mischaracterizes how the testing actually works.

First, the defence conflates two different roles for pre-theoretical judgment: (1) identifying paradigm cases as anchors, and (2) determining all test outcomes. GWT uses pre-theoretical judgment for (1) but not (2). Yes, we use reports to initially correlate global broadcast with consciousness in paradigm cases. But then GWT makes *novel predictions* that could dissociate from those initial judgments. For instance, it predicts that subliminal priming (which involves substantial neural processing) should not produce consciousness because it lacks broadcast—and this can be tested through forced-choice discrimination, confidence ratings, and neural measures that go beyond simple "did you see it?" reports. If subjects showed perfect discrimination with zero confidence and no P3, that would challenge GWT's architecture, not just confirm our intuitions.

Second, the defence claims we determine "when" consciousness occurs by checking reports, making this parasitic on intuition. But this misses how mechanistic theories generate testable predictions through *convergent constraints*. GWT predicts that consciousness requires broadcast, which implies: (a) disrupting broadcast should eliminate consciousness, (b) restoring broadcast should restore it, (c) the temporal dynamics of broadcast should match the temporal dynamics of conscious access, (d) brain regions necessary for broadcast should be necessary for consciousness, (e) conditions that preserve broadcast should preserve consciousness even when other features change. These predictions can come apart from simple reportability and can be tested against each other. When Dehaene found that masked stimuli show early visual processing but no P3/global ignition, and subjects report no awareness, this is *convergent evidence*, not circular confirmation.

Most critically, the defence's own framing defeats the thesis. It admits that theories "agree about paradigm cases but differ on mechanisms," and that we can test which mechanism is correct. But if we can empirically determine which mechanism underlies consciousness, then our paradigm-case intuitions are *not* "empirically useless"—they're serving as anchors that enable empirical investigation of mechanisms. The thesis claimed these intuitions are useless; the defence shows they're essential anchoring points that make empirical testing possible. That's not instability; that's how theory-testing works.

The thesis overstates a real epistemic limitation (we cannot test theories that completely abandon paradigm cases) into a false dilemma (theories are either trivial or incredible). GWT shows the middle ground: theories can preserve paradigm intuitions while making substantive, testable mechanistic predictions that distinguish them from rivals and could be falsified by empirical evidence.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis presents itself as revealing an instability, but it actually just restates the uncontroversial methodological principle that theories must make predictions beyond their training data to be testable. This is trivially true across all sciences and isn't a discovery about consciousness specifically.

The weak premise is the framing that this creates a problematic "instability" rather than describing normal scientific constraint. Every theory faces the same structure: it must fit paradigm cases (Newton's theory must predict apples fall, atomic theory must explain paradigmatic chemical reactions, evolutionary theory must explain obvious adaptations) while also making novel predictions that could fail. The thesis treats "theories must go beyond systematizing what we already believe" as if it's a problematic tension specific to consciousness, when it's simply the definition of empirical testability.

The alleged instability—"testable theories are ones we reject on intuitive grounds, while intuitively acceptable theories are empirically unfalsifiable"—is a false dichotomy dressed as insight. Theories like GWT are both intuitively acceptable (humans are conscious) AND testable (specific mechanistic predictions). The thesis verbally stipulates that such theories don't count as "genuinely tested" because they preserve paradigm cases, but this just defines away counterexamples rather than identifying a real problem.

What's presented as philosophical discovery is actually just: "To test a theory, it must make predictions we don't already know are true." That's not an instability in consciousness science; it's a tautology about what testing means.

# STRENGTH: 2/5

The attack has some force because the thesis does overstate a methodological truism, but it doesn't fully land. The thesis isn't merely saying "theories must make novel predictions"—it's claiming something more specific: that in consciousness science, the cases where theories make genuinely novel predictions (boundaries like insects, cerebellums) are systematically where we're tempted to reject theories on intuitive grounds, creating a distinctive tension. That's not quite trivial, even if overstated.

**DEFENDER** — ## BEST DEFENCE

The triviality charge misunderstands the specificity of the problem. Yes, all theories must make predictions beyond their training data—but consciousness has a distinctive feature that makes this requirement unstable in a way it isn't for apples or chemical reactions.

The difference: For apples, chemical reactions, and adaptations, we have multiple independent empirical constraints. Newton's theory must predict apples fall (paradigm case) AND predict planetary orbits AND predict pendulum periods AND predict projectile trajectories. These are all empirically accessible, and they can conflict—a theory might fit apples but fail on planets. The paradigm cases don't exhaust our empirical access.

But for consciousness, the paradigm cases (normal humans) essentially exhaust our confident empirical access. We have no independent empirical constraint analogous to planetary orbits. The only way to test beyond the paradigm is to make predictions about boundary cases (insects, fetuses, cerebellums, split-brains)—and these are precisely where empirical evidence is weakest and intuitive judgment is most contested.

This creates the instability. In physics, "going beyond paradigm cases" means making predictions about different empirical domains where we have independent measurement access. In consciousness science, "going beyond paradigm cases" means making predictions about cases where we have no independent access—where the theory itself must tell us what counts as consciousness, but we have no way to check except through intuition, which is exactly what we're trying to transcend.

The GWT example actually demonstrates this. The objection claimed GWT makes testable mechanistic predictions. But notice: all those predictions are about *normal human consciousness*—the paradigm case. Global broadcast in humans, masking in humans, P3 waves in humans, prefrontal deficits in humans. GWT becomes "testable" by making fine-grained predictions within the paradigm case, not by genuinely extending beyond it. When it does extend beyond (what about global broadcast in insects? in cerebellums? in split brains?), we face exactly the instability the thesis describes.

The triviality objection says every theory must fit paradigm cases while making novel predictions. True—but for consciousness, the novel predictions are either (a) fine-grained mechanistic details within paradigm cases, which don't test whether we've correctly identified consciousness, or (b) boundary extensions, which we're systematically tempted to reject on intuitive grounds. That's not a tautology; it's a structural feature of theorizing about a phenomenon where first-person access is asymmetrically distributed.

Here's the sharp version: In normal science, paradigm cases are a *subset* of our empirical access. In consciousness science, paradigm cases are *coextensive* with our confident empirical access. That's why the requirement to go beyond them creates instability rather than just normal scientific constraint.

## VERDICT

The defence fails. The distinction it draws between consciousness and other domains is not sustainable, and the thesis does reduce to a dressed-up triviality.

The defence claims that for consciousness, "paradigm cases essentially exhaust our confident empirical access," while for physics we have "multiple independent empirical constraints." But this is false. We have extensive empirical access to non-paradigm cases of consciousness through the same methods we use for paradigm cases: behavioral reports, neural correlates, functional capacities, and converging evidence.

Consider the parallel more carefully. For apples falling, our "confident empirical access" is just watching objects drop. Newton's theory makes predictions about planetary orbits—cases where our empirical access is actually *more uncertain and theory-laden* than for falling apples. We couldn't directly observe planetary masses or forces; we inferred them from positional measurements interpreted through theoretical frameworks. Yet this didn't create an "instability." The theory made convergent predictions that could be checked against multiple lines of evidence.

The same structure applies to consciousness. For insects, we have: behavioral flexibility, learning capacity, nociceptive responses, neural architecture, pharmacological effects, evolutionary considerations. These are independent lines of empirical evidence, just as planetary positions and pendulum periods are independent lines. The defence claims these boundary cases are "where empirical evidence is weakest and intuitive judgment is most contested"—but contested intuitive judgment is not the same as lacking empirical evidence. The contest exists precisely *because* we have empirical evidence that pulls against some intuitions.

More fatally, the defence's own move defeats the thesis. It concedes that GWT "becomes testable by making fine-grained predictions within the paradigm case." But then it claims these predictions "don't test whether we've correctly identified consciousness." This is the trivializing move. If making fine-grained mechanistic predictions that distinguish between rival theories doesn't count as "testing," then the thesis has defined "genuine testing" so narrowly that it excludes paradigmatic cases of successful scientific testing.

The defence says: "In consciousness science, paradigm cases are coextensive with our confident empirical access." But this just restates the complaint that we're most confident about paradigm cases—which is true of literally every domain. We're most confident that apples fall down, that water is wet, that humans are mammals. Theories must venture beyond maximum confidence to be testable. That's not a distinctive instability of consciousness science; it's what testing means.

The thesis presents a false dichotomy: either theories stay within paradigm cases (and are "unfalsifiable") or they extend to boundary cases (and are "intuitively rejectable"). But this ignores that theories can be tested through: (1) mechanistic predictions within paradigm cases that distinguish rivals, (2) convergent evidence at boundary cases that overcomes initial intuitive resistance, and (3) systematic connections between paradigm and boundary cases that make the extensions non-arbitrary. All of these are exemplified in actual consciousness science.

The core insight—that consciousness science faces epistemic challenges from asymmetric access—is real but not new. The thesis inflates this into a false instability by treating "must go beyond what we're most confident about" as if it were a problematic tension rather than the definition of empirical testing. That's trivial.

</details>

### O9. Testing consciousness theories is impossible not because consciousness is private, but because all existing theories make their predictions contingent on stipulations about what counts as the 'relevant' physical substrate.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 4/5 · worst 4/5 · dead
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** Both eliminativists who think consciousness talk is hopeless AND researchers who think we just need better experiments
- **Sketch:** The argument observes that IIT, GWT, HOT, and other theories appear to make testable predictions, but each contains a flexible parameter about which physical processes count as implementing their functional/structural requirements. When faced with anomalous data (like split-brain patients, or cerebellar patients with intact consciousness despite massive neuron loss), theories don't get falsified—defenders adjust which substrate was 'really' implementing the theory's requirements. The key premise is that this flexibility is not an accidental feature of current theories but a necessary feature of any theory trying to map consciousness onto physical systems, because the mapping relation itself requires stipulation. Therefore, the testing problem isn't about consciousness being subjective, it's about the substrate-specification problem being unfalsifiable.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Substrate-specification flexibility is a real problem but the observation is fairly straightforward.
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence claimed a 'constitutive' difference from Duhem-Quine but failed to establish it; consciousness theories face familiar underdetermination, not a unique structural problem
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence's appeal to 'criterion-independent behavioral signatures' collapsed when shown that memory criteria are equally theory-laden and consciousness has constraining signatures too
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 3/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence showed IIT adjusts specifications but thereby revealed theories face standard holistic testing, not unique unfalsifiability; accumulating evidence does constrain even flexible theories
  - TRIVIAL — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence's distinction between privacy and substrate-specification collapsed; the latter exists precisely because consciousness lacks objective criteria due to its subjective constitution
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Testing consciousness theories faces distinctive difficulties because consciousness lacks criterion-independent behavioral signatures in ways that cognitive functions like memory do not, making theory-testing more holistic and incremental than in other domains of cognitive neuroscience—though not impossible or uniquely unfalsifiable
- **Flaw:** The thesis redescribes the traditional privacy/subjectivity problem in theory-testing vocabulary without establishing that substrate-specification is a distinct or novel issue; the flexibility it identifies is either standard Duhem-Quine underdetermination (not unique to consciousness) or directly traceable to consciousness lacking objective verification criteria (the privacy problem itself)

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT. This position is essentially a specification of the Duhem-Quine thesis applied to consciousness science. Duhem (1906) and Quine (1951) established that empirical tests never target individual hypotheses in isolation, but always test conjunctions of hypotheses plus auxiliary assumptions. The candidate's "substrate-specification problem" is precisely such an auxiliary assumption—the claim that physical processes P implement functional requirements F is an auxiliary hypothesis that can always be adjusted to preserve the core theory when anomalies arise. Lakatos (1970) further developed this into the concept of a "protective belt" of auxiliary hypotheses surrounding theoretical cores, showing that theories are research programs with adjustable peripheries rather than directly falsifiable units. The candidate's observation about IIT, GWT, and HOT theories adjusting "which substrate was 'really' implementing" their requirements is exactly what Lakatos called a protective belt maneuver. The weak point in claiming novelty is that the candidate presents as a discovery about consciousness theories what is actually a well-known feature of theory testing in general—the underdetermination of theory by evidence. The candidate hasn't shown why consciousness theories face this problem more acutely than, say, theories in physics or biology.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This substantially undermines the thesis's claim to novelty. The observation is real but it's a known epistemological problem, not a distinctive reframing of the consciousness testing problem.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies the level at which the thesis operates. Yes, Duhem-Quine establishes that all theories face underdetermination by auxiliary assumptions. But the thesis claims something stronger and more specific: that consciousness theories face a *constitutive* substrate-specification problem that is not merely one auxiliary assumption among others, but is *necessarily unfalsifiable* in a way that makes the entire enterprise structurally different from ordinary science.

Consider the contrast. In physics, when we test quantum mechanics, auxiliary assumptions about experimental apparatus are in principle independently checkable. We can verify whether our detector actually detects photons by using other theoretical frameworks. The protective belt is adjustable but not infinitely so—there are external constraints from other domains of inquiry.

But consciousness theories face a unique problem: the mapping between physical processes and conscious states cannot be independently verified because consciousness is the only phenomenon where the explanandum (subjective experience) is *by definition* not accessible through the same evidential channels as the explanans (physical processes). When IIT claims that certain integrated information structures are conscious, there is no independent way to verify the mapping claim except by appeal to intuitions about what "seems" conscious—which is precisely what's in dispute.

The thesis identifies this as a structural feature: any theory mapping consciousness to physical substrates must stipulate the mapping relation, and this stipulation cannot be tested without already assuming an answer to the hard problem. This isn't just Duhem-Quine underdetermination—it's a closed epistemic loop specific to consciousness.

VERDICT

The defence fails. The purported distinction between "ordinary" Duhem-Quine underdetermination and a "constitutive" substrate-specification problem collapses under examination.

First, the claim that auxiliary assumptions in physics are "independently checkable" while consciousness mappings are not overstates the difference. When testing string theory's predictions, the auxiliary assumptions about what counts as the relevant energy scale or compactification scheme face similar issues—we cannot independently verify these mappings without already working within the theoretical framework. The "external constraints from other domains" exist for consciousness too: neuroscience, cognitive psychology, and behavioral data all provide constraints on which substrate specifications are viable.

Second, the alleged "closed epistemic loop" is not unique to consciousness. The same structure appears in any theory positing unobservables: we cannot verify that quarks exist independently of the theoretical framework that defines them. The thesis hasn't shown that consciousness theories face a *different kind* of problem, only a familiar problem in a domain where our intuitions make it feel more acute.

The objection stands: this is Duhem-Quine applied to consciousness science, not a novel reframing.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. If the argument works, it would equally show that all functional theories in cognitive science and neuroscience are untestable. Consider memory theories: when testing whether the hippocampus implements episodic memory, researchers face exactly the same substrate-specification problem. If patient H.M. shows intact procedural memory despite hippocampal damage, defenders can adjust which neural substrate "really" implements episodic memory functions (perhaps perirhinal cortex, perhaps distributed networks). When testing whether Broca's area implements syntactic processing, anomalous cases of intact syntax despite Broca's damage lead to respecification of the relevant substrate. The candidate's logic—that theories making predictions "contingent on stipulations about what counts as the 'relevant' physical substrate" are untestable—would render untestable virtually every claim in cognitive neuroscience about structure-function mappings. Yet we clearly do make progress testing such claims; we distinguish better from worse theories of memory, language, attention, etc. The weak point is the premise that substrate-specification flexibility makes theories untestable. This flexibility exists throughout neuroscience but doesn't prevent genuine empirical progress and theory discrimination. The candidate needs to show why consciousness is special, but the argument's logic proves too much—it would eliminate most of cognitive neuroscience as untestable.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This seriously damages the thesis. The argument, if sound, has implications far beyond consciousness that seem clearly false. Cognitive neuroscience does make testable progress despite substrate-specification flexibility. The candidate must either accept this devastating scope expansion or identify a principled difference that hasn't been established.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection fails to recognize that memory, language, and attention differ from consciousness in a decisive way: they have **criterion-independent behavioral signatures** that constrain substrate specification in ways consciousness cannot.

When we test hippocampal theories of episodic memory, we have independent behavioral criteria: can the subject recall specific past episodes? These criteria aren't theory-laden stipulations about substrate—they're operationalizable performance measures. When H.M. shows intact procedural but not episodic memory, this isn't a substrate respecification problem; it's empirical discrimination between memory types based on dissociable behavioral profiles. The hippocampus-episodic memory link gets refined, not arbitrarily adjusted. Crucially, we can be *wrong* about whether something counts as episodic memory in ways that behavioral evidence can correct.

Consciousness has no such independent criterion. The thesis's point is precisely this: when cerebellar patients retain consciousness despite massive neuron loss, or when split-brain patients show ambiguous phenomenology, there is no behavior-based fact of the matter about whether consciousness is present that doesn't already presuppose an answer to which substrate matters. We cannot operationalize "is conscious" without stipulating the mapping we're trying to test.

Consider: if a system passes all behavioral tests for episodic memory but we discover the hippocampus isn't involved, we conclude either (a) our behavioral criteria were wrong, or (b) episodic memory has a different substrate than we thought. Evidence can decide between these. But if a system passes all behavioral tests for consciousness yet lacks the substrate our theory predicts, we face an undecidable question: is it conscious with a different substrate, or does it merely *behave* as if conscious? No evidence can decide this without begging the question about the substrate-consciousness mapping.

The proves-too-much objection works only if cognitive functions and consciousness are epistemically parallel. They aren't. Cognitive functions are individuated by their functional roles in producing discriminable behavior. Consciousness is individuated by subjective character, which has no criterion-independent behavioral signature. That's why the substrate-specification problem is constitutive for consciousness but not for memory or language.

VERDICT

The defence fails, though it identifies something important before collapsing.

The defence is right that consciousness lacks simple behavioral read-outs in the way episodic memory does. But it's wrong that this creates a categorical difference in testability.

First, the defence overstates the independence of behavioral criteria in cognitive neuroscience. Whether a performance counts as "genuine episodic memory" versus "semantic memory misclassified" or "lucky guessing" is itself theory-laden. The criteria aren't given independently—they're operationalizations that themselves depend on theoretical commitments about what episodic memory *is*. When researchers debate whether recognition memory involves recollection or familiarity, they're disputing precisely what behavioral signatures indicate what functional kinds. This is substrate-specification flexibility by another name.

Second, consciousness science *does* have behavioral and neural signatures that constrain theory: reportability, integration across sensory modalities, global availability for action control, neural complexity measures, response to perturbation. These aren't perfect criterions, but neither are memory's behavioral tests. The defence claims we face an "undecidable question" with consciousness, but we face similar undecidability with memory: does implicit knowledge without explicit recall count as memory? Researchers make progress despite this.

Third, and fatally: the defence's own example defeats it. It says if a system "passes all behavioral tests for consciousness yet lacks the substrate our theory predicts," we cannot decide if it's conscious. But this is exactly the H.M. situation with memory—he passes some memory tests (procedural) but not others (episodic), and we *do* make progress by refining our substrate theories. The parallel holds.

The objection stands: the argument proves too much. Substrate-specification flexibility exists throughout cognitive neuroscience without preventing testability.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. Consider Integrated Information Theory (IIT) tested against cerebellar contribution to consciousness. IIT predicts consciousness correlates with integrated information (Φ) in neural substrates. The cerebellum contains ~69 billion neurons (80% of brain's total) yet cerebellar damage rarely affects consciousness while small cortical lesions can eliminate it. IIT defenders respond: the cerebellum has low Φ despite high neuron count because it's modular/feedforward, so it doesn't implement IIT's requirements. But now test IIT against cortical mini-columns, which are also highly modular and feedforward in structure. IIT must predict these shouldn't contribute much to consciousness—yet they're paradigmatic cortical structures where consciousness clearly resides. The theory faces a concrete dilemma: either (a) modularity/feedforward structure disqualifies substrates from consciousness (contradicting cortical evidence), or (b) some other unmeasured feature distinguishes cerebellum from cortex—but this "other feature" is precisely the substrate stipulation the thesis identifies. The weak point is the claim that this stipulation is *unfalsifiable*—because here we have a concrete case where IIT must make a determinate prediction that can be empirically wrong. Either mini-columns generate significant Φ or they don't; either this correlates with their conscious contribution or it doesn't. The measurements are in principle possible, making this a genuine empirical test, not an unfalsifiable stipulation.

STRENGTH: 3/5 — This is a genuine problem for the thesis. The counterexample shows that substrate specifications can generate concrete, measurable predictions that discriminate between theoretical options. However, it doesn't completely defeat the thesis because defenders could argue that measuring Φ in mini-columns versus cerebellum would still require stipulations about the relevant grain of analysis and temporal scale—pushing the unfalsifiability one level deeper.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The counterexample actually demonstrates the thesis rather than refuting it. The objection claims IIT faces a "concrete dilemma" that forces determinate predictions, but look at what happens in practice: IIT escapes through *exactly* the substrate-specification flexibility the thesis identifies.

When confronted with the cerebellum-cortex asymmetry, IIT doesn't make a simple prediction about "modularity disqualifies substrates." Instead, it introduces stipulations about the *relevant level of organization* for computing Φ. Tononi and colleagues specify that Φ must be calculated over the "main complex"—the substrate with maximal integrated information *at the right grain of analysis*. But what's the "right grain"? This itself requires stipulation.

The objection says "either mini-columns generate significant Φ or they don't" as if this were straightforward measurement. But calculating Φ requires specifying:
- The temporal scale of integration (milliseconds? seconds?)
- The spatial grain (individual neurons? columns? regions?)
- The partition scheme for measuring integration
- The background conditions (awake? task-engaged?)

Each specification is adjustable. When cerebellar modules show low Φ, defenders say: "See, modularity means low integration at the relevant scale." When cortical mini-columns must show high Φ despite modularity, defenders adjust: "Cortical columns integrate at a different temporal scale" or "The relevant complex spans multiple columns" or "Recurrent cortical connections create integration despite modular feedforward structure."

The objection claims this generates "measurable predictions that discriminate between theoretical options." But discriminate between *what* options? The theory never risks genuine falsification because the substrate specification—what scale, what grain, what temporal window counts—remains adjustable. This isn't a bug in current IIT; it's a structural feature. Any theory mapping consciousness to physical substrates must specify *which* physical organization matters, and this specification cannot be derived from the theory itself—it must be stipulated based on prior intuitions about what's conscious.

The cerebellum-cortex case perfectly illustrates the thesis: IIT doesn't get falsified by the asymmetry; it accommodates it through substrate respecification. And this accommodation is always available because the mapping relation is underconstrained.

VERDICT

The defence succeeds partially but reveals a deeper problem for the thesis.

The defence is correct that IIT's defenders do adjust grain, scale, and partition schemes when facing anomalies—this is well-documented in the literature. And it's right that these adjustments prevent simple falsification. The cerebellum case doesn't straightforwardly refute IIT; the theory absorbs it through respecification.

However, the defence's success exposes that the thesis has quietly shifted from "untestable" to "difficult to test" or "not yet falsified"—which is not the same thing. The counterexample shows that IIT *does* make concrete commitments that could, in principle, go wrong in ways that accumulate into genuine theoretical pressure.

Consider: if every modular, feedforward structure showed high Φ, or if no cortical structure showed high Φ, or if Φ measurements systematically failed to correlate with any consciousness markers across hundreds of cases, IIT would face mounting problems. Yes, defenders could adjust specifications each time, but these adjustments aren't cost-free. Each respecification narrows the theory's scope, adds epicycles, or contradicts previous specifications. The theory can *survive* any single anomaly through adjustment, but it cannot survive *all possible patterns* of evidence equally well.

This is just Lakatos's point about degenerating versus progressive research programs. The thesis claims consciousness theories face a *unique* unfalsifiability problem, but the defence shows they face the *standard* problem: theories are highly flexible but not infinitely so, and empirical pressure accumulates even when single experiments don't provide clean falsifications.

The counterexample stands: IIT's substrate specifications, while flexible, do generate measurable predictions that constrain the theory in practice. The thesis has not established that consciousness theories are untestable—only that they're tested holistically and adjusted incrementally, like theories everywhere.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis claims to offer a reframing of why consciousness theories are untestable, attributing it to "substrate-specification problems" rather than privacy of consciousness. But this is merely redescribing what "privacy of consciousness" means in practice, not offering an alternative explanation. When philosophers say consciousness is "private" or "subjective," they precisely mean that we cannot establish third-person criteria for when consciousness is present—which is exactly the substrate-specification problem. The thesis presents as a discovery that "the mapping relation itself requires stipulation," but this just unpacks what privacy entails: we cannot publicly verify consciousness mappings because consciousness is only accessible first-personally. The weak point is that the thesis treats "privacy" as merely about introspective access, ignoring that philosophical discussions of privacy have always centrally concerned the criterion problem—how do we know what's conscious from the outside? Nagel's "What is it like to be a bat?" (1974), Jackson's knowledge argument, and Chalmers' hard problem all turn on exactly this issue: physical facts underdetermine consciousness facts because the mapping between them cannot be established by third-person evidence alone. The thesis's "substrate-specification problem" is just this privacy problem wearing technical language. It's a verbal repackaging that makes it sound like a novel structural insight about theory-testing when it's actually the same old problem that consciousness is subjectively constituted and therefore resists objective verification criteria.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This substantially damages the thesis's claim to reframe the debate. The "substrate-specification problem" appears to be a notational variant of the privacy/subjectivity problem rather than a genuine alternative. The thesis hasn't shown it's offering a different explanation, just a different vocabulary for the same underlying issue that consciousness lacks public verification criteria.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection conflates two entirely different problems by equivocating on "privacy." The thesis can distinguish them sharply:

**Privacy Problem (Traditional):** We cannot know what it's like to be a bat because subjective experience is only accessible from the first-person perspective. This is an *epistemic access* problem about knowing quale character.

**Substrate-Specification Problem (Thesis):** We cannot determine which physical systems are conscious because any mapping from physical organization to consciousness requires stipulations that cannot be empirically grounded. This is a *theory-structure* problem about testability.

These come apart. Consider: even if we solved the privacy problem—even if we had perfect first-person access to all experiences, or even if we eliminated qualia-talk entirely—the substrate-specification problem would remain. Here's why:

Suppose we're functionalists who deny privacy matters. We say consciousness just IS certain functional organizations, nothing private about it. We still face the thesis's problem: when testing whether system S implements the relevant functional organization, we must stipulate what counts as implementing it. Does a silicon system running the same algorithm as neurons implement it? Does it count if the implementation is slower? Distributed differently? The theory itself doesn't determine these answers—we must stipulate the mapping between abstract functional descriptions and concrete physical realizations.

Or suppose we're eliminativists who think consciousness-talk is confused. We still face the problem: when building theories of "access consciousness" or "reportability," we must specify which physical substrates implement these functions, and this specification remains flexible in exactly the way the thesis describes.

The objection claims Nagel, Jackson, and Chalmers were "always centrally concerned" with the criterion problem. But read them carefully: Nagel asks what bat experience is *like*—a question about phenomenal character, not about which physical systems have it. Jackson's Mary learns what red experiences are *like*—not which systems have color experiences. Chalmers' hard problem asks why physical processes are accompanied by experience *at all*—not which physical processes count as the relevant ones.

The thesis identifies a *different* problem: even granting that consciousness exists and has phenomenal character, our theories about which physical systems instantiate it are untestable because the instantiation relation itself requires unfalsifiable stipulation. This problem doesn't disappear if we solve privacy—it's about the structure of physical-to-consciousness mappings, not about first-person access.

The substrate-specification problem is not "privacy wearing technical language." It's a distinct structural feature of consciousness theories that would persist even in worlds where consciousness was perfectly public.

# VERDICT

The defence fails. The purported distinction collapses when examined closely.

The defence claims the substrate-specification problem would remain "even if we solved the privacy problem" and offers functionalist and eliminativist scenarios. But both scenarios actually demonstrate that the problems are inseparable, not distinct.

**First, the functionalist case:** The defence says even if consciousness just IS functional organization, we'd still face stipulation problems about what counts as implementing the function. But *why* do we face this problem for consciousness specifically? For other functional kinds—like "being a mousetrap" or "being a calculator"—we can specify implementation conditions without unfalsifiable flexibility. We can say: "A system is a calculator if it reliably produces outputs standing in the right mathematical relations to inputs." We can test this. The reason consciousness is different is precisely that we cannot specify the function independently of first-person facts. What makes a functional organization conscious isn't just its causal profile but that "there is something it's like" to be that organization—and this is the privacy problem reappearing. The substrate-specification flexibility exists *because* we cannot pin down the functional definition without first-person criteria.

**Second, the eliminativist case:** If we eliminate consciousness-talk for "access" and "reportability," these ARE testable in the way memory and attention are (as the earlier objection established). The substrate-specification problem dissolves for these functions precisely because they're not private. The defence's own example shows that substrate-specification becomes problematic exactly when we're dealing with phenomenal consciousness—the private aspect.

**Third, the textual claim fails:** The defence misreads the canonical sources. Nagel explicitly discusses the criterion problem: "Without consciousness the mind-body problem would be much less interesting. With consciousness it seems hopeless." Why hopeless? Because we lack objective criteria for consciousness presence. Chalmers' hard problem is precisely about why physical facts don't determine consciousness facts—which is the substrate-specification problem by another name. The privacy and criterion problems have always been intertwined in this literature.

**Finally, the crucial test:** The defence claims substrate-specification would remain "even in worlds where consciousness was perfectly public." But what would such a world look like? If consciousness were perfectly public, we'd have objective, third-person-accessible criteria for its presence—which is exactly what would solve the substrate-specification problem. We could test mappings against public facts. The reason we can't is privacy.

The objection stands: the substrate-specification problem is the privacy problem in different vocabulary. Both concern the same underlying issue: consciousness lacks objective verification criteria because it's subjectively constituted. The thesis has not provided a genuine reframing—it's redescribed the traditional problem in theory-testing terminology.

</details>
