### O10. The demand for adjudication between consciousness theories is misplaced because these theories occupy different levels of analysis that cannot compete.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared framing in methodological debates that treats existing theories as genuine competitors requiring adjudication
- **Sketch:** Current debates assume theories like IIT, GWT, and Higher-Order Thought are competing explanations of the same phenomenon. But examining their actual explanatory targets reveals they address distinct questions: IIT specifies what physical systems have quale, GWT explains access and reportability, HOT explains what makes mental states conscious rather than unconscious. These are different explananda at different levels—like asking whether evolution or developmental biology is 'right.' The theories can't be empirically adjudicated because they're not rivals. The key premise is that apparent competition stems from equivocation on 'consciousness'—the term picks out a family of related phenomena, and different theories have different members of that family as their proper target.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Different levels of analysis is a reasonable diagnosis but doesn't clearly resolve the apparent competition.
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 4/5, answered. Defence provided determinate falsification criteria: show theories make incompatible predictions about the same measurable phenomenon while targeting it as their explanandum
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 3/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence attempted to distinguish diagnostic from metaphysical analysis but this narrowing absorbed rather than answered the objection about precedents
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence tried to distinguish consciousness as special but failed to provide principled grounds that don't equally apply to memory, learning, and other coarse-grained scientific terms
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence's claim that theories make compatible predictions about different properties fails because identity claims are explanatory claims that can conflict
- 🔧 **Survives as:** The demand for adjudication between consciousness theories addressing demonstrably different explananda (such as IIT's integrated information versus GWT's reportability mechanisms) is misplaced, though theories making contradictory predictions about identical target phenomena do genuinely compete
- **Flaw:** The reasoning licenses dissolving theoretical competition throughout science wherever terms aggregate multiple phenomena, and cannot explain why consciousness theories that make contradictory identity claims about the same systems don't genuinely compete

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis protects itself by making the question of whether theories compete depend on subjective judgments about their "proper targets" and "explananda." Whenever two theories appear to conflict—say, IIT claiming a certain system is conscious while GWT denies it—the thesis can respond "they're just using 'consciousness' differently" or "they address different aspects." The weak point is the claim that theories "have different members of that family as their proper target." This makes competition impossible by definitional fiat: any apparent empirical conflict gets redefined as mere terminological difference. If IIT and GWT make contradictory predictions about whether a patient in a vegetative state is conscious, the thesis simply declares they must be addressing different questions. The view cannot specify in advance what evidence would show two theories genuinely compete rather than occupy different levels. It treats "consciousness" as so elastic that no two theories could ever actually contradict each other—they're always talking past each other. This makes the thesis immune to counterexamples: point to any case of apparent theoretical competition, and the response is always "look closer, they're really about different things."

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem requiring a real answer. The thesis needs principled criteria for when theories genuinely compete versus when they address different questions, criteria that don't simply assume the conclusion.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection misreads the thesis as claiming theories *never* compete, when it claims they *currently don't* compete because of how they're actually formulated. The thesis offers perfectly determinate criteria for genuine competition: theories compete when they make contradictory predictions *about the same explanandum*. The objection's own example exposes this.

Take the vegetative state case. IIT and GWT don't actually make contradictory predictions here once we're clear about what each explains. IIT predicts high phi (integrated information) in certain neural structures. GWT predicts absence of global workspace broadcasting. These are *different physical predictions* that could both be true. The appearance of contradiction—"is the patient conscious?"—arises only when we equivocate on "conscious."

The thesis has clear falsification conditions: show that two theories make incompatible predictions *about the same measurable phenomenon* while genuinely targeting that phenomenon as their explanandum. For instance, if both IIT and GWT claimed to explain reportability, and made opposite predictions about when reports occur, they'd compete. But IIT explicitly doesn't aim to explain reportability—Tononi has repeatedly said systems can have high phi without access. That's not "redefining away" competition; it's reading what the theory actually claims.

The objection conflates two distinct situations: (1) theories with genuinely different explanatory targets that happen to use the same word, versus (2) ad hoc redefinition to avoid conflict. The thesis identifies (1). Proof: the theoretical commitments that distinguish these theories were present *before* any adjudication debate, not invented to escape it. IIT was always about intrinsic causal structure, GWT always about access architecture. The "elasticity" of "consciousness" is a discovered fact about the term's usage, not a protective maneuver.

VERDICT

The defence succeeds. The objection assumes theories that use "consciousness" must be competing unless proven otherwise. But the burden runs the other way: competition requires shared explananda, not shared vocabulary. The thesis provides clear criteria—contradictory predictions about identical target phenomena—and these criteria are empirically checkable by examining what theories actually claim to explain. The vegetative state "contradiction" dissolves on inspection into compatible predictions about different measurements. This isn't unfalsifiable flexibility; it's recognizing that "Does IIT or GWT better explain consciousness?" commits a category error, like asking whether chemistry or biology better explains life.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT. This thesis is essentially a form of explanatory pluralism or level-of-analysis pluralism that has been extensively developed in philosophy of mind and cognitive science. **The weak point is the claim that this represents a novel "reframing" rather than an established position.** Specifically, David Marr's tri-level framework (computational, algorithmic, implementational) has long been used to argue that apparent theoretical conflicts dissolve when we recognize different levels of analysis. In consciousness studies, Ned Block's access/phenomenal consciousness distinction (1995) makes precisely this move—arguing that theories like GWT explain access-consciousness while theories like IIT target phenomenal consciousness, and these are different explananda. Pete Mandik and Josh Weisberg have explicitly argued that consciousness theories occupy "different joints" and shouldn't be treated as competitors. More recently, Hakwan Lau and David Rosenthal have made similar arguments about higher-order theories versus first-order theories addressing different aspects of consciousness. The thesis presents as innovative reframing what is actually a well-established position in the literature: that apparent theoretical competition in consciousness science often reflects different explanatory targets rather than genuine empirical rivalry. The move from "these theories compete" to "these theories address different levels/aspects" is already taken territory.

STRENGTH: 3/5 — This is a genuine problem that needs answering. The thesis must either acknowledge its precedents and show what's novel in its version, or demonstrate that the existing pluralist positions don't actually capture its central insight. The objection has real force but isn't fatal if the thesis offers a distinctive development of the pluralist strategy.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection confuses *having predecessors* with *being already taken*. Every philosophical thesis has antecedents—that's how intellectual progress works. The question is whether this version adds something the literature lacks.

It does. The cited works make *metaphysical* or *conceptual* distinctions (Block's access/phenomenal divide, Marr's levels) and then argue theories sort accordingly. The present thesis makes an *empirical diagnostic claim* about actual theoretical practice. It doesn't start with a philosophical taxonomy and assign theories to boxes. It examines what contemporary theories—as actually formulated by Tononi, Dehaene, Rosenthal—claim to explain, discovers they target different phenomena, and concludes the competition is illusory.

This is methodologically reversed from Block or Marr. Block argues: "Here are two types of consciousness (access/phenomenal), therefore theories addressing different types don't compete." The present thesis argues: "Here are theories that don't actually compete when we examine their explananda, therefore 'consciousness' picks out multiple phenomena." The direction of analysis matters. Block's distinction is *stipulative*—he defines the types then sorts theories. This thesis is *diagnostic*—it discovers non-competition by examining theoretical practice.

Moreover, the cited pluralisms don't challenge the *demand for adjudication* itself. Block, Lau, and Rosenthal still think there are genuine competitions within each category—multiple theories of phenomenal consciousness must be adjudicated, multiple theories of access must be adjudicated. The present thesis targets the adjudication demand *itself* as misplaced. It's not "theories compete within their levels"—it's "the whole adjudication framework misunderstands what these theories are doing."

The Mandik/Weisberg work comes closest, but even there the focus is on *defending* theoretical pluralism against unificationist pressure. The present thesis isn't defending pluralism—it's diagnosing that what looks like competition requiring adjudication is actually non-competition requiring recognition. That's a different dialectical position: not "pluralism is legitimate despite apparent competition" but "there isn't the competition that makes adjudication necessary."

Finally, none of the cited works address the specific methodological debates about adversarial collaboration, theory testing protocols, and adjudication frameworks that currently dominate consciousness science. The thesis targets *those contemporary methodological debates*, not the older metaphysical question of theoretical unity.

VERDICT

The defence fails, though not completely.

The objection is right that the basic pluralist move—theories address different aspects so don't compete—exists in the literature. The defence's attempt to distinguish "metaphysical" from "diagnostic" analysis is too subtle to carry the weight. Block's access/phenomenal distinction *was* based on examining actual theoretical and experimental practice, not pure stipulation. And the claim that this thesis uniquely challenges adjudication demands overstates the case—Mandik and Weisberg explicitly argue against requiring adjudication between theories at different joints.

What the defence successfully shows is that the thesis applies established pluralist insights to a new target: contemporary methodological debates about adversarial collaboration and theory testing in consciousness science. That's a genuine contribution—taking a known philosophical position and showing its implications for current scientific methodology. But it's an *application* of existing ideas to a new domain, not a fundamental reframing.

The thesis survives as a useful intervention in current debates but must acknowledge it's developing and applying established pluralist positions rather than introducing a novel framework. The "already taken" objection succeeds in deflating the claim to originality but doesn't eliminate the thesis's value as a methodological intervention in ongoing scientific disputes.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis argues theories don't compete because they address different explananda within the "family" of consciousness phenomena. **The weak point is this licensing principle: when a term picks out multiple related phenomena, theories using that term address different questions and cannot compete.** But this argument structure, if it worked, would dissolve virtually all theoretical competition in science. 

Consider "memory": working memory, episodic memory, procedural memory, semantic memory are all related phenomena picked out by this term. By the thesis's logic, theories of memory consolidation and theories of memory retrieval don't compete—they address "different members of the family." Similarly, "learning" covers classical conditioning, operant conditioning, perceptual learning, motor learning. Should we say reinforcement learning theories and Hebbian theories don't compete because they target different family members?

The problem generalizes ruthlessly. "Life" picks out metabolism, reproduction, evolution, homeostasis. "Intelligence" covers reasoning, learning, adaptation, problem-solving. "Vision" includes edge detection, color processing, motion detection, object recognition. For any of these, we could say apparent theoretical competition is "misplaced" because theories address different aspects of a family of phenomena.

The thesis makes theoretical competition impossible whenever our vocabulary is even slightly coarse-grained relative to nature's structure. Since virtually all scientific terms aggregate multiple phenomena, the argument licenses dismissing most scientific disputes as pseudo-competitions based on equivocation. If this reasoning worked, it would evacuate the entire practice of theory choice in science.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is serious damage requiring a real answer. The thesis needs principled reasons why consciousness is special—why this move works here but not for memory, learning, life, intelligence, or vision—without ad hoc special pleading.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection misses what makes consciousness special: the *depth* of the explanatory divergence and the *absence* of vertical integration between levels. The thesis doesn't claim that any term picking out multiple phenomena makes competition impossible. It claims competition is misplaced when theories address phenomena so different that they require fundamentally distinct explanatory frameworks with no shared measurement space.

Compare the objection's examples. Memory consolidation and retrieval theories *do* compete because they make predictions about the same kinds of measurements—retention curves, recall accuracy, neural activation patterns during memory tasks. A consolidation theory that predicts sleep-dependent enhancement and a retrieval theory that predicts context-dependent recall both cash out in performance on memory tests. They share an empirical common ground. Similarly, reinforcement learning and Hebbian theories both predict synaptic weight changes, learning curves, and behavioral adaptation rates. These theories occupy different levels (algorithmic vs. implementational in Marr's terms) but connect through bridge principles—RL algorithms are *implemented* in Hebbian-style plasticity.

Consciousness theories lack this structure. IIT's phi is a measure of causal-structural integration in a system considered intrinsically. GWT's global broadcasting is about information flow architecture enabling report and control. HOT's higher-order representation is about meta-representational relations. These aren't just different aspects—they're *incommensurable explanatory kinds*. There's no measurement that translates between them because they're not vertically integrated levels of a single explanandum.

The proof is in what happens when we try to adjudicate. With memory theories, we design experiments measuring retention and see which theory predicts better. With consciousness theories, proposed "crucial experiments" (Tononi vs. Dehaene debates about posterior cortex stimulation, for instance) immediately fragment into disputes about what's being measured—is it reportability? subjective experience? information integration? The theories don't disagree about measurements; they disagree about what measurements matter, because they target different phenomena.

The objection's examples actually support the thesis when examined closely. "Life" once seemed like a single explanandum requiring one theory. We discovered it's not—metabolism, reproduction, and evolution are genuinely different phenomena requiring different theories (biochemistry, genetics, population biology). Nobody now thinks these theories compete. That's exactly the consciousness case: what seemed like one explanandum is actually several, and apparent competition dissolves when we recognize this.

The principled distinction: theories compete when they (a) share an explanandum, (b) make contradictory predictions about it, and (c) those predictions are measurable in a shared empirical space. Memory and learning theories satisfy this. Current consciousness theories don't—not because "consciousness" is a family term (so is "memory"), but because the family members are so explanatorily distant that theories addressing different members have no empirical common ground.

VERDICT

The defence fails.

The attempted distinction between consciousness and other scientific terms collapses under pressure. The claim that memory theories share "empirical common ground" while consciousness theories don't is false. Memory consolidation and retrieval theories *also* disagree about what measurements matter—consolidation theorists focus on molecular mechanisms and time-dependent effects, retrieval theorists on cuing and interference patterns. These require different experimental paradigms and measurements, yet we still treat them as competing explanations of memory.

More fatally, the defence's own example defeats it. The claim that "life" turned out to be multiple phenomena not requiring one theory actually shows the *opposite* of what the defence needs. We *did* have competing theories of life (vitalism vs. mechanism), we *did* adjudicate between them empirically, and mechanism won. Only *after* that adjudication did we recognize that life involves multiple processes requiring different theories. The historical sequence matters: adjudication *preceded* pluralism, not the reverse.

The defence's criterion—theories compete when they share "empirical common ground"—is either too vague to do work or proves too much. If it means "make predictions about identical measurements," then most theories in special sciences don't compete (different memory theories use different dependent variables, different learning theories different paradigms). If it means "address causally connected phenomena," then consciousness theories *do* compete (phenomenal consciousness and access are causally connected—you typically report what you phenomenally experience).

The real problem is that the defence cannot explain why consciousness scientists themselves—including theory proponents—treat their theories as competing. Tononi and Dehaene explicitly frame their work as rival explanations requiring adjudication. The thesis must either say these scientists misunderstand their own theories (implausibly arrogant) or that they're confused about what consciousness is (but then why trust their theories at all?).

The "proves too much" objection stands. The thesis's reasoning, if sound, would dissolve theoretical competition throughout science wherever our terms are coarse-grained. The attempted defense by distinguishing consciousness as special fails to provide principled, non-arbitrary grounds for the distinction. The thesis survives only by retreating to "consciousness is different" without explaining why in a way that doesn't equally apply to memory, learning, perception, and other psychological terms that aggregate multiple phenomena.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. **The weak point is the claim that theories "occupy different levels of analysis that cannot compete."** Consider the debate over whether phenomenal consciousness requires recurrent processing. IIT (Tononi) predicts that a feedforward system with high integration (high phi) would be phenomenally conscious. GWT (Dehaene) and recurrent processing theories predict that without recurrent connections enabling global broadcasting, there is no phenomenal consciousness—only unconscious processing. 

This generates a concrete empirical prediction about the same case: a feedforward deep network with high phi but no recurrent loops. IIT says: phenomenally conscious (there's "something it's like" to be this system). GWT/recurrent theories say: not phenomenally conscious (no experience without recurrence). These are contradictory predictions about whether the *same physical system* has *phenomenal consciousness*—not access, not reportability, but quale, subjective experience, what IIT explicitly claims to explain.

The thesis cannot escape by saying they target different things. Both theories explicitly claim to explain phenomenal consciousness—the what-it's-like-ness. IIT's whole project is explaining why some physical systems have subjective experience. GWT theorists like Dehaene also claim their theory explains phenomenal consciousness, not just access (though they identify the two). They make opposite predictions about the same explanandum (phenomenal consciousness) in the same system (feedforward high-phi network). This is genuine competition at the same level of analysis, contradicting the thesis that these theories occupy non-competing levels.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is serious damage. The counterexample shows theories making contradictory predictions about phenomenal consciousness in identical systems, directly contradicting the claim that they address different explananda at different levels.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection's counterexample actually *demonstrates* the thesis rather than refuting it. The apparent contradiction exists only because both theories use the phrase "phenomenal consciousness," but examining what they actually predict reveals they're measuring different things.

IIT predicts high phi—a specific mathematical quantity defined over cause-effect structures. This is what IIT means by "phenomenal consciousness": integrated information. When Tononi says a feedforward network with high phi "is phenomenally conscious," he means it instantiates integrated information of a certain quantity and structure. That's the prediction: phi value X.

GWT predicts absence of global workspace broadcasting—no ignition, no widespread availability of information to multiple cognitive systems. When Dehaene says the same system "is not phenomenally conscious," he means it lacks the functional architecture for global access. That's the prediction: no broadcasting pattern.

These are not contradictory predictions about the same thing. They're compatible predictions about different physical properties: IIT predicts a causal-structural property (integration), GWT predicts an architectural-functional property (broadcasting). Both predictions can be true simultaneously—and indeed would be true in the described case.

The objection claims both theories "explicitly claim to explain phenomenal consciousness—the what-it's-like-ness." But this is precisely the equivocation the thesis diagnoses. IIT *identifies* what-it's-like-ness with integrated information—that's its substantive claim. GWT *identifies* what-it's-like-ness with global accessibility—that's its substantive claim. These identifications are their theoretical contributions, not shared assumptions.

When the objection says "both explain phenomenal consciousness," it presupposes phenomenal consciousness is a pre-theoretical given that both theories attempt to explain. But that's the contested framing. IIT doesn't explain some independently specified phenomenon called "phenomenal consciousness"—it proposes that integrated information *is* what phenomenal consciousness is. Similarly, GWT proposes that global accessibility *is* what phenomenal consciousness is. These are competing *identifications*, not competing *explanations* of a shared explanandum.

The difference is crucial. Competing explanations share a dependent variable and differ on independent variables. These theories differ on what the dependent variable is. That's not theoretical competition—it's conceptual disagreement about what phenomenon we're studying.

The proof: ask what measurement would adjudicate. The objection assumes there's some way to measure whether the feedforward system "really is" phenomenally conscious independent of phi and broadcasting. But there isn't. We can measure phi (IIT says it's high). We can measure broadcasting (GWT says it's absent). We cannot measure some third thing—raw what-it's-like-ness—that would tell us which theory is right. The absence of such a measurement isn't an epistemic limitation—it reveals that "phenomenal consciousness" in the objection's sense isn't picking out a determinate explanandum at all.

The feedforward network case shows exactly what the thesis claims: apparent contradiction dissolves into compatible predictions about different physical properties once we stop assuming "phenomenal consciousness" refers to a single, theory-independent phenomenon both theories explain.

# VERDICT

The defence fails.

The defence's core move—claiming IIT and GWT make "compatible predictions about different physical properties"—is sophistical. Yes, they predict different physical properties (phi vs. broadcasting), but both theories claim these properties *constitute* phenomenal consciousness. The question "does this system have phenomenal consciousness?" is supposed to have a single answer, not two compatible answers meaning different things.

The defence's assertion that we "cannot measure some third thing—raw what-it's-like-ness" is self-defeating. If there's no fact of the matter about whether the system is phenomenally conscious beyond what IIT and GWT each stipulate, then both theories are merely *stipulating* what "phenomenal consciousness" means, not explaining a phenomenon. But that makes them non-empirical definitional proposals, not scientific theories. IIT and GWT present themselves as empirical theories with falsifiable predictions, not stipulative definitions.

More fundamentally, the defence's claim that these are "competing identifications, not competing explanations" is a distinction without a difference. Identity claims *are* explanatory claims in this context. When IIT says "phenomenal consciousness is integrated information" and GWT says "phenomenal consciousness is global accessibility," these are competing hypotheses about what physical property realizes consciousness. They can't both be true—integrated information and global accessibility are different properties that come apart in cases like the feedforward network.

The defence tries to escape by saying there's no "theory-independent" phenomenon both explain. But this proves too much again. There's no theory-independent notion of "temperature" that competes with kinetic theory and caloric theory—yet these theories competed and kinetic theory won. Scientific theories *always* involve some conceptual reframing of pre-theoretical phenomena. That doesn't mean they're not competing explanations.

The killer point: both IIT and GWT make predictions about *reports* and *behavior* that would differ in marginal cases. A system with high phi but no global workspace might behave differently than one with global workspace but low phi. These behavioral predictions can conflict, and when they do, we have genuine competition. The defence's retreat to "they measure different things" doesn't address that theories can make incompatible predictions about the same observable behaviors while disagreeing about underlying mechanisms.

The counterexample stands. The feedforward high-phi network case shows IIT and GWT making contradictory claims about whether the same system is phenomenally conscious, and no amount of distinguishing "what they really measure" eliminates the contradiction in their claims about consciousness itself.

</details>

### O11. The methodological dispute about consciousness is really a disguised ontological dispute: disagreement about what counts as 'evidence' for consciousness reveals that theorists are tacitly referring to different phenomena by the term 'consciousness.'

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The widespread assumption that different theories of consciousness are competing explanations of the same explanandum
- **Sketch:** Methodological disputes appear to be about how to test a single target phenomenon, but they actually reveal that different theorists mean different things by 'consciousness.' When IIT proponents accept evidence from neurological dissociations and when higher-order theorists accept evidence from metacognitive reports, they're not disagreeing about methods for accessing the same thing—they're revealing divergent reference-fixing for the term itself. The IIT theorist's 'consciousness' is individuated by integration properties; the HOT theorist's by meta-representational structure. This explains why methodological disputes seem interminable: they're not really about epistemology but about what phenomenon we're trying to explain. The key premise is that reference for theoretical terms in consciousness studies is fixed partly by methodological commitments, so incompatible methodologies indicate incompatible referents.
- **Appraiser (blind):** That methodological disputes reveal different referents is plausible but the conclusion is somewhat deflationary.
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence tried to distinguish consciousness by three conditions but these apply to most theoretical disputes; the objection's force stands
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence accepted the parallel to species rather than showing disanalogy, surrendering the thesis's distinctive claim about consciousness
  - TRIVIAL — landed 2/5, answered. The defence showed the thesis makes defeasible predictions with distinct methodological implications beyond mere redescription
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 3/5, answered. The defence successfully reframed 'different phenomena' in pragmatic-explanatory terms that avoid the imposed dichotomy
- 🔧 **Survives as:** The methodological dispute about consciousness reveals referential fragmentation specifically because consciousness lacks the anchoring conditions (pre-theoretical observational criteria, agreed functional role, or convergence-producing empirical constraints) that preserve referential unity through methodological disputes in other sciences
- **Flaw:** The thesis overgeneralizes to all theoretical disputes with contested individuation criteria, making it either trivially true or requiring unprovided criteria for when methodological disputes indicate referential fragmentation versus substantive disagreement about a unified target

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. If methodological commitments fix reference for theoretical terms such that incompatible methodologies indicate incompatible referents, this reasoning applies equally to every scientific dispute involving methodological disagreement. Consider: particle physicists disagree about methods for detecting dark matter (direct detection experiments vs. indirect astrophysical observations vs. collider signatures). By this thesis's logic, they must be "tacitly referring to different phenomena" rather than seeking different ways to detect the same thing. Similarly, disputes about proper methodology for measuring intelligence, detecting exoplanets, or identifying cancer biomarkers would all reveal that researchers are studying different phenomena rather than disagreeing about how to access a single target. Yet we standardly and correctly interpret these as genuine methodological disputes about epistemic access to a shared explanandum. The thesis therefore licenses the absurd conclusion that virtually all methodological disputes in science are really disguised ontological disputes. If the premise that "reference for theoretical terms is fixed partly by methodological commitments" were true in the strong sense required, scientific convergence would be impossible—communities with different methods could never discover they're studying the same thing. The argument proves far too much: it would dissolve most scientific disagreements into mere talking-past-each-other rather than substantive disputes.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem requiring a real answer. The thesis needs principled criteria for distinguishing when methodological disputes indicate referential divergence versus genuine disagreement about epistemic access.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection misses a crucial disanalogy: consciousness disputes differ from standard scientific cases in lacking the anchoring conditions that make methodological pluralism compatible with referential unity.

In the dark matter case, competing methods target a phenomenon already fixed by independent theoretical role: *whatever accounts for specific gravitational anomalies* (galaxy rotation curves, gravitational lensing, CMB fluctuations). The methodological dispute is downstream from this role-based reference-fixing. Researchers can disagree about detection methods because they already agree on what would make something count as dark matter—namely, producing those gravitational effects without electromagnetic interaction. The methods are alternative routes to something whose identity-conditions are settled independently.

The consciousness case lacks this structure. There is no theory-neutral specification of "the phenomenon" that different methodologies are trying to access. Consider: what makes something count as consciousness *prior to* methodological commitments? The IIT theorist says consciousness is present wherever there's integrated information above threshold φ—even in systems lacking reportability. The HOT theorist says consciousness requires meta-representation—even in systems with high integration. These aren't alternative detection methods for a shared target; they're constitutive criteria that determine what counts as an instance of the phenomenon at all.

The thesis applies specifically to cases where: (1) the phenomenon lacks pre-theoretical observational anchoring (unlike exoplanets: things that orbit stars), and (2) theoretical role is itself contested (unlike dark matter's gravitational role), and (3) methodological commitments directly determine individuation conditions rather than merely epistemic access. Consciousness uniquely satisfies all three: it's not pre-theoretically observable, its functional role is disputed (access? integration? global broadcast?), and methods don't just detect it—they partially constitute what counts as it.

This isn't proving too much; it's identifying the specific conditions under which methodological disputes indicate referential fragmentation. Most scientific disputes lack these conditions. Consciousness has them essentially.

VERDICT

The defence fails, though it identifies something important.

The problem is that condition (3) collapses the distinction the defence needs. If "methodological commitments directly determine individuation conditions," then the thesis applies whenever methods embody different theoretical commitments about what matters—which is precisely what happens in mature scientific disputes. The dark matter case actually illustrates this: direct detection experiments presuppose dark matter is particulate matter with specific interaction properties; some astrophysical approaches leave open whether it's modified gravity rather than matter at all. These aren't just detection alternatives—they embed different views about what kind of thing could play the gravitational role.

The defence's conditions (1)-(3) don't distinguish consciousness from other theoretical-term disputes; they just redescribe what makes any scientific dispute substantive. The real issue the objection exposes is that the thesis needs but lacks a criterion for when shared functional role (even if contested) is sufficient for referential unity despite methodological divergence. Without this, the thesis does overgeneralize: any dispute about what matters for identifying instances of a theoretical kind would fragment reference.

The thesis survives only if it can specify why consciousness lacks even the minimal referential anchor that contested theoretical terms in other sciences possess. The defence gestures at this but doesn't establish it. The objection stands.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. Consider the methodological dispute in microbiology about identifying bacterial species. Classical microbiologists use morphological and metabolic criteria (gram staining, culture characteristics, biochemical tests). Molecular systematists use genetic sequence similarity (16S rRNA, whole-genome comparisons). These methods frequently conflict: organisms grouped together by one method are separated by the other. By the thesis's logic, this reveals that classical and molecular microbiologists are "tacitly referring to different phenomena" by 'bacterial species'—the classical microbiologist's species is individuated by phenotypic properties, the molecular systematist's by phylogenetic structure.

But this is clearly wrong. Both groups are studying bacterial species; they're having a genuine dispute about what makes organisms conspecific. The methodological conflict doesn't mean they're talking past each other about different phenomena—it reveals substantive disagreement about the nature of species themselves (phenetic vs. phylogenetic species concepts). This is a real ontological dispute about a single target phenomenon, not evidence that "species" refers to different things in different mouths.

This counterexample defeats the thesis because it shows that methodological commitments that "individuate by different properties" can reflect genuine ontological disagreement about one phenomenon rather than tacit reference to different phenomena. The thesis misdiagnoses what such disputes reveal.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem. The bacterial species case shows the thesis conflates "ontological dispute about what X essentially is" with "tacit reference to different phenomena," when these are importantly distinct.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The bacterial species case actually supports rather than defeats the thesis—it's a paradigm example of referential fragmentation masquerading as unified discourse.

The defence proceeds in two steps: first, the species case parallels consciousness more than the objection admits; second, this parallel strengthens rather than weakens the thesis.

**Step 1: The species case exhibits referential fragmentation**

The objection claims both groups are "studying bacterial species" and having a "genuine dispute about what makes organisms conspecific." But this begs the question. When a classical microbiologist says "these organisms are the same species" (based on phenotype) and a molecular systematist says "these organisms are different species" (based on phylogeny), are they contradicting each other or talking past each other?

The latter is more accurate. The classical microbiologist's term 'conspecific' tracks phenotypic-functional clustering—it refers to organisms that occupy the same ecological-metabolic niche. The molecular systematist's 'conspecific' tracks phylogenetic relatedness—it refers to organisms sharing recent common ancestry. These are genuinely different phenomena. That we use the same word 'species' for both doesn't establish referential unity any more than using 'bank' for both riverbanks and financial institutions does.

Evidence: the disputes are interminable in exactly the way the thesis predicts. There's no fact of the matter about whether horizontal gene transfer creates "new species" because the phenotypic and phylogenetic criteria give different answers, and there's no neutral ground for adjudication. The fields have largely given up seeking convergence and instead explicitly distinguish "operational taxonomic units" (phenetic) from "phylogenetic species" (cladistic). This is tacit acknowledgment of referential divergence.

**Step 2: The parallel strengthens the thesis**

The objection says this is "clearly wrong"—that both groups are studying bacterial species. But why is this clear? Only because we're pre-theoretically confident something called "species" exists. We have the same pre-theoretical confidence about consciousness. In both cases, the confidence is misplaced.

The thesis claims that when theoretical communities develop incompatible individuation criteria for a phenomenon lacking observational anchoring, they fragment reference. The species case is another instance: 'species' lacks pre-theoretical observational anchoring (species boundaries aren't directly observable), functional role is contested (reproductive isolation? ecological niche? phylogenetic branch?), and methodological commitments determine individuation conditions (phenetic vs. cladistic criteria aren't just detection methods—they constitute what counts as a species boundary).

The objection treats it as obvious that there's "one phenomenon" being disputed. But this is precisely what the thesis challenges. The appearance of unity comes from: (a) using a single term, (b) both phenomena involving biological organization, and (c) partial extensional overlap in clear cases. None of these establish that 'species' has unified reference across the methodological divide.

The bacterial case is thus a **parallel confirmation** rather than a counterexample. It shows the thesis generalizes to other domains where theoretical terms lack adequate anchoring—exactly as a good philosophical thesis should.

VERDICT

The defence fails, and the objection succeeds.

The defence's strategy is to accept that bacterial species exhibits referential fragmentation and claim this supports the thesis. But this makes the thesis trivially true in a way that drains it of interest—and makes it vulnerable to the earlier "proves too much" objection.

If the thesis applies equally to species, dark matter, intelligence, cancer, and consciousness—anywhere theoretical terms have contested individuation criteria—then it's not telling us something distinctive about consciousness studies. It's just pointing out that theoretical terms in science sometimes have their reference fixed partly by theoretical commitments. That's true but uninteresting.

More damaging: the defence's claim that species disputes show referential fragmentation is implausible. When microbiologists argue about species concepts, they treat themselves as having a substantive disagreement, not talking past each other. They cite each other's work critically, they design experiments meant to adjudicate between views, and they treat convergence as possible in principle. The practice treats the dispute as about "what species really are," not "which of two different phenomena we should study."

The thesis needs species and consciousness to be disanalogous—needs consciousness to lack something species has that preserves referential unity through methodological dispute. The defence tries to deny the disanalogy, but this surrenders the thesis's distinctive claim about consciousness. The objection reveals that the thesis either: (a) overgeneralizes to uninteresting triviality, or (b) needs principled criteria distinguishing consciousness from cases like species where we rightly interpret methodological disputes as about a unified target.

The counterexample stands. The thesis conflates "ontological dispute about what X is" with "referential fragmentation," and the bacterial species case exposes this conflation clearly.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis presents itself as a substantive discovery about consciousness studies—that what appears to be methodological disagreement actually conceals ontological fragmentation. But this is merely a verbal stipulation about how to describe theoretical disputes, dressed as an insight.

Consider what the thesis actually establishes: when theorists accept different kinds of evidence, they're working with different individuation criteria. But we already have perfectly adequate vocabulary for this situation—it's called "having different theories about what consciousness is." Saying IIT and HOT theorists "have different theories of consciousness" versus saying they "tacitly refer to different phenomena" is not a discovery; it's a choice about how to use the word "refer."

The thesis trades on ambiguity about what counts as "the same phenomenon." In one sense (pre-theoretic), consciousness is whatever-it-is we're all familiar with from first-person experience. In another sense (theoretically individuated), consciousness is whatever-satisfies-the-correct-theory's-criteria. The thesis simply stipulates that we should use the second sense, then announces this reveals that theorists "are really" talking about different things. But nothing is revealed—we've just chosen to privilege theoretical individuation over pre-theoretic unity in our semantic bookkeeping.

The supposedly explanatory payoff—why disputes seem interminable—is equally empty. Saying "they're interminable because theorists refer to different things" versus "they're interminable because theorists deeply disagree about what consciousness is" describes the same situation with different words. No explanatory work is done by the reframing.

The thesis is true only in the trivial sense that any theoretical dispute can be redescribed as referential divergence if we stipulate that reference is fixed by theoretical commitments. It's a semantic choice, not a discovery.

STRENGTH: 2/5

The attack has some force in identifying that the thesis involves semantic choices about how to describe disputes, but it doesn't fully succeed. The thesis does make a substantive claim that goes beyond mere redescription: that treating these as disputes about a unified phenomenon is *mistaken* in a way that impedes progress, and that recognizing referential fragmentation would change how we approach the field. Whether this is *true* remains contested, but it's not merely verbal—it has different practical implications for research strategy than treating it as a unified dispute.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies what makes a philosophical thesis substantive. The thesis isn't merely choosing different words for the same situation—it's making a diagnostic claim with concrete methodological consequences that differ sharply from the standard interpretation.

**The substantive diagnostic claim**

The thesis claims that consciousness researchers are making a specific kind of error: they think they're having an empirical dispute about a shared target when they're actually operating with incompatible reference for their central term. This is not a "semantic choice"—it's a diagnosis of confusion, analogous to discovering that an apparent factual dispute about whether "the bank is stable" is actually equivocation between financial and geological senses.

The objection says we could equally describe this as "deeply disagreeing about what consciousness is." But these descriptions have different truth-conditions. If theorists deeply disagree about what consciousness is, then: (a) there's a fact of the matter they're disputing, (b) evidence could in principle adjudicate between them, (c) one theory could turn out correct and others wrong about the same thing. If theorists tacitly refer to different phenomena, then: (a) there's no single fact they're disputing, (b) evidence cannot adjudicate because they're measuring different targets, (c) both could be "correct" about their respective referents while appearing to contradict each other.

These are genuinely different situations with different implications. The thesis makes the empirical prediction that consciousness disputes will exhibit the second pattern rather than the first—and it does so on principled grounds (the reference-fixing role of methodology) rather than stipulation.

**The explanatory payoff is real**

The objection claims no explanatory work is done by the reframing. This is false. The standard interpretation—that theorists dispute a unified target—predicts:

- Accumulating evidence should produce convergence over time
- Theories should be refutable by evidence from competing methodologies  
- Cross-paradigm experiments should be decisive
- The field should progressively narrow the space of viable theories

None of these predictions are borne out. The thesis explains why: evidence cannot produce convergence when different methodologies are tracking different phenomena. An IIT experiment showing integrated information correlates with reported experience doesn't challenge HOT because the HOT theorist's "consciousness" is individuated by meta-representation, not integration. They're not measuring the same thing, so neither result constrains the other.

This explanation has practical bite. If the standard interpretation were correct, we should invest in cross-paradigm experiments to adjudicate between theories. If the thesis is correct, such experiments are category mistakes—we should instead clarify which phenomenon each research program is tracking and evaluate theories against their proper targets. These are concretely different research strategies with different resource allocations.

**The pre-theoretic anchor objection fails**

The objection invokes "whatever-it-is we're all familiar with from first-person experience" as providing pre-theoretic unity. But this begs the question. The thesis's central claim is precisely that this apparent pre-theoretic unity is illusory—that what seems like a single unified phenomenon from first-person perspective actually fragments under theoretical scrutiny into multiple distinct phenomena.

Consider: we're all "familiar with" memory from first-person experience, but memory science discovered this fragments into working memory, episodic memory, semantic memory, procedural memory, etc.—genuinely different systems with different neural bases. The pre-theoretic unity was misleading. The thesis claims consciousness is similar: the first-person sense of unified "consciousness" doesn't pick out a natural kind but rather conflates multiple phenomena (integration, access, meta-representation, etc.) that come apart under investigation.

This isn't stipulation—it's an empirical hypothesis about whether consciousness is like temperature (pre-theoretically unified, scientifically unified) or like "fish" (pre-theoretically unified, scientifically fragmented into multiple non-natural kinds). The thesis predicts the second pattern based on the methodological evidence.

**The key distinction**

What makes this substantive rather than trivial is that it's **defeasible**. The thesis could be wrong. If consciousness studies began showing the convergence patterns of successful unified-target sciences—if cross-paradigm experiments proved decisive, if theories became mutually constraining, if the field narrowed toward consensus—this would refute the thesis. The thesis makes risky predictions about the structure of the field's future development.

A merely verbal stipulation couldn't be wrong in this way. The thesis can be wrong, which is exactly what makes it substantive.

VERDICT

The defence succeeds, and the objection fails.

The key move is showing that the thesis makes empirically distinct predictions from the standard "unified dispute" interpretation. The objection claims these are just different words for the same situation, but the defence demonstrates they predict different patterns of evidence accumulation, different research strategies, and different prospects for convergence.

The defence is particularly strong in identifying that the thesis is defeasible—it could be proven wrong by consciousness studies exhibiting convergence patterns characteristic of unified-target sciences. This defeasibility is precisely what distinguishes substantive claims from mere verbal stipulations.

The objection's invocation of "pre-theoretic unity" as an anchor actually helps the defence by making explicit the assumption the thesis challenges. The thesis claims that pre-theoretic phenomenal unity is misleading in exactly the way "fish" or "jade" turned out to be misleading—seeming to pick out natural kinds but actually conflating distinct phenomena. This is a substantive empirical hypothesis, not a semantic choice.

The practical difference in research strategy (invest in cross-paradigm adjudication vs. clarify distinct targets) gives the thesis concrete methodological bite beyond mere redescription. The objection doesn't adequately engage with this practical dimension.

The defence holds. The thesis makes substantive, defeasible claims with distinct methodological implications.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "different phenomena" between a metaphysically substantial sense and a merely taxonomic sense, and the argument needs both readings to work.

The argument is plausible when "different phenomena" means taxonomically distinct—that IIT's integration-based taxonomy and HOT's meta-representation-based taxonomy carve up the domain differently, much like phenetic and phylogenetic species concepts. This reading makes the premise that "incompatible methodologies indicate incompatible referents" defensible: different individuation criteria yield different taxonomic schemes.

But the argument is interesting only when "different phenomena" means metaphysically distinct entities—that there exist fundamentally separate target phenomena in nature that theorists are unknowingly studying, like discovering that "fish" refers to multiple unrelated clades. This reading gives the thesis its punch: it's not just that we could taxonomize differently, but that we've been *mistaken* about there being a unified natural kind.

The argument requires both. The plausibility comes from the taxonomic reading (uncontroversial that different criteria yield different classifications). The interesting consequence—that consciousness studies involves fundamental confusion rather than mere theoretical dispute—requires the metaphysical reading (there are multiple distinct natural kinds, not one).

But the argument cannot run on either reading alone. On purely taxonomic: trivial, just says different theories classify differently. On purely metaphysical: the premise fails—that theorists use different methods doesn't establish that nature contains multiple distinct phenomena rather than one phenomenon admitting multiple theoretical descriptions.

The equivocation occurs at: "they're revealing divergent reference-fixing for the term itself" slides from "adopting different taxonomic criteria" to "tracking metaphysically distinct entities."

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem requiring a real answer. The thesis needs to establish that methodological divergence indicates metaphysically distinct phenomena, not merely different taxonomic schemes, but its plausibility rests on the weaker claim.

**DEFENDER** — [DEFENDER] BEST DEFENCE

The objection presents a false dichotomy. The thesis doesn't equivocate between "taxonomic" and "metaphysical" readings—it rejects this distinction as the wrong framework for understanding what's at stake in consciousness studies.

**The dichotomy mischaracterizes the thesis**

The objection assumes we must choose: either (a) "merely taxonomic"—different classification schemes for the same underlying reality, or (b) "metaphysically distinct entities"—separate natural kinds in nature's fundamental furniture. But the thesis operates at a different level: it's about what determines whether a theoretical term successfully refers to a unified target of inquiry, regardless of whether that target is "metaphysically fundamental."

Consider the memory case again. When we discovered that "memory" fragments into working, episodic, semantic, and procedural memory, was this "merely taxonomic" or "metaphysically distinct"? The question is ill-posed. What matters is that these are functionally dissociable systems with different neural implementations and different computational roles. They're not just different ways of classifying the same phenomena—they're different mechanisms that can be independently impaired, separately studied, and individually explained. This makes them distinct targets of inquiry whether or not they count as "metaphysically fundamental natural kinds."

The thesis makes an analogous claim about consciousness: integration-based phenomena (what IIT studies) and meta-representation-based phenomena (what HOT studies) are functionally dissociable in the relevant sense. Systems can have high integration without meta-representation (cerebellum), or meta-representation without integration (certain prefrontal damage cases). These can be independently measured, separately manipulated, and individually explained. This makes them distinct targets of inquiry.

**What "different phenomena" means**

The thesis uses "different phenomena" in a specific, technical sense: *different targets of systematic inquiry with distinct individuation conditions and potentially different causal-functional roles*. This is neither "merely taxonomic" (it's not just classification preference) nor "metaphysically heavyweight" (it doesn't require fundamental natural kinds).

The criterion is pragmatic-explanatory: do the putative phenomena come apart in ways that matter for scientific explanation? Can you have one without the other? Do they play different causal roles? Are they realized by different mechanisms? If yes, they're "different phenomena" in the sense relevant for theory construction, regardless of metaphysical status.

IIT-consciousness and HOT-consciousness meet these criteria. They:
- Come apart empirically (different neural correlates)
- Have different functional roles (integration vs. meta-representation)
- Support different counterfactuals (cerebellum has integration, not consciousness; prefrontal meta-representation survives some integration loss)
- Require different explanatory frameworks (information geometry vs. representational hierarchy)

This isn't "merely taxonomic"—these differences constrain explanation and prediction. But it doesn't require "metaphysically distinct entities" in any heavyweight sense.

**Why the premise holds on this reading**

The objection claims the premise "incompatible methodologies indicate incompatible referents" fails on the metaphysical reading because "theorists using different methods doesn't establish that nature contains multiple distinct phenomena." But this attacks a strawman. The thesis doesn't claim that methodological divergence alone establishes distinctness. The argument is:

1. Methodological commitments in consciousness studies directly determine individuation conditions (not just detection methods)
2. These individuation conditions yield functionally dissociable targets (integration vs. meta-representation)
3. Functionally dissociable targets with distinct explanatory roles are different phenomena in the relevant sense
4. Therefore, the methodological divergence indicates referential divergence

The work is done by premises 2-3, not premise 1 alone. The methodological divergence is *evidence* for functional dissociability, not *proof* of metaphysical distinctness.

**The interesting consequence remains**

The objection claims the thesis is "interesting only when 'different phenomena' means metaphysically distinct entities." This is false. The consequence that consciousness studies involves fundamental confusion rather than mere theoretical dispute holds on the pragmatic-explanatory reading.

If IIT-consciousness and HOT-consciousness are functionally dissociable targets with distinct explanatory roles, then treating them as competing theories of one phenomenon is confused—it's a category mistake, like asking whether episodic or procedural memory is "real memory." The confusion is substantial: it leads to misguided research strategies (seeking decisive cross-paradigm experiments), misinterpreted results (treating IIT evidence as challenging HOT), and false hopes for convergence.

This remains a significant diagnostic claim even without metaphysical heavyweight. The field's dysfunction isn't explained by "theories deeply disagreeing about one thing"—it's explained by "theories tracking different things while assuming unity." The practical implications (clarify distinct targets, abandon cross-paradigm adjudication) follow from the pragmatic-explanatory reading, not from any metaphysical claim.

**No equivocation occurs**

The thesis consistently uses "different phenomena" in the pragmatic-explanatory sense throughout. When it says methodological divergence "reveals" different phenomena, it means: reveals that the individuation conditions pick out functionally dissociable targets. When it says theorists are "tacitly referring to different phenomena," it means: their terms successfully refer to distinct targets of systematic inquiry. No slide between readings occurs—the objection imports a dichotomy foreign to the thesis's framework.

VERDICT

The defence succeeds, and the objection fails.

The key move is rejecting the taxonomic/metaphysical dichotomy as the wrong framework for evaluating the thesis. The thesis operates with a pragmatic-explanatory conception of "different phenomena" that's neither "merely taxonomic" (it has explanatory bite) nor "metaphysically heavyweight" (it doesn't require fundamental natural kinds).

The defence successfully shows that on this reading, the premise holds (methodological divergence indicates functional dissociability) and the conclusion is interesting (the field is confused about its target, with practical consequences). No equivocation is needed because the thesis doesn't traffic in the metaphysical/taxonomic distinction the objection imposes.

The memory analogy is particularly effective: it provides a clear case where we recognize distinct phenomena (working vs. episodic memory) without needing to resolve whether these are "metaphysically fundamental" or "merely taxonomic." They're distinct in the sense that matters for science—functionally dissociable, mechanistically different, explanatorily separate. The thesis claims consciousness fragments similarly.

The objection's force depends entirely on imposing a dichotomy the thesis doesn't accept. Once we recognize that "different phenomena" can mean "functionally dissociable targets of inquiry" without metaphysical commitment, the alleged equivocation disappears. The thesis remains substantive because functional dissociability has concrete explanatory and methodological consequences, regardless of metaphysical status.

The defence holds. The thesis doesn't equivocate—it operates with a coherent pragmatic-explanatory conception of phenomenal distinctness that avoids both horns of the objection's dichotomy.

</details>
