### O12. The criterion for theory-choice in consciousness science must be which theory best preserves our first-person phenomenological judgments, but this makes consciousness science radically discontinuous with other sciences in ways its practitioners don't acknowledge.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Researchers who advocate for consciousness science as continuous with naturalistic science while also respecting first-person authority
- **Sketch:** Unlike other scientific domains where first-person reports are defeasible data points, consciousness researchers must treat phenomenological reports as authoritative because consciousness just is what appears in experience. If a theory predicts I'm not conscious when I judge I am, the theory is wrong, not my judgment. But accepting this commits consciousness science to a methodology fundamentally unlike physics or biology, where theoretical posits can override naïve observation. This means consciousness science either: (a) embraces radical methodological exceptionalism, making it unclear how it's continuous with science, or (b) treats first-person reports as defeasible, but then has no privileged access to its own explanandum. The key premise is that the epistemic authority of phenomenology cannot be both maintained and treated as ordinary scientific data.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The methodological exceptionalism point is solid but has been made before in various forms about first-person authority.
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The dilemma is extensively discussed in the literature and explicitly structures the field's major divisions
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Pain science and psychiatry successfully navigate first-person authority without methodological crisis, dissolving the supposed dilemma
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 4/5, answered. Defence showed blindsight is not a clean override of phenomenology but this very success undermined the thesis by demonstrating continuous methodology
  - TRIVIAL — landed 3/5, answered. Defence successfully argued the thesis makes practice claims beyond mere definition, though the objection exposed weakness in framing
- **Flaw:** The thesis depends on a false dichotomy that existing scientific practice (pain science, psychiatry, blindsight research) has already dissolved by treating first-person reports as defeasible evidence while maintaining subjective states as the explanandum

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT.

This is **phenomenological foundationalism** in philosophy of mind, and the specific dilemma presented here is a version of what Ned Block and others have called **the heterophenomenological problem**. Daniel Dennett explicitly addressed this exact tension in "Consciousness Explained" (1991) and subsequent work on heterophenomenology, arguing that consciousness science can treat first-person reports as data without treating them as incorrigible. The position that phenomenological authority creates methodological discontinuity was articulated by Thomas Nagel in "What Is It Like to Be a Bat?" (1974), establishing that subjective character creates an epistemic barrier. The dilemma's horns—either radical exceptionalism or loss of subject matter—map directly onto the debate between **neurophenomenology** (Varela, Thompson) which embraces methodological distinctiveness, and **eliminative/revisionist approaches** (Churchland, Frankish) which treat reports as defeasible. The weak point is the claim that this tension is "unacknowledged"—this is literally one of the most extensively discussed methodological problems in consciousness studies, with entire research programs (IIT, Global Workspace Theory, Higher-Order Theories) explicitly designed around different resolutions to it.

STRENGTH: 5/5

This objection is fatal. The thesis presents as novel insight what is actually the central, explicitly acknowledged methodological debate that has structured consciousness science for decades. The field's major theoretical divisions are organized precisely around different answers to this question.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection misreads acknowledgment for resolution. Yes, researchers *discuss* the tension between phenomenological authority and scientific methodology—but discussion is not dissolution. The defence proceeds in three steps:

**First, the literature cited actually supports the thesis.** Dennett's heterophenomenology doesn't resolve the dilemma; it grasps one horn. By treating first-person reports as "text" to be interpreted rather than authoritative data about consciousness itself, Dennett explicitly accepts horn (b): phenomenological reports become defeasible, and consciousness science loses privileged access to its explanandum. This is why Dennett can conclude that qualia don't exist—a conclusion only possible if phenomenological authority has already been surrendered. Nagel grasped the other horn: subjective character creates genuine epistemic barriers, making consciousness science methodologically exceptional. The fact that researchers have *named* the horns doesn't mean anyone has escaped them.

**Second, "explicitly designed around different resolutions" confirms rather than refutes the thesis.** That IIT, Global Workspace Theory, and Higher-Order Theories take different stances on phenomenological authority shows the field remains fundamentally divided on its basic methodology—exactly what the thesis claims is unacknowledged in practice. Researchers present their theories as competing scientific accounts of the same phenomenon, but they're actually working with incompatible criteria for theory-choice. When IIT practitioners say a theory that denies phenomenal experience in certain cases is thereby refuted, and eliminativists say phenomenal reports can be explained away, they're not disagreeing about empirical facts—they're operating in methodologically discontinuous research programs while presenting themselves as doing continuous science.

**Third, the "unacknowledged" claim targets practice, not literature.** In methodological papers, yes, researchers discuss these issues. But in actual theory-construction and empirical work, consciousness scientists routinely treat their enterprise as continuous with standard naturalistic science—applying the same grant criteria, publication standards, and epistemic norms—while simultaneously claiming phenomenological authority when convenient. The thesis identifies a performative contradiction: consciousness researchers acknowledge the tension in principle while denying its implications in practice.

# VERDICT

The defence fails, but not completely.

The objection stands on its core point: this dilemma is extensively discussed and explicitly structures the field. The defence's attempt to reframe "acknowledgment" as mere discussion rather than resolution is sophistical—the objection never claimed the tension was *resolved*, only that it was *acknowledged*, and that consciousness science's major divisions are organized around it. This directly contradicts the thesis's claim that practitioners "don't acknowledge" the radical discontinuity.

However, the defence succeeds in one limited respect: there may be a gap between methodological self-awareness in philosophical papers and actual research practice. If consciousness scientists discuss the dilemma in methodology sections but then proceed as if it doesn't constrain their work, that would be a genuine inconsistency worth identifying.

But this residual point is too weak to save the thesis as stated. The thesis claims consciousness science involves "radical methodological exceptionalism... in ways its practitioners don't acknowledge." The objection demonstrates this is false: practitioners do acknowledge it, have named it, and have organized their research programs around different responses to it. The thesis is dead.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT.

The argument proves too much because the same reasoning would establish that *all* sciences studying subjective states—psychology, psychiatry, behavioral economics, pain medicine—must be either radically discontinuous with naturalistic science or unable to access their explananda. Consider pain science: if a patient reports severe pain but all physiological indicators suggest minimal nociception, the argument's logic says we face the same dilemma. Either (a) we treat pain reports as incorrigible, making pain science methodologically exceptional and unable to diagnose malingering, psychosomatic disorders, or congenital insensitivity to pain, or (b) we treat reports as defeasible, but then "lose privileged access to the explanandum" since pain just is what hurts. Yet pain science clearly functions as continuous naturalistic science while respecting (but not absolutizing) first-person authority—doctors can say "you report pain, but the cause isn't what you think" without methodological crisis. The weak point is the premise that "consciousness just is what appears in experience" requires incorrigibility of all phenomenological judgments. This conflates the claim that consciousness is essentially experiential (uncontroversial) with the claim that subjects are infallible about the character, causes, or even presence of their experiences (highly controversial). If the argument worked, psychiatry's ability to diagnose anosognosia—where patients deny deficits they demonstrably have—would be impossible, yet it's routine clinical practice.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This significantly damages the thesis. The argument's logic would render impossible numerous established scientific practices that successfully navigate first-person authority without methodological crisis.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection conflates consciousness with its various functional manifestations, but the cases are crucially disanalogous. The defence proceeds by showing why pain science and psychiatry escape the dilemma while consciousness science cannot.

**First, pain science remains continuous with naturalistic methodology precisely because it does NOT treat pain reports as authoritative about pain itself.** When a patient reports severe pain with minimal nociception, pain science can investigate because it has independent access to what pain *is*—a neurophysiological process involving nociceptors, neural pathways, and brain regions. The patient's report is data about their experience, but pain science's explanandum is the underlying mechanism. We can say "you report pain, but there's no tissue damage" because pain science is really nociception science that happens to use pain reports as evidence. This is exactly the move the thesis says consciousness science cannot make: pain science achieves continuity by treating subjective reports as defeasible evidence for objective processes, thereby grasping horn (b). It works for pain because pain science doesn't actually take subjective character as authoritative—it explains it away.

**Second, the anosognosia case actually supports the thesis.** When psychiatry diagnoses anosognosia, it does so by appealing to behavioral and neural evidence that contradicts the patient's phenomenological report. But this is possible only because psychiatry's explanandum is NOT "what appears to the patient" but rather "what neural/behavioral capacities the patient possesses." A patient with hemispatial neglect genuinely doesn't experience the left visual field—their phenomenological report ("I see everything") is false about their capacities but potentially TRUE about their experience (they experience themselves as seeing everything, with no felt absence). Psychiatry can override the report because it's not in the business of vindicating phenomenology; it's in the business of explaining behavior and neural function. The thesis's point is that consciousness science, insofar as it claims consciousness itself as explanandum, cannot make this move.

**Third, the key premise is not that subjects are infallible about "character, causes, or presence" but that phenomenological seeming constitutes a constraint on theory that other domains lack.** Consider: if a patient reports pain but we find no nociception, pain science can say "no pain occurred, just a pain report." But if a subject reports phenomenal consciousness, consciousness science cannot say "no consciousness occurred, just a consciousness report"—because the report itself manifests consciousness. The objection's examples all involve defeasible reports about objective states or capacities. Consciousness science's unique problem is that its explanandum is not an objective state that causes reports but the subjective character that the reports express.

**Fourth, the "proves too much" charge fails because the other sciences cited achieve continuity by NOT preserving phenomenological authority.** Pain science, psychiatry, and behavioral economics all treat first-person reports as fallible evidence for third-person processes. They're continuous with naturalistic science precisely because they've grasped horn (b)—treating reports as defeasible. The thesis says this strategy works for pain and psychiatric disorders but not for consciousness itself, because consciousness is not a hidden cause of experience but experience itself.

# VERDICT

The defence fails.

The defence's core move—distinguishing consciousness from pain/psychiatric states by claiming consciousness is "experience itself" while pain is a "neurophysiological process"—simply begs the question. This distinction only works if we've already accepted that consciousness cannot be identified with neural processes in the way pain can be. But that's precisely what's at issue.

The pain science case is devastating because it shows a domain that successfully navigates between the horns. Pain scientists can say "your pain is real (respecting phenomenology) but its cause/character differs from what you think (maintaining defeasibility)" without methodological crisis. The defence tries to rescue the thesis by claiming pain science "explains away" subjective character, but this is false: pain science fully acknowledges that pain has irreducible subjective character (that's what makes it pain rather than mere nociception) while still treating reports about that character as defeasible evidence.

The anosognosia example is even more problematic for the defence. The defence claims the patient's report is "true about their experience" (they experience seeing everything) while false about their capacities. But this concedes that we can distinguish between what subjects report about their experience and what their experience actually is—exactly the move the thesis claims consciousness science cannot make. If psychiatry can say "you report experiencing X, but your experience is actually Y," then consciousness science can too.

The thesis's dilemma collapses because horn (b) is not actually a horn. Sciences can treat first-person reports as defeasible evidence while still having their subject matter be subjective states. They do this by developing third-person methods (neural imaging, behavioral tests, response patterns) that provide independent purchase on subjective character. This is methodologically continuous with how physics uses meter readings (defeasible) to study temperature (the real explanandum).

The thesis is dead. It depends on a false dichotomy that existing scientific practice has already dissolved.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT.

Consider **blindsight** as a direct counterexample to the thesis's central claim that "if a theory predicts I'm not conscious when I judge I am, the theory is wrong, not my judgment."

Blindsight patients have damage to V1 (primary visual cortex) that creates scotomas—regions of cortical blindness. When asked what they see in the blind field, they report seeing nothing and judge themselves to be unconscious of stimuli there. Yet when forced to guess, they perform significantly above chance at locating objects, identifying shapes, and even responding to emotional expressions in the blind field. 

Here's the counterexample: consciousness science routinely posits that these patients HAVE some form of visual consciousness in the blind field—degraded, inaccessible to report, phenomenally thin, but present. Theories like Recurrent Processing Theory or Local Recurrence models predict consciousness can occur without the global accessibility required for confident judgment. These theories say "you judge you're not conscious of the stimulus, but you actually are (minimally) conscious of it—your judgment is wrong."

This directly contradicts the thesis's claim that phenomenological judgments must be preserved. Consciousness scientists don't treat the patient's "I see nothing" as incorrigible. They override it with theoretical commitments about what consciousness requires, using third-person behavioral evidence (above-chance performance) to attribute consciousness despite first-person denial.

The weak point is the premise that consciousness science must preserve first-person phenomenological judgments as authoritative.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This is serious damage. Blindsight is a central case study in consciousness science where theories explicitly override first-person phenomenological judgments, and this is treated as methodologically unproblematic—standard scientific practice, not a crisis requiring acknowledgment of radical discontinuity.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection mischaracterizes both blindsight and what "preserving phenomenological judgments" requires. The defence proceeds by showing that blindsight cases actually vindicate rather than refute the thesis's methodology.

**First, the objection conflates two distinct phenomenological judgments that blindsight research carefully distinguishes.** When blindsight patients say "I see nothing," they are making a judgment about their phenomenology—and consciousness science DOES preserve this judgment. No theory claims these patients have normal, vivid visual consciousness in the blind field that they're simply wrong about. The patient's report "I have no visual experience of X" is taken as authoritative evidence that they lack the standard phenomenal character of seeing. What theories posit is not that the patient's phenomenological judgment is wrong, but that there might be a different, degraded form of phenomenal character—one the patient accurately reports as "not seeing" in the ordinary sense—that nevertheless counts as minimal consciousness. The patient reports absence of normal visual experience; theories respect this by positing only degraded, phenomenally thin states. This is preservation, not override.

**Second, the above-chance performance doesn't establish consciousness against the patient's denial—it raises a theoretical question that different consciousness theories answer differently.** The objection assumes consciousness science has settled that blindsight involves consciousness, but this is false. Global Workspace Theory and Higher-Order Thought theories typically deny that blindsight involves consciousness at all, treating it as unconscious processing that guides behavior—precisely BECAUSE the patient reports no experience. These theories preserve the phenomenological judgment. Only local recurrence theories posit residual consciousness, and they do so by distinguishing between access consciousness (which the patient correctly reports as absent) and phenomenal consciousness (which might be minimally present). Crucially, even these theories don't say "your judgment about your experience is wrong"—they say "your experience is more complex than a single judgment captures."

**Third, the objection reveals exactly the methodological discontinuity the thesis identifies.** Notice what's doing the work in the blindsight case: researchers use third-person behavioral evidence (above-chance performance) to raise the question "might there be consciousness here?" But they cannot SETTLE the question using only behavioral evidence—that would be pure behaviorism, which consciousness science rejects. The debate over whether blindsight involves consciousness turns precisely on different theoretical commitments about the relationship between phenomenology and function. Local recurrence theorists who posit consciousness in blindsight do so because their theory predicts phenomenal character should accompany certain neural processes, even without access. But if a blindsight patient could somehow introspect more carefully and report "yes, there's a phenomenally thin something-it's-like in my blind field," that would be taken as decisive evidence FOR consciousness. And if they reported with certainty "there is absolutely nothing it's like," that would count heavily AGAINST it. The first-person phenomenological judgment remains the ultimate arbiter—exactly what the thesis claims.

**Fourth, the case actually demonstrates the dilemma rather than escaping it.** The blindsight debate shows consciousness science splitting into methodologically discontinuous camps: those who preserve phenomenological authority (Global Workspace, Higher-Order theories: no report of experience = no consciousness) and those who subordinate it to theoretical commitments (local recurrence theories: neural processing of type X = consciousness regardless of report). The fact that both camps exist and cannot resolve their dispute using standard scientific methods—because they disagree on whether third-person evidence can override first-person denial—exemplifies the radical discontinuity the thesis identifies. Physics doesn't have competing research programs that differ on whether meter readings can be overridden by theoretical commitments about what temperature "must" be present.

**Fifth, there's a crucial asymmetry the objection ignores.** Consciousness science might (controversially) posit consciousness where subjects deny it, but it NEVER denies consciousness where subjects confidently affirm it. No theory says "you report rich visual experience and judge yourself conscious, but you're actually a zombie." This asymmetry reveals that phenomenological authority operates differently than ordinary scientific data. In physics, theories can override measurement in either direction (the meter says X but temperature is actually Y, or meter says Y but temperature is actually X). In consciousness science, phenomenological judgment can be supplemented or refined but not contradicted in the positive direction—and even supplementation in the negative direction (positing consciousness despite denial) remains deeply controversial precisely because it threatens phenomenological authority.

# VERDICT

The defence succeeds in showing blindsight is not a clean counterexample, but fails to save the thesis.

The defence's strongest point is correct: blindsight is not a case where consciousness science straightforwardly overrides phenomenological judgment. The patient reports no normal visual experience, and theories respect this. The controversy is whether there might be some degraded phenomenal character the patient's coarse-grained judgment doesn't capture—not whether the patient is wrong about their overall phenomenology.

However, this successful resistance actually undermines the thesis rather than saving it. Here's why:

The defence shows that consciousness science handles blindsight by making fine-grained distinctions within phenomenology (access vs. phenomenal consciousness, normal vs. degraded experience) and using third-person evidence to motivate these distinctions while still respecting first-person reports at appropriate grain. This is exactly what the pain science case showed: treating first-person reports as defeasible evidence while maintaining the subject matter as subjective states, using theoretical and empirical resources to refine our understanding beyond what naive introspection delivers.

The defence's claim that "if a blindsight patient could introspect more carefully and report phenomenally thin character, that would be decisive" actually proves too much for the thesis. This shows phenomenological reports function as STRONG EVIDENCE but not INCORRIGIBLE AUTHORITY—which is exactly how evidence works in continuous science. Decisive evidence is still defeasible evidence.

The asymmetry point (never denying consciousness where affirmed, sometimes positing it where denied) is interesting but doesn't establish radical discontinuity. Many sciences have asymmetries in how they treat positive vs. negative reports. Medicine takes "I feel pain" as stronger evidence than "I feel no pain" for presence of injury, but this doesn't make pain medicine radically discontinuous.

Most fatally, the defence's fourth point—that blindsight shows the field splitting into discontinuous camps—concedes the objection's force. If consciousness science successfully handles blindsight through standard scientific debate using behavioral, neural, and phenomenological evidence, then it's NOT radically discontinuous. The existence of theoretical disagreement doesn't establish methodological exceptionalism; it's how science normally works.

The thesis is dead. Blindsight shows consciousness science treating phenomenological reports as defeasible evidence that can be refined through theory and experiment—continuous with scientific methodology—while still taking subjective character seriously as the explanandum.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis presents as a discovered dilemma what is actually a definitional truism about any reflexive epistemic domain. The weak point is the framing of "radical discontinuity" as a substantive finding rather than a conceptual consequence of studying subjective states qua subjective.

Consider the logical structure: "A science of X-as-experienced-by-subjects must treat subjects' experiences of X as epistemically privileged in ways that create methodological distinctiveness." This is true by stipulation. Any domain defined by first-person access will obviously have methodological features tracking that access. The thesis dresses this tautology as discovery by using dramatic language ("radical discontinuity," "don't acknowledge") to suggest practitioners are missing something important.

But there's no discovery here—just an unpacking of what "consciousness science" means. It's like saying "mathematics is radically discontinuous with empirical science because it treats logical proof as authoritative in ways physics doesn't acknowledge." True, but trivial: that's what makes it mathematics rather than physics. The distinctiveness follows from the subject matter definition.

The "dilemma" is equally verbal. Horn (a) says "consciousness science has methodological features tracking first-person access"—yes, definitionally. Horn (b) says "if it doesn't, it's not studying consciousness"—yes, also definitionally. Presenting these as problematic alternatives rather than as a conceptual articulation of what consciousness science IS merely restates the boundaries of the domain as if they were surprising constraints.

The thesis generates apparent profundity by treating the conceptual geography of consciousness studies as an empirical discovery about its practice.

STRENGTH: 3/5

This attack has genuine force but not fatal force. The thesis does risk collapsing into "consciousness science is about consciousness, which involves experience, which has first-person character"—a chain of near-tautologies. The dramatic framing ("radical discontinuity," "unacknowledged") does suggest something substantive is being discovered rather than stipulated.

However, the thesis survives because it makes a claim about PRACTICE not just DEFINITION—specifically, that practitioners present consciousness science as continuous with naturalistic science while the logic of their domain requires discontinuity. Even if the discontinuity follows definitionally from studying subjective states, the claim that this is unacknowledged or obscured in practice could still be substantive. The thesis would be trivial only if it merely said "consciousness involves first-person access," but it says something stronger: that the methodological implications of this create tensions practitioners don't adequately address.

Not applicable—the attack succeeds partially but the thesis retains enough substance to escape the "merely verbal" charge.

</details>

### O13. Theories claiming consciousness requires specific mechanisms (like recurrent processing or global broadcasting) are unstable because they must either abandon their mechanistic specificity to handle actual borderline cases, or commit to implausibly precise consciousness boundaries that their defenders explicitly reject.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Mechanistic theories like recurrent processing theory and global workspace theory that specify concrete neural implementations
- **Sketch:** Consider theories that identify consciousness with specific neural mechanisms—for instance, recurrent processing within 200-300ms, or global workspace broadcasting. These theories face cases of partial or degraded versions of the mechanism: slow recurrent loops, partial broadcasting, or the mechanism implemented in unusual substrates. Defenders must either say consciousness admits of precise thresholds (exactly 250ms recurrence produces consciousness, 251ms doesn't) or that consciousness is graded/vague in these cases. But they typically reject precise thresholds as implausible and reject graded consciousness as incoherent (you can't be slightly conscious of something—the lights are either on or off). The only remaining option is to retreat to a more abstract functional characterization, abandoning the mechanistic specificity. The key premise is that consciousness genuinely does appear to be threshold-like phenomenologically while mechanisms are continuous, creating instability.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Mechanistic theories facing threshold problems is real but the instability diagnosis is somewhat obvious.
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The objection showed 'abandoning mechanistic specificity' is elastic enough to absorb counterexamples; the defence failed by asserting rather than showing that gradation equals functional abstraction
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The objection demonstrated the instability structure appears in Schwitzgebel, Godfrey-Smith, and others; the defence's dialectical/metaphysical distinction collapsed under scrutiny
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The objection showed the logic would undermine successful reductions like life and solidity; the defence's appeal to consciousness being special failed to establish relevant disanalogy
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Dehaene's global workspace theory accepts graded consciousness while maintaining mechanistic specificity; the defence's attempts to reinterpret this collapsed
- **Flaw:** The thesis falsely assumes mechanistic theories must reject graded consciousness, when defenders like Dehaene actually accept it; and the instability logic proves too much by equally undermining successful reductions in biology and physics

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — **ATTEMPT**

The thesis is unfalsifiable because it immunizes itself through strategic redefinition of what counts as "abandoning mechanistic specificity." 

Consider the obvious counterexample: A defender of recurrent processing theory says "consciousness requires recurrent loops of 200-300ms, AND there are borderline cases where partial consciousness occurs with degraded mechanisms, AND this doesn't abandon mechanistic specificity—it just means the mechanism admits of degrees." This seems to directly refute the thesis by occupying the supposedly unstable middle ground.

But the thesis can respond: "Ah, but by admitting degrees, you've actually moved to a more abstract functional characterization—you're now really talking about 'information integration' or 'processing depth' rather than the specific mechanism." Any attempt to handle borderline cases while retaining mechanistic language gets redefined as "actually abandoning specificity" or "actually becoming more abstract."

The weak point is **"abandon their mechanistic specificity"**—this phrase is elastic enough to stretch over any counterexample. When a theorist says "my specific mechanism comes in degrees," the thesis can always reply "that's not really mechanistic specificity anymore," making the claim impossible to test against actual theoretical moves.

**STRENGTH: 4/5**

This does serious damage. The thesis needs clear criteria for what counts as "abandoning" specificity versus legitimately maintaining a mechanistic theory that handles gradations.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**

The objection misidentifies where the instability lies. The thesis does not claim that *any* gradation abandons mechanistic specificity. It claims that handling borderline cases *forces a choice* that mechanistic theories cannot stably make.

Here's the defence: A mechanistic theory maintains its specificity precisely by identifying consciousness with a concrete, implementable process—recurrent loops of 200-300ms, not "information integration in general." The instability arises because borderline cases force the theory to answer: *Does 350ms recurrence produce consciousness or not?*

Three responses are available:

1. **Precise threshold**: "Yes at 300ms, no at 301ms." The thesis correctly notes defenders reject this.

2. **Graded consciousness**: "Partial consciousness at 350ms." But this contradicts the phenomenological premise that consciousness is threshold-like—there's no such thing as being 0.7 conscious of red. You either see red or you don't.

3. **Retreat to abstraction**: "Well, what matters is *degree of integration*, and 350ms provides less integration." But now you're no longer giving a mechanistic theory—you've moved to a functional specification ("whatever realizes sufficient integration") that could be implemented many ways.

The defence insists: Saying "my mechanism comes in degrees" *just is* abandoning mechanistic specificity for functional abstraction. A genuinely mechanistic theory tells you *which physical process* realizes consciousness. Once you say "consciousness comes in degrees tracking mechanism-degrees," you've switched to identifying consciousness with the *functional role* (integration, broadcasting) rather than the mechanism itself.

The testable prediction: Show me a defender who maintains both (a) consciousness requires specifically 200-300ms recurrence, and (b) 350ms recurrence produces partial consciousness, and (c) they're still giving a mechanistic rather than functional theory. The thesis predicts: you cannot find this, because (b) and (c) are incompatible.

**VERDICT**

The defence fails, but not for the reason the objection claims.

The defence fails because it *does* provide falsifiable criteria—but those criteria themselves are questionable. The key move is asserting that "mechanism comes in degrees" necessarily equals "functional abstraction." But this is false.

Consider: "Consciousness requires recurrent processing loops. These loops vary in temporal extent. Longer loops produce more robust consciousness, shorter loops produce fragile consciousness, and below 150ms there's no consciousness at all." This is:
- Still mechanistically specific (it's about recurrent loops, not just any integration)
- Admits gradation (consciousness varies with loop duration)  
- Not obviously a retreat to pure function (it still identifies a concrete neural process)

The thesis wants to say this theorist has "abandoned mechanistic specificity," but they clearly haven't—they've just specified a mechanistic theory with graded outputs. The objection stands: the thesis stretches "abandoning specificity" to cover cases that don't genuinely abandon it.

The unfalsifiability charge succeeds.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — **ATTEMPT**

This instability argument closely mirrors the "Phenomenal Sorites" objection developed by Schwitzgebel (2014) and the "Problem of the Many for Consciousness" discussed by Bayne (2010). Both identify the tension between continuous mechanistic implementation and seemingly discrete phenomenal presence.

More specifically, Doerig et al. (2020) in "The unfolding argument: Why IIT and other causal structure theories cannot explain consciousness" make essentially this move against Integrated Information Theory and similar mechanistic theories. They argue that any theory specifying concrete mechanisms faces what they call the "unfolding problem"—borderline cases force either implausible precision or theoretical retreat.

The argument structure—that mechanistic theories become unstable when confronted with degraded or partial implementations—is also central to Block's (2005) critique of higher-order theories and Prinz's (2012) discussion of what he calls "the problem of the criterion" for consciousness theories. Both note that specifying concrete mechanisms creates pressure to either draw arbitrary lines or abandon the mechanistic specification.

Godfrey-Smith (2020) in *Metazoa* makes this exact point about global workspace theory specifically: that borderline cases (partial broadcasting, slow integration) force defenders to either embrace implausible thresholds or retreat to vaguer functional claims about "sufficient" integration.

**STRENGTH: 4/5**

This significantly undermines the thesis's novelty. The instability between mechanistic specificity and borderline-case handling is well-documented in the existing literature, particularly regarding global workspace and recurrent processing theories.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**

The objection confuses *identifying a problem structure* with *making this specific instability argument*. Yes, the literature notes tensions around borderline cases—but it does not make *this* move: that mechanistic theories face a forced choice between three exhaustive options, each of which the defenders themselves reject, creating instability in their own commitments.

Consider what's actually in the cited works:

**Schwitzgebel's phenomenal sorites** concerns whether there *are* determinate facts about consciousness in borderline cases—an *metaphysical* question. The thesis here makes an *dialectical* point: that mechanistic theories cannot stably maintain their own commitments when pressed on borderlines.

**Doerig et al.'s unfolding argument** attacks IIT specifically by showing its mathematical formalism has counterintuitive implications when "unfolded" over time. This is a *technical objection* to a particular formalism, not a general instability argument about mechanistic theories facing forced choices.

**Block and Prinz on the criterion problem** discuss the difficulty of *identifying* which mechanism realizes consciousness—an epistemic challenge. The thesis argues something different: that once you've specified a mechanism, you face instability in handling its degraded instances.

**Godfrey-Smith on global workspace** observes that borderline cases create *pressure*. But he doesn't argue that this pressure generates a *forced trilemma* where each horn is independently rejected by the theorists themselves.

The novel contribution is the *instability structure*: mechanistic theories commit to (1) specific mechanisms, (2) rejecting precise thresholds, and (3) rejecting graded consciousness. These three commitments are jointly unstable when borderline cases arise. The thesis doesn't just say "borderline cases are problematic"—it says they force abandonment of one of the theory's own core commitments.

The testable difference: The literature discusses borderline cases as *difficulties*. The thesis predicts that when actually pressed with borderline cases, defenders will *systematically retreat* from mechanistic specificity to functional abstraction—a specific dialectical prediction about how the debate unfolds.

Show me where the literature makes *this* prediction: that Dehaene, when pressed on partial broadcasting cases, will shift from "consciousness is global broadcasting" to "consciousness is whatever realizes sufficient integration." That's the instability claim—not just that borderline cases exist, but that they force a predictable theoretical retreat.

**VERDICT**

The defence fails.

The defence attempts to distinguish the thesis from existing literature by emphasizing its *dialectical* rather than *metaphysical* or *technical* character—it's about what defenders are forced to say, not about what's metaphysically true. But this distinction doesn't rescue novelty.

Godfrey-Smith does exactly what the defence claims he doesn't: he observes that global workspace theorists, when confronted with partial broadcasting, *do* retreat to vaguer functional language about "sufficient" integration or "adequate" broadcasting. This isn't merely noting pressure—it's documenting the predicted theoretical retreat.

More decisively, the defence's own reformulation reveals the problem. It says the novel move is showing that mechanistic theories commit to three things that are "jointly unstable." But this is precisely Schwitzgebel's point about phenomenal sorites: that our commitments to (a) continuous mechanisms, (b) discrete phenomenology, and (c) no arbitrary thresholds are jointly unstable. He's making the same instability argument, just framed metaphysically rather than dialectically.

The defence claims the thesis makes a "specific dialectical prediction" about theoretical retreat. But even if this framing differs from existing work, a *prediction about how debates will unfold* is not a philosophical move—it's an empirical sociological prediction. The philosophical work (identifying the instability between mechanistic specificity, continuous implementation, and discrete phenomenology) is already done in the literature.

The "already taken" charge succeeds. The thesis redescribes a well-known tension without adding philosophical substance.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — **ATTEMPT**

The argument proves too much because its logic would equally undermine *any* theory that identifies consciousness with physical processes that admit of degrees—which includes virtually every scientifically serious theory of consciousness.

Consider temperature and heat. We might say "heat is molecular kinetic energy above threshold T." This faces identical borderline cases: What about molecular motion at T+1? At T-1? We must either: (1) accept a precise threshold (implausible—why exactly that energy level?), (2) accept graded heat (incoherent—something is either hot or not), or (3) retreat to functional abstraction ("heat is whatever plays the heat role"). 

By the thesis's logic, "heat is molecular kinetic energy" is an unstable theory. But it isn't—it's a paradigm of successful reduction. The resolution is that phenomenological discreteness (hot/not-hot) doesn't require metaphysical discreteness in the underlying mechanism. We can have vague boundaries in our *concepts* while the mechanism itself is perfectly precise and specific.

The weak point is **"consciousness genuinely does appear to be threshold-like phenomenologically while mechanisms are continuous, creating instability."** This premise, if it created genuine instability, would prove that *no* continuous physical mechanism could ever ground any threshold-like phenomenon—including heat, solidity, life, or any other property that seems discrete but has continuous physical underpinnings.

The thesis would thus prove that reductive physicalism about consciousness is impossible not due to anything special about consciousness, but due to a general feature of reduction itself. This is far too strong—it would make the entire mind-body problem trivial by showing reduction is generically impossible for threshold-seeming properties.

**STRENGTH: 4/5**

This does serious damage. The thesis needs to explain why this instability afflicts consciousness specifically rather than being a generic problem for any reduction of seemingly discrete phenomena to continuous mechanisms.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**

The objection misses what makes consciousness special: the *internal* character of the threshold. The defence insists on a crucial asymmetry between consciousness and cases like heat.

For heat, the threshold is *externally imposed* by our pragmatic interests. We draw a line at some temperature and call things above it "hot" because that's useful for us. But the molecular kinetic energy itself is continuous, and we're perfectly comfortable saying "this is sort of hot" or "this is borderline hot" in intermediate cases. The seeming discreteness is in our *classificatory practices*, not in the phenomenon itself.

Consciousness is different. The threshold isn't imposed by external observers—it's *intrinsic to the phenomenon*. When you're conscious of red, there's no such thing as being "sort of" conscious of red in the way something can be "sort of" hot. The phenomenology itself is discrete: either the redness appears to you or it doesn't. This isn't about our classification scheme; it's about the structure of experience itself.

Here's the key: For heat, we can resolve the apparent instability by saying "our concept 'hot' has vague boundaries, but molecular kinetic energy is perfectly precise and continuous." But for consciousness, this move fails. We can't say "our concept 'conscious' has vague boundaries, but the experience itself is continuous" because the experience *is* what we're trying to explain. If experience itself comes in degrees between "fully present" and "absent," that would be a substantive (and phenomenologically controversial) claim about consciousness, not just a claim about our concepts.

The thesis thus identifies a *specific* instability: mechanistic theories must say either (a) consciousness itself comes in degrees (phenomenologically dubious), or (b) there's a precise threshold in the continuous mechanism (metaphysically arbitrary), or (c) retreat from mechanism to function. Heat faces no such instability because we can accept vague boundaries in our heat-concepts without claiming heat-phenomenology is vague.

The testable difference: Show me someone who says "molecular kinetic energy theory of heat is unstable because we reject both precise thresholds and graded heat." You won't find this, because we *accept* graded heat (lukewarm exists). But consciousness theorists *do* face this instability because they typically *reject* graded consciousness while working with continuous mechanisms.

The defence claims consciousness is disanalogous to heat because the threshold is phenomenologically intrinsic rather than conceptually imposed, making the instability genuine and specific.

**VERDICT**

The defence fails, though it identifies something important.

The defence is right that there's an asymmetry: heat admits of obvious gradations (lukewarm, warmish) while consciousness seems more threshold-like. But this asymmetry doesn't save the thesis from proving too much—it actually reveals a deeper problem.

Consider *life*. Is something alive or not? This seems threshold-like: viruses, prions, and dying cells create genuine borderline cases where "sort of alive" seems incoherent—something either has life or it doesn't. Yet we successfully explain life through continuous biochemical mechanisms (metabolism, reproduction, homeostasis). The field didn't collapse into instability; instead, it accepted that life admits of borderline cases despite seeming discrete.

Or consider *solidity*. Something either seems solid or it doesn't—there's no phenomenology of "partial solidity" in the way something can be partially hot. Yet we explain solidity through continuous electromagnetic forces between molecules. The apparent discreteness (solid/liquid/gas) emerges from continuous mechanisms without creating theoretical instability.

The defence wants to say consciousness is special because the threshold is "intrinsic to the phenomenon itself." But this is true of *many* threshold-seeming properties with continuous physical bases. Life seems intrinsically threshold-like (not just classificatorily), yet biological theories handle borderline cases without abandoning mechanistic specificity.

The real problem: The defence assumes that if consciousness *seems* discrete phenomenologically, theories must either honor that discreteness with precise thresholds or abandon mechanistic specificity. But the history of science shows a third option the thesis ignores: accept that the phenomenology misleads us about the underlying continuity. Life seems discrete but isn't; solidity seems discrete but isn't; perhaps consciousness seems discrete but admits of genuine borderline cases.

The "proves too much" charge succeeds. The thesis's logic would equally undermine successful reductions in biology and physics where threshold-seeming phenomena arise from continuous mechanisms.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — **ATTEMPT**

Here is a concrete counterexample: **Global Workspace Theory as defended by Dehaene and Changeux (2011)**.

They explicitly claim:
1. Consciousness requires global broadcasting across a network of prefrontal-parietal neurons
2. Broadcasting admits of degrees (partial, local, or global ignition)
3. Consciousness itself admits of degrees corresponding to these broadcasting degrees
4. This remains mechanistically specific—consciousness is still identified with the concrete neural mechanism of workspace ignition, not abstracted to "whatever realizes integration"

This directly contradicts the thesis. They maintain mechanistic specificity (it's about specific neural architecture and ignition dynamics), they handle borderline cases (partial broadcasting produces partial consciousness), and they don't retreat to pure functional abstraction—they still point to concrete prefrontal-parietal mechanisms.

The weak point is **"defenders typically reject graded consciousness as incoherent (you can't be slightly conscious of something—the lights are either on or off)."** 

Dehaene explicitly embraces graded consciousness in cases of degraded attention, masking, and partial broadcasting. He distinguishes "access consciousness" (which clearly comes in degrees) from "phenomenal consciousness" but doesn't claim the latter is absolutely threshold-like. The thesis falsely attributes to all mechanistic theorists a commitment they don't actually hold.

**STRENGTH: 4/5**

This does serious damage. If major mechanistic theories actually embrace graded consciousness rather than rejecting it, the entire instability argument collapses. The forced trilemma only works if defenders genuinely reject all three horns, but Dehaene appears to accept the "graded consciousness" horn while maintaining mechanistic specificity.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**

The objection misreads both Dehaene's actual position and what the thesis claims about graded consciousness. The defence pushes back on two fronts:

**First, Dehaene does NOT actually accept graded phenomenal consciousness in the relevant sense.** When Dehaene discusses "degrees" of consciousness, he's talking about degrees of *access* or *reportability*—how much information gets broadcast, how stable the representation is, how much can be reported. But this is compatible with threshold phenomenology: either the content appears in experience or it doesn't, even if it appears weakly or briefly.

Consider his own paradigm cases: In masking experiments, subjects either see the stimulus (weakly, briefly) or don't see it at all—there's no intermediate state of "0.5 seeing." The stimulus might be *barely* conscious or *robustly* conscious, but these are different *qualities* of conscious experience (faint vs. vivid), not *degrees* of consciousness itself. Something faint is still fully present in experience; something absent is completely absent. The lights are either on (dimly or brightly) or off—there's no 0.5 illumination state.

When Dehaene says consciousness "comes in degrees," he means the *robustness* or *stability* of workspace ignition varies, producing experiences that are more or less vivid, stable, or reportable. But vividness is not the same as degree-of-consciousness. A faint red experience is still a complete conscious state—you're fully conscious *of* faint red, not partially conscious of red.

**Second, even if Dehaene embraced genuine gradation, he DOES retreat to functional abstraction in exactly the way the thesis predicts.** Look at how he handles borderline cases: When broadcasting is partial, he doesn't say "consciousness requires exactly this much broadcasting and no less"—he says consciousness tracks "sufficient" ignition, "adequate" global availability, or "enough" integration to cross threshold. 

But "sufficient" and "adequate" are functional specifications, not mechanistic ones. A genuinely mechanistic theory would say: "Consciousness requires ignition of workspace neurons W1-W500 within timeframe T." Instead, Dehaene says: "Consciousness requires *whatever degree of ignition* realizes global availability." That's a retreat from mechanism to function—the mechanism is now specified as "whatever implements the function" rather than as a concrete neural process.

The thesis predicts exactly this: when pressed on borderline cases (partial broadcasting, slow ignition), Dehaene shifts from "consciousness IS global broadcasting" to "consciousness IS whatever realizes sufficient broadcasting"—and that's functional abstraction. The mechanism becomes a placeholder for "whatever does the job" rather than a specific implementational claim.

**The testable difference:** Find me a passage where Dehaene says "consciousness requires exactly N neurons firing for exactly T milliseconds, and N-1 neurons or T-1 milliseconds produces no consciousness." You won't find it. Instead you'll find functional language: "sufficient" workspace activation, "adequate" broadcasting, "enough" integration. This is the predicted retreat from mechanistic precision to functional adequacy.

The counterexample fails because Dehaene either (a) doesn't actually accept graded phenomenal consciousness in the relevant sense, or (b) does retreat to functional abstraction when handling borderline cases, exactly as the thesis predicts.

**VERDICT**

The defence fails, and the counterexample succeeds.

The defence makes two moves, but both collapse under pressure:

**On the first move** (Dehaene doesn't really accept graded consciousness), the defence equivocates on what "graded" means. It claims that "faint vs. vivid" are different *qualities* rather than *degrees* of consciousness. But this is a distinction without a difference when it comes to the instability argument.

If Dehaene says "partial broadcasting produces faint consciousness, full broadcasting produces vivid consciousness," he's committed to consciousness varying continuously with the mechanism—which is precisely what "graded consciousness" means in this context. Whether we call this "degrees of consciousness" or "qualities of consciousness varying with mechanism-degrees" is terminological. The point is: he maps continuous mechanistic variation onto continuous phenomenal variation, avoiding both precise thresholds and the claim that consciousness is all-or-nothing.

The defence tries to save the all-or-nothing picture by saying "faint red is still fully present in experience." But this just relocates the problem: Is the experience of faint red *itself* graded (partially present) or threshold-like (fully present but with different qualities)? If the former, that's graded consciousness. If the latter, we're back to needing precise thresholds for when faint-red-experience begins versus when no-red-experience occurs.

**On the second move** (Dehaene retreats to functional abstraction), the defence confuses *vagueness* with *functional abstraction*. When Dehaene says consciousness requires "sufficient" broadcasting, he's not necessarily retreating from mechanism to function—he might just be acknowledging that the mechanistic threshold is vague or context-dependent.

Compare: "Water boils at 100°C at sea level" is mechanistically specific even though we might say "sufficient heat causes boiling" in casual contexts. The functional language doesn't mean we've abandoned the mechanistic theory—it's just a less precise way of stating it.

More importantly, the defence's own test fails. It demands a passage where Dehaene specifies "exactly N neurons for exactly T milliseconds." But NO scientific theory of complex systems provides that level of precision—not theories of life, not theories of solidity, not theories of computation. This isn't "retreat to functional abstraction"; it's normal scientific practice with complex, context-sensitive mechanisms.

The counterexample stands: Dehaene maintains a mechanistic theory (consciousness involves specific prefrontal-parietal broadcasting mechanisms), handles borderline cases by accepting graded/variable consciousness, and doesn't retreat to pure functional abstraction any more than other mechanistic theories in biology or neuroscience do. The thesis's predicted instability doesn't materialize.

</details>
