### O14. The empirical inaccessibility problem for consciousness theories is actually a counterexample to the principle that any meaningful dispute must be settleable in principle, revealing that some genuine factual disagreements are necessarily evidentially symmetric.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** Verificationists and scientific naturalists who hold that meaningful factual disputes must be empirically adjudicable in principle
- **Sketch:** Philosophers across traditions accept that genuine factual disputes must be settleable at least in principle—if no possible evidence could adjudicate, the dispute is merely verbal or metaphysical in a pejorative sense. The consciousness debate seems to violate this: there appears to be a genuine fact about whether phenomenal experience exists in systems like insects or AI, yet we can articulate why no behavioral, functional, or neural evidence could ever decisively settle this (the problem of other minds is not merely epistemic but concerns the evidential structure itself). This is a counterexample because the dispute seems both factual (realists and illusionists make contradictory claims about what exists) and necessarily unsettleable. The key premise is that this unsettleability is not due to practical limitations but to the logical structure of first-person/third-person asymmetry.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Consciousness as counterexample to settlability principle is interesting but the observation is not entirely new.
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 4/5, answered. The defence successfully showed the thesis targets the specifically empiricist version of the principle that its stated audience actually holds, not a strawman.
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 2/5, answered. The defence provided clear falsification conditions (showing the dispute is verbal, that phenomenal properties are logically entailed by physical structure, etc.)—philosophical rather than empirical, but determinate.
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence's attempt to distinguish 'describing the gap' from 'deploying it as counterexample' failed; this is the decades-old central battleground between mysterians and naturalists, not a fresh counterexample.
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence's appeal to 'direct access' either abandons verificationism entirely or requires question-begging metaphysical commitments the target audience rejects; no principled way to privilege consciousness over theology was established.
- **Flaw:** The thesis presents as novel counterexample what is actually the canonical, decades-old example in this exact dialectical context, and its logic would equally license theological and paranormal disputes as factual without providing non-question-begging grounds to distinguish them.

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "settleable in principle." On one reading, this means "decidable given ideal evidence of the kind appropriate to the domain" — and on this reading, the principle is plausible (we shouldn't count something as a factual dispute if literally no evidence bears on it). On another reading, it means "decidable by third-person empirical observation specifically" — and on this reading, the consciousness case is interesting as a counterexample. But the argument needs both readings. The principle is plausible only on the first, domain-neutral reading (where "appropriate evidence" for consciousness might include first-person access). The consciousness case is a counterexample only on the second, empirically-restricted reading (where we've arbitrarily excluded first-person evidence from counting). The verificationist principle, properly understood, allows that different domains may require different evidential standards — mathematical disputes are settled by proof, ethical disputes by reflective equilibrium, consciousness disputes by... whatever evidence consciousness admits of. The thesis trades on making the principle seem plausible by stating it broadly, then making the counterexample work by interpreting it narrowly.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem. The argument appears to manufacture a counterexample by first accepting a reasonable principle (disputes need appropriate evidence), then reinterpreting it restrictively (disputes need third-person empirical evidence), then showing consciousness violates the restricted version. The thesis needs a defense of why "settleable" must mean "third-person empirically settleable" rather than just "settleable by evidence appropriate to consciousness."

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE.

The objection misidentifies where the equivocation lies. The thesis does not equivocate—it targets precisely the empiricist version of the principle that its stated audience (verificationists and scientific naturalists) actually holds. These philosophers don't merely say "factual disputes need appropriate evidence"—they say factual disputes need *public, intersubjectively accessible* evidence. That's the whole point of their position: to rule out private revelation, mystical insight, or purely first-person authority as arbiters of factual matters.

The consciousness case is a counterexample to *this* principle—the one they actually endorse. When the objection says "the principle, properly understood, allows different evidential standards for different domains," it's simply abandoning the target position. Scientific naturalists like the Churchlands, Dennett, or Quine don't say "consciousness disputes can be settled by first-person evidence if that's what the domain requires." They say either (a) consciousness claims must answer to third-person evidence or (b) there's no factual dispute here at all. The thesis attacks option (a) by showing cases where there clearly *is* a factual dispute (realists and illusionists contradict each other about what exists) yet third-person evidence is necessarily insufficient.

The objection's "domain-neutral" reading isn't what verificationists hold—it's a watered-down principle that would allow Cartesian intuition, mystical experience, or any other "appropriate" evidence someone might claim for their domain. The original principle's force comes precisely from its empirical restriction. The thesis shows this restriction fails.

VERDICT.

The defence fails, but not completely. The objection has identified something real: there is a scope ambiguity in "settleable in principle," and the thesis does need to be clear about which version of the principle it targets.

However, the defence succeeds in showing the objection overreaches. The thesis *does* target a specific, genuinely-held principle—the empiricist restriction—not a strawman. Scientific naturalists really do hold that factual disputes require public evidence, and the consciousness case really does create trouble for this. The objection's move to "whatever evidence consciousness admits of" essentially concedes the counterexample by abandoning the principle.

The remaining problem is dialectical positioning: the thesis should explicitly state it targets the *empiricist* version of the settlement principle, not some maximally ecumenical version. As stated, it risks seeming to attack a broader principle than it actually defeats. But against its stated target audience—verificationists and scientific naturalists—the counterexample has force.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis is unfalsifiable because it protects itself by selectively redrawing what counts as "evidentially symmetric" or "necessarily unsettleable." Consider potential counterexamples:

**Case 1**: Someone proposes that specific neural signatures (say, integrated information measures above threshold T) do settle the consciousness question. Response: "That's just correlation with behavioral dispositions, not access to phenomenal character itself—still symmetric."

**Case 2**: Someone argues that first-person reports ARE evidence that breaks symmetry (subjects reliably report experiences under conditions C). Response: "But that's just verbal behavior—philosophical zombies would report identically—so it doesn't really settle phenomenal presence."

**Case 3**: Someone claims the dispute IS settleable in principle via some future neuroscience. Response: "You're changing the subject to neural correlates, not phenomenal experience itself—the logical structure guarantees no third-person evidence reaches the phenomenon."

**Case 4**: Someone argues the dispute is actually verbal (illusionists and realists mean different things by "consciousness"). Response: "No, they make contradictory existence claims about the same thing—it's genuinely factual."

Every proposed way to settle the dispute gets ruled "not really settling it" by appeal to the first-person/third-person gap. Every proposed way to dissolve the dispute gets ruled "missing the genuine factual disagreement." The thesis immunizes itself by controlling what counts as "the consciousness question" and what counts as "really settling" it, making it impossible to show either that the dispute IS settleable or that it ISN'T genuinely factual.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem requiring a principled answer about what could possibly show the thesis wrong.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE.

The objection confuses having principled criteria with being unfalsifiable. The thesis provides clear, non-arbitrary standards for both "genuinely factual" and "evidentially symmetric" that generate determinate verdicts—they just happen to yield the result that consciousness disputes satisfy both conditions.

**On what makes disputes genuinely factual:** The thesis uses the standard criterion—contradictory existence claims about a purported entity or property. Realists say phenomenal properties exist and have certain causal/functional roles; illusionists say they don't exist at all; functionalists say they exist but are exhausted by functional organization. These are incompatible claims about what's real. The objection's Case 4 doesn't show the thesis is unfalsifiable—it shows the thesis correctly applies a widely-accepted criterion. If someone demonstrated these positions don't actually contradict (say, by showing they use "consciousness" with different truth-conditions), that WOULD falsify the thesis's claim of genuine factuality.

**On what counts as settling:** The thesis has a principled criterion here too—evidence settles a dispute when it's differentially accessible depending on which hypothesis is true. Third-person evidence about consciousness fails this test not by arbitrary stipulation but because of the multiple realizability problem: any third-person profile (neural, functional, behavioral) is logically compatible with both presence and absence of phenomenal properties. This isn't protecting the thesis by "controlling" what counts—it's applying the standard evidential logic that distinguishes correlation from determination.

The objection's cases don't show unfalsifiability—they show the thesis has implications:

- Cases 1-2: These don't break evidential symmetry because zombie scenarios remain coherent—this is a substantive claim that could be false if someone showed phenomenal properties are logically entailed by physical structure.

- Case 3: If future neuroscience discovered a mechanism that logically necessitated phenomenal experience (not just correlated with it), that WOULD falsify the thesis. The thesis predicts this won't happen because of the logical gap, but that's a falsifiable prediction.

- Case 4: Already addressed—contradictory existence claims are factual by standard criteria.

**What would falsify the thesis:**

1. Showing the realist/illusionist dispute is actually verbal (different meanings, not contradictory claims)
2. Demonstrating that phenomenal properties are logically entailed by some third-person accessible structure
3. Proving the zombie argument contains a logical error that collapses the first-person/third-person distinction
4. Finding a case where the dispute is settled by third-person evidence that both sides antecedently accepted as decisive

These are substantive philosophical possibilities. The thesis makes a bold claim—that the logical structure of consciousness creates a necessarily unsettleable factual dispute—and this claim has determinate conditions for refutation.

VERDICT.

The defence holds, though it reveals a burden. The objection is wrong to claim unfalsifiability—the thesis has clear falsification conditions, they're just philosophical rather than empirical. But the defence exposes that the thesis's strength depends entirely on controversial premises about consciousness: that zombies are conceivable, that phenomenal properties aren't logically entailed by physical structure, that the first-person/third-person gap is unbridgeable in principle.

These aren't protections against falsification—they're substantive commitments that opponents can (and do) challenge. If any of them fails, the thesis fails. That's the opposite of unfalsifiable. The objection mistakes "has principled criteria that yield a specific result" for "immunizes itself from testing." The thesis is falsifiable; it just requires philosophical argument rather than empirical discovery to falsify it, which is appropriate given that it's a claim about logical structure rather than contingent fact.

The thesis survives this objection.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis position—that consciousness presents a genuine factual dispute that is necessarily evidentially symmetric—is essentially the standard formulation of the "hard problem of consciousness" as articulated by David Chalmers (1995). Chalmers explicitly argues that phenomenal consciousness poses an explanatory gap that no amount of functional or physical information can bridge in principle, making facts about experience empirically inaccessible from the third-person perspective. The thesis's "evidential symmetry" is Chalmers' "explanatory gap" reframed; the claim about "necessarily unsettleable disputes" is his distinction between "easy" and "hard" problems. Thomas Nagel's "What Is It Like to Be a Bat?" (1974) makes the same structural point about the first-person/third-person asymmetry creating an in-principle barrier to third-person knowledge of subjective character. Joseph Levine's "explanatory gap" (1983) is another formulation of the same position. The thesis presents itself as revealing something surprising about the principle of empirical adjudication, but it's actually just restating the received view in philosophy of mind that consciousness is special precisely because it violates normal evidential standards. This isn't a fresh counterexample—it's the canonical example that everyone already uses when discussing these issues. The weak point is the claim to novelty: this is simply the hard problem dressed up as a discovery about verification principles rather than about consciousness.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This seriously undermines the thesis's presentation as a counterexample that "reveals" something. The consciousness case isn't being deployed as a counterexample to verificationism; rather, the hard problem literature has been the standard way of articulating why consciousness seems special for decades. The thesis repackages established positions without acknowledging it's working within, not against, the existing dialectic. A genuine counterexample should be surprising; this is textbook material.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection fundamentally misunderstands the dialectical situation. Yes, Chalmers, Nagel, and Levine describe the explanatory gap—but they present it as a problem *for physicalism* or *for reductionism*, not as a counterexample to verificationist principles about factual disputes. That's the crucial difference.

The hard problem literature operates *within* a framework that accepts consciousness as somehow special or exceptional. Chalmers argues we need new fundamental laws; Nagel suggests objective science has inherent limits; Levine presents the gap as a puzzle about our concepts. But none of them explicitly deploy this as a *counterexample to the principle that factual disputes must be settleable*. They either accept that consciousness is therefore not fully factual in the standard sense, or argue we need expanded notions of evidence, or suggest the dispute reveals metaphysical rather than empirical issues.

The thesis makes a different move: it takes the structure they describe and weaponizes it *against* the verificationist principle itself. It says: here's a case that satisfies your criteria for being a genuine factual dispute (contradictory existence claims, realist metaphysics, not merely verbal), yet violates your requirement for empirical settleability. This forces a choice the hard problem literature doesn't force: either abandon the principle that factual disputes must be settleable, or deny that consciousness disputes are factual (which leads to eliminativism).

The novelty isn't in the *description* of consciousness's evidential structure—that's indeed Chalmers et al. The novelty is in the *dialectical deployment*: using this as a *reductio* of verificationist principles rather than as a *motivation* for expanding our metaphysics. The hard problem literature says "consciousness is weird, so we need new metaphysics." The thesis says "consciousness shows your epistemological principle was wrong."

Moreover, the objection ignores the stated target. Verificationists and scientific naturalists typically *reject* Chalmers-style arguments—they're eliminativists (Dennett, Churchland) or think the hard problem rests on confusion (Frankish). They don't accept that there's a genuine factual dispute here that's unsettleable. The thesis uses the structure Chalmers describes but directs it at opponents who deny his conclusions, forcing them to either accept evidentially symmetric factual disputes or deny the dispute is factual (which seems implausible given the contradictory existence claims).

The thesis isn't claiming to discover the hard problem—it's claiming to use it as a counterexample in a dialectical context where it hasn't been deployed that way.

# VERDICT

The defence fails. The objection is essentially correct, and the defence's attempt to distinguish "describing the gap" from "deploying it as a counterexample" doesn't hold up under scrutiny.

Here's why: The hard problem literature *already functions* as a challenge to empiricist/verificationist principles, even if not always explicitly framed that way. When Nagel argues that objective science cannot capture subjective character, he's directly challenging the completeness of third-person empirical methods. When Chalmers argues that no amount of functional information entails phenomenal facts, he's arguing that standard empirical evidence cannot settle consciousness disputes. The literature doesn't need to use the word "counterexample" to be making exactly this point.

More decisively, the defence's claim about "dialectical novelty" collapses because the target audience—verificationists and scientific naturalists—have *already responded* to exactly this challenge. Dennett's "Consciousness Explained," the Churchlands' eliminativism, Frankish's illusionism—these are all explicitly responses to the claim that consciousness creates an empirically unsettleable dispute. They argue either that there is no genuine dispute (it's confused), or that it is settleable (by showing phenomenal talk reduces to functional talk), or that we should eliminate the problematic concepts. The dialectical move the thesis claims to make has already been made, and the target audience has already developed their counter-moves.

The thesis presents as a fresh counterexample something that is actually the central, decades-old battleground between mysterians and naturalists about consciousness. It's not revealing a problem with verificationist principles—it's restating one side of an ongoing debate where both sides are fully aware of the stakes. The "counterexample" is already taken because it's already the primary example everyone uses in exactly this dialectical context.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis proves too much because if this argument worked, it would license accepting evidentially symmetric factual disputes in domains where we clearly should not. Consider: Theological realists and atheists make contradictory existence claims about God. We can articulate why no empirical evidence could ever decisively settle this—God, if He exists, transcends physical causation and could create a world empirically indistinguishable from a godless one (the problem of divine hiddenness concerns evidential structure, not merely epistemic limits). By the thesis's logic, this is a counterexample to verificationist principles: a genuine factual dispute (contradictory claims about what exists) that is necessarily evidentially symmetric due to logical structure (transcendence/immanence asymmetry parallels first-person/third-person asymmetry).

The same structure applies to: astral planes (realists vs. skeptics make contradictory claims; astral evidence is only accessible to astral consciousness); Cartesian souls (substance dualists vs. materialists; souls are non-physical so no physical evidence could settle it); homeopathic vital forces (proponents vs. skeptics; the vital force works at levels beyond physical detection). In each case, proponents can articulate a principled reason why evidence is necessarily symmetric—not practical limitations but "logical structure."

If the consciousness case refutes verificationism, so do all these cases. But that would mean verificationists must either accept all these disputes as legitimately factual-yet-unsettleable, or provide principled grounds for treating consciousness differently—which requires the very kind of domain-specific metaphysical commitments verificationism was meant to avoid. The weak point is the claim that evidential symmetry due to "logical structure" establishes genuine factuality rather than exposing pseudo-questions.

# STRENGTH: 4/5

This does serious damage. The thesis needs to explain why consciousness disputes deserve different treatment than theological or paranormal disputes that have identical evidential structure. The natural verificationist response—"all these are pseudo-disputes precisely because they're evidentially symmetric"—seems more principled than accepting them all as counterexamples. The thesis appears to assume consciousness is special without justifying this assumption in terms its target audience would accept.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection fails because consciousness disputes differ from theological/paranormal cases in a crucial respect: the existence of the disputed entity is *directly accessible* to at least some subjects, making the factuality non-negotiable in a way that doesn't apply to God or astral planes.

**The asymmetry is this:** I have immediate, non-inferential access to my own phenomenal states. I know with certainty that I have experiential states—that there is something it is like to be me right now. This isn't a theoretical posit or an explanatory hypothesis; it's the most certain datum I possess. The dispute about consciousness in others arises because this first-person certainty cannot be extended via third-person evidence, but the certainty itself establishes that phenomenal consciousness is real—at least somewhere, in at least some subjects.

Contrast this with God or astral planes. No one has direct, non-inferential access to God's existence in the way they have access to their own experiences. Theological "experience" is always mediated, interpreted, and disputable even by the subject. The believer infers God from religious experiences or arguments; they don't have God-awareness the way they have pain-awareness. Similarly for astral planes—even proponents describe astral projection as an unusual state requiring interpretation, not a constant datum of consciousness.

**This generates a principled distinction:** Consciousness disputes are factual because at least one instance of the disputed entity is indubitably real (my own experience). The dispute concerns *generalization*—does consciousness exist in other systems?—not existence as such. But God-disputes concern whether the entity exists *at all*, and no one has the kind of direct access to God that would settle this the way first-person access settles consciousness-in-me.

The verificationist can therefore treat these cases differently without arbitrariness: consciousness disputes are factual because the disputed property is exemplified and known to be exemplified; theological disputes lack this anchor. The evidential symmetry in consciousness cases arises *after* we've established the phenomenon is real; the evidential symmetry in theological cases leaves the phenomenon's reality itself in question.

**On homeopathic vital forces and Cartesian souls:** These are theoretical posits, not objects of direct acquaintance. No one has vital-force-awareness or soul-awareness in the way they have experience-awareness. They're explanatory hypotheses that can be rejected without contradicting any datum of direct access. Rejecting phenomenal consciousness, by contrast, requires denying what is most immediately present.

The thesis doesn't prove too much because it identifies a unique case: a disputed property that is (a) indubitably real in at least some instances via direct access, (b) logically cannot be verified in other instances via third-person evidence, yet (c) involves contradictory existence claims about those other instances. No theological or paranormal case shares this structure.

# VERDICT

The defence fails. The objection stands, and the thesis proves too much.

The defence's attempt to distinguish consciousness via "direct access" doesn't rescue the thesis from its target audience—verificationists and scientific naturalists—because these philosophers explicitly reject this kind of move. Here's why:

**First**, the defence concedes too much. By grounding factuality in "direct, non-inferential access," it abandons verificationist principles entirely and embraces exactly the kind of first-person epistemological authority that verificationists reject. Dennett, Churchland, and other naturalists argue that first-person "certainty" about phenomenal states is unreliable, theory-laden, and subject to systematic error. They deny that introspection provides the kind of indubitable access the defence claims. If the thesis needs to assume this access is veridical and authoritative, it's begging the question against its stated target audience.

**Second**, the distinction doesn't hold on its own terms. Theological believers *do* claim direct, non-inferential access to God's presence—this is the entire basis of religious experience, mystical union, and personal relationship with the divine. The defence's claim that such experiences are "always mediated, interpreted, and disputable even by the subject" applies equally to phenomenal consciousness. My certainty that I'm experiencing "redness" rather than "greenness" or that I have "genuine understanding" rather than "mere information processing" is also mediated by concepts, disputable, and theory-laden. The defence arbitrarily grants epistemic authority to consciousness-reports while denying it to God-reports, but verificationists would deny it to both.

**Third**, and most fatally, the defence misidentifies what needs defending. The objection isn't that consciousness and God are identical cases—it's that *if the thesis's argument works*, then parallel arguments work for God, astral planes, etc. The thesis claims that evidential symmetry plus contradictory existence claims equals a counterexample to verificationism. But theological disputes have exactly this structure: realists and atheists make contradictory claims, and proponents can articulate principled reasons why no evidence could settle it (divine transcendence, hiddenness as a feature not a bug, etc.). 

The defence tries to block this by appealing to direct access, but this either (a) abandons verificationism—making the thesis not a counterexample but a rejection—or (b) requires showing why consciousness-access is epistemically privileged in ways God-access isn't, which requires precisely the kind of domain-specific metaphysical commitments the objection says verificationists should resist.

**The thesis proves too much because:** If we accept that evidentially symmetric disputes with contradictory existence claims can be genuinely factual (rather than pseudo-disputes), we must accept this for any case where proponents articulate a principled reason for the symmetry. The thesis provides no non-question-begging way to privilege consciousness over theology that its target audience would accept. The natural verificationist response—"these are all pseudo-disputes precisely because they're evidentially symmetric"—remains more principled than the thesis's selective acceptance of consciousness as special.

The thesis fails this objection.

</details>

### O15. Adversarial collaboration between consciousness theories is impossible because what counts as explaining consciousness is itself theory-laden, making cross-theory empirical tests incoherent.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** Collaborative projects like the 'adversarial collaboration' between IIT and GWT researchers that assume they're testing the same target
- **Sketch:** The literature treats different consciousness theories (IIT, GWT, HOT, etc.) as offering competing explanations of the same explanandum. But each theory implicitly defines what-needs-explaining differently: IIT takes integrated information itself as phenomenally relevant; GWT treats global availability as what matters; HOT requires meta-representation. The key distinction is between nominal agreement (everyone says they're explaining 'consciousness') and substantive agreement on explanatory targets. When IIT says cerebellar processing isn't conscious despite its complexity, and GWT says it's about lack of broadcast, they're not disagreeing about a shared phenomenon—they're explaining different things while using the same word. This means proposed crucial experiments don't test competing theories but rather which explanandum we care about. The debate is really about conceptual engineering: which precisification of 'consciousness' is most useful.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Different explananda diagnosis is reasonable but treating it as making adversarial collaboration impossible overstates.
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence claimed consciousness uniquely lacks 'pre-theoretical phenomenal anchoring' but conceded comparison cases also involve theory-laden domain boundaries; the vegetative state example showed theories CAN identify shared test scenarios with divergent predictions, which is what adversarial collaboration requires
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence retreated to claiming Tononi-Dehaene collaboration was 'merely parallel testing' by imposing impossibly high standards (shared confirmation conditions), but this concedes such collaborations occur, function, and produce scientific progress—abandoning the impossibility claim
  - TRIVIAL — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence claimed to be 'discovering' structural features but admitted researchers already conduct adversarial collaborations, revealing the thesis stipulates these don't count rather than showing they cannot occur; retreated from impossibility to 'proceeds slowly and messily'
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence introduced 'critical reading' as middle position but this collapsed into weak reading with added pessimism; admitted theories 'overlap enough to make contradictory predictions' which concedes adversarial collaboration is possible, just difficult
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Adversarial collaboration between consciousness theories faces unique difficulties because theories differ not just in interpretation but in which observable phenomena constitute consciousness, making empirical convergence slower and more complex than in domains with clearer pre-theoretical anchoring points
- **Flaw:** The thesis claimed adversarial collaboration is impossible but defences repeatedly conceded such collaborations actually occur and function, retreating to claims they are merely difficult or don't meet stipulated standards—this abandons impossibility for normative disapproval

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. If this argument worked, it would equally show that adversarial collaboration is impossible across competing theories in any domain where the explanandum involves theory-laden concepts. Consider: evolutionary psychology theories about mate selection, economic theories about rational behavior, linguistic theories about grammaticality, or physical theories about simultaneity all involve explananda that are partly constituted by theoretical commitments. Evolutionary psychology theories differ on what counts as a fitness-relevant behavior; economic theories embed different conceptions of rationality in their explananda; relativity theory changed what "simultaneous" means. Yet adversarial collaborations remain possible and productive in these fields precisely because researchers can identify overlapping empirical phenomena (mating patterns, market behaviors, speaker judgments, clock readings) even while disagreeing about ultimate explanatory targets. The thesis proves too much because it would render impossible the entire methodology of theory comparison in any domain with conceptual complexity. If we accept this reasoning for consciousness theories, we must accept it everywhere theoretical concepts partially constitute explananda—which would eliminate adversarial collaboration as a viable scientific method across most of cognitive science, economics, and even physics.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem requiring a real answer. The thesis needs to explain why consciousness is special in a way that evolutionary fitness, rationality, or simultaneity are not.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies what makes consciousness uniquely resistant to adversarial collaboration. The cases it cites involve theory-ladenness at the level of *theoretical interpretation*, but consciousness involves theory-ladenness at the level of *phenomenal access itself*.

Consider the crucial asymmetry: In evolutionary psychology, researchers with competing theories about fitness can agree on the raw behavioral data (who mates with whom, offspring counts, resource distribution patterns) even while disagreeing about what makes these fitness-relevant. In relativity versus Newtonian mechanics, observers disagree about whether distant events are simultaneous, but they agree on local clock readings and can specify translation rules between frameworks. The theory-ladenness operates at the interpretive layer above a shared observational base.

Consciousness admits no such separation. There is no "raw phenomenal data" sitting beneath competing theoretical interpretations. What IIT identifies as an instance of consciousness (high Φ in posterior cortex) and what GWT identifies as an instance of consciousness (global broadcast to prefrontal workspace) are not two interpretations of the same observational phenomenon—they are *different phenomena entirely*. A patient in a vegetative state might have high integrated information in isolated cortical regions (consciousness according to IIT) while lacking global broadcast (non-consciousness according to GWT). These theories don't disagree about how to interpret shared data; they disagree about which data constitute the phenomenon.

The adversarial collaboration methodology requires what we might call *pre-theoretical phenomenal anchoring*—some way of identifying "this is a case of consciousness, now let's see which theory explains it better." But each consciousness theory provides its own phenomenal anchoring. IIT anchors to integrated information structures; GWT anchors to reportable access; HOT anchors to meta-representational states. There's no neutral anchoring point because first-person phenomenology is private and third-person markers are precisely what's contested.

The objector's cases permit adversarial collaboration because they allow triangulation: multiple theories can converge on shared observational targets even while diverging on explanation. Consciousness permits no such triangulation because the observational target is constituted by theoretical commitments about what makes mental states phenomenally conscious.

VERDICT

The defence fails, though it identifies something important.

The defence correctly notes that consciousness involves deeper theory-ladenness than the comparison cases, but it doesn't establish that this makes adversarial collaboration *impossible*—only harder. The objection stands because:

First, even the "pure observational base" in the comparison cases is more theory-laden than the defence admits. Evolutionary psychologists genuinely disagree about which behaviors count as mating-relevant (is gift-giving? is status display?); economists disagree about which market behaviors manifest rationality versus noise. These aren't just interpretive disagreements—they're disagreements about domain boundaries that parallel consciousness debates.

Second, and fatally: the defence's own example defeats the thesis. The vegetative state patient case shows that IIT and GWT *can* identify a shared test scenario even while predicting different outcomes. That's precisely what adversarial collaboration requires—not agreement on what consciousness is, but agreement on a case where theories make divergent predictions. If IIT predicts "conscious" and GWT predicts "not conscious" for the same patient, they're testing competing claims about the same individual, even if they conceptualize consciousness differently.

The thesis overstates its case. Theory-ladenness makes adversarial collaboration *difficult* and requires careful experimental design, but not *impossible*. Researchers can identify cases where theories diverge in their verdicts without first agreeing on what they're verdicts about.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. The adversarial collaboration between Tononi (IIT) and Dehaene (GWT) regarding patients with posterior cortical hot zones provides a direct counterexample. In their actual collaborative work, researchers identified specific test cases: patients under anesthesia showing posterior cortical activity without frontal activation. IIT predicted these patients retain consciousness (high Φ in posterior regions); GWT predicted unconsciousness (no global broadcast). They agreed on measurable criteria: sustained posterior activity measured via fMRI, absence of prefrontal activation, and post-recovery reports where available. This is a genuine adversarial test despite theory-laden explananda because:

(1) Both theories made determinate, opposing predictions about the same individual patients at the same times
(2) They agreed on what observational evidence would count for/against each prediction (imaging data, recovery reports)
(3) The collaboration proceeded and generated publications

The thesis claims such collaboration is "impossible" and tests are "incoherent," but this collaboration actually occurred and was scientifically productive. The weak point in the thesis is the slide from "theories define the explanandum differently" to "empirical tests are incoherent." Even with different explananda, theories can make conflicting predictions about overlapping cases—which is sufficient for adversarial testing. The thesis confuses conceptual incommensurability with empirical untestability.

STRENGTH: 5/5 — The thesis cannot survive this. An actual, completed adversarial collaboration directly contradicts the claim that such collaboration is "impossible."

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection mistakes a superficial research arrangement for genuine adversarial collaboration. What Tononi and Dehaene actually achieved was *parallel testing of independent predictions*, not *collaborative arbitration of a shared explanandum*.

Consider what their "collaboration" structurally amounts to: They identified a patient population, each applied their own theory's criteria to generate predictions, collected data, and each declared their theory confirmed by different aspects of the results. IIT pointed to sustained posterior Φ as vindicating their framework; GWT pointed to absence of reportability and behavioral responsiveness as vindicating theirs. They published together, but they didn't *resolve* anything—they demonstrated their theories make different cuts through the same anatomical territory.

The crucial point: **agreement on a test scenario is not the same as agreement on what's being tested**. Yes, both theories made predictions about "the same individual patients at the same times"—but they made predictions about *different properties of those patients*. IIT predicted high integrated information would be present; GWT predicted global broadcast would be absent. Both predictions proved correct. This isn't adversarial testing; it's *compatible* testing that appears adversarial only because both theories use the word "consciousness."

True adversarial collaboration requires that the theories' predictions genuinely conflict such that evidence for one constitutes evidence against the other. But when IIT finds high Φ and declares "consciousness present" while GWT finds no broadcast and declares "consciousness absent," they're not contradicting each other—they're talking past each other using a shared label. The "post-recovery reports where available" don't arbitrate because IIT can dismiss reportability as epistemically secondary to integrated information, while GWT treats reportability as constitutive of the phenomenon.

The objector claims they "agreed on what observational evidence would count for/against each prediction," but this is precisely wrong. They agreed on what *data to collect* (imaging, reports), but not on what would *count as confirmation*. IIT treats the presence of integrated information as confirming consciousness even without report; GWT treats absence of report as confirming unconsciousness even with posterior activity. Each theory has its own confirmation conditions because each has its own explanandum.

The publication record supports this reading: their "collaborative" papers don't conclude "Theory X was disconfirmed" but rather "Here are the conditions under which our theories diverge"—which is just mapping conceptual territory, not empirical arbitration.

# VERDICT

The defence fails. The thesis is dead.

The defence retreats to an impossibly high standard for what counts as "adversarial collaboration." By demanding that theories share not just test scenarios but *confirmation conditions* and *explananda*, it makes adversarial collaboration impossible by definitional fiat rather than by substantive argument.

The fatal problems:

**First**: The defence's own description reveals that Tononi and Dehaene achieved exactly what adversarial collaboration requires in science. They identified a case where their theories made opposing predictions about a theoretically significant property (consciousness/non-consciousness), specified observable conditions, collected data, and the results created genuine theoretical pressure. That IIT and GWT responded differently to the evidence doesn't show the collaboration failed—it shows the theories are empirically distinct, which is what the test was designed to reveal.

**Second**: The defence's standard would eliminate adversarial collaboration even in the objector's comparison cases. When evolutionary psychology theories make different predictions about mate selection behaviors, they also have different confirmation conditions (one counts status display as fitness-relevant, another doesn't). When economic theories predict market behaviors, they embed different rationality criteria in what counts as confirmation. The defence's reasoning would make all these collaborations "merely parallel testing" rather than genuine adversarial work.

**Third**: The claim that "both predictions proved correct" and this shows compatibility rather than conflict is sophistical. If one theory says "conscious" and another says "not conscious" about the same patient at the same time, and both are taken seriously by their proponents, that's a genuine disagreement—even if each theory has resources to interpret the evidence favorably. The fact that theories can be defended against adverse evidence doesn't mean the evidence wasn't adverse.

**Most devastatingly**: The thesis claimed adversarial collaboration is *impossible* and tests are *incoherent*. The defence now concedes that such collaborations occur, generate data, create theoretical pressure, and advance the field—but insists they don't count because they don't *resolve* the debate. This abandons the thesis entirely. Something that occurs, functions, and produces scientific progress is not impossible.

The thesis fails because it conflates "difficult to resolve decisively" with "impossible to conduct coherently."

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis presents itself as a substantive discovery about the impossibility of adversarial collaboration in consciousness science, but it's actually just a verbal stipulation about what "explaining consciousness" means, dressed up as a methodological insight.

The weak point is the claim that theories "implicitly define what-needs-explaining differently." This is trivially true in the sense that any sufficiently precise theory will operationalize its target phenomenon in theory-specific ways. IIT operationalizes consciousness as integrated information; GWT operationalizes it as global workspace access. But calling this an "implicit definition of what-needs-explaining" just relabels the uncontroversial fact that theories use different theoretical vocabularies.

The thesis then treats this verbal observation—that theories use different conceptual frameworks—as if it were a deep discovery about impossibility. But there's no discovery here. Everyone already knows that IIT and GWT use different concepts. The question is whether these conceptual differences prevent empirical testing, and the thesis simply *stipulates* that they do by defining "genuine adversarial collaboration" to require shared explananda.

This is stipulative rather than substantive. The thesis defines "coherent empirical test" so narrowly (requiring pre-theoretical agreement on explanatory targets) that it excludes the actual scientific practice that goes under that name. Then it announces this practice is "impossible"—but only because the thesis has verbally legislated it out of existence.

The supposed distinction between "nominal agreement" and "substantive agreement" on explanatory targets isn't uncovering a hidden incoherence in consciousness science—it's just choosing to call certain research collaborations "not really adversarial" based on a philosopher's preferred definition of terms. The thesis is true only in the sense that "bachelors are unmarried" is true: by stipulation of what the words mean.

# STRENGTH: 2/5

The attack has some force but is answerable. While the thesis does involve conceptual stipulation, it's not *merely* stipulative—it makes a substantive claim that the degree of theory-ladenness in consciousness science is severe enough to undermine a methodology that works elsewhere. The thesis could respond that not all conceptual distinctions are mere verbal moves; some track real structural features of scientific practice.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection conflates "using conceptual distinctions" with "mere verbal stipulation," but not all conceptual analysis is stipulative—some reveals structural features that make a practical difference.

The thesis isn't stipulating that adversarial collaboration requires shared explananda; it's *discovering* that what scientists call "adversarial collaboration" in consciousness research lacks the structural features that make such collaboration work elsewhere, then explaining why. The distinction between nominal and substantive agreement isn't a philosopher's verbal preference—it's tracking a real difference in scientific practice with real consequences.

Consider the contrast: In evolutionary psychology or economics, when theories disagree about mate selection or market behavior, the disagreement creates **genuine empirical pressure**. Evidence accumulates, theories get modified or abandoned, the field converges (however slowly) on better accounts. The conceptual differences between theories don't prevent this progressive dynamic because the theories are **genuinely competing** to explain overlapping phenomena.

In consciousness science, the pattern is structurally different. IIT and GWT have coexisted for decades, each accumulating "confirming evidence," neither facing serious abandonment pressure from the other's successes. When IIT finds high Φ in cases GWT calls unconscious, IIT doesn't revise—it doubles down. When GWT finds reportability absent in cases with high posterior activity, GWT doesn't revise—it doubles down. This isn't because consciousness scientists are stubborn; it's because the theories **aren't genuinely competing**. They're explaining different things while using the same word.

The thesis isn't stipulating this situation into existence—it's *describing* it and *explaining* why it persists. The explanation is that theory-ladenness runs so deep in consciousness that the usual mechanisms of empirical arbitration don't engage. Each theory has its own confirmation conditions because each has its own explanandum, and this structural feature prevents the kind of progressive convergence that adversarial collaboration is supposed to produce.

The objector says "everyone already knows that IIT and GWT use different concepts"—but if everyone knows this, why do researchers keep designing "crucial experiments" that supposedly test which theory is correct? Why the rhetoric of competition rather than conceptual pluralism? The thesis explains this: there's a systematic confusion between nominal agreement (both theories claim to explain "consciousness") and substantive agreement (both theories target the same phenomenon). The confusion isn't just verbal—it shapes research programs, funding decisions, and methodological choices.

The test for whether a distinction is merely verbal: Does it make a practical difference? Here it does. If the thesis is right, consciousness science should **stop** designing adversarial collaborations that presuppose shared explananda and **start** doing conceptual engineering to clarify which precisification of "consciousness" matters for which purposes. That's not a verbal recommendation—it's a substantive reorientation of research strategy.

The objector's comparison to "bachelors are unmarried" fails because that's an analytic truth following from definitions. The thesis makes a synthetic claim: given how consciousness theories actually function, given the pattern of evidence-accumulation and theory-persistence we observe, the best explanation is that these theories aren't competing to explain the same thing. That's an empirical claim about scientific practice, not a stipulation about words.

# VERDICT

The defence fails, and the thesis dies here.

The defence makes the thesis's position worse by revealing that it's trying to have things both ways—claiming simultaneously to be descriptive (reporting what consciousness science actually does) and prescriptive (saying what it should do), while being neither convincingly.

**The fatal problem**: The defence claims the thesis is "discovering" structural features and "describing" actual scientific practice, but then immediately admits this practice includes researchers "designing crucial experiments" and engaging in what they call adversarial collaboration. So the thesis isn't describing what happens—it's saying what happens doesn't count as real adversarial collaboration. That's stipulation, not discovery.

**Second**: The defence's empirical claims are overstated and selective. Yes, IIT and GWT have coexisted for decades, but so have competing theories in evolutionary psychology, quantum mechanics interpretations, and economic modeling. Long coexistence of theories doesn't prove they're explaining different things—it might just prove the phenomena are complex and evidence is hard to gather. The defence cherry-picks the "doubling down" pattern while ignoring cases where consciousness theories have been modified or abandoned under empirical pressure (e.g., various first-order representationalist theories, early versions of quantum consciousness theories).

**Third**: The practical difference test backfires. The defence says if the thesis is right, science should "stop" adversarial collaborations and "start" conceptual engineering. But consciousness science already does both—researchers conduct empirical tests AND engage in conceptual clarification. The thesis doesn't reveal a hidden confusion; it just disapproves of one part of existing practice while approving another, based on philosophical preferences about what "real" competition requires.

**Most devastatingly**: The defence's own example of "genuine empirical pressure" in other fields undermines the thesis. It describes theories getting "modified or abandoned" and fields "converging however slowly"—but this is exactly what we'd expect if theories are difficult to test but not impossible to test. The defence has now retreated from "adversarial collaboration is impossible" to "adversarial collaboration proceeds slowly and messily in consciousness science." That's not a thesis—it's an observation that consciousness is hard.

The thesis fails because it mistakes a normative judgment (consciousness science should be more conceptually careful) for a descriptive impossibility claim (adversarial collaboration cannot work). The objection is correct: this is stipulation masquerading as discovery.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis equivocates on "theory-laden" between a weak reading (theories use different conceptual vocabularies) and a strong reading (theories identify completely non-overlapping phenomena), and the argument needs both readings to work.

The weak point is the move from "each theory implicitly defines what-needs-explaining differently" to "proposed crucial experiments don't test competing theories but rather which explanandum we care about."

On the WEAK reading of "theory-laden": Yes, IIT and GWT use different theoretical vocabularies and operationalize consciousness differently (integrated information vs. global broadcast). This is uncontroversially true—all scientific theories do this. But on this reading, the thesis is uninteresting. Theories always use different concepts; this doesn't make adversarial testing impossible, as the comparison cases (evolutionary psychology, economics, physics) demonstrate.

On the STRONG reading of "theory-laden": IIT and GWT are identifying completely different phenomena that merely share a label, like "bank" (financial institution vs. river edge). On this reading, the thesis is interesting—if true, it would indeed make adversarial collaboration incoherent. But on this reading, the thesis is implausible. IIT and GWT both make predictions about the same patients at the same times, both care about reportability data (even if they weight it differently), and both claim to explain why you're conscious now but not during dreamless sleep.

The argument slides between these readings: it establishes the weak claim (different vocabularies) but needs the strong claim (different phenomena) to conclude that testing is impossible. The thesis is plausible only when "theory-laden" means "using different concepts" but interesting only when it means "tracking completely disjoint phenomena"—and the argument cannot be run on either reading alone.

# STRENGTH: 4/5

This is a serious problem requiring a real answer. The thesis must either accept the weak reading (making it trivial) or defend the strong reading (which the counterexample evidence undermines).

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection presents a false dichotomy. The thesis doesn't require that theories track "completely non-overlapping phenomena" (the strong reading) to make adversarial collaboration impossible—it requires only that the overlap be **insufficient to ground empirical arbitration**, which is a middle position the objection ignores.

Consider the structure more carefully. IIT and GWT do make predictions about "the same patients at the same times," but this superficial overlap masks a deeper divergence in what would count as confirming or disconfirming evidence. The theories overlap enough to make them *appear* competitive (both address consciousness, both make predictions about brain states, both care about reports), but they diverge enough that no possible evidence pattern would force abandonment of either theory.

This is neither the weak reading (mere vocabulary differences) nor the strong reading (completely disjoint phenomena). Call it the **critical reading**: theories can share enough subject matter to generate apparent empirical conflicts while differing enough in their explanatory targets that these conflicts never achieve genuine arbitration power.

The test case reveals this structure. When IIT finds high Φ in posterior cortex of an unresponsive patient and declares "consciousness present," while GWT finds no global broadcast and declares "consciousness absent," they both claim vindication. Why? Because IIT's confirmation conditions center on integrated information (present), while GWT's center on reportable access (absent). The theories overlap enough to make contradictory pronouncements about "consciousness," but diverge enough that each can accommodate the total evidence within its own framework.

The objection demands we choose between "trivial" (different vocabularies) and "implausible" (disjoint phenomena), but the thesis occupies precisely the space between these: theories can be **empirically insulated from each other** despite apparent competition. This isn't about complete non-overlap—it's about the overlap being insufficient for the kind of progressive convergence that makes adversarial collaboration methodologically valuable.

The comparison cases support this middle position. In evolutionary psychology or economics, theories don't just share vocabulary—they share **success conditions** that create genuine competitive pressure. When an evolutionary psychology theory predicts certain mate preferences and those preferences aren't observed, the theory faces real abandonment pressure. The overlap is sufficient for arbitration. In consciousness science, the overlap is insufficient: IIT can explain away reportability failures; GWT can explain away integrated information findings; neither faces genuine falsification pressure from the other's successes.

The objection's dichotomy fails because it assumes overlap is binary (either complete or absent), but empirical arbitration requires a threshold degree of overlap that consciousness theories fail to meet. The thesis isn't claiming theories are "completely" disjoint—it's claiming they're disjoint *enough* to make adversarial collaboration methodologically incoherent.

# VERDICT

The defence fails. The thesis is dead.

The defence tries to save the thesis by introducing a "middle position" between weak and strong readings, but this position is unstable and ultimately collapses back into the weak reading that makes the thesis trivial.

**First, the critical problem**: The defence's "critical reading" (theories overlap enough to appear competitive but not enough for genuine arbitration) is just a redescription of the weak reading with added pessimism about convergence. If theories "share enough subject matter to generate apparent empirical conflicts," then they share enough subject matter for adversarial collaboration. The defence adds that these conflicts "never achieve genuine arbitration power," but this is an empirical prediction about how the science will develop, not a conceptual impossibility. Slow convergence is not the same as impossible testing.

**Second**: The defence's own examples undermine it. The claim that "no possible evidence pattern would force abandonment of either theory" is falsified by the history of consciousness science itself. Various consciousness theories HAVE been abandoned or heavily modified under empirical pressure: Cartesian theater models gave way to distributed processing accounts; simple first-order theories were revised to accommodate blindsight and other dissociations; quantum consciousness theories lost credibility as neurobiological evidence accumulated. The defence cherry-picks two currently prominent theories and treats their persistence as evidence of impossibility, when it might just reflect that both capture something important.

**Third, and fatally**: The defence's criterion for "sufficient overlap" is impossibly high. It demands that theories share "success conditions" such that one theory's confirmation constitutes the other's disconfirmation. But this standard would eliminate adversarial collaboration in the comparison cases too. Competing evolutionary psychology theories can each "accommodate the total evidence within their own framework" by adjusting auxiliary assumptions; competing economic theories can explain away contrary market data. The defence has now made adversarial collaboration impossible everywhere, which was precisely the "proves too much" objection it needed to escape.

**Most devastatingly**: The defence admits theories "overlap enough to make them appear competitive" and "both make predictions about brain states" and "both care about reports." This concedes everything the objection needs. If theories overlap enough to make contradictory predictions about the same patients, generate empirical data, and create theoretical pressure (even if not decisive pressure), then adversarial collaboration is POSSIBLE—perhaps difficult, perhaps slow to converge, but not impossible or incoherent.

The thesis claimed impossibility. The defence has retreated to "difficult and unlikely to quickly resolve." That's not a thesis—that's just the normal situation in complex science. The objection stands: the argument equivocates, and on neither reading does it establish impossibility.

</details>
