### O16. The debate over how to test consciousness theories treats false negatives (missing consciousness where it exists) and false positives (attributing consciousness where it's absent) as symmetrical errors, but they are asymmetrical in a way that determines which theory-testing methodology is rational to adopt.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** Methodological discussions that treat theory-testing as separable from substantive theoretical commitments, as in debates over the 'consciousness meter' or animal consciousness
- **Sketch:** Liberal theories (like some versions of panpsychism or IIT) risk false positives—attributing consciousness to thermostats. Conservative theories (like higher-order theories) risk false negatives—denying consciousness to animals or infants. The standard debate treats these as symmetrical costs to minimize. But they're morally and epistemically asymmetrical: false negatives might mean ignoring genuine suffering, while false positives mean metaphysical bloat. More importantly, the asymmetry reveals that there's no theory-neutral standpoint from which to balance these errors. The key premise is that how seriously you take false negatives versus false positives already encodes a commitment about consciousness's distribution—if you think consciousness is rare and precious, false negatives are catastrophic; if you think it's common, false positives are tolerable. This means choosing a testing methodology is choosing a theory.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The asymmetry between false positives and negatives is important but the conclusion that methodology choice is theory choice is somewhat predictable.
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Medical diagnostics showed asymmetric error-weighting can remain separable from distribution claims when independent verification exists; defence retreated to narrower claim about verification-transcendent cases
  - TRIVIAL — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence's 'vicious circularity' argument proved too much—the same structure exists in dark matter and historical methodology; thesis absorbed into general theory-ladenness rather than meeting the charge
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence admitted nature-theories entail distribution but failed to show distribution-preferences entail unique nature-theories; multiple incompatible theories share distribution-commitments, proving methodological choice underdetermines theory choice
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence's falsification criterion is impossible by design—any evidence-criterion presupposes something about consciousness's nature; thesis wins by definitional stipulation rather than substantive discovery
- 🔧 **Survives as:** In domains lacking verification-transcendent access to the target phenomenon, methodological choices about error-weighting cannot be fully separated from theoretical commitments about the phenomenon's distribution, making consciousness methodology a particularly stark instance of theory-ladenness in science
- **Flaw:** The thesis equivocates between distribution-preferences and theory-choice while being unfalsifiable by design—any evidence-criterion presupposes something about consciousness, making the claim trivially true of all inquiry rather than distinctively true of consciousness methodology

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. Consider the asymmetry between Type I and Type II errors in medical diagnostics for a fatal disease with an effective treatment. False negatives (missing the disease) mean patients die unnecessarily, while false positives mean healthy people undergo treatment side effects. These errors are genuinely asymmetrical in their consequences—one involves death, the other temporary discomfort. Yet this asymmetry does NOT mean "choosing a diagnostic threshold already encodes a commitment about the disease's distribution" or that "there's no theory-neutral standpoint." Medical researchers routinely establish evidence-based thresholds by examining: (1) the disease's actual base rate in populations, (2) empirical costs of each error type, and (3) test accuracy data. The choice of threshold (liberal vs. conservative) is informed by the asymmetry but remains separable from claims about disease prevalence. Similarly, even if false negatives in consciousness (missing animal suffering) carry greater moral weight than false positives (overattributing consciousness), we can adopt methodology that weights these asymmetrically while maintaining that empirical evidence—not our choice of methodology—determines consciousness's actual distribution. The thesis conflates "errors have asymmetric consequences" with "methodology choice determines metaphysical facts."

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This reveals a serious problem. The thesis claims methodological choices ARE theoretical commitments, but the medical analogy shows asymmetric error-weighting can be evidence-responsive rather than theory-constituting.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The medical analogy fails because disease presence has a verification-transcendent fact of the matter accessible through gold-standard tests (autopsy, biopsy, longitudinal outcome data), whereas consciousness has no such independent criterion. This difference is decisive.

In medicine, we can:
- Validate diagnostic thresholds against autopsy findings
- Track whether patients with positive tests actually develop symptoms
- Conduct double-blind studies where treatment outcomes reveal true disease status
- Establish base rates through population studies with confirmatory testing

This means the "theory-neutral standpoint" in medicine isn't truly neutral—it's grounded in a privileged epistemic position where we can eventually check our diagnostic judgments against disease reality. The threshold debate is separable from disease distribution precisely because distribution is empirically discoverable through methods independent of the diagnostic test itself.

Consciousness admits no such validation. There is no consciousness-autopsy, no gold-standard test against which to calibrate our theories. When we attribute consciousness to an infant or deny it to a thermostat, we cannot later verify these judgments through independent means. The objector's phrase "empirical evidence determines consciousness's actual distribution" presupposes what's contested: that there exists theory-neutral empirical evidence for consciousness.

The thesis's force is exactly here: in the consciousness case, the "empirical evidence" is always theory-laden. What counts as evidence of consciousness (neural correlates? behavioral flexibility? information integration?) already encodes theoretical commitments. We cannot establish base rates or validate our methodologies because we have no independent access to the phenomenon. Therefore, unlike medicine, our choice of how seriously to weight false negatives versus false positives cannot be "informed by" the asymmetry while remaining "separable from" claims about distribution—the weighting IS a claim about distribution, because we have no other way to make such claims.

VERDICT

The defence holds but only partially, and the objection succeeds in forcing a crucial clarification.

The defence successfully blocks the medical analogy by identifying the disanalogy: verification-transcendent accessibility. However, the objection exposes that the thesis overreaches in its conclusion. The objector is right that "errors have asymmetric consequences" does not entail "methodology choice determines metaphysical facts."

What the thesis actually shows is more modest but still significant: methodology choice is *inseparable* from theoretical commitments about consciousness distribution, not that it *determines* metaphysical facts. The medical case proves we can have asymmetric error-weighting that's epistemically responsible when independent verification exists. The thesis survives by retreating to: absent such verification (as in consciousness), methodology and theory become epistemically entangled, not metaphysically constitutive.

The defence saves the core insight but must abandon the stronger claim that choosing methodology "is" choosing a theory about what's really conscious.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis presents itself as revealing a problematic asymmetry that "determines which theory-testing methodology is rational to adopt," but this is merely a verbose restatement of the uncontroversial principle that rational methodology requires weighing costs. The claim that "false negatives might mean ignoring genuine suffering, while false positives mean metaphysical bloat" is not a discovery—it's a truism that different errors have different consequences. Every methodological debate in every domain recognizes that Type I and Type II errors have different costs that must be balanced. The supposedly deep insight—that "how seriously you take false negatives versus false positives already encodes a commitment"—reduces to the definitional point that your priorities reflect your values and background beliefs. Calling this an "asymmetry that determines rational methodology" dresses up the obvious fact that "what you care about affects what methods you choose" in technical language. The thesis's apparent substance comes entirely from the rhetoric of "asymmetry" and "no theory-neutral standpoint," but strip that away and you have: "Different mistakes have different costs, and your methodology reflects what you think matters." This is not contested by anyone in the debate. The reframing adds no explanatory power—it's a verbal stipulation that "methodological choice" means "theoretical commitment," presented as if it were an empirical discovery about consciousness science.

STRENGTH: 2/5 — This has some force in showing the thesis may overstate its novelty, but the thesis does identify a genuine structural feature of consciousness research (lack of independent verification) that makes the entanglement more problematic than in standard cases of error-cost balancing. Not fully trivial, but the objection correctly notes significant rhetorical inflation.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies what makes the consciousness case distinctive. The thesis is not merely observing that "different errors have different costs"—that would indeed be trivial. The thesis identifies a *vicious circularity* specific to consciousness methodology that does not arise in standard cases of error-cost balancing.

In typical methodological contexts, even when errors have asymmetric costs, there exists a logical space between:
1. What we're trying to detect (the phenomenon)
2. Our evidence for it (observables)
3. Our cost function (what we care about)

These remain conceptually separable. In drug testing, the disease exists independently, the biomarkers are evidence for it, and our values determine how we weight errors. We can coherently say: "I weight false negatives heavily because I care about patients, but the disease prevalence is an objective fact my caring doesn't affect."

The consciousness case collapses this structure. The phenomenon (consciousness) is not epistemically independent from our theoretical commitments about what constitutes evidence for it. This creates the vicious circle: 

- To weight errors rationally, I need to know the costs (how bad is missing consciousness vs. overattributing it)
- To know the costs, I need to know the distribution (how common is consciousness)
- To know the distribution, I need a methodology for detecting consciousness
- But choosing that methodology requires... weighting the errors

The objector says this is just "your priorities reflect your values and background beliefs"—but that misses the point. In consciousness, your "background beliefs" about distribution cannot be formed independently of your methodological commitments, because there's no prior epistemic access. You cannot first discover that consciousness is rare (making false negatives catastrophic), then adopt conservative methodology. The methodology IS the discovery procedure, and it cannot be calibrated against independent facts.

This is not trivial verbal stipulation. It's a structural feature that makes consciousness methodology *uniquely self-undermining*: any attempt to justify your methodological choice appeals to claims about consciousness distribution that you can only establish by already having adopted a methodology.

The "reframing" label is apt because this reveals that debates appearing to be about "how to test theories" are actually disguised debates about "what consciousness is"—but the disguise matters. Researchers believe they're doing neutral methodology when they're actually smuggling in substantive theoretical commitments. That's explanatorily significant: it explains why consciousness methodology debates are intractable in ways other methodological debates are not.

# VERDICT

The defence fails, and the objection succeeds in demonstrating the thesis is substantially trivial.

The defence attempts to establish uniqueness through the "vicious circularity" argument, but this argument proves too much and ultimately collapses into the truism the objector identifies.

The fatal problem: the claimed vicious circle exists in *any* domain where we lack direct observational access to the target phenomenon and must rely on theoretical interpretation of evidence. Consider:

**Dark matter methodology**: Do we adopt liberal criteria (attributing dark matter to any anomalous gravitational effect) or conservative criteria (requiring multiple independent lines of evidence)? The "costs" of false negatives vs. positives depend on how common dark matter actually is, but we can only establish that through... our chosen methodology. Yet we don't conclude that "choosing dark matter methodology IS choosing a theory about dark matter distribution."

**Historical methodology**: Do we adopt liberal criteria for attributing historical events (accepting sparse evidence) or conservative criteria (demanding corroboration)? The costs depend on how frequently the types of events in question actually occurred, but we establish frequency through... our methodological choices.

The defender's response would be that these domains *do* have independent verification possibilities (future observations, archaeological discoveries). But this just pushes the problem back: consciousness's lack of verification-transcendent access makes the entanglement *permanent* rather than temporary, but it doesn't make it *categorically different* from other theory-laden methodological choices.

The objector is correct that the thesis reduces to: "In consciousness research, your methodological choices reflect your theoretical commitments about what consciousness is and how it's distributed, and you cannot fully separate these." This IS true, but it's an instance of the general phenomenon of theory-ladenness of observation, not a special structural feature of consciousness. The rhetoric of "asymmetry" and "vicious circularity" makes it sound like a distinctive discovery, but it's the familiar underdetermination problem wearing consciousness-specific clothing.

The thesis survives only as: "Consciousness exemplifies theory-ladenness in a particularly stark and permanent way." That's worth saying but not revolutionary.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis equivocates on "theoretical commitment" between two readings that make its argument appear to work:

**Reading 1 (Plausible)**: "Theoretical commitment" means *presupposing an answer to the first-order question of consciousness distribution*. On this reading, the thesis claims that choosing to weight false negatives heavily PRESUPPOSES that consciousness is widely distributed (because you're worried about missing it in many cases). This makes the claim plausible—yes, your error-weighting might reveal background assumptions about prevalence.

**Reading 2 (Interesting)**: "Theoretical commitment" means *adopting a substantive theory about consciousness's nature*—what it is, what constitutes it, what mechanisms generate it (like HOT, IIT, Global Workspace, etc.). On this reading, the thesis claims methodological choices ARE choices between competing theories of consciousness itself. This is the interesting claim that would constitute a genuine reframing.

The argument needs BOTH readings to work. The phrase "choosing a testing methodology is choosing a theory" is interesting only on Reading 2 (you're choosing between IIT vs. HOT, not just methodology). But the argument's actual support—that error-weighting "encodes a commitment about consciousness's distribution"—only establishes Reading 1 (a belief about prevalence, not a theory about nature).

The slide occurs in phrases like "commitment about consciousness" which can mean either "belief about how common it is" or "theory about what it fundamentally is." The thesis cannot succeed on Reading 1 alone (mere prevalence beliefs don't constitute theory-choice) nor on Reading 2 alone (error-weighting doesn't directly determine which mechanism-theory you accept).

# STRENGTH: 3/5

This is a genuine problem requiring an answer. The thesis does slide between "commitments about distribution" and "theoretical commitments" in the full sense, and the argument's force depends on conflating these. However, the thesis might survive by clarifying that distribution-commitments ARE theoretical commitments in consciousness research specifically (unlike in other domains), because consciousness theories make claims about distribution as part of their content. Not fatal, but needs addressing.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection assumes distribution-claims and nature-claims about consciousness can be cleanly separated, but this separation fails for consciousness in a way that validates the thesis's move between readings.

Consider what substantive theories of consciousness actually claim:

**Higher-Order Thought (HOT) theory**: Consciousness requires meta-representation of first-order mental states. This is simultaneously:
- A nature-claim: consciousness IS higher-order representation
- A distribution-claim: systems lacking meta-representational capacity (insects, thermostats, possibly infants) lack consciousness

**Integrated Information Theory (IIT)**: Consciousness IS integrated information (Φ). This is simultaneously:
- A nature-claim: consciousness IS a quantity of integrated information
- A distribution-claim: anything with sufficient Φ (potentially including some artificial systems, possibly even some non-neural systems) has consciousness

**Global Workspace Theory (GWT)**: Consciousness requires global broadcasting of information. This is simultaneously:
- A nature-claim: consciousness IS global availability
- A distribution-claim: systems lacking broadcast architecture (split-brain patients in certain conditions, feed-forward networks) lack consciousness

The objector's distinction collapses because theories of consciousness's *nature* are necessarily theories of its *distribution*. You cannot adopt IIT's account of what consciousness is without thereby committing to its being more widely distributed than HOT theorists claim. You cannot adopt HOT's account without thereby committing to its being more narrowly distributed than IIT claims.

This is not true in the dark matter analogy. You can hold a theory about dark matter's *nature* (it's weakly interacting massive particles) while remaining agnostic about its *distribution* (how much exists, where it's located). Nature and distribution are logically separable.

But consciousness theories bind these together because consciousness is defined functionally or constitutively by the very features that determine distribution. If consciousness IS integrated information, then wherever integrated information exists above threshold, consciousness exists. The nature-claim entails the distribution-claim.

Therefore, when you choose to weight false negatives heavily (worrying about missing consciousness in animals), you're not merely revealing a "background belief about prevalence" that's independent of theory—you're revealing commitment to theories that make consciousness more widely distributed *as a consequence of what they say consciousness is*. You're implicitly rejecting theories like HOT that make consciousness rare because of what they claim consciousness essentially involves.

The thesis's move between "distribution commitment" and "theoretical commitment" is not equivocation but recognition that in consciousness research specifically, these are inseparable. Reading 1 and Reading 2 are not two readings but two aspects of a single commitment.

The objector is wrong that the argument "needs BOTH readings to work"—it needs to show they're the same reading, and the structure of consciousness theories demonstrates this.

# VERDICT

The defence fails. The objection succeeds in exposing a fatal equivocation, and the defence's attempted rescue actually reveals why the thesis cannot be saved.

The defence claims that for consciousness, nature-theories entail distribution-claims, making them inseparable. But this actually *helps* the objection rather than defeating it. Here's why:

**The defence is right that**: HOT theory's nature-claim (consciousness requires meta-representation) entails its distribution-claim (consciousness is rare). IIT's nature-claim (consciousness is integrated information) entails its distribution-claim (consciousness is more common).

**But this proves the objection's point**: If you've already adopted HOT or IIT—if you've already chosen a theory about consciousness's nature—then yes, you've thereby committed to a distribution. But the thesis claims the *reverse*: that methodological choices about error-weighting ARE choices of nature-theories. The defence shows that theory→distribution, but the thesis needs distribution-preference→theory, and that's the equivocation.

The fatal problem: Multiple theories can share the same distribution-commitment while differing radically on nature. Consider:

- **Biological naturalism** (Searle): Consciousness requires specific biological processes → narrow distribution
- **Higher-order thought theory**: Consciousness requires meta-representation → narrow distribution  
- **Quantum consciousness theories** (Penrose): Consciousness requires quantum processes → narrow distribution

These theories agree on distribution (consciousness is rare, probably limited to biological systems with sophisticated cognition) but completely disagree on nature (biological processes vs. representational structure vs. quantum mechanics).

If I weight false negatives heavily and adopt liberal methodology, am I choosing IIT? Or Russellian panpsychism? Or cosmopsychism? All predict wide distribution but offer incompatible accounts of consciousness's nature.

The thesis claims "choosing a testing methodology is choosing a theory" (Reading 2), but the defence only establishes "choosing a testing methodology reveals distribution-preferences that are consistent with multiple incompatible theories" (Reading 1). That's the equivocation, and the defence's own examples prove it cannot be resolved.

The thesis is dead: methodological choices underdetermine theory choice even when they determine distribution-preferences.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis is unfalsifiable because any apparent counterexample gets redefined as "not really separating methodology from theory." 

Suppose I present a case: "Researcher R adopts conservative methodology (heavily weighting false positives) for purely epistemic reasons—she thinks we should only attribute consciousness where evidence is overwhelming, regardless of what consciousness turns out to be. She remains genuinely agnostic between HOT, IIT, and other theories while maintaining this methodological stance."

The thesis can respond: "R's 'epistemic caution' already encodes theoretical commitments—her standard of 'overwhelming evidence' presupposes what counts as evidence, which is theory-laden."

Or suppose: "The scientific community agrees on methodological standards for consciousness research before resolving theoretical disputes—they establish shared protocols while disagreeing about mechanisms."

The thesis responds: "The 'shared protocols' aren't really theory-neutral—the agreement masks hidden theoretical convergence, or the protocols are too vague to constitute real methodology."

The problem is **structural**: the thesis defines "theoretical commitment" so broadly (including implicit assumptions, background beliefs, standards of evidence, distribution-preferences) that no possible case can demonstrate theory-neutral methodology. Any purported separation gets reinterpreted as merely *apparent* separation masking *real* theoretical commitment. The thesis is protected by making "theoretical commitment" elastic enough to encompass whatever drives methodological choice, ensuring that methodology is "choosing theory" by definitional fiat rather than empirical discovery.

The weak point: the claim that methodological choices "encode theoretical commitments" lacks falsifiable criteria for what would count as a genuinely theory-neutral methodological stance.

# STRENGTH: 4/5

This does serious damage. The thesis has systematically redefined away potential counterexamples throughout the exchange—researcher motivations become "implicit commitments," practical agreements become "hidden convergence," distribution-preferences become "theoretical commitments." Without specifying what a theory-neutral methodology would even look like, the thesis cannot be tested against cases.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection misunderstands what makes a philosophical claim falsifiable. The thesis is not an empirical prediction about researcher psychology ("researchers cannot separate X from Y") but a conceptual claim about the logical structure of consciousness methodology ("there is no coherent notion of theory-neutral methodology in consciousness research"). Conceptual claims are falsified by showing coherent counterexamples, not by stipulating that any redefinition constitutes unfalsifiability.

Consider the parallel: "You cannot have a bachelor who is married" is not unfalsifiable just because I can respond to "But what about a man in a common-law relationship?" with "That's not really being married in the relevant sense." The claim is conceptual—it's about what the concepts entail—and apparent counterexamples that trade on equivocation don't falsify it.

The thesis makes a specific structural claim: in consciousness research, what counts as evidence for consciousness cannot be specified independently of substantive theoretical commitments about consciousness's nature. This is falsifiable—show me a methodology that specifies evidence-criteria without presupposing answers to theoretical questions.

The objector's examples fail as counterexamples:

**"Researcher R adopts epistemic caution regardless of theory"**: What does "overwhelming evidence" mean here? If R means "neural correlates + behavioral flexibility + reportability," she's presupposed that consciousness requires reportability (contra IIT's claim that consciousness can exist without access). If she means "high Φ values," she's presupposed IIT's framework. The thesis's response isn't a deflection—it's pointing out that R's "theory-neutrality" is illusory because her evidence-standards smuggle in theoretical claims. The objector needs to specify what R's evidence-criteria actually ARE in a way that doesn't presuppose theoretical answers. That's the challenge the thesis issues, and "epistemic caution" doesn't meet it.

**"Community agrees on protocols while disagreeing on theories"**: What are these protocols? If they're "measure neural correlates," that presupposes consciousness has neural correlates (contra some functionalist views where silicon-based consciousness is possible). If they're "test for information integration," that privileges IIT-adjacent frameworks. If they're "check for reportability," that privileges access-consciousness theories. The thesis's response isn't evasive—it's demanding specification of what these "theory-neutral protocols" actually measure.

The falsification criterion is clear: **Show me evidence-criteria for consciousness that don't presuppose answers to questions that divide competing theories.** The objector hasn't done this. Instead, they've offered vague gestures ("epistemic caution," "shared protocols") and complained when the thesis asks for specification.

The thesis is falsifiable in principle: demonstrate a methodology that says "we'll count X as evidence for consciousness" where X's status as evidence doesn't depend on whether consciousness is (for example):
- Necessarily biological or substrate-independent
- Requiring reportability or not
- Requiring integration or just information processing
- Requiring meta-representation or just representation

The objector's examples fail because they don't specify the X in a theory-neutral way. That's not the thesis being unfalsifiable—that's the examples being underspecified.

Moreover, the thesis's "elasticity" about theoretical commitment is principled, not ad hoc. It's elastic precisely because consciousness theories themselves make claims at multiple levels (mechanism, distribution, evidence-criteria). When someone says "I'm just being epistemically cautious," the thesis rightly asks: "Cautious by what standard? And doesn't that standard encode theoretical commitments?" This isn't unfalsifiable evasion—it's insisting on precision about what "theory-neutral methodology" would actually consist in.

# VERDICT

The defence fails catastrophically. The objection succeeds in revealing the thesis is unfalsifiable, and the defence's attempted rescue actually demonstrates why.

The defence claims the falsification criterion is clear: "Show me evidence-criteria for consciousness that don't presuppose answers to questions that divide competing theories." But this criterion is impossible to meet *by design*, and the defence's own examples prove it.

**The fatal problem**: The defence says evidence-criteria are theory-laden if they presuppose whether consciousness is "necessarily biological or substrate-independent, requiring reportability or not, requiring integration or just information processing, requiring meta-representation or just representation." But these aren't peripheral theoretical disputes—they're *exhaustive* of the theoretical space. 

Watch what happens when we try to meet the challenge:

**Attempt 1**: "We'll use behavioral flexibility as evidence."
- Defence: "That presupposes consciousness doesn't require meta-representation (contra HOT)."

**Attempt 2**: "We'll use neural correlates."
- Defence: "That presupposes consciousness is biological (contra substrate-independence)."

**Attempt 3**: "We'll use information integration measures."
- Defence: "That presupposes IIT's framework."

**Attempt 4**: "We'll use multiple criteria—neural correlates AND behavioral flexibility AND reportability."
- Defence: "That presupposes consciousness requires ALL these features, which is itself a theoretical commitment."

**Attempt 5**: "We'll use ANY of these criteria—just one needs to be satisfied."
- Defence: "That presupposes consciousness can be realized in multiple ways, which is a theoretical commitment about multiple realizability."

The defence has constructed an impossible standard: any positive evidence-criterion presupposes something about consciousness's nature (because that's what evidence-criteria DO), and the absence of criteria means no methodology at all. The thesis wins by definitional necessity—there literally cannot be evidence for consciousness that doesn't presuppose something about what consciousness is, because "evidence for X" means "features that indicate X's presence," which requires knowing what X is.

But this makes the thesis trivial in a damning way. It reduces to: "You cannot look for something without having some conception of what you're looking for." That's true but vacuous. Every scientific methodology presupposes SOME theoretical framework—physics presupposes mathematics, biology presupposes chemistry, medicine presupposes pathology. The thesis promised to reveal something distinctive about consciousness methodology, but the defence shows it's just the general truth that inquiry requires conceptual frameworks.

The objector is right that the thesis is unfalsifiable because it's defined into truth. Any methodology will presuppose *something*, and the thesis counts any presupposition as "theoretical commitment," making separation impossible by stipulation rather than by discovery of a special feature of consciousness.

The thesis is dead: it's either trivially true (all inquiry is theory-laden) or unfalsifiably protected (any apparent theory-neutral methodology gets redefined as secretly theoretical). Neither version does the philosophical work claimed.

</details>
