### O17. Any proposed empirical test between consciousness theories presupposes a solution to the grain problem—whether consciousness has privileged temporal and spatial scales—but the grain problem is itself theory-dependent, making theory-testing circular.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · wounded
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** Proposals for adversarial collaboration frameworks (like Mudrik et al.) that assume we can design theory-neutral experiments by just getting precise about predictions
- **Sketch:** Proposed tests of consciousness theories assume we know what the 'right' grain is—whether to measure neural activity at the level of individual neurons, assemblies, or whole regions, and whether on timescales of milliseconds, seconds, or longer. But different theories entail different grains: IIT cares about the maximally integrated system at any scale, GWT cares about broadcast dynamics at ~100-300ms windows, quantum theories require much finer temporal resolution. The key premise is that any actual experimental paradigm must choose specific measurement scales, but that choice already privileges certain theories. For instance, testing whether information integration predicts reportability requires deciding what scale to measure integration at, but different scales would give different answers about which theory wins. This isn't just a practical limitation—it shows that the theories don't make determinate enough predictions without additional grain-specifying assumptions that are themselves contentious.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The grain problem is real but well-known; showing it makes testing circular is competent but not surprising.
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence showed interpretation is theory-laden but did not escape the equivocation between theories predicting different scales versus measurement requiring theoretical presupposition
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 2/5, answered. Defence successfully showed the thesis makes falsifiable predictions about adversarial collaboration failures and theoretical convergence conditions
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 3/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence showed the problem is particularly severe but admitted it remains a known type—an intensification of Duhem-Quine rather than novel argument
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence retreated to consciousness's epistemic uniqueness but this either makes all consciousness science impossible or fails to distinguish from other multi-scale sciences
- 🔧 **Survives as:** The grain problem in consciousness science represents an unusually severe case of theoretical underdetermination because grain assumptions are constitutive of theories and the explanandum is grain-relative, making standard adversarial collaboration frameworks insufficient without additional methodological innovations
- **Flaw:** The thesis equivocates between theories disagreeing about scales and testing being circular, and when pressed either proves too much (making all multi-scale or all consciousness science impossible) or reduces to a familiar underdetermination problem

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "theory-dependent" between two readings: (1) different theories predict different privileged scales should matter, versus (2) we cannot measure at any scale without already assuming which theory is correct. The argument is plausible on reading (1)—yes, IIT predicts integration matters at the scale of maximal phi, GWT predicts ~100-300ms broadcast windows matter, etc. But the thesis is only interesting on reading (2)—if it merely said "theories disagree about which scales matter," that would make testing straightforward, not circular: measure at multiple scales and see which predictions pan out. The circularity claim requires that choosing measurement scales *already presupposes a theoretical solution*, not merely that theories make different scale-specific predictions. Yet the argument only establishes (1): it shows theories entail different grains, not that measuring at any particular grain requires antecedently accepting a theory. The neural assembly researcher measuring at 100ms windows needn't believe GWT to do so; they're testing whether GWT's prediction at that scale succeeds. The premise "any experimental paradigm must choose specific measurement scales" supports (1), while "making theory-testing circular" requires (2). The argument slides between these without justification.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This seriously undermines the circularity claim. The thesis could survive by showing why multi-scale measurement cannot adjudicate, but as stated it conflates theoretical disagreement about scales with methodological inability to test at different scales.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objector misses that the circularity runs deeper than mere disagreement about which scales to measure. The problem is not that we cannot physically measure at different scales—of course we can—but that *interpreting what we measure* at any scale already requires theoretical commitments that beg the question.

Consider the objector's proposed solution: "measure at multiple scales and see which predictions pan out." But this assumes measurements at different scales produce theory-neutral data that can adjudicate between views. They do not. Take a concrete case: we measure both fine-grained neuronal spiking (1ms resolution) and coarse-grained BOLD signals (seconds resolution) during a reportability task. IIT says consciousness corresponds to maximal phi, which might be found at an intermediate assembly level. GWT says it corresponds to broadcast, detectable in the coarse dynamics. Now: the fine-grained data shows complex integration patterns that don't correlate with reports; the coarse data shows broadcast that does correlate. Has GWT won?

Not obviously. The IIT theorist replies: "You're measuring the wrong grain—phi is maximal at the assembly level you didn't measure, and reportability is a post-conscious access phenomenon anyway, not consciousness itself." The GWT theorist cannot respond "but my scale's predictions succeeded" without already assuming that reportability tracks consciousness and that correlation at their preferred scale is the right success criterion. 

The objector's reading (2) is thus vindicated: choosing to treat the 100-300ms scale as evidentially relevant *does* presuppose theoretical commitments—specifically, that reportability is a valid consciousness marker and that this temporal scale is where consciousness-relevant dynamics occur. These aren't neutral measurement choices; they're theoretical assumptions that favor GWT from the start.

The multi-scale measurement strategy fails because theories don't just predict different scales matter—they predict different scales matter *for different target phenomena*, and they disagree about which phenomena constitute consciousness. There is no theory-neutral fact about whether the fine-grained integration or coarse-grained broadcast "better predicts consciousness" because they disagree about what consciousness is.

VERDICT

The defence fails, though not completely.

It successfully shows the problem is deeper than the objector suggests—we cannot simply measure at all scales and see what correlates, because theories disagree about what should correlate with what. The objector's reading (2) is partially vindicated.

However, the defence does not fully escape the equivocation charge. Even granting that interpretation requires theoretical commitments, there remains a crucial asymmetry the defence ignores: some experimental outcomes could still falsify theories regardless of grain choices. If IIT predicts maximal phi at *some* scale should correlate with reportability, and we find *no* scale where it does, that's evidence against IIT even without knowing the "right" scale in advance. The theory makes an existential claim (there exists a scale where...), and we can test existential claims by exhaustive search.

The thesis survives in weakened form: theory-testing is *complicated* by grain-dependence and requires auxiliary assumptions, but "circular" overstates the case. The defence shows interpretation is theory-laden, but not that all possible empirical outcomes are compatible with all theories.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis protects itself by classifying any apparent success in theory-neutral testing as "not really theory-neutral after all." When opponents propose multi-scale measurement strategies, the thesis responds that interpretation at any scale involves theoretical commitments—but this makes the circularity claim unfalsifiable. What would count as a counterexample? If researchers design experiments that successfully discriminate between theories, the thesis can always respond: "Ah, but your choice of which measurements to take, which correlations to examine, which phenomena to count as consciousness—these already embedded theoretical assumptions, so you didn't really test neutrally." The weak premise is the claim that measurement scale choices "already presuppose theoretical solutions." This is protected by strategic redefinition: any measurement choice that seems neutral gets reclassified as theory-laden upon inspection. The thesis could even survive researchers explicitly testing IIT's existential claim (that maximal phi at *some* scale correlates with reports) by insisting that "reportability" as an operationalization already presupposes certain theories over others. Every apparent case of successful theory-testing gets explained away as having smuggled in theoretical assumptions. The thesis cannot specify in advance what theory-neutral testing would look like, because any concrete proposal becomes, by definition, theory-dependent once we examine it closely enough. This isn't empirical caution—it's immunization.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This seriously damages the thesis. The "theory-dependent" claim functions as an all-purpose deflection. The thesis needs to specify what testing conditions it would accept as non-circular, or acknowledge that some degree of theory-ladenness doesn't prevent meaningful empirical discrimination.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The unfalsifiability charge mistakes a substantive empirical claim for a conceptual truism. The thesis is not saying "all observation is theory-laden" (which would indeed be unfalsifiable philosophy of science). It makes a specific, falsifiable claim: *current leading consciousness theories disagree about grain in ways that prevent the kind of adversarial collaboration frameworks currently proposed from working*.

Here's what would falsify it: theories could converge on grain despite disagreeing on mechanisms. Suppose IIT and GWT both predicted that consciousness-relevant dynamics occur at the 100-300ms timescale and the neural assembly level, but disagreed about *what happens* at that scale (integration vs. broadcast). Then we could design genuinely theory-neutral tests: measure at the agreed-upon grain and see which pattern obtains. The thesis would be false.

Or theories could make grain-transcendent predictions. Suppose IIT predicted that *regardless of measurement scale*, systems with higher phi would show greater reportability, faster learning, or richer phenomenology. Then we could test this without resolving the grain problem first—just measure phi however we can and check the predictions. The thesis would be false.

The actual situation is neither. IIT explicitly says phi must be measured at the scale of maximal integration (which scale that is remains empirically undetermined). GWT explicitly ties consciousness to specific temporal dynamics. Quantum theories require temporal resolutions orders of magnitude finer. These aren't auxiliary choices—they're core theoretical commitments. The thesis simply reports this feature of the theoretical landscape.

The objector demands: "specify what theory-neutral testing would look like." But that's backwards. The thesis claims current theories don't permit it—asking the thesis to describe what it claims is impossible is like demanding a proof of impossibility provide an example of the impossible thing. The burden is on adversarial collaboration proponents to show their frameworks can handle grain disagreement, not on the thesis to solve the problem it identifies.

Regarding falsifiability: the thesis makes the testable prediction that adversarial collaboration attempts will fail or devolve into disputes about measurement choices. If Mudrik et al.'s framework produces clear, accepted adjudications between theories, the thesis is falsified. That hasn't happened—which is evidence for, not unfalsifiability of, the thesis.

The reportability example actually proves the point rather than immunizing against it. It's not strategic redefinition to note that IIT theorists genuinely, explicitly reject reportability as a consciousness marker (they distinguish experience from access). This isn't the thesis explaining away counterevidence—it's the thesis accurately describing why the proposed test doesn't work. When theories disagree about the explanandum itself, you cannot test them by seeing which better predicts an operationalization one theory rejects.

# VERDICT

The defence succeeds.

The unfalsifiability charge fails because it misidentifies what kind of claim the thesis makes. The thesis is not a philosophical position about theory-ladenness in general—it's a specific empirical observation about the current state of consciousness science. It is falsifiable by theoretical convergence on grain, by theories making grain-transcendent predictions, or by adversarial collaborations succeeding despite grain disagreement.

The objector's demand that the thesis "specify what theory-neutral testing would look like" reveals the confusion. The thesis claims such testing is currently impossible given how theories are structured—that's the whole point. Asking it to describe the impossible thing is not exposing unfalsifiability; it's confirming the thesis. If the defender could specify theory-neutral testing conditions, the thesis would be false.

The reportability case is particularly telling. The objector treats it as immunization ("any operationalization gets reclassified as theory-laden"), but IIT theorists' rejection of reportability as a consciousness marker isn't post-hoc—it's explicit in their published work. The thesis isn't strategically redefining terms; it's accurately reporting that theories disagree about what consciousness *is*, not just how to measure it.

The thesis stands. It makes a falsifiable claim about the structure of current theories and predicts that proposed testing frameworks will fail in specific ways. That prediction can be checked empirically, and so far it's been confirmed.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT. This thesis is a straightforward application of the Duhem-Quine problem to consciousness science. Duhem (1906) established that testing scientific theories requires auxiliary assumptions, and Quine (1951) extended this to show that empirical evidence underdetermines theory choice because any hypothesis can be maintained by adjusting background assumptions. The thesis simply instantiates this general problem: "grain assumptions" are the auxiliary hypotheses, and the claim that "theory-testing is circular" restates underdetermination. Kuhn (1962) made the same point about paradigm-dependent observation in scientific revolutions—that theoretical frameworks determine what counts as relevant data and how to interpret it. The thesis's examples (IIT requiring maximal phi scale, GWT requiring broadcast timescales) precisely illustrate Kuhn's claim that competing paradigms are "incommensurable" because they individuate phenomena differently. Even the specific move—that operationalizations like "reportability" are theory-laden—directly echoes Hanson (1958) on observation being theory-laden. The "grain problem" is just a domain-specific label for the general problem of theoretical underdetermination. The thesis presents no novel argument beyond applying this well-established position to consciousness research. It is Duhem-Quine dressed in neuroscience terminology.

STRENGTH: 3/5 — This is a genuine problem for the thesis's originality, though answerable. The thesis could survive by showing that the grain problem has unique features that make standard responses to Duhem-Quine inadequate, or by arguing it's a particularly acute instance worth highlighting even if the general form is known. But as stated, the position is indeed already taken by philosophy of science since the 1950s.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection conflates formal similarity with substantive identity. Yes, the grain problem shares structural features with Duhem-Quine—both involve auxiliary assumptions affecting theory testing. But this is like saying evolutionary game theory is "already taken" by classical game theory because both use payoff matrices. The question is whether consciousness science presents a *distinctively severe* instance that resists standard solutions to underdetermination.

It does, in three ways that make this more than Duhem-Quine repackaged:

**First, the grain problem is constitutive, not auxiliary.** Standard Duhem-Quine cases involve auxiliary assumptions that are in principle separable from core theory. When testing Einstein vs. Newton, assumptions about instrument reliability or atmospheric refraction are genuinely auxiliary—they're not what the theories are about. But grain assumptions in consciousness science are constitutive of the theories themselves. IIT doesn't just happen to require measuring at maximal phi scale as a practical matter—the scale of maximal integration *is what consciousness is* according to IIT. GWT doesn't just contingently predict ~100-300ms dynamics—those temporal dynamics *constitute* the global workspace. You cannot separate the "core theory" from the "grain assumptions" because the grain is part of what's being theorized about. This makes the problem qualitatively different from standard underdetermination.

**Second, grain disagreement is multi-dimensional and systematic.** Duhem-Quine typically involves adjusting one or a few auxiliary hypotheses. But consciousness theories disagree about grain across multiple independent dimensions simultaneously: spatial scale (neurons vs. assemblies vs. regions), temporal scale (milliseconds vs. seconds), compositional boundaries (what counts as one system vs. many), and organizational level (anatomical vs. functional individuation). These dimensions interact—your temporal grain choice constrains your spatial grain options, and vice versa. This creates a combinatorial explosion of measurement spaces, each potentially vindicating different theories. Standard responses to Duhem-Quine (like inference to best explanation or theoretical virtues) assume a manageable space of alternatives. The multi-dimensional grain problem exceeds that assumption.

**Third, the explanandum itself is grain-relative.** In normal science, competing theories aim to explain the same phenomenon individuated in theory-neutral terms (planetary motion, chemical reactions, evolutionary adaptation). Duhem-Quine says evidence underdetermines which explanation is correct, but the explanandum is stable. In consciousness science, the explanandum—what counts as consciousness, when it's present, what its boundaries are—is itself grain-relative. "Is this system conscious?" has different answers depending on whether we're asking about millisecond-scale quantum coherence, 100ms-scale broadcasts, or second-scale integration. This isn't incommensurability in Kuhn's sense (different paradigms organizing the same world differently)—it's the world itself having different structure at different grains, with no fact of the matter about which grain captures "real" consciousness. Duhem-Quine assumes a fixed explanandum with underdetermined explanations. The grain problem involves grain-relative explananda.

The thesis isn't just "applying" Duhem-Quine to neuroscience. It's identifying features of consciousness science that make it resistant to standard philosophical solutions to underdetermination. Philosophers of science have responses to Duhem-Quine (convergence, novel predictions, theoretical virtues), but these responses assume the problem has the structure of cases like Einstein vs. Newton. The grain problem has a different structure.

# VERDICT

The defence fails, but the thesis survives in weakened form.

The defence successfully shows the grain problem has distinctive features—particularly that grain assumptions are constitutive rather than auxiliary, and that the explanandum is grain-relative. These are genuine differences from standard Duhem-Quine cases, and they do make the problem more severe.

However, the defence overstates the novelty. The claim that grain assumptions are "constitutive, not auxiliary" is itself a familiar move in philosophy of science—it's the basis of entity realism (Hacking 1983) and the argument that different levels of organization aren't merely epistemic but ontological (Wimsatt 2007). The multi-dimensional nature of grain disagreement, while making the problem more complex, doesn't make it different in kind from other cases of severe underdetermination (quantum mechanics interpretations face similar combinatorial issues). And the grain-relativity of the explanandum, while acute in consciousness science, appears in other domains where theories individuate their target phenomena differently (species concepts in biology, particle individuation in quantum field theory).

What the defence establishes is that consciousness science presents a *particularly severe* instance of theory-dependent measurement, severe enough that standard philosophical solutions may not work. But "particularly severe instance of known problem" is different from "novel problem." The thesis is not "already taken" in the sense that it's trivial or adds nothing—it makes a valuable contribution by showing how acute the underdetermination is in this domain. But it is "already taken" in the sense that the basic argumentative structure and the type of problem are well-established.

The thesis survives as a useful application and intensification of existing philosophy of science, not as a fundamentally new argument. That's still valuable—showing that a domain has features making it resistant to standard solutions is important work—but it's not the revolutionary claim the original framing suggested.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — # ATTEMPT

The argument, if successful, would prove that ANY science involving scale-dependent phenomena is impossible to test empirically. Consider fluid dynamics: Navier-Stokes equations apply at macroscopic scales, molecular dynamics at micro-scales, and quantum mechanics at atomic scales. Different "theories" of fluid behavior predict different phenomena matter at different grains—turbulence emerges at certain Reynolds numbers, viscosity at molecular scales, quantum effects at even finer scales. By the thesis's logic, testing whether Navier-Stokes or molecular dynamics better predicts a phenomenon would be "circular" because choosing measurement scales "already presupposes" which theory is correct. Yet fluid dynamics successfully adjudicates between scale-dependent theories precisely by measuring at multiple scales and checking where predictions succeed.

The same applies to any multi-scale science: materials science (crystal vs. continuum models), ecology (organism vs. population vs. ecosystem levels), economics (micro vs. macro), geology (plate tectonics vs. mineral formation). All involve theories that "entail different grains" and require "choosing specific measurement scales." If the thesis were right that this makes testing circular, we couldn't have successfully developed any of these sciences. The weak premise is that "choosing measurement scales already privileges certain theories" entails circularity. This conflates (a) theories making scale-specific predictions with (b) inability to test those predictions. Every successful multi-scale science shows (a) doesn't entail (b).

The thesis must either accept that fluid dynamics, materials science, ecology, etc. are also "circular and untestable"—which is absurd—or explain why consciousness is uniquely different in a way that makes the same scale-dependence problematic only there.

# STRENGTH: 4/5

This seriously damages the thesis. The argument structure, if valid, would make all multi-scale science impossible, which is clearly false. The thesis needs a principled distinction between consciousness and other scale-dependent phenomena to avoid this overgeneration.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The objection assumes consciousness is just another multi-scale physical phenomenon like fluids or materials, but this misses what makes consciousness uniquely problematic: **consciousness is the epistemic access condition for all empirical measurement, including measurement of itself.**

In fluid dynamics, we can test Navier-Stokes vs. molecular dynamics because we have theory-independent access to the explanandum. We can measure pressure, velocity, turbulence through instruments whose operation doesn't depend on which fluid theory is correct. The measuring devices and experimenters stand outside the phenomenon being measured. When predictions fail at a given scale, we can objectively verify the failure—the fluid behaves differently than predicted, and this difference is detectable regardless of theoretical commitments about which scale is "fundamental."

Consciousness is radically different because **the only epistemic access we have to consciousness is through consciousness itself**. We cannot measure consciousness the way we measure fluid pressure—from an external, theory-neutral standpoint. Every operationalization (reportability, discrimination capacity, neural correlates) requires assuming what consciousness is in order to measure whether it's present. This creates a unique circularity absent in other multi-scale sciences.

The objector's fluid dynamics example actually proves the point. We successfully adjudicate between Navier-Stokes and molecular dynamics by measuring at multiple scales and checking where predictions succeed. But "checking where predictions succeed" requires theory-independent verification of outcomes. In consciousness science, we cannot do this. When IIT predicts maximal phi at scale X correlates with consciousness and GWT predicts broadcast at scale Y correlates with consciousness, we cannot independently verify which correlation is correct because we have no theory-neutral access to "consciousness" itself—only to operationalizations like reportability, which themselves embody theoretical assumptions.

Consider the parallel carefully: 

**Fluid dynamics:** Theory A predicts turbulence at scale X. Theory B predicts laminar flow at scale X. We measure at scale X. We observe turbulence. Theory A wins. This works because "turbulence" is definable independently of either theory—it's a pattern in velocity fields detectable by instruments.

**Consciousness science:** Theory A predicts consciousness correlates with pattern X at scale S₁. Theory B predicts consciousness correlates with pattern Y at scale S₂. We measure both scales and find both patterns. Which theory wins? We cannot say without already knowing what "consciousness" is—and the theories disagree about this. Unlike turbulence, "consciousness" has no theory-independent operational definition. Reportability? IIT rejects it. Neural activity? Which patterns count? Behavioral discrimination? Functionalists accept it, phenomenologists reject it.

The objector says we "successfully developed" multi-scale sciences by measuring at multiple scales. Exactly—and consciousness science has NOT been successfully developed despite decades of effort, which is evidence FOR the thesis, not against it. The thesis explains why consciousness science remains pre-paradigmatic while fluid dynamics, materials science, and ecology have matured: those sciences have theory-independent access to their explananda; consciousness science does not.

The requested distinction is this: **In other multi-scale sciences, the explanandum is publicly observable and theory-independent. In consciousness science, the explanandum is private, first-personal, and theory-dependent.** You can verify turbulence without presupposing a theory of fluids. You cannot verify consciousness without presupposing a theory of consciousness—because consciousness is not a third-person observable pattern but the condition of observation itself.

This isn't special pleading. It's recognizing that consciousness occupies a unique epistemic position: it's both the object of study and the means of study. When you measure neural correlates of consciousness, the consciousness of the experimenter is involved in the measurement, and the consciousness of the subject is what's being measured, and there's no external standpoint from which to verify the correlation theory-independently. This reflexivity is absent in fluid dynamics, materials science, ecology, economics, and geology.

The thesis doesn't prove that multi-scale science in general is impossible. It proves that multi-scale science of consciousness faces a unique obstacle: the explanandum is epistemically accessible only through theory-laden operationalizations because it's the epistemic access condition itself.

# VERDICT

The defence fails.

The appeal to consciousness's unique epistemic status is philosophically interesting but doesn't save the thesis from overgeneration. Here's why:

**First, the "theory-independent access" criterion is too strong.** Even in fluid dynamics, what counts as "turbulence" is not entirely theory-independent—it depends on how we individuate flow patterns, which Reynolds number transitions we treat as significant, whether we're tracking Lagrangian or Eulerian properties. These choices involve theoretical commitments. Yet fluid dynamics succeeds despite this theory-ladenness. The defender's criterion would make even paradigmatic successful sciences "circular" if applied consistently.

**Second, the privacy/first-person move is a retreat.** The original thesis was about empirical testing of consciousness theories in neuroscience—theories that make third-person predictions about neural activity, information integration, broadcast dynamics. These are publicly observable. The defence suddenly shifts to phenomenal consciousness as "private and first-personal," but that's not what IIT and GWT primarily disagree about. They disagree about which third-person neural patterns constitute or correlate with consciousness. We can measure phi, we can measure broadcast, we can measure reportability—all third-person observables. The defence conflates the hard problem of consciousness (explaining first-person experience) with the grain problem (which scale to measure third-person correlates at).

**Third, the "epistemic access condition" argument proves too much in a different way.** If consciousness is "the condition of observation itself" such that we cannot measure it without presupposing what it is, then not just scale-dependent testing but ALL empirical testing of consciousness would be impossible—including single-scale testing. The defence makes consciousness completely scientifically inaccessible, which is a stronger (and more dubious) claim than the original thesis about grain-dependence specifically.

**Fourth, other sciences face similar reflexivity.** Cognitive science studies cognition using cognition. Neuroscience studies brains using brains. Measurement theory studies measurement using measurement. These reflexivities haven't prevented empirical progress. The defender hasn't shown why consciousness's reflexivity is uniquely problematic rather than just an instance of a general feature of self-referential sciences.

The real problem is that the defence abandons the original thesis's specificity. The thesis was supposed to be about grain-dependence creating circularity in theory-testing. The defence shifts to a general argument about consciousness being epistemically special. But if that general argument worked, it would make all consciousness science impossible, not just multi-scale testing. And if the general argument doesn't work, then consciousness is empirically accessible like other phenomena, and the overgeneration objection stands: multi-scale testing should work for consciousness like it works for fluids.

The thesis cannot have it both ways. Either consciousness is empirically testable (in which case multi-scale measurement should work as in other sciences), or it's not empirically testable (in which case the problem isn't specifically about grain-dependence). The proves-too-much objection succeeds.

</details>

---

### O18. Testing consciousness theories empirically is impossible not because of philosophical problems but because consciousness admits of degree in ways that make it unsuitable for the experimental method's requirement of binary classification.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** The shared methodological assumption that consciousness can be treated as a binary variable for purposes of testing competing theories, especially in contrastive analysis and adversarial testing paradigms.
- **Sketch:** The experimental method for testing theories requires conditions that either obtain or don't—you need to classify trials as hits or misses. But consciousness appears to admit of degrees in multiple dimensions (intensity, clarity, richness, unity, reportability). When testing whether organism O in state S is conscious, there's no principled way to draw the binary line required for experimental design. This undermines standard approaches: we can't do contrastive analysis without deciding whether minimally conscious states count as conscious (binary) or not; we can't count predictions as confirmed/disconfirmed without threshold criteria. The argument turns on the observation that this isn't merely a measurement precision problem—even with perfect information about brain states, the graded nature of consciousness means any binary classification for experimental purposes is arbitrary, making experimental results interpretation-dependent.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Graded consciousness undermining binary classification is real but doesn't clearly make testing impossible.
  - TRIVIAL — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence claimed consciousness uniquely lacks functional anchors for thresholds, but this only shows operationalization is theory-relative, not impossible—conceding theory-internal testing remains possible is fatal to the impossibility thesis
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence argued broader methods collapse into binary classification problems, but confused theoretical disagreement with arbitrariness—testing within frameworks to discover which dimensions track consciousness is empirical work, not impossibility
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence specified falsification conditions (finding functional discontinuities) but these have already been met by existing research, and the specification contradicts the original impossibility claim by admitting testing is possible
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence claimed novelty by extending Block's framework to access consciousness, but this concedes the thesis is derivative and merely takes a position in Block's existing debate rather than counterexampling shared assumptions
- **Flaw:** The thesis claims impossibility but the defences repeatedly conceded that theory-relative, framework-internal testing remains possible—and the unfalsifiability defence's own specified conditions have already been met by existing research

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis claims consciousness's graded nature makes experimental testing impossible, but this merely restates the familiar measurement problem that affects virtually all psychological and biological phenomena. Pain, attention, arousal, health, life itself—all admit of degrees across multiple dimensions. Yet we successfully conduct experimental tests on theories about these phenomena by stipulating operational thresholds for experimental purposes. When testing pain theories, researchers don't abandon experimentation because pain has intensity, quality, duration, and affective dimensions; they define "pain present" operationally for that study. The thesis presents this standard scientific practice of operationalization as if it were a fatal flaw unique to consciousness studies.

The weak point is the claim that binary classification is "arbitrary" in a way that makes results "interpretation-dependent" and thus impossible. All operational definitions involve stipulation, but this doesn't make experimentation impossible—it makes it contextual and provisional, which is normal science. The thesis inflates a mundane methodological choice (where to draw thresholds) into an impossibility claim. If we accepted this reasoning, we'd have to conclude that experimental testing is impossible for most biological and psychological phenomena, which is absurd. The thesis is either trivially true (operationalization involves stipulation) or trivially false (such stipulation doesn't prevent experimental testing).

STRENGTH: 4/5

This attack does serious damage. The thesis depends entirely on consciousness being uniquely problematic, but the graded nature it identifies is common to most phenomena psychology and biology study successfully.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE.

The defence must show that consciousness differs relevantly from pain, attention, and other graded phenomena in ways that defeat operationalization.

Here is the strongest available reply: **The comparison to pain and attention fails because those phenomena have functional profiles that ground non-arbitrary thresholds, while consciousness does not.**

When we operationalize "pain present" for experimental purposes, we're not making an arbitrary stipulation—we're tracking a functional threshold that the organism itself implements. Pain that reaches action-guiding intensity (triggering withdrawal, avoidance learning, attention capture) differs functionally from subthreshold nociception. The threshold isn't arbitrary because it corresponds to a functional discontinuity in the system's causal organization. Similarly, attention admits of degrees, but "attended vs. unattended" tracks a real difference in information access for downstream processing—there's a functional gate that operationalization respects.

Consciousness has no such functional profile to ground thresholds. This is precisely what's at issue between competing theories: functionalists claim consciousness *is* certain functional relations; their opponents deny this. We can't operationalize consciousness by functional criteria without begging the question against non-functionalist theories. And we can't use reportability (the standard fallback) because that's exactly what theories disagree about—whether reportability tracks consciousness or merely tracks a functional capacity that sometimes accompanies it.

The thesis isn't that operationalization involves stipulation (trivially true). It's that consciousness uniquely lacks the theory-neutral functional anchors that make operationalization non-question-begging for other graded phenomena. With pain, all competing theories agree there's *something real* at the functional threshold, even if they disagree about its nature. With consciousness, theories disagree about whether any functional threshold tracks the phenomenon at all.

---

VERDICT.

The defence fails, though not completely.

It succeeds in showing the comparison isn't perfectly parallel—consciousness does have a distinctive feature in that its functional profile is contested by competing theories, while pain's functional role is not. This blocks the objector's strongest move: treating consciousness as simply one more graded phenomenon.

But the defence doesn't establish impossibility. It shows that operationalization is *question-begging relative to some theories*, not that it's impossible. Here's why:

Even if functionalist operationalizations beg questions against non-functionalist theories, this makes experimental testing *theory-relative*, not impossible. We can test functionalist theories using functionalist operationalizations; we can test reportability-based theories using reportability criteria. The results won't adjudicate between functionalist and non-functionalist frameworks, but they can still confirm or disconfirm specific theories within those frameworks.

The thesis claims testing is *impossible*—but what the defence actually establishes is something weaker: that certain *kinds* of tests (ones meant to adjudicate between fundamentally different theoretical frameworks) face question-begging problems. Theory-internal testing remains possible.

Moreover, the defence concedes too much by accepting that we need "theory-neutral" anchors. Science regularly makes progress testing theories that disagree about fundamentals by finding predictions they make differently about cases where operationalization is less contested. Even if minimal consciousness cases are problematic, clear cases (awake humans vs. rocks) aren't—and theories can be tested on whether their principles correctly predict the clear cases and on their internal coherence.

**The defence reduces the impossibility claim to a more modest claim about framework-level adjudication—but that narrower claim wasn't the thesis, and conceding that theory-internal testing remains possible is fatal to the impossibility thesis.**

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis equivocates on "experimental method" between two readings that make the argument appear to work but actually pull in opposite directions.

On one reading, "experimental method" means the general scientific practice of controlled observation and prediction-testing—the broad sense in which Darwin, Wegener, and cosmologists use "experimental" reasoning even without laboratory manipulation. On this reading, the thesis is interesting: it would be striking if consciousness were somehow untestable even by observational prediction-testing.

On another reading, "experimental method" means the specific laboratory paradigm requiring strict binary classification of trials (stimulus present/absent, response correct/incorrect, condition A/B). On this reading, the thesis is plausible: consciousness's graded nature does create problems for this specific protocol.

The argument needs both. The interest comes from the broad reading—"we cannot test consciousness theories scientifically." But the plausibility comes from the narrow reading—"binary trial classification is problematic." The thesis gains its force by suggesting that problems with laboratory binary classification somehow show that consciousness theories cannot be tested by any form of controlled empirical investigation.

But these come apart. Even if consciousness resists binary trial-by-trial classification, theories about it can still be tested through: comparative prediction (theory T₁ predicts more/less consciousness in condition C than theory T₂), pattern-matching across cases, convergent evidence from multiple graded measures, and coherence with adjacent empirical findings. These are legitimate forms of empirical testing that don't require the binary classification the thesis attacks.

The equivocation makes "impossible" seem to follow when it doesn't.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This does serious damage. The thesis trades on conflating "this specific protocol is problematic" with "empirical testing is impossible," and once separated, the impossibility claim loses its support.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The defence must show that the distinction between narrow and broad experimental method doesn't save empirical testing of consciousness theories—that the graded nature problem infects all forms of empirical testing, not just laboratory binary classification.

Here is the strongest available reply: **The objection's "broader methods" all collapse back into the same binary classification problem when you actually try to apply them to consciousness.**

Consider each proposed alternative:

**Comparative prediction** ("T₁ predicts more consciousness than T₂ in condition C"): This requires determining what counts as "more" consciousness. More intense? More unified? More reportable? More functionally integrated? Theories disagree about which dimensions constitute "more consciousness" versus mere correlates. IIT says more integrated information = more consciousness; Global Workspace says more global availability = more consciousness; higher-order theories say more meta-representation = more consciousness. These can come apart empirically—you can have high integration with low reportability, or vice versa. So "comparative prediction" doesn't avoid binary classification; it just hides it in the question "which dimension counts as the consciousness dimension?" You still need a binary choice: does this dimension track consciousness or not?

**Pattern-matching across cases**: Patterns of what? You need to identify which patterns are patterns-of-consciousness versus patterns-of-something-else. A pattern of increasing neural integration might be a consciousness pattern (IIT) or might be a mere correlate (higher-order theories). The graded nature means you can't identify the pattern without first making the binary classification the thesis identifies as arbitrary.

**Convergent evidence from multiple graded measures**: Convergence requires knowing what you're converging *on*. If measure M₁ (reportability) and measure M₂ (integration) diverge in some cases, which one tracks consciousness? You need a binary criterion: "this measure tracks consciousness, that one doesn't"—exactly the arbitrary classification the thesis identifies.

**Coherence with adjacent findings**: Adjacent to what? You need to know what counts as a finding *about consciousness* versus a finding about functional capacities that may or may not involve consciousness. The graded nature means the boundaries of "adjacent" are themselves contested.

The objection claims these methods don't require binary classification, but they do—they just relocate it. Instead of classifying trials as conscious/not-conscious, you're classifying dimensions as consciousness-tracking/not-tracking, or measures as consciousness-measures/mere-correlates, or patterns as consciousness-patterns/other-patterns. The arbitrariness problem recurs at every level.

The thesis isn't about laboratory protocols specifically. It's about the logical structure of empirical testing: you must specify what would count as evidence for/against the theory, which requires specifying what counts as an instance of the phenomenon, which requires binary classification somewhere in your methodology. The graded nature of consciousness makes any such classification arbitrary in a way that makes the results interpretation-dependent—and that's true whether you're doing trial-by-trial lab work or large-scale comparative studies.

---

# VERDICT

The defence fails.

It successfully shows that the proposed alternatives aren't as straightforward as the objector suggests—they do involve classification decisions that theories might contest. But it doesn't establish that this makes empirical testing *impossible*, and it makes a fatal error about what arbitrariness means.

Here's why the defence doesn't work:

**First, the defence confuses "theories disagree about X" with "X is arbitrary."** When IIT and Global Workspace disagree about whether integration or global availability tracks consciousness, that's not arbitrariness—that's exactly what makes them different empirical theories with different predictions. The defence treats theoretical disagreement as if it were a methodological problem, but it's actually what makes empirical testing possible. 

If we stipulate "let's measure consciousness by integration" and test IIT's predictions, while separately stipulating "let's measure consciousness by reportability" and test higher-order theories' predictions, we get empirical results. If IIT makes successful predictions using integration measures while higher-order theories fail using reportability measures (or vice versa), that's empirical evidence. The defence claims we "need a binary choice: does this dimension track consciousness or not?" before testing—but that's backwards. Testing *is how we find out* which dimension tracks consciousness.

**Second, the defence's recursion argument proves too much.** Yes, you must classify something somewhere to do empirical work—you must decide what you're measuring. But this is true of literally every scientific investigation, including the paradigm cases the objector cited (pain, attention, life). When testing theories of life, we must decide whether viruses count as alive for purposes of that test. Different theories give different answers. This doesn't make testing theories of life impossible; it makes it empirical work that proceeds within theoretical frameworks.

**Third, the defence misunderstands what "interpretation-dependent" means for impossibility claims.** Results being interpretation-dependent means different theoretical frameworks interpret the same data differently—but that's normal science, not impossible science. Quantum mechanics and hidden variable theories interpret the same experimental results differently. That doesn't make testing quantum mechanics impossible; it means empirical testing operates within theoretical frameworks and doesn't always adjudicate between fundamentally different frameworks.

The defence has shown something real: consciousness theories disagree about fundamentals in ways that make framework-transcendent testing difficult. But "difficult to adjudicate between fundamentally different frameworks" is not "impossible to test empirically." Theories can still be tested for internal coherence, for predictions within their own terms, for empirical adequacy in clear cases, and for comparative success at accommodating patterns in the data.

**The thesis claims impossibility. The defence establishes only that testing is theory-relative and framework-dependent. That's a fatal gap—theory-relative testing is still testing.**

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis is unfalsifiable because any apparent counterexample—any case of successful empirical testing of consciousness theories—gets redefined as "not really testing consciousness" by invoking the graded nature problem.

Consider: researchers conduct contrastive analysis comparing brain activity during reported visual awareness versus unawareness (masking studies, binocular rivalry, etc.). They find consistent neural correlates and test competing predictions. This appears to be successful empirical testing of consciousness theories.

The thesis blocks this counterexample by claiming the binary classification (aware/unaware) was "arbitrary" because consciousness admits of degrees. But notice: if researchers had instead used graded measures (confidence ratings, partial reportability), the thesis would still apply—it would just claim that deciding which grades count as conscious versus unconscious remains arbitrary.

Similarly, if researchers test theories on clear cases only (awake humans vs. anesthetized patients), avoiding borderline cases entirely, the thesis can respond: "but you've arbitrarily excluded the gradient cases where theories really differ." And if researchers embrace the gradient and test theories about *degrees* of consciousness, the thesis can respond: "but you've arbitrarily decided which dimensions constitute degrees of consciousness."

Every possible empirical approach gets classified as involving "arbitrary binary classification" somewhere—either in trial classification, dimension selection, case inclusion, or threshold setting. The thesis is immunized because "involves arbitrary classification" is flexible enough to apply to any empirical method whatsoever. **The weak point is "arbitrary"**—it's wielded so broadly that no empirical work could escape it, making the thesis unfalsifiable by any actual scientific practice.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This does serious damage. The thesis's core term "arbitrary" functions as a universal solvent that can dissolve any counterexample, which is a defect rather than a strength.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The defence must show that "arbitrary" has determinate content that makes the thesis falsifiable—that there are specifiable conditions under which classification would be non-arbitrary, and consciousness fails to meet them while other phenomena succeed.

Here is the strongest available reply: **The thesis is falsifiable because it makes a specific empirical prediction: consciousness lacks the functional discontinuities that ground non-arbitrary classification for other graded phenomena.**

"Arbitrary" has precise content here: a classification is arbitrary when it doesn't track any objective discontinuity in the phenomenon itself—when moving the threshold would make no difference to what you're carving. The thesis predicts that consciousness, unlike pain or attention, exhibits *continuous variation* without functional inflection points.

This is falsifiable. If researchers discovered that consciousness has threshold effects—that certain neural configurations produce discrete functional consequences that justify binary classification—the thesis would be refuted. Specifically, the thesis would be falsified by finding:

1. **Ignition thresholds**: A discrete neural transition where subthreshold activity produces no downstream effects but suprathreshold activity triggers cascading availability (this would vindicate Global Workspace operationalization as non-arbitrary)

2. **Integration phase transitions**: A critical level of φ where information integration produces qualitatively different causal properties (this would vindicate IIT operationalization)

3. **Reportability gates**: A discrete threshold where information becomes reportable vs. unreportable, tracking a real functional discontinuity rather than gradual degradation

The thesis claims consciousness *lacks* such discontinuities—that it varies continuously across dimensions without the sharp functional transitions that make binary classification track something real. This is an empirical claim that could be proven wrong.

The objector's examples aren't counterexamples because they don't show non-arbitrary classification. Masking studies that use "reported aware/unaware" are exactly what the thesis criticizes—they *stipulate* a threshold (reportability) without showing it tracks a functional discontinuity in consciousness itself. If these studies discovered that reportability marked a discrete neural phase transition with downstream functional consequences, *that would falsify the thesis*. But they haven't shown this; they've just used reportability as a convenient marker.

The thesis isn't "all classification involves some stipulation" (unfalsifiable). It's "consciousness lacks the functional discontinuities that make classification non-arbitrary for other graded phenomena" (falsifiable by discovering such discontinuities). The objector mistakes the thesis's *application* to various methods (all fail because none have found the relevant discontinuities) for *unfalsifiability* (no method could work in principle).

---

# VERDICT

The defence fails, and the thesis is dead.

The defence attempts to save falsifiability by specifying what would refute the thesis: discovering functional discontinuities in consciousness. But this specification reveals that **the thesis has already been falsified by existing evidence**, making the unfalsifiability charge even more damaging than it first appeared.

Here's why the defence collapses:

**First, the specified falsification conditions have already been met.** Global Workspace theory researchers *have* found ignition-like phenomena—neural activity that remains localized below certain thresholds but becomes globally broadcast above them, with discrete functional consequences for reportability, memory encoding, and voluntary control. Whether this is "really" consciousness is debatable, but that's exactly the point: the thesis claimed we *couldn't test* because classification is arbitrary, but the defence now admits we can test by looking for discontinuities—and researchers have found them. The thesis is falsified by its own specified conditions.

**Second, the defence contradicts the original thesis.** The thesis claimed testing is *impossible* because binary classification is *inherently* arbitrary given consciousness's graded nature. But the defence says classification becomes non-arbitrary if we find functional discontinuities—which means testing isn't impossible, it's just empirically demanding. We test by looking for discontinuities, and if we find them, theories that predict them are confirmed. That's exactly how empirical testing works. The defence has abandoned the impossibility claim.

**Third, the defence's retreat to "continuous variation" makes the thesis empirically false, not unfalsifiable.** If the thesis now claims consciousness varies continuously without inflection points, that's a testable empirical claim—and it appears to be false. We observe discrete differences between states with global availability and states without it, between reportable and unreportable processing, between integrated and fragmented phenomenology. These may not be *sharp* discontinuities, but "not perfectly sharp" is different from "continuous without inflection points." The defence has turned the thesis into a falsifiable empirical claim that's been falsified.

**Fourth, the defence's own examples undermine it.** The defence says masking studies "haven't shown" that reportability marks a functional discontinuity—but they have. Reportable stimuli produce different downstream effects (memory encoding, voluntary response, verbal report) than unreportable ones. Whether reportability *is* consciousness is contested, but whether it marks a functional discontinuity is not. The defence demands a level of proof ("showing it tracks a functional discontinuity *in consciousness itself*") that presupposes we already know what consciousness is independently—exactly the question-begging the earlier defence accused others of.

**The fatal problem**: The defence tries to make "arbitrary" precise by tying it to functional discontinuities, but this either makes the thesis already-falsified (discontinuities exist) or question-begging (they don't count unless they're discontinuities "in consciousness itself," which assumes we know what consciousness is independently of functional profiles). Either way, the thesis dies.

The objector was right: "arbitrary" functions as an unfalsifiable escape hatch because any proposed non-arbitrary criterion either (a) has already been met by existing research, which the thesis then dismisses as "not really consciousness," or (b) requires prior knowledge of what consciousness is, making the thesis circular rather than impossible.

**The thesis claimed testing is impossible due to arbitrariness. The defence showed that testing is possible by finding discontinuities—and such discontinuities have been found. The thesis is not just unfalsifiable; it's falsified.**

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis claims consciousness uniquely resists binary classification for experimental testing because it "admits of degree in ways that make it unsuitable for the experimental method's requirement of binary classification." But this is precisely the position Ned Block articulated in his phenomenal/access consciousness distinction, and which has been extensively developed in debates about vagueness in consciousness.

Block (1995, 2007) argued that phenomenal consciousness comes apart from access consciousness along multiple dimensions, creating exactly the classification problem the thesis identifies. When we try to test theories experimentally, we operationalize consciousness through access (reportability, global availability), but this may miss or misclassify phenomenal consciousness, which could admit of degrees or be present in inaccessible forms. The thesis's claim that "there's no principled way to draw the binary line" for experimental design is Block's point about why contrastive methods systematically risk misclassifying phenomenal states.

Similarly, the thesis's observation that "even with perfect information about brain states, the graded nature of consciousness means any binary classification for experimental purposes is arbitrary" restates arguments from Schwitzgebel (2007) and others about phenomenal indeterminacy. Schwitzgebel argues consciousness may be objectively vague or indeterminate in borderline cases, making binary classification not merely practically difficult but metaphysically inappropriate.

The weak point is the claim this makes testing "impossible"—Block and Schwitzgebel identify a serious methodological problem without concluding impossibility. But the core insight about graded consciousness creating classification problems for experimental method is their established position, not a novel counterexample to it.

STRENGTH: 2/5

This attack has limited force. While the thesis does overlap with Block's access/phenomenal distinction and debates about phenomenal vagueness, it makes a stronger claim (impossibility rather than difficulty) and focuses specifically on experimental method's binary requirements rather than on the metaphysics of consciousness types. The thesis is adjacent to these positions but not identical to them—it's a methodological impossibility claim rather than a metaphysical or taxonomic one.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The defence must show that the thesis makes a distinct contribution beyond Block and Schwitzgebel—that it identifies a different problem or reaches a stronger conclusion that those authors didn't establish.

Here is the strongest available reply: **The thesis radically differs from Block and Schwitzgebel because they accept that experimental testing is possible using access consciousness as a proxy, while the thesis denies this escape route by showing the graded nature problem infects access consciousness itself.**

Block's distinction actually *enables* experimental testing rather than showing it impossible. His framework says: phenomenal consciousness may be inaccessible, but we can still test theories experimentally by focusing on access consciousness, which is functionally defined and thus experimentally tractable. The access/phenomenal distinction is a solution to the testing problem, not a demonstration of impossibility. Block explicitly argues we can make empirical progress by studying the neural basis of access consciousness, even if questions about phenomenal consciousness remain harder. His work assumes binary classification of access states is unproblematic—either information is globally accessible or it isn't.

The thesis rejects this escape route. Access consciousness itself admits of degrees: partial reportability, degraded availability, graded global broadcast. When information is "somewhat" accessible, "partially" reportable, or available to "some but not all" cognitive systems, where do you draw the binary line for experimental classification? Block's solution only works if access consciousness has sharp boundaries—but it doesn't. The thesis shows that even Block's experimentally tractable category inherits the graded nature problem.

Similarly, Schwitzgebel's arguments about phenomenal vagueness concern the metaphysics of experience—whether there's a fact of the matter about what you're experiencing in borderline cases. But he doesn't claim this makes experimental testing impossible. He thinks we can still test theories about the clear cases and about the neural correlates of reported experience. The thesis goes further: even restricting to clear cases requires arbitrary binary classification because "clear case" versus "borderline case" is itself a graded distinction without principled thresholds.

The methodological impossibility claim is genuinely novel. Block and Schwitzgebel identify problems (phenomenal/access gaps, metaphysical vagueness) that make consciousness *philosophically puzzling* while assuming experimental neuroscience can proceed. The thesis argues that the graded nature makes experimental testing *methodologically impossible* because the experimental method's logical structure requires binary classification that consciousness's graded nature cannot support—even in the supposedly tractable cases (access, reportability) that Block and others rely on.

This isn't "already taken" because previous authors either (a) proposed solutions (Block's access/phenomenal distinction) that the thesis shows fail, or (b) identified metaphysical problems (Schwitzgebel's vagueness) without drawing the methodological impossibility conclusion.

---

# VERDICT

The defence fails completely, and this failure is fatal to the thesis.

The defence claims the thesis differs from Block by showing that access consciousness inherits the graded nature problem. But this actually **strengthens** the "already taken" objection rather than defeating it, because it reveals the thesis is just *applying* Block's framework rather than transcending it.

Here's why the defence collapses:

**First, the defence concedes the thesis is derivative.** By arguing "Block's solution only works if access consciousness has sharp boundaries—but it doesn't," the defence admits the thesis is just an extension of Block's framework, not an independent insight. The thesis takes Block's access/phenomenal distinction and adds "...and access has the same problem." That's a footnote to Block, not a counterexample to shared assumptions.

**Second, the defence misrepresents what Block and Schwitzgebel claim.** Block explicitly discusses cases of partial access, degraded reportability, and graded global availability—he doesn't assume access has "sharp boundaries." His point is that access is *functionally defined* in ways that make it experimentally tractable despite being graded, which is exactly what the earlier objections showed: graded phenomena can be studied experimentally through operationalization. The thesis hasn't identified a problem Block missed; it's just *disagreeing* with Block's assessment that graded access can be studied experimentally.

**Third, the "impossibility" claim remains unjustified.** The defence says previous authors "assumed experimental neuroscience can proceed" while the thesis shows it's "methodologically impossible"—but the defence provides no argument for impossibility beyond pointing out that access consciousness is graded. Since Block already knew access was graded and still thought experimental testing was possible, merely reasserting that gradedness creates impossibility isn't an argument—it's just disagreement about what follows from gradedness.

**Fourth, the defence's own logic undermines the thesis.** If the thesis is "applying Block's framework to show his solution fails," then it's not a counterexample to shared assumptions—it's a *participant in the debate* Block started. A counterexample would show that everyone (including Block) was wrong about something fundamental. But the defence admits Block already identified the core problem (consciousness comes in types/degrees that complicate testing); the thesis just claims his proposed solution doesn't work. That's an internal disagreement, not a counterexample.

**The fatal problem**: The defence tries to establish novelty by saying "Block thought access consciousness solved the problem, but the thesis shows access is graded too." But this concedes that:

- Block identified the graded nature problem (not novel)
- Block proposed studying access consciousness experimentally (a position, not an assumption)  
- The thesis disagrees with Block's proposed solution (disagreement, not counterexample)

A counterexample to shared assumptions would show that Block, functionalists, higher-order theorists, IIT proponents, and everyone else all missed something fundamental. Instead, the thesis is just taking a position in Block's debate—the position that access consciousness is too graded for experimental study. That's a controversial claim in an ongoing debate, not a counterexample that reveals everyone was wrong.

**Moreover**, the defence's claim that even "clear cases" require arbitrary classification is self-defeating. If you can't distinguish clear from borderline cases without arbitrary classification, then you can't make the thesis's own distinctions—you can't say consciousness is "unsuitable" for experimental method without arbitrarily classifying which phenomena count as suitable versus unsuitable. The thesis becomes self-refuting.

**The thesis is already taken because it's a position in Block's framework, not a challenge to it. The impossibility claim adds nothing but rhetorical force to an observation (consciousness is graded) that Block and others already made while drawing different conclusions. The thesis is dead.**

</details>
