### O19. We can definitively test consciousness theories in cases of disagreement with subjects' reports, making first-person report the bottleneck for theory testing rather than a neutral data point.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The methodological consensus that treats all data streams (neural, behavioral, phenomenological) as equally foundational for theory testing
- **Sketch:** The standard picture treats first-person reports as one data stream alongside neural/behavioral data, with theories tested by how well they predict all streams. But actually, the crucial test cases are where theories DISAGREE with reports—where your theory says someone is conscious but they report they're not, or vice versa. Different theories take different stances on whether to override reports: IIT is willing to say patients who report no experience are actually conscious, while higher-order theories would defer to reports. The key premise is that these disagreement cases—not cases where theory and report align—are where theories make their boldest, most testable claims. This redraws the dialectic: instead of asking 'how do we test theories?', we should ask 'under what conditions would we override a subject's report?', and theories should be evaluated by whether their answers to this question are principled. The testability problem dissolves into an ethical/methodological problem about authority.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Competent point about disagreement cases being crucial, but the observation that these are where theories diverge is somewhat obvious.
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence clarified 'principled' but substituted coherence-checking for empirical testing, which the verdict correctly identified as absorption
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 3/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence's distinction between using disagreement as evidence versus testing ground was too thin; thesis is Block's methodology relabeled
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 4/5, answered. Defence successfully blocked the parallel by showing consciousness lacks detector-independent access, though this exposed a deeper problem
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence shifted between testing 'in' disagreement cases versus testing 'later' when reports arrive, which is absorption not meeting
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Disagreement cases between consciousness theories and first-person reports reveal theories' core metaphysical commitments in ways that enable coherence-testing (whether theories handle such cases consistently with their stated identity claims), though not definitive empirical testing at the time of disagreement
- **Flaw:** The thesis promised definitive empirical testing in disagreement cases but can only deliver coherence-checking (logical analysis) or retrospective testing once reports resolve the disagreement, which returns to conventional methodology

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — **ATTEMPT**

The thesis claims we can "definitively test" consciousness theories in disagreement cases, but it systematically protects itself from falsification by redefining what counts as a "principled" stance on overriding reports. 

The weak point is the premise that **theories can be evaluated by whether their answers about overriding reports are "principled."** This appears to be an empirical testing criterion, but it's actually a moving target that immunizes the thesis from counterexamples.

Suppose we find a theory that sometimes overrides reports and sometimes doesn't, with no apparent pattern. The thesis can respond: "That's unprincipled, so it fails our test." But suppose we find a theory with a perfectly consistent rule: "Always override reports when neural activity X is present." The thesis can equally respond: "That rule isn't genuinely principled—it's arbitrary/ethically problematic/metaphysically confused." 

The term "principled" does all the protective work here. Any apparent counterexample—a theory that seems to give clear, testable answers about when to override reports but doesn't actually advance our ability to test consciousness theories—can be dismissed as "not really principled." The thesis never specifies what makes a stance principled versus unprincipled in a way that could be checked independently of whether we antecedently think it solves the testing problem.

**STRENGTH: 4/5**

This is a serious problem requiring a real answer. The thesis needs to specify what "principled" means independently of success.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**

The objection misreads what "principled" means in this context. It's not a moving target—it's a technical requirement with clear, checkable criteria drawn from the structure of consciousness theories themselves.

A principled stance on overriding reports means one that follows from the theory's core commitments about what consciousness IS. Consider:

- **IIT says**: Consciousness = integrated information above threshold Φ. Therefore, if a system has Φ > threshold, it's conscious regardless of reports. That's principled because it follows deductively from the theory's identity claim.

- **Higher-order theories say**: Consciousness = higher-order representation of first-order states. Therefore, sincere reports (themselves higher-order representations) are constitutive evidence, not merely correlational. Deferring to reports follows from the theory's architecture.

- **Your hypothetical "always override when neural activity X is present"** is unprincipled not because we arbitrarily deem it so, but because it's ad hoc—it doesn't derive from any account of what consciousness is. It's just a correlation masquerading as explanation.

The falsification criterion is perfectly clear: a theory fails if its stance on disagreement cases doesn't follow from its metaphysical commitments about consciousness. This is independently checkable—you examine the theory's account of what consciousness consists in, then check whether its stance on reports is entailed by that account.

The thesis doesn't claim we can test whether theories are TRUE in disagreement cases—it claims we can test whether they're COHERENT. That's a logical relation, not a moving target. A theory that says "consciousness is X" but then handles disagreement cases in ways inconsistent with X-being-consciousness is internally inconsistent. That's definitive failure.

**VERDICT**

The defence fails, but not quite where the objection strikes.

The problem isn't that "principled" is undefined—the defence shows it means "following from the theory's core metaphysical commitments." That's checkable. The problem is that **coherence testing isn't empirical testing**. 

Even if we can definitively determine that a theory handles disagreement cases consistently with its own commitments, we haven't tested whether those commitments are correct. IIT might be perfectly coherent in overriding reports given its Φ-based definition, but that doesn't test whether Φ actually tracks consciousness—it only tests whether IIT is internally consistent.

The thesis promised we could "definitively test consciousness theories" in disagreement cases. But what we can actually do is test their internal coherence. That's valuable but it's not empirical theory testing—it's logical analysis. The thesis collapses the distinction between these two kinds of evaluation.

The objection is right that something protects the thesis from falsification, but it's not the term "principled"—it's the substitution of coherence-checking for empirical testing.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — **ATTEMPT**

The position that disagreement cases between theory and first-person reports constitute the crucial test sites for consciousness theories is already held by **Ned Block** in his work on access consciousness versus phenomenal consciousness, particularly in his arguments about inattentional blindness and overflow cases.

Block explicitly argues that the most theoretically significant cases are precisely those where theories diverge from what subjects report. In his classic 1995 paper and subsequent work, he uses cases where subjects report not seeing stimuli (inattentional blindness) but where theories might predict phenomenal consciousness is present, to argue for the access/phenomenal distinction. His entire framework depends on the premise that **disagreement between theory-predicted phenomenal consciousness and subject reports of access consciousness reveals fundamental theoretical structure**.

Similarly, **Eric Schwitzgebel** in "Perplexities of Consciousness" (2011) builds his skepticism about introspection precisely on cases where our best theories suggest conscious states that subjects don't report, or vice versa. His argument that introspection is unreliable centrally features disagreement cases as the diagnostic tool.

The current thesis's claim that we should evaluate theories by "whether their answers to this question are principled" is essentially Block's methodology of using disagreement cases to adjudicate between theoretical frameworks. The move from "how do we test theories?" to "when would we override reports?" is Block's access/phenomenal distinction reframed as a methodological principle.

**STRENGTH: 3/5**

This is a genuine problem requiring an answer. The thesis needs to show how its approach differs substantively from Block's established framework beyond mere reformulation.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**

The objection conflates using disagreement cases as evidence with making them the fundamental testing ground. Block and Schwitzgebel use disagreement cases *within* the standard multi-stream framework—they're arguing about which data stream to privilege when streams conflict. The thesis makes a deeper methodological claim: that disagreement cases aren't just useful examples but are where theories become *maximally falsifiable*.

Consider the structural difference:

**Block's approach**: Access/phenomenal distinction is a *theoretical posit* that explains why reports and neural data sometimes diverge. Disagreement cases support this particular theory. Other theories (like higher-order theories) use the same cases to support different conclusions. The cases are evidence *for* competing theories.

**This thesis's approach**: Disagreement cases are where theories *expose their commitments* in ways that make them testable regardless of which theory is correct. When IIT says "override the report" and higher-order theory says "defer to the report," they're not just making predictions—they're revealing what they think consciousness *is* in a way that forces a choice.

The key difference: Block uses disagreement to argue for his specific theory. The thesis uses disagreement to argue for a *methodological reorientation* of how we test *any* theory. It's not "Block's methodology reframed"—it's a claim that disagreement cases have a special epistemic status that Block himself doesn't recognize. For Block, inattentional blindness is evidence for access/phenomenal distinction. For this thesis, inattentional blindness is a *testing ground* where theories must take stances that reveal their core commitments.

Moreover, Block never claims these cases make first-person reports "the bottleneck" for testing. He treats reports as one fallible data stream. The thesis claims something stronger: that reports have a unique methodological role because disagreement with them forces theories to show their hand about what consciousness is. That's not in Block.

**VERDICT**

The defence fails. The distinction it draws is too thin to escape the objection.

The defence claims Block uses disagreement cases *as evidence for his theory* while the thesis uses them *as testing grounds for any theory*. But this is a difference without a substantive distinction. When Block argues that inattentional blindness cases reveal the access/phenomenal distinction, he's simultaneously:
1. Using them as evidence for his theory
2. Claiming they're diagnostic for testing theories generally
3. Arguing they force theories to reveal their commitments

The thesis's "methodological reorientation" is just making explicit what Block already does implicitly. Saying disagreement cases "expose commitments" versus "provide evidence" is a verbal distinction—exposing commitments *is* providing evidence about what theories really claim.

More fatally, the defence's claim that disagreement cases make theories "maximally falsifiable" is precisely Block's point about why these cases matter theoretically. Block chose inattentional blindness and overflow *because* they maximally distinguish his framework from competitors. The thesis hasn't identified a new methodological principle—it's just named what Block was already doing.

The "bottleneck" language doesn't save it either. When Block says disagreement cases are theoretically crucial, he's already making first-person reports methodologically special—not as infallible data, but as the reference point against which theories must justify their divergence. That's the bottleneck function.

The thesis is Block's methodology abstracted and relabeled, not a novel position.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — **ATTEMPT**

The thesis argues that disagreement cases between theories and first-person reports are where consciousness theories make their "boldest, most testable claims," and that we should evaluate theories by whether they have "principled" stances on when to override reports. The weak point is the premise that **disagreement cases provide superior testability compared to agreement cases**.

If this argument worked, it would prove far too much: it would establish that disagreement between theory and data is generally epistemically superior to agreement, which is absurd.

Consider parallel cases in other sciences:

**Physics**: Suppose a theory predicts a particle's mass is 125 GeV but the detector reports 130 GeV. By the thesis's logic, this disagreement case is where the theory makes its "boldest, most testable claim" and we should evaluate the theory by whether it has a "principled" reason to override the detector reading. But that's backwards—the disagreement is evidence the theory is *wrong*, not evidence it's maximally testable. Theories are tested by whether they *match* data, not by how boldly they contradict it.

**Medicine**: A theory predicts a patient should feel pain, but the patient reports no pain. The thesis would say this disagreement is the crucial test case where the theory reveals its commitments. But actually, this is just straightforward disconfirmation. The theory's "principled stance" on overriding the patient's report is irrelevant—the theory failed the test.

The thesis conflates two different things: (1) cases where theories make risky, falsifiable predictions, and (2) cases where theories contradict available evidence. Disagreement cases are the latter, not the former. A theory that frequently disagrees with data isn't "maximally testable"—it's maximally *disconfirmed*. The argument, if sound, would make scientific failure a virtue.

**STRENGTH: 4/5**

This is serious damage requiring a real answer. The thesis needs to explain why consciousness is special in making theory-data disagreement epistemically valuable rather than simply being evidence of theory failure.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**

The objection misses the unique epistemic situation with consciousness: we have no detector-independent access to the phenomenon. This makes consciousness categorically different from the physics and medicine cases, where the "proves too much" charge gains no purchase.

In physics, when theory predicts 125 GeV and the detector reads 130 GeV, we have independent grounds for trusting the detector—we can calibrate it against known standards, check it with other detectors, verify its mechanism. The disagreement is straightforward disconfirmation because **the detector's authority is established independently of any particular theory about particles**.

But with consciousness, the subject's report IS the detector, and we have no independent calibration method. We cannot check first-person reports against a consciousness-meter we trust for independent reasons. This creates a fundamentally different epistemic structure.

When IIT predicts consciousness but the subject reports none, this isn't like the particle case because **we have no theory-independent way to determine whether the subject or the theory is the better guide**. The disagreement doesn't straightforwardly disconfirm IIT—it raises a prior question: what makes something the right guide to consciousness in the first place?

The thesis's insight is that this prior question—"when would we override reports?"—is where theories reveal their deepest commitments about what consciousness *is*. IIT's willingness to override reports isn't a bug or a bold prediction that might fail; it's a direct consequence of IIT's identity claim that consciousness = integrated information. Higher-order theory's deference to reports similarly follows from its claim that consciousness = higher-order representation.

The medicine case fails for the same reason. When a patient reports no pain despite tissue damage, we don't face a genuine disagreement about whether pain is present—we have independent behavioral, neural, and physiological indicators of pain beyond the report. We can determine the report is anomalous using other evidence streams. But with consciousness *itself* (not pain specifically, but the presence of experience), we lack these independent indicators. That's precisely what makes consciousness theories untestable by standard methods.

The thesis doesn't claim disagreement is epistemically superior in general—it claims that in the unique case where we lack detector-independent access to the phenomenon, disagreement cases force theories to specify what they think the phenomenon *is* in a way that agreement cases don't. When theory and report align, multiple incompatible theories can accommodate the data. When they disagree, theories must commit to what consciousness consists in.

This isn't "making failure a virtue"—it's recognizing that without independent access, the concept of "failure" itself is theory-laden in a way it isn't in physics or medicine.

**VERDICT**

The defence succeeds in blocking the "proves too much" objection, but this success reveals a deeper problem that may be fatal.

The defence is right that consciousness differs from physics and medicine because we lack detector-independent access to the phenomenon. This does block the parallel: disagreement in physics is straightforward disconfirmation because we can calibrate detectors independently, while disagreement in consciousness raises a prior question about what counts as evidence.

But this successful defence exposes that the thesis has misidentified what it's actually claiming. If we truly lack independent access to consciousness, then disagreement cases don't make theories "maximally testable"—they make them *untestable by empirical standards*. 

The defence admits that when IIT and reports disagree, "we have no theory-independent way to determine whether the subject or the theory is the better guide." But that means we cannot definitively test which is right—we can only test whether theories are internally consistent with their own commitments about what consciousness is. That's the coherence-checking problem the earlier exchange identified.

The thesis claimed we can "definitively test consciousness theories in cases of disagreement." The defence shows we cannot—we can only test whether theories handle disagreement consistently with their metaphysical commitments. That's not empirical testing; it's logical analysis of coherence.

Moreover, the defence's success undermines the thesis's claim that disagreement cases are special. If we lack independent access to consciousness, then *all* cases—agreement and disagreement alike—face the same problem. Agreement cases don't provide theory-independent confirmation any more than disagreement cases provide theory-independent disconfirmation. The defence's own reasoning suggests first-person reports aren't "the bottleneck" in some special way—rather, the entire enterprise of empirical theory testing is blocked by lack of independent access.

The defence saves the thesis from "proves too much" only by revealing it proves nothing at all about empirical testability.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — **ATTEMPT**

The thesis claims that disagreement cases between theories and first-person reports are where we can "definitively test consciousness theories" because they force theories to reveal their core commitments. The weak point is the premise that **disagreement cases provide definitive tests that distinguish good theories from bad ones**.

Counterexample: **Locked-in syndrome patients who are fully conscious but cannot report anything**.

Consider a patient with complete locked-in syndrome—fully conscious but unable to produce any behavioral or verbal reports. Two theories make opposite predictions:

- **Theory A** (report-deferring): "No report = no consciousness." Predicts patient is unconscious.
- **Theory B** (report-overriding): "High cortical integration = consciousness regardless of reports." Predicts patient is conscious.

This is a maximal disagreement case—one theory says conscious, the other unconscious, and there's literally no first-person report to agree or disagree with. By the thesis's logic, this should be the ultimate "definitive test" where theories "reveal their commitments most clearly."

But we cannot definitively test which theory is right. We now know from rare cases where locked-in patients recovered partial communication that they were conscious all along. This vindicates Theory B retrospectively—but only because we eventually got reports. Without eventual reports, we'd have no way to test which theory was correct.

This shows that disagreement cases don't enable definitive testing—they just reveal theoretical commitments we still cannot adjudicate. The "test" the thesis describes is really just: "which theory's commitments do we find more plausible?" That's not empirical testing; it's philosophical preference. The counterexample demonstrates that even in the clearest disagreement cases, we cannot definitively test theories without ultimately returning to first-person reports as the ground truth.

**STRENGTH: 4/5**

This does serious damage. The thesis needs to explain how disagreement cases enable definitive testing when even the clearest disagreement cases (complete absence of reports) cannot be adjudicated without eventual access to reports.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**

The objection gets the dialectical structure backwards. Locked-in syndrome isn't a counterexample to the thesis—it's a *confirmation* of it, and the objection's own reasoning proves this.

Notice what the objection concedes: "We now know from rare cases where locked-in patients recovered partial communication that they were conscious all along. This vindicates Theory B retrospectively—but only because we eventually got reports."

This concession reveals that **first-person reports are indeed the bottleneck**—exactly what the thesis claims. Theory B wasn't vindicated by neural data alone (we had that all along), nor by behavioral data (absent by definition), but by eventual reports. The disagreement case forced Theory B to make a bold commitment (override the absence of reports based on neural integration), and that commitment was testable once reports became available. Theory A failed the test; Theory B passed it.

The objection complains this only works "because we eventually got reports," as if this undermines the thesis. But that's the thesis's central point: first-person reports are the methodological bottleneck—the thing theories must ultimately answer to. Disagreement cases are where theories make their riskiest commitments *about* reports (when to trust them, when to override them, when their absence is informative), and these commitments are tested when reports become available or when we can explain their absence.

The locked-in case is actually the perfect illustration. Theory A's commitment ("no report = no consciousness") was definitively falsified when reports emerged. Theory B's commitment ("high cortical integration = consciousness even without reports") was definitively confirmed. The disagreement case *did* enable definitive testing—it forced theories to make falsifiable predictions about what reports would say if they became available.

The objection claims we "cannot definitively test theories without ultimately returning to first-person reports as the ground truth." But that's not a bug in the thesis—that's the thesis itself. Reports are the ground truth; disagreement cases are where theories make their boldest, most testable claims *about* that ground truth. The locked-in case shows this perfectly: theories that made wrong commitments about the relationship between consciousness and reports were falsified when reports became available.

The objection also ignores that we can test theories in locked-in cases even without eventual reports by examining whether their stance is principled. Theory A's "no report = no consciousness" is unprincipled because it conflates consciousness with report-capacity, making consciousness depend on motor function rather than mental states. Theory B's stance follows from its account of what consciousness is (integrated information/cortical activity). We can test coherence even when we cannot yet test accuracy.

**VERDICT**

The defence fails, though it exposes an ambiguity in the thesis that makes the failure complex.

The defence is right that locked-in syndrome confirms that first-person reports are methodologically central—we needed eventual reports to vindicate Theory B. But this actually strengthens the counterexample rather than defeating it.

The problem is that the defence shifts between two incompatible claims about what "definitive testing" means:

**Claim 1**: We can definitively test theories *in* disagreement cases (the thesis's explicit language: "definitively test consciousness theories in cases of disagreement").

**Claim 2**: Disagreement cases force theories to make commitments that can be definitively tested *later* when reports become available (the defence's position).

These are not the same thing. The counterexample shows we cannot do Claim 1—we cannot definitively test which theory is correct *during* the locked-in period when the disagreement exists. The defence retreats to Claim 2—we can test theories once the disagreement is resolved by eventual reports.

But Claim 2 undermines the thesis's distinctive methodological point. If disagreement cases are only testable once we get reports that resolve the disagreement, then we're back to the standard framework: first-person reports are just another data stream, and we test theories by whether they correctly predict what reports will say when available. That's not a "redrawn dialectic"—it's the conventional methodology.

The defence's fallback to coherence-testing ("Theory A is unprincipled because it conflates consciousness with report-capacity") just reinforces the earlier problem: we can check logical coherence, but that's not empirical testing of whether theories are true.

The counterexample stands: locked-in syndrome shows that disagreement cases do not enable definitive empirical testing at the time of disagreement. They only enable testing retrospectively when reports resolve the disagreement—which means reports, not disagreement, are doing the testing work.

</details>

### O20. The standard methodology of testing consciousness theories through dissociations and lesion studies is self-defeating because it presupposes the very thing at issue: which physical changes preserve the subject whose consciousness we're tracking.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Empirically-minded consciousness researchers who think philosophical debates about personal identity are separate from consciousness science
- **Sketch:** When we use split-brain patients to test theories, we assume the pre- and post-surgery entity are the 'same subject' whose consciousness changed. But theories of consciousness have direct implications for personal identity and subject-boundaries—IIT suggests split-brain creates two subjects, while GWT suggests one subject with degraded integration. The argument shows that treating empirical tests as decisive commits you to having already decided which subjects exist across experimental conditions, but that decision itself depends on having already picked the right consciousness theory. The key premise is that empirical methodology requires re-identifying subjects across conditions, but consciousness theories determine subject-boundaries, creating a vicious circle.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Subject-reidentification circularity is genuine but has been discussed in personal identity literature.
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence's constitutive/contingent distinction was overstated; researchers use organism-tracking (pre-theoretical) to test consciousness-identity (theoretical), avoiding the circularity
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence retreated to claiming methodology only fails in 'theory-decisive cases,' conceding it works in standard cases—a limited methodology is not a self-defeating one
  - TRIVIAL — landed 3/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence's Type 2 circularity claim mistakes underdetermination (data compatible with multiple theories) for vicious circularity (cannot get data without theory)
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence failed to fuse methodological and metaphysical senses of 'subject'; organism-tracking provides neutral coordinates for testing theories, not a presupposition that predetermines answers
- 🔧 **Survives as:** The standard methodology faces significant limitations when testing consciousness theories in cases where subject-boundaries are contested (such as split-brain patients), because such cases require theoretical commitments about subject-identity that cannot be fully resolved by organism-tracking alone
- **Flaw:** The thesis equivocates between methodological subjects (organisms tracked pre-theoretically) and metaphysical subjects (loci of consciousness); once distinguished, researchers can use the former to test theories about the latter without circularity

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. If this argument worked, it would paralyze all cognitive neuroscience, not just consciousness studies. Consider memory research: testing whether the hippocampus is necessary for declarative memory requires tracking "the same subject" before and after hippocampal damage. But theories of memory have implications for personal identity too—if psychological continuity constitutes personal identity and memory is central to psychological continuity, then different memory theories entail different judgments about whether pre- and post-lesion entities are "the same person." By the thesis's logic, we'd need to settle personal identity theory before doing any memory neuroscience. The same applies to emotion research (emotion theories affect identity through narrative self-constitution), perception research (perceptual continuity matters for subject-tracking), and even motor control (embodied cognition theories link bodily continuity to personal identity). Indeed, ANY neuroscientific study of a psychological capacity that ANYONE has EVER claimed matters for personal identity would face this circularity. The thesis proves too much because it would render impossible the entire enterprise of lesion-based cognitive neuroscience, which has been productively practiced for over a century despite these theoretical entanglements. The weak premise is that consciousness theories uniquely determine subject-boundaries in a methodologically vicious way.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem. The thesis needs to explain why consciousness creates special circularity that memory, emotion, and perception research somehow escape.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE.

The defence distinguishes consciousness from other cognitive capacities by appeal to *constitutive* versus *contingent* connections to subject-identity.

Memory, emotion, and perception have at most *contingent* connections to subject-boundaries. Yes, some philosophers claim memory matters for personal identity, but that's a *philosophical theory about what grounds identity*—it doesn't make memory *constitutive of* there being a subject at all. We can coherently ask "Is this the same person despite memory loss?" precisely because the subject can be identified independently of their memory states. Hippocampal damage changes what a subject can remember; it doesn't threaten whether there *is* a subject there to have or lack memories.

Consciousness is different. Consciousness doesn't just *matter for* subject-identity—it *constitutes* the subject qua experiencing entity. There is no conceptual gap between "X is conscious" and "there is a subject of experience present." When IIT says split-brain creates two subjects, it's not making a contingent claim about what grounds identity—it's making a direct claim about *how many loci of consciousness exist*. The subject-count just *is* the consciousness-count.

This means consciousness research faces unique circularity: we cannot identify "the same subject" across conditions without already knowing how consciousness distributes, but testing consciousness theories requires already having identified the subject. Memory research faces no parallel problem because subject-identification is logically prior to and independent of memory-state identification.

The objection's examples fail because they confuse *theories that have implications for personal identity* with *theories that directly determine whether subjects exist*. Only consciousness does the latter.

VERDICT.

The defence fails, though it identifies something real.

The distinction between constitutive and contingent connections is overstated. The objector can respond: researchers *in practice* track subjects across split-brain surgery using biological/bodily continuity (same organism, same brain stem, continuous spatiotemporal path), not by presupposing a consciousness theory. This tracking criterion is pre-theoretical and works perfectly well—we can say "organism O before surgery, organism O after surgery" without taking any stand on whether O houses one subject or two.

The thesis conflates *tracking the organism* with *tracking the subject of consciousness*. But that's precisely what lets the methodology work: we use organism-identity (uncontroversial) to test claims about consciousness-identity (theoretical). The "subject" whose consciousness we're testing is just the organism picked out biologically.

Moreover, the defence's own logic backfires. If consciousness *constitutes* the subject so completely that we cannot identify subjects without a consciousness theory, then the thesis becomes even more self-defeating: researchers couldn't even *begin* an experiment without already knowing the answer. But they do begin experiments, using organism-tracking. The defence makes the thesis worse, not better.

The original objection stands: the thesis proves too much because the supposed circularity doesn't actually constrain practice.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. Consider testing Global Workspace Theory against Integrated Information Theory using binocular rivalry experiments. A subject views different images presented to each eye (say, a house to the left eye, a face to the right eye) and reports alternating conscious perception—sometimes seeing the house, sometimes the face. Both theories make predictions about neural correlates during these switches, and researchers test which predictions match the data.

Now apply the thesis: it claims we cannot conduct this test without presupposing "which physical changes preserve the subject whose consciousness we're tracking." But in binocular rivalry, there is NO subject-boundary question at stake. Everyone agrees—IIT, GWT, and all researchers—that there is exactly one subject throughout the experiment. The organism remains biologically continuous, no surgical interventions occur, and no theory suggests subject-division. Yet this is paradigmatic consciousness science using the exact "standard methodology" the thesis targets (testing theories through dissociations—here, dissociating physical stimulus from conscious report).

The thesis claims the methodology is "self-defeating" because it "presupposes the very thing at issue." But the counterexample shows the methodology works perfectly well in cases where subject-identity is uncontroversial. The weak premise is that testing consciousness theories *requires* presupposing answers about subject-boundaries. In fact, most consciousness experiments involve no subject-boundary questions whatsoever—researchers simply use uncontroversial organism-identity to track the subject.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This significantly undermines the thesis. The claimed circularity only arises in exotic cases (split-brain, severe lesions), not in the "standard methodology" generally.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The defence accepts that *some* consciousness experiments avoid subject-boundary questions, but argues this actually *supports* rather than undermines the thesis—by revealing that the "standard methodology" rests on a hidden restriction that becomes visible only when we examine its limits.

Binocular rivalry works as a test precisely *because* it's experimentally designed to hold subject-identity fixed. The defender can say: "You've shown that when we artificially constrain experiments to cases where all theories agree on subject-boundaries, we can test other aspects of those theories. Granted. But this proves my point—the methodology *requires* background agreement on subject-identity to function."

The thesis claims the methodology is self-defeating *as a general approach to deciding between consciousness theories*, not that every individual experiment fails. The problem emerges when we try to use the methodology to adjudicate the *fundamental* disagreements between theories. IIT and GWT don't just disagree about neural correlates during rivalry—they disagree about what happens in split-brain cases, cerebellum-damaged patients, and other conditions where subject-boundaries are genuinely at issue.

Here's the key move: binocular rivalry experiments can only test *conditional* claims—"*If* there's one subject, then GWT predicts X and IIT predicts Y." But consciousness science needs to test *categorical* claims—"There *is* one subject here and two subjects there." The moment we move from rivalry (safe) to split-brain (contested), the methodology encounters the circularity. The objector has merely identified the safe zone, not dissolved the problem.

Moreover, the objector's phrase "most consciousness experiments" concedes the game. The thesis targets the methodology's ability to handle *theory-decisive* cases—the ones that would actually force a choice between frameworks. If the methodology only works where theories already agree, it cannot be what settles which theory is correct. The exotic cases aren't exotic *accidents*—they're where the theories make their most distinctive predictions.

# VERDICT

The defence fails.

The defender tries to reframe the counterexample as merely showing a "safe zone," but this move collapses under scrutiny. If the methodology works perfectly well for testing conditional predictions in uncontroversial cases, and these tests *do* yield evidence distinguishing theories (as binocular rivalry experiments demonstrably have), then the methodology isn't self-defeating—it's just *limited* in scope.

A limited methodology is not a self-defeating one. The thesis claimed the *standard methodology* is self-defeating, but the counterexample shows the standard methodology functions successfully across the vast majority of consciousness research. The defender's retreat to "it only fails in theory-decisive cases" is a fatal concession: it means the methodology isn't inherently circular, it just cannot *alone* resolve every theoretical dispute.

Furthermore, the defence's distinction between "conditional" and "categorical" claims is artificial. Science regularly settles categorical questions by accumulating conditional evidence. If GWT consistently outperforms IIT in every case where subject-identity is uncontroversial, this *is* evidence for GWT even regarding contested cases—absent special reason to think the contested cases operate by completely different principles.

The defender's final move—that exotic cases are "theory-decisive"—actually undermines the thesis. If split-brain cases are where theories make distinctive predictions, then researchers *can* use biological organism-tracking (as the previous objection noted) to set up the test: "Same organism, do we see one or two streams of reportable consciousness?" The theory that better predicts what we find wins. No circularity arises because we're using organism-identity (pre-theoretical) to test consciousness-identity (theoretical).

The counterexample stands: the methodology works in standard cases, and the thesis has not shown why it cannot be extended to contested cases using organism-tracking.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis presents itself as revealing a deep methodological circularity, but it actually just restates the obvious truism that consciousness theories have implications for what counts as a subject of consciousness. This is definitional, not a discovered problem. Of course if you adopt IIT, you'll count subjects differently than if you adopt GWT—that's what it *means* for theories to disagree about consciousness. The thesis dresses up this verbal stipulation as though it were a methodological discovery.

The supposedly problematic "presupposition" is simply the unremarkable fact that to test any theory, you need some way to set up your experiment. Researchers use organism-identity to track experimental subjects precisely because this is a pre-theoretical, uncontested baseline that *everyone* can agree on regardless of their consciousness theory. The thesis points to the fact that different theories will *interpret* what's happening to that organism differently (one subject vs. two subjects), but this is just what theoretical disagreement looks like—it's not a vicious circle that makes testing "self-defeating."

The weak premise is that there's something methodologically problematic about theories having implications for subject-boundaries. But this is trivial: theories about X naturally have implications for X-related concepts. The thesis hasn't identified a real circularity that prevents testing—it's merely noted that consciousness theories make claims about consciousness, dressed up as a methodological crisis. Every theory-testing enterprise faces the "problem" that you need some framework to interpret your results; this isn't a special defect of consciousness science.

STRENGTH: 2/5 — This has some force in showing the thesis overstates its novelty, but the thesis does identify a genuine structural feature (even if not a fatal one) about how subject-boundaries interact with experimental design in ways that differ from testing other cognitive capacities.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The defence insists the thesis identifies a *structural* circularity, not merely a verbal observation about theoretical disagreement.

The key is to distinguish between two kinds of theoretical commitment:

**Type 1**: Theories that require interpretive frameworks to read results, but where the *data itself* is theory-neutral. Testing memory theories, you need a framework to interpret what "remembering" means, but the behavioral/neural data (subject recalls/fails to recall, hippocampus fires/doesn't fire) is observable independently of your memory theory.

**Type 2**: Theories where the *data itself* cannot be identified without presupposing the theory. The thesis claims consciousness research involves Type 2 circularity.

Here's why: The objector says researchers use "organism-identity" as a pre-theoretical baseline. But what *data* are they collecting from that organism? In split-brain experiments, the data is things like "left hemisphere reports seeing X, right hemisphere responds motorically to Y." Now the question: is this data about *one subject with divided access* or *two subjects with different experiences*? 

The objector claims this is just "interpretation," but it's not—it's a question about *what the data is*. If IIT is right, the data is "subject₁ experiences X, subject₂ experiences Y"—two data points. If GWT is right, the data is "subject experiences X-with-left-access and Y-with-right-access"—one data point with internal structure. The *number of data points* depends on the theory.

This isn't like memory research, where we can count "one organism, two memory tests, one passed and one failed" without theoretical commitment. In consciousness research, we cannot even count *how many conscious experiences occurred* without already knowing which theory is correct. That's not trivial definitional disagreement—it's methodological circularity, because the evidence that would decide between theories cannot be identified as evidence without presupposing one theory.

The objector's phrase "theories make claims about consciousness" misses this: the problem isn't that theories disagree, but that the disagreement infects the *individuation of evidence itself*, not just its interpretation.

# VERDICT

The defence fails, but reveals why the thesis seems compelling.

The defender correctly identifies that consciousness research has a distinctive feature: theories disagree about *how many* conscious experiences occur, not just their properties. But this doesn't create vicious circularity—it creates *underdetermination*, which is importantly different.

The defence's "Type 2" circularity would be vicious if researchers literally *could not collect data* without presupposing a theory. But they can and do. The data in split-brain experiments is: "Organism O, when stimulus X presented to left visual field, produces verbal report R₁; when stimulus Y presented to right visual field, produces motor response R₂ but no verbal report." This data is genuinely theory-neutral—it's just behavioral and neural measurements from an organism.

What's *not* theory-neutral is the *significance* of this data for subject-counting. But that's exactly what we should expect when testing theories. The thesis mistakes underdetermination (data compatible with multiple theories) for circularity (cannot get data without theory). These are not the same.

The defender's claim about "counting data points" reveals the confusion. Scientists don't need to know "how many experiences occurred" to collect evidence—they need to know what the organism did and what its brain showed. Whether that constitutes one experience or two is precisely what the theory predicts and the data tests. IIT predicts: "If two subjects, then we'll see X pattern of integration." GWT predicts: "If one subject, then we'll see Y pattern." We look and see which pattern obtains. The data (X-pattern or Y-pattern) is identifiable independently; the theories differ on what it means for subject-count.

The objector is right: the thesis inflates a real but unremarkable feature (theories about consciousness make claims about what counts as a subject) into a false methodological crisis (testing is impossible). The fact that evidence underdetermines theory choice is a general feature of science, not a special defect making consciousness research "self-defeating."

The thesis is trivial in the sense the objector identifies: it mistakes the ordinary structure of theory-testing for a unique pathology.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — # ATTEMPT

The thesis equivocates on "subject" between a metaphysical sense and a methodological sense, and the argument needs both readings to generate apparent force.

**Reading 1 (Methodological)**: "Subject" means the experimental unit we track across conditions—the organism identified by biological continuity, spatiotemporal path, and practical re-identification criteria. On this reading, "which physical changes preserve the subject" is unproblematic: we use standard organism-tracking that all researchers accept regardless of consciousness theory.

**Reading 2 (Metaphysical)**: "Subject" means the locus of unified conscious experience—the thing picked out by "subject of consciousness" in philosophical debates about phenomenal unity and personal identity. On this reading, consciousness theories genuinely disagree about subject-boundaries (IIT's two subjects vs. GWT's one).

The argument *needs* Reading 2 to make the thesis interesting—to show that theories genuinely disagree about something important (subject-boundaries in the metaphysical sense). But it *needs* Reading 1 to generate the circularity problem—the claim that we must "presuppose which physical changes preserve the subject" only creates methodological trouble if "subject" means the experimental unit we're tracking.

The thesis trades on this equivocation: it's *interesting* because theories disagree about metaphysical subjects, but appears *problematic* only if we confuse this with disagreement about methodological subject-tracking. Once we disambiguate, the circularity dissolves: researchers track methodological subjects (organisms) to test theories about metaphysical subjects (loci of consciousness). No circularity arises.

**Weak premise**: "empirical methodology requires re-identifying subjects across conditions, but consciousness theories determine subject-boundaries" — this only works by equivocating on "subject."

# STRENGTH: 4/5

This significantly damages the thesis. The apparent circularity depends entirely on sliding between two senses of "subject." Once distinguished, we see that researchers use an uncontroversial sense (organism-tracking) to test theories about a controversial sense (consciousness-loci), which is exactly how theory-testing should work—not a vicious circle.

**DEFENDER** — # BEST DEFENCE

The defence rejects the proposed disambiguation and argues that the two alleged "readings" of "subject" cannot actually be separated in consciousness research—they necessarily collapse into one another in a way that reinstates the circularity.

The objector claims researchers can track "methodological subjects" (organisms) independently of theories about "metaphysical subjects" (loci of consciousness). But this separation only works if we assume that organisms and subjects of consciousness come in a stable one-to-one correspondence. The entire point of the thesis is that consciousness theories *deny this stable correspondence* in precisely the cases that matter for testing.

Consider: what makes organism-tracking a legitimate "methodological subject" for consciousness research? Only the assumption that tracking the organism *tracks what we care about*—namely, consciousness. We don't track the organism as such (we're not doing biology); we track it *as a proxy for the subject of consciousness*. The objector's "methodological subject" just *is* "the thing we assume houses the consciousness we're investigating."

But now the problem resurfaces: When we track "one organism" through split-brain surgery, are we tracking one subject of consciousness or two? The objector says this is merely a "metaphysical" question that doesn't affect "methodological" tracking. But that's false. If IIT is right and there are now two subjects, then our "methodological" decision to treat the organism as a single experimental unit has *methodologically* conflated two distinct subjects of consciousness—we're literally measuring two different consciousnesses and treating the measurements as if they came from one. That's not a harmless metaphysical interpretation; it's a methodological error that invalidates the experimental design.

The objector's move only works by *presupposing* that organism-boundaries track consciousness-boundaries closely enough that the slippage doesn't matter. But that presupposition is precisely what's at issue between consciousness theories. You cannot use organism-identity as a theory-neutral proxy for subject-identity when the theories *disagree about whether organisms and subjects have the same boundaries*.

The supposed "equivocation" is actually a *substantive disagreement* about whether biological continuity suffices for conscious subject continuity. Calling one sense "methodological" and one "metaphysical" just disguises the fact that the methodological practice *embeds* a metaphysical commitment.

# VERDICT

The defence fails.

The defender makes a last-ditch effort to fuse the two senses of "subject," but the fusion doesn't hold. The key error is the claim that tracking organisms "as proxies" for consciousness creates methodological invalidity if the proxy is imperfect.

This is false. Proxies don't need to be perfect to be methodologically legitimate—they need to be *measurable* and *systematically related to the target*. Organism-tracking provides exactly this: a clear, uncontroversial way to set up experiments that *all theories can then interpret*. 

The defender says: "If IIT is right and there are two subjects, then treating the organism as one unit is a methodological error." But this misunderstands how theory-testing works. It's not an error—it's the *test setup*. We establish: "One organism, post-surgery, shows these neural patterns and behavioral responses." IIT then *predicts* what we'll see if its two-subject claim is correct (perhaps: independent reportability, separate integration metrics in each hemisphere). GWT predicts what we'll see if its one-subject claim is correct (perhaps: degraded global broadcasting, but unified metacognitive monitoring). We run the experiment and see which prediction matches.

The defender's claim that we're "conflating two distinct subjects" only makes sense if we've *already accepted IIT*. But we haven't—that's what we're testing. The whole point is that organism-tracking gives us a neutral frame to *discover* whether there are one or two subjects by seeing which theory's predictions pan out.

The defender's final move—that organism-tracking "embeds a metaphysical commitment"—proves too much. Every experimental setup involves *some* framework for individuating what you're measuring. The question is whether that framework *predetermines* the answer. It doesn't. Organism-tracking is compatible with either theory's being correct; it simply provides stable coordinates for testing which is.

The objection stands: the thesis equivocates between methodological and metaphysical senses of "subject," and once distinguished, no circularity remains. The "substantive disagreement" the defender points to is real, but it's exactly what the experiments are *designed to resolve*, not what makes them impossible.

</details>
