### O21. Consciousness theories fail to be testable not because we lack third-person access, but because they systematically confuse the question 'what realizes consciousness?' with 'what consciousness enables us to do?', making them empirically equivalent regarding all behavioral and reportable evidence.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The shared assumption across consciousness science that finding NCCs or testing specific predictions (like IIT's phi calculations) will settle theoretical disputes
- **Sketch:** The argument distinguishes between substrate questions (what physically constitutes consciousness) and capacity questions (what abilities consciousness provides). Most tests actually target the capacity question: does this damage impair report, integration, attention, etc.? But competing theories can agree on all capacity-facts while disagreeing on substrate-facts—IIT and GWT might agree that posterior hot zone damage impairs reportability while disagreeing on whether consciousness was already lost or merely unreportable. The key premise is that all evidence philosophers and scientists actually cite—reports, behavior, neural correlates—bears on capacities, not substrates. The distinction collapses what looked like theoretical disagreements into mere terminological choices about what to call 'consciousness.'
- **Appraiser (blind):** Substrate vs. capacity distinction is useful but the conclusion that theories are terminological choices undersells genuine disagreement.
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 3/5, **UNANSWERED**. Split-brain case shows theories make different capacity-level predictions, revealing the thesis's empirical equivalence claim fails even within its own framework
  - TRIVIAL — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence admits substrate tests are 'imaginable but unavailable,' showing the distinction explains nothing about actual theoretical confusion versus methodological limits
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 2/5, answered. Defence successfully shows univocal use of 'capacity' as functional abilities, though this victory exposes that no genuine confusion is demonstrated
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence's criterion for substrate evidence requires detecting physical properties independent of functional roles—an incoherent standard that makes the thesis irrefutable
- **Flaw:** The substrate/capacity distinction generates an unfalsifiable framework because the criterion for substrate evidence—detecting physical constitution independent of functional effects—cannot be empirically instantiated, making any possible evidence classifiable as 'capacity evidence' by necessity rather than discovery

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. Consider split-brain patients: when the corpus callosum is severed, visual information presented to the left visual field (processed by right hemisphere) cannot be verbally reported, yet patients can use their left hand to point to matching objects. Here we have a clear case where "what realizes consciousness" and "what consciousness enables us to do" come decisively apart in empirically distinguishable ways. 

If the right hemisphere substrate realizes consciousness (substrate question), we predict left-hand accurate pointing despite no verbal report. If only left hemisphere verbal-report capacity counts as consciousness (capacity question), we predict the patient is unconscious of left-field stimuli. These are different empirical predictions about the same behavioral evidence. The patient either can or cannot point accurately with their left hand—this is not "empirically equivalent regarding all behavioral and reportable evidence." The thesis claims competing theories agree on all capacity-facts while differing only on substrate-facts, but split-brain cases show substrate differences (one vs. two conscious centers) generate different predictions about behavioral capacities (integrated vs. dissociated responses). The behavioral dissociation IS the evidence that adjudicates between substrate theories.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This seriously challenges the claim of empirical equivalence, showing substrate questions generate distinct behavioral predictions, though the thesis might survive by refining what counts as "reportable evidence."

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The split-brain case actually reinforces rather than refutes the thesis. What you've described are competing *capacity* attributions wearing substrate clothing.

Consider what's actually being tested: Can the right hemisphere support the capacity for visual discrimination? Can it support the capacity for motor guidance? Can it support reportability-via-pointing as opposed to reportability-via-speech? These are all capacity questions. The "substrate" language (one vs. two conscious centers) is doing no empirical work—it's a post-hoc interpretation of the capacity facts.

Watch the sleight of hand in your objection. You say "if the right hemisphere substrate realizes consciousness, we predict left-hand pointing." But that's backwards. We observe left-hand pointing, then *infer* something about substrates. The prediction flows from capacity assumptions: if visual-discrimination-capacity and motor-guidance-capacity can be dissociated from verbal-report-capacity, then we predict the pointing behavior. Whether we then *call* this "a second conscious center" or "unconscious processing with preserved capacities" is the terminological choice the thesis identifies.

Your claim that "substrate differences generate different predictions about behavioral capacities" inverts the actual epistemic order. We don't derive capacity-predictions from substrate-theories; we observe capacity-dissociations and then argue about substrate-interpretations. The split-brain patient's behavior tells us which capacities travel together and which separate. Calling the right hemisphere "conscious" or "unconscious" adds nothing to this functional map—it's precisely the kind of label-choice that remains empirically idle once all capacity-facts are fixed.

VERDICT

The defence fails, but not for the reason the objection claims.

The real problem is more subtle: the objection correctly identifies that different theories make different predictions, but the defence successfully shows these are still capacity-level predictions. However, this reveals a fatal weakness in the original thesis. The thesis claims theories are "empirically equivalent regarding all behavioral and reportable evidence" because they confuse substrate and capacity questions. But if they're making different predictions about *which capacities are present*—even if these are capacity-level disputes—then they're not empirically equivalent at all.

The split-brain case shows theories disagree about capacity-attributions themselves (does the right hemisphere have report-capacity-via-pointing or not?), not just about what substrates underlie agreed-upon capacities. The thesis's distinction collapses because the real action is in disputes about what counts as a relevant capacity and whether it's present—disputes that generate different empirical predictions even while remaining at the capacity level throughout.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis presents itself as a substantive discovery about why consciousness theories fail to be testable, but it actually just stipulates a verbal distinction and then observes that evidence can be redescribed in its preferred terms. The move is: (1) Define "substrate questions" and "capacity questions" as separate categories, (2) Note that all actual evidence involves behavior/reports, (3) Declare that behavior/reports are by definition about "capacities," (4) Conclude theories only differ on "substrates" and are thus empirically equivalent.

But this is trivial redescription, not discovery. Any evidence can be labeled "capacity evidence" if you define capacities broadly enough. The weak point is the premise that "all evidence philosophers and scientists actually cite...bears on capacities, not substrates." This isn't an empirical finding—it's true by definitional fiat. Once you've stipulated that anything behavioral counts as "capacity" evidence, of course all behavioral evidence counts as capacity evidence. 

The supposedly important distinction between substrate and capacity is just a choice about how to taxonomize questions, not a discovered fact about consciousness science. The thesis offers no independent criterion for what makes something a substrate-fact versus a capacity-fact beyond "can we observe it behaviorally?"—which makes the conclusion that behavioral evidence only tests capacities a tautology. We haven't learned why theories are untestable; we've just been given new labels for old problems.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious challenge. The thesis needs to show its distinction tracks something real rather than just relabeling the testability problem in new vocabulary.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection mistakes a clarificatory distinction for a stipulative one. The substrate/capacity distinction tracks a real methodological confusion in consciousness science, not a verbal preference.

Here's what makes it non-trivial: scientists and philosophers *think* they're testing substrate claims when they're actually only testing capacity claims, and this confusion explains the persistence of theoretical disputes despite mounting evidence. The distinction has independent content because substrate and capacity questions have different logical properties that generate different evidential requirements.

Substrate questions ask: what physical system constitutes the conscious state itself? Capacity questions ask: what abilities does a system possess? These differ in kind, not by stipulation. A substrate claim says "consciousness *is* this physical process." A capacity claim says "consciousness *enables* these functions." The first is a constitutive identity; the second is a causal-functional relationship.

The non-triviality appears precisely where the objection claims fiat: in showing that *all* the evidence types consciousness scientists actually deploy—NCCs, lesion studies, report paradigms, phi measurements—have the logical structure of capacity tests, not substrate tests. This isn't true by definition; it's a substantive (and surprising) claim about what our experimental methods can actually access.

Consider: if we damage area X and report disappears, we've learned that X is necessary for report-capacity. To conclude X is the substrate of consciousness requires an additional premise: that consciousness and report-capacity are necessarily coupled. But that premise is precisely what's disputed between theories! IIT and GWT disagree on whether unreportable states count as conscious—i.e., whether report-capacity is constitutive or merely evidential. No behavioral test can adjudicate this without begging the question.

The distinction isn't "anything behavioral = capacity"; it's "anything that tests what a system can do rather than what consciousness is made of = capacity test." These come apart: you could imagine substrate tests (direct inspection of the physical constitution of conscious states) that aren't capacity tests. We just don't have them.

The thesis reveals something substantive: the empirical equivalence isn't a brute fact about consciousness being hard to study—it's a structural consequence of our methods only accessing functional profiles while our theories make constitutive claims. That's a diagnosis, not a relabeling.

VERDICT

The defence fails.

The problem is that the defence proves too much while delivering too little. If substrate questions really do have "different logical properties that generate different evidential requirements" from capacity questions, and if our methods genuinely cannot access substrate facts, then the thesis hasn't explained why consciousness theories fail to be testable—it's shown they're asking unanswerable questions, full stop. That's a much stronger (and different) claim than "they confuse two questions."

But worse: the defence's own example destroys its case. When it says "you could imagine substrate tests (direct inspection of the physical constitution of conscious states) that aren't capacity tests. We just don't have them," it admits the distinction is doing no work in explaining actual scientific practice. If we *could* have substrate tests, then substrate and capacity questions aren't confused—they're just both legitimate questions we currently lack methods to separate.

The triviality objection succeeds because the thesis needs the distinction to be both substantive enough to explain empirical equivalence AND sharp enough to show theories are genuinely confused rather than just addressing hard-to-test questions. The defence's move—claiming substrate tests are imaginable but unavailable—shows the distinction is real but irrelevant to explaining why *actual theories* fail to be testable. The theories aren't confused about question-types; they're making claims that outrun available evidence. That's not confusion; that's just theoretical ambition meeting methodological limits.

The thesis collapses into either trivial relabeling (if the distinction is just about how we describe evidence) or an admission that consciousness theories fail to be testable for the obvious reason: we can't directly access the relevant facts. Neither version does the explanatory work promised.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "what consciousness enables us to do" between two readings that make the argument appear to work when it actually fails.

**Reading 1 (Plausible)**: Capacity questions ask about functional abilities—what tasks a system can perform, what information it can access, what behaviors it can produce. On this reading, it's plausible that "all evidence philosophers and scientists actually cite...bears on capacities"—we do test what subjects can report, discriminate, integrate, etc.

**Reading 2 (Interesting)**: Capacity questions include metaphysical questions about what consciousness *consists in* or what its *essential nature* is. On this reading, the claim that theories "systematically confuse" substrate and capacity questions becomes interesting—it would mean major theories are making a category error.

The argument needs both. The weak point is the premise that competing theories "agree on all capacity-facts while disagreeing on substrate-facts." This only works if "capacity-facts" means functional abilities (Reading 1)—otherwise theories clearly disagree on capacity-facts too. But the conclusion that theories make a "confusion" rather than just facing hard questions only works if "capacity questions" include the metaphysical questions theories actually care about (Reading 2).

When IIT and GWT disagree about whether unreportable states are conscious, they're not confusing two question-types. On Reading 1, they're disagreeing about capacity-facts (which functional profiles constitute consciousness). On Reading 2, they're both asking capacity questions (about consciousness's nature). Either way, no systematic confusion is shown—just theoretical disagreement the thesis relabels.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — The thesis cannot survive without clarifying which reading it intends, but neither reading alone supports the argument's conclusion that theories are *confused* rather than simply empirically underdetermined.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection fabricates an equivocation that doesn't exist. The thesis uses "capacity" univocally throughout: capacities are functional abilities—what a system can do. The appearance of two readings comes from the objector conflating the *questions theories ask* with the *evidence that could answer them*.

Here's the actual structure: Theories make metaphysical claims about what consciousness is (substrate claims). They test these claims using evidence about what systems can do (capacity evidence). The confusion isn't between two meanings of "capacity"—it's between thinking capacity-evidence can settle substrate-claims.

The objector's "Reading 2" isn't a different reading of capacity at all—it's a misdescription of what the thesis identifies as substrate questions. When IIT says consciousness is integrated information, that's a substrate claim (consciousness *is* phi). When GWT says consciousness is global workspace broadcasting, that's a substrate claim (consciousness *is* global access). These are constitutive identities, not functional profiles.

Now watch what happens experimentally: Both theories test their claims by looking at functional abilities. Does the system integrate information across domains? Does it broadcast to multiple processors? Can it report? Can it guide behavior? These are all capacity tests. The theories then make an inference: "because this capacity-profile is present, our substrate-claim is vindicated."

The thesis's point is that this inference is invalid because different substrate-claims can predict identical capacity-profiles. IIT and GWT might agree completely on which functional abilities a system has while disagreeing on whether those abilities constitute consciousness or merely correlate with it. That's not equivocation on "capacity"—it's showing that capacity-facts underdetermine substrate-facts.

The objector claims "when IIT and GWT disagree about whether unreportable states are conscious, they're disagreeing about capacity-facts (which functional profiles constitute consciousness)." But this misses the thesis's central point: "which functional profiles constitute consciousness" is *precisely* the confused question. It treats constitution-claims (substrate) as if they were settled by functional-profile-facts (capacity). That's the confusion the thesis diagnoses.

On the univocal reading—capacities as functional abilities—the argument goes through cleanly: (1) All our evidence tests functional abilities, (2) Theories make claims about what consciousness is constituted by, (3) Different constitution-claims can accommodate the same functional abilities, (4) Therefore theories are empirically equivalent regarding our actual evidence. No equivocation required.

VERDICT

The defence succeeds in blocking the equivocation charge but thereby exposes a deeper failure in the original thesis.

If capacities really do mean just functional abilities throughout, then the thesis's claim that theories "systematically confuse" the two questions becomes unmotivated. The defence correctly shows that theories make substrate claims (constitution) and test them with capacity evidence (function). But calling this a "confusion" rather than a legitimate theoretical strategy requires showing the inference from capacity-evidence to substrate-claims is *invalid in principle*, not just underdetermining.

And here the defence's own formulation reveals the problem: "different substrate-claims can predict identical capacity-profiles" only shows empirical equivalence, not confusion. Scientists routinely make theoretical claims that outrun current evidence, hoping future evidence or theoretical considerations will break the tie. That's not confusion—that's how theory-building works.

The defence's winning move against equivocation—insisting capacities univocally mean functional abilities—makes the "confusion" diagnosis unsustainable. If theories clearly distinguish between what consciousness *is* (their substrate claims) and what conscious systems *can do* (the capacity evidence), then they're not confused about the difference. They're just betting that capacity-patterns will eventually reveal substrate-facts, either through finer-grained functional analysis or through theoretical constraints we haven't yet discovered.

The thesis survives the equivocation objection only by retreating to a much weaker claim: theories are currently empirically underdetermined by behavioral evidence. But that's not a discovered confusion—it's a familiar epistemic situation that doesn't impugn the coherence of the theoretical project itself.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis is unfalsifiable because any apparent counterexample—any case where consciousness theories seem genuinely testable or where evidence seems to distinguish them—gets redefined as "really just a capacity question in disguise." 

The weak point is the premise that "all evidence philosophers and scientists actually cite...bears on capacities, not substrates." This premise is protected by definitional retreat: whenever someone presents evidence that seems to test substrate claims (NCCs, phi measurements, lesion studies affecting specific neural populations), the thesis responds "that's actually testing capacities—what the system can do." But there's no independent criterion for what would count as substrate evidence versus capacity evidence except "whatever evidence we actually have counts as capacity evidence."

Watch the protection mechanism: If split-brain studies seem to test whether consciousness is unified or divided (a substrate question), the thesis replies "no, that's really testing dissociable capacities." If phi calculations seem to test what physical property consciousness is identical to, the thesis replies "no, that's really testing what enables reportability." If future direct neural reading technology seemed to detect conscious states, the thesis could reply "no, that's really testing neural capacities for information processing."

The thesis cannot be wrong because it has no specification of what substrate evidence would look like independent of the capacity/substrate distinction itself. It's immune to counterexamples not because it's true, but because it reserves the right to reclassify any evidence as "capacity evidence" after the fact. The distinction becomes a lens that always confirms itself rather than a claim the world could refute.

STRENGTH: 5/5 — The thesis cannot survive this. It has no falsifiable core because its central distinction is applied post-hoc to whatever evidence exists, making it a self-sealing interpretive framework rather than a testable claim about consciousness science.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The unfalsifiability charge fails because the thesis *does* provide independent criteria for distinguishing substrate from capacity evidence—criteria that could be violated but happen not to be.

Here's the criterion: Evidence tests substrates when it directly reveals what physically constitutes the conscious state itself. Evidence tests capacities when it reveals what a system can do—what functions it can perform, what information it can access, what behaviors it can produce. This distinction is not post-hoc; it's grounded in the metaphysical difference between constitution and causation.

The thesis makes a falsifiable empirical claim: that all evidence currently deployed in consciousness science has the logical structure of capacity tests. This could be false. Imagine we developed technology that let us directly observe the intrinsic physical properties of neural states—their integrated information, their quantum coherence, their electromagnetic field configurations—*independent of* testing what these states enable the system to do. That would be substrate evidence, and if theories made different predictions about these directly-observable properties, the thesis would be refuted.

The thesis isn't "protected by definitional retreat"—it's making a substantive claim about what our actual experimental methods access. Consider the structure of every example the objection cites:

- NCCs: We identify them by correlating neural activity with reports or behavioral discriminations. That's testing what neural states enable (reportability, discrimination)—capacity evidence.

- Phi measurements: IIT predicts phi values, but we test these predictions by checking whether high-phi systems show the reportability and integration patterns IIT predicts. We're still testing functional profiles—capacity evidence.

- Lesion studies: We damage area X and observe loss of function Y. That's testing what X enables—capacity evidence.

The objection's hypothetical "direct neural reading technology" actually proves the thesis's point. If such technology just detected neural correlates of report-capacity, it would still be capacity evidence. Only if it revealed intrinsic physical properties whose conscious status was *independent of* any functional role would it constitute substrate evidence.

The thesis is falsifiable: show me evidence that tests what consciousness *is made of* rather than what it *enables*, and the thesis falls. The fact that all actual evidence has the capacity-structure isn't definitional retreat—it's an empirical discovery about the methods consciousness science actually employs.

VERDICT

The defence fails catastrophically.

The defence's own criterion—"Evidence tests substrates when it directly reveals what physically constitutes the conscious state itself"—is either circular or impossible, and either way, it proves the unfalsifiability charge.

If we can only identify "what physically constitutes the conscious state" by *already knowing* which physical properties are constitutive, then the criterion is circular: substrate evidence is evidence that tests substrate properties, where substrate properties are defined as... the properties substrate evidence tests. We have no independent access to what "physically constitutes consciousness" except through the very theories whose testability is in question.

Worse, the defence's own examples destroy its case. It admits that "direct neural reading technology" would count as substrate evidence only if it revealed "intrinsic physical properties whose conscious status was independent of any functional role." But this is impossible in principle. ANY physical property we could detect would be detectable only through its effects—its causal role in producing some measurable outcome. Even "directly observing" integrated information requires measuring information integration, which means testing functional relationships. Even detecting "quantum coherence" requires measuring quantum states through their physical effects.

The defence has specified a criterion for substrate evidence that nothing could satisfy: evidence that reveals physical constitution without revealing functional properties. This makes the thesis unfalsifiable not because it's protected by post-hoc redefinition, but because it's protected by demanding an incoherent standard for counterevidence.

The killer blow: The defence says "show me evidence that tests what consciousness *is made of* rather than what it *enables*, and the thesis falls." But on the defence's own account, anything we could empirically detect about what consciousness is made of would necessarily tell us something about what it enables (since detection requires causal interaction). The thesis has made itself unfalsifiable by setting up a distinction that cannot be empirically instantiated.

The thesis is dead. It's not making a substantive claim about consciousness science—it's applying a metaphysical distinction (constitution vs. causation) that generates an unfalsifiable interpretive framework. Any evidence gets classified as "capacity" because any evidence we could actually obtain operates through causal-functional relationships. The thesis survives not because it's true, but because it's structured to be irrefutable.

</details>

### O22. The demand that theories of consciousness make empirically distinguishable predictions smuggles in functionalism by assuming consciousness must have characteristic causal profiles.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Methodological consensus across the consciousness science community, including critics of functionalism who nonetheless accept empirical testability as the gold standard
- **Sketch:** Arguments about how to test consciousness theories typically assume we need different behavioral or neural predictions to choose between them. But this methodological criterion itself presupposes that consciousness has systematic causal relations with observable phenomena—precisely what functionalism claims and what theories like epiphenomenalism or certain forms of panpsychism deny. If consciousness could exist without characteristic causal signatures, then empirical indistinguishability wouldn't show theories are equally good; it would show empirical methods are incomplete. The dialectical trap is that non-functionalists who accept the 'empirical distinguishability' criterion for theory choice have already conceded the game to functionalism.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Correctly identifies that empirical distinguishability presupposes functionalism, but this is a fairly standard observation.
  - TRIVIAL — landed 2/5, answered. The objection claimed mere redescription, but the defence showed the thesis identifies a substantive trap about when methodological standards presuppose contested metaphysics.
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence could not sustain the collapse of 'having a causal role' into 'being constituted by that role'—property dualism coherently accepts the first while rejecting the second.
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence's own formulation revealed that any distinction between causal efficacy and functional constitution gets dismissed as 'collapsing,' making the smuggling charge undetectable in principle.
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The Chalmersian framework doesn't make the thesis redundant but refutes it by showing how to use empirical methods without functionalist metaphysics—the division of labor the thesis cannot show is illegitimate.
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Consciousness researchers who acknowledge the hard problem nonetheless treat empirical distinguishability as the sole criterion of theoretical legitimacy, creating a performative contradiction between their stated metaphysics and their methodological practice
- **Flaw:** The thesis cannot distinguish between smuggling functionalism and openly restricting it to the empirical domain while maintaining non-functionalist metaphysics—a coherent position exemplified by Chalmersian property dualism

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis merely redescribes what "empirical testability" means without discovering anything contested. To demand empirically distinguishable predictions just IS to ask what observable differences theories generate—this is definitional to empirical methodology, not a smuggled assumption. The supposed revelation that "empirical methods require causal connections to observables" is like announcing that "mathematical proofs require logical inference" or "historical evidence requires past events leaving traces." 

The author treats as a dialectical trap what is actually an open, explicit commitment: scientists studying consciousness DO assume it has causal relations with observable phenomena because they're doing empirical science. Non-functionalists who reject this don't "accept then violate" the criterion—they explicitly reject empirical methodology for consciousness, as many property dualists and panpsychists openly do. There's no hidden smuggling when epiphenomenalists straightforwardly acknowledge their view makes consciousness empirically undetectable.

The thesis repackages "empirical science studies causal relations" as if it were a discovery about consciousness debates, when it's merely the definition of empirical investigation applied to this domain.

STRENGTH: 2/5

This has some force because the thesis does identify a real methodological assumption, but it's weakened by the fact that this assumption is neither hidden nor contested—it's the explicit starting point of empirical consciousness science that some theorists openly reject rather than unwittingly accept.

**DEFENDER** — ## BEST DEFENCE

The objection misses the target by conflating two different claims about empirical methodology.

**The dialectical trap is not about what empirical science is in general, but about what follows for consciousness specifically.** Yes, empirical methods require causal connections to observables—that's definitional. But the trap springs when theorists assume this methodological truism settles a substantive metaphysical question: whether consciousness itself must have such connections.

Consider the structure: A non-functionalist critic might say "Global Workspace Theory and Integrated Information Theory are both wrong about consciousness, but at least they make testable predictions, unlike pure functionalism." The thesis reveals this critic has already lost. By accepting "makes testable predictions" as a criterion of theoretical virtue, they've endorsed the view that consciousness must leave causal traces. But that's not a neutral methodological standard—it's precisely the functionalist claim that consciousness has a characteristic causal profile.

**The objection's examples actually prove the thesis.** It says epiphenomenalists "openly acknowledge their view makes consciousness empirically undetectable." Exactly! And this is why epiphenomenalism is dismissed as unscientific by the methodological consensus the thesis targets. The trap isn't that epiphenomenalists secretly accept empirical testability—it's that the broader community treats empirical testability as the legitimacy criterion, thereby ruling out epiphenomenalism not by argument but by methodological fiat.

The thesis identifies a genuine question-begging: the community demands theories meet an empirical standard that presupposes consciousness has the causal structure that's supposed to be under investigation. It's not "redescription"—it's exposing how a methodological requirement smuggles in a substantive metaphysical commitment.

## VERDICT

The defence fails, but not for the reason the objection claims.

The problem is that the thesis overclaims about who falls into this trap. The objection is right that many non-functionalists **explicitly reject** empirical methodology for consciousness rather than unwittingly accepting it. Chalmers, for instance, openly distinguishes the "easy problems" (empirically tractable) from the "hard problem" (not empirically tractable in the standard way). He doesn't accept then violate the empirical criterion—he denies it applies to phenomenal consciousness.

But the thesis **does** identify something real: there exists a middle group of theorists who criticize functionalism while simultaneously insisting their alternative theories are empirically superior. These theorists—perhaps some higher-order thought theorists or certain predictive processing advocates who claim anti-functionalist credentials—do fall into the trap. They want to reject functionalism while keeping empirical respectability, not seeing that the latter presupposes the former.

The thesis dies not from triviality but from **overgeneralization**. It targets a "methodological consensus" too broad to sustain the claim. The trap exists, but it catches fewer theorists than claimed—only those who try to have it both ways, not the consistent anti-functionalists who explicitly reject empirical adequacy as the standard.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis equivocates on "functionalism" between two readings, and the argument's force requires sliding between them.

**Reading 1 (Narrow):** Functionalism as a specific metaphysical thesis that mental states are constituted by their functional/causal roles—the view associated with Putnam, Lewis, and standard philosophy of mind debates. On this reading, the thesis is interesting: it would be a genuine discovery that demanding empirical testability presupposes this controversial metaphysical position.

**Reading 2 (Broad):** Functionalism as merely the claim that consciousness has *some* systematic causal relations with observable phenomena—that it "does something" detectably. On this reading, the thesis is plausible: empirical methodology does indeed assume consciousness isn't causally inert.

**The equivocation:** The thesis needs the narrow reading to be interesting (claiming that empirical methodology smuggles in a specific, controversial metaphysical theory) but needs the broad reading to be plausible (since empirical methodology really does assume causal detectability). 

The argument works by suggesting that accepting "consciousness has characteristic causal profiles" (broad—just meaning detectable patterns) commits you to "functionalism" (narrow—the constitutive metaphysical thesis). But a dualist could accept that consciousness has causal signatures without accepting that it's *constituted by* functional roles. The thesis conflates having causal effects with being nothing-but-causal-role.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This significantly damages the thesis. The argument appears to reveal methodological question-begging only by conflating a minimal causal assumption (consciousness does something observable) with a maximal metaphysical claim (consciousness is functional organization). Once distinguished, the supposed "smuggling" disappears.

**DEFENDER** — ## BEST DEFENCE

The objection's distinction between narrow and broad functionalism actually **strengthens** rather than weakens the thesis, because it exposes how the supposed "minimal" assumption does more work than advertised.

**The broad reading isn't actually minimal—it's already substantive.** Consider what "consciousness has systematic causal relations with observable phenomena" really entails. Not just that consciousness sometimes affects behavior (even epiphenomenalists might grant occasional correlation), but that it has **reliable, lawlike, investigable** causal patterns—the kind that let us design experiments, make predictions, and distinguish theories. This isn't a thin methodological assumption; it's a thick metaphysical commitment.

**The objection's own dualist example proves the point.** It says "a dualist could accept that consciousness has causal signatures without accepting that it's constituted by functional roles." But what dualist? Not an epiphenomenalist (consciousness causes nothing). Not a panpsychist who thinks consciousness is fundamental and ubiquitous (no characteristic profile distinguishes conscious from non-conscious systems). The only dualists who can accept systematic causal profiles are **interactionist** dualists—and interactionism is precisely the view that consciousness occupies a functional role in the causal economy, even if it's not metaphysically reducible to that role.

**The supposed equivocation is actually a revealing entailment.** The thesis claims that accepting the broad reading (systematic causal detectability) commits you to something very close to the narrow reading (functional-role metaphysics). Why? Because if consciousness has systematic, reliable causal relations that we can investigate empirically, then it occupies a specifiable position in the causal structure of cognition—and that's what it means to have a functional role. You might add "and it has intrinsic properties beyond this role" (property dualism), but you've already conceded the functionalist's core claim: consciousness is individuated by its place in causal patterns.

The thesis isn't equivocating—it's arguing that the "broad" and "narrow" readings collapse into each other once you take seriously what systematic empirical testability requires.

## VERDICT

The defence fails. The objection's distinction holds.

The problem is that **having a causal role is not the same as being constituted by that role**, and this difference matters even for systematic, lawlike causal relations. The defence tries to collapse this distinction but cannot.

Consider property dualism of the kind Chalmers defends: phenomenal properties are fundamental, irreducible properties that nonetheless have systematic causal effects on behavior and cognition via psychophysical laws. This view accepts that consciousness has characteristic, empirically investigable causal profiles (satisfying the "broad" requirement) while denying that consciousness is nothing but its functional role (rejecting the "narrow" claim). The phenomenal properties have an intrinsic nature beyond their causal role—that's precisely what makes it dualism rather than functionalism.

The defence's claim that "occupying a functional role" equals "being individuated by functional role" is exactly the move the objection identifies as equivocation. Interactionist dualists accept consciousness plays a causal role; they deny it's **only** that role. This is a coherent position that accepts empirical testability without accepting functionalist metaphysics.

The thesis dies because the supposed smuggling evaporates once we distinguish:
- **Causal efficacy**: consciousness has systematic effects (required for empirical testability)
- **Functional constitution**: consciousness is nothing but its causal role (functionalism proper)

Accepting the first doesn't smuggle in the second. The thesis needed them to be inseparable but they come apart.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis protects itself by strategically redefining what counts as "functionalism" to make the charge of "smuggling" unfalsifiable. 

Whenever someone accepts empirical testability, the thesis declares they've "smuggled in functionalism." When critics point out that non-functionalists like property dualists can accept causal efficacy without accepting functional constitution, the thesis responds by expanding "functionalism" to include anyone who thinks consciousness has systematic causal profiles—thereby redefining the target to ensure it hits.

But when pressed on whether this makes the claim trivial (since empirical science by definition studies causal relations), the thesis contracts "functionalism" back to the narrow metaphysical doctrine to preserve interestingness. The term becomes a moving target: broad enough to catch anyone doing empirical consciousness science, narrow enough to seem like a substantive discovery.

The unfalsifiability appears in the dialectic: What would show the thesis wrong? If property dualists accept empirical testability while rejecting functionalism, the thesis replies "they've accepted something close enough to functionalism." If they explicitly distinguish causal role from constitution, the thesis insists the distinction "collapses under scrutiny." Every apparent counterexample—theorists who embrace empirical methods while denying functionalist metaphysics—gets redefined as actually confirming the thesis by revealing "hidden functionalist commitments."

The thesis cannot lose because it controls the meaning of its central term to ensure any engagement with empirical methodology counts as "smuggling functionalism."

STRENGTH: 3/5

This is a genuine problem requiring an answer. The thesis does seem to adjust what "functionalism" means to protect its core claim, making it difficult to specify what evidence would refute it. However, it's not completely unfalsifiable—one could potentially show specific theorists maintaining both empirical rigor AND a clear distinction between causal efficacy and functional constitution over sustained argument.

**DEFENDER** — ## BEST DEFENCE

The objection mistakes **diagnostic flexibility** for unfalsifiable equivocation. The thesis isn't arbitrarily redefining functionalism to win arguments—it's tracking a genuine conceptual entanglement that appears at different levels of explicitness.

**The thesis makes a falsifiable empirical claim about the consciousness science community.** It claims there exists a "methodological consensus" that treats empirical distinguishability as the gold standard for theory choice. This is straightforwardly falsifiable: survey the major consciousness science journals, conferences, and funding priorities. If the field actually treats non-empirically-testable theories as legitimate contenders, the thesis is wrong. But the evidence supports it: theories get traction by offering experimental paradigms (GWT, IIT, HOT, recurrent processing theory), and purely philosophical proposals without empirical handles are marginalized as "not science."

**What would actually refute the thesis?** A clear case of: (1) a theory that denies consciousness has systematic causal profiles characteristic enough to generate distinctive predictions, (2) which is nonetheless treated as empirically legitimate by the consciousness science community, (3) where this legitimacy isn't parasitic on some functionalist-friendly auxiliary hypotheses. The thesis predicts this won't exist—not because it's rigged definitions, but because the methodological commitment really does presuppose the metaphysical claim.

**The "moving target" charge misreads the dialectical structure.** The thesis isn't saying "anyone who studies consciousness empirically is a functionalist" (trivial). It's saying "the methodological demand for empirical distinguishability presupposes that consciousness has the kind of causal structure functionalists claim it has" (substantive). When defenders point out this entailment holds even for property dualists who accept causal efficacy, that's not moving goalposts—it's showing the entailment is **broader** than initially apparent. The thesis wins bigger, not by changing terms, but by showing more positions fall on the functionalist side of the divide than their proponents realize.

**The objection's own example proves the point.** It suggests showing "theorists maintaining both empirical rigor AND a clear distinction between causal efficacy and functional constitution" would refute the thesis. But this is precisely what the thesis denies is possible! If you maintain empirical rigor—designing experiments, making predictions, distinguishing theories by their observable consequences—you've already committed to consciousness having a systematic causal profile. And having such a profile just is occupying a functional role, even if you insist there's "something more" to consciousness. The property dualist's "intrinsic nature beyond the causal role" becomes empirically idle—present in their metaphysics but absent from their science.

The thesis is falsifiable: show me a non-functionalist theory that the empirical consciousness science community treats as legitimate without that theory making functionalist-style predictions. The reason this seems impossible isn't that the thesis rigs the game, but that the game is rigged from the start by methodological commitments the field doesn't acknowledge.

## VERDICT

The defence fails. The thesis is unfalsifiable in practice, though not quite for the reason the objection claims.

The problem isn't that the thesis **arbitrarily** redefines functionalism—it's that it defines functionalism so broadly that the supposed "smuggling" becomes **undetectable even in principle**. The defence's own formulation reveals this: "having such a profile just is occupying a functional role, even if you insist there's 'something more' to consciousness."

This makes the charge of "smuggling" empty. If accepting that consciousness has systematic causal relations **just is** accepting functionalism (properly understood), then there's no smuggling—it's an open equation. The metaphor of "smuggling" implies something hidden or unacknowledged, but the defence admits the equation is explicit once properly analyzed.

**The deeper problem:** The thesis needs non-functionalists to be **confused** about their own commitments—to think they're rejecting functionalism while actually accepting it. But the defence's property dualist accepts exactly what they claim to accept: consciousness has causal effects (empirically tractable) and intrinsic properties beyond those effects (metaphysically non-functionalist). The thesis insists the second part is "empirically idle," but that's not confusion—that's the **explicit position**. Property dualists know their phenomenal properties don't show up in the functional story; that's why they're dualists.

The unfalsifiability appears here: any theorist who distinguishes causal role from constitution gets told "the distinction collapses" or "the non-causal part is idle." But this just restates the thesis rather than engaging the counterexample. The property dualist says "I accept empirical testability for the causal profile while maintaining consciousness has intrinsic properties beyond that profile." The thesis replies "then you've accepted functionalism about what's empirically accessible." But the dualist never denied that! They explicitly restrict functionalism to the empirical domain while maintaining something beyond it.

The thesis dies because it cannot distinguish between:
- **Smuggling**: covertly presupposing what you claim to reject
- **Restricting**: openly limiting a doctrine's scope while rejecting its universal application

Property dualists don't smuggle in functionalism—they openly accept it for the empirical domain while denying it exhausts consciousness. That's not a trap; it's a deliberate, explicit position. The thesis has no way to show this is confused rather than simply a different view, making its central charge of "smuggling" unfalsifiable.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis is a standard Chalmersian move about the limits of empirical methodology when applied to consciousness, dressed up as a novel dialectical observation. Chalmers' distinction between "easy problems" (functional/causal questions amenable to empirical investigation) and the "hard problem" (why there's something it's like, not reducible to causal structure) already establishes that empirical methodology captures only the functional profile of consciousness, not phenomenal consciousness itself.

The supposed revelation that "demanding empirical testability presupposes functionalist assumptions" is precisely Chalmers' point in arguing that no amount of empirical evidence about neural correlates or functional organization can close the explanatory gap. When the thesis claims that "non-functionalists who accept empirical distinguishability have already conceded the game," this just restates Chalmers' argument that empirical methods are inherently limited to the easy problems.

The thesis presents as a discovery about methodological question-begging what is actually the established anti-functionalist position: empirical science can study consciousness's causal role but cannot capture its intrinsic phenomenal nature. This isn't exposing a hidden trap in the consciousness science community—it's rehearsing the standard hard-problem argument that the community has been explicitly debating for thirty years. The position is "already taken" by Chalmers (1995, 1996) and the entire subsequent literature on the explanatory gap.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This seriously damages the thesis's claim to novelty. The core insight—that empirical methodology presupposes causal/functional structure—is the foundational premise of the most influential anti-functionalist position in contemporary consciousness studies. The thesis cannot be a revealing discovery when it restates the explicit starting point of the hard problem literature.

**DEFENDER** — ## BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies the thesis's contribution by conflating **what Chalmers argues about consciousness** with **what the thesis argues about methodology and its practitioners**.

**Chalmers makes a metaphysical claim; the thesis makes a sociological-dialectical one.** Yes, Chalmers argues that empirical methods capture only functional profiles and miss phenomenal consciousness. But the thesis isn't repeating this—it's observing that **the consciousness science community, including many who cite Chalmers approvingly, nonetheless structure their practice around empirical distinguishability as the criterion of theoretical legitimacy**. The thesis identifies a performative contradiction in the field's actual behavior.

**The "already taken" charge misses the target population.** The thesis doesn't target Chalmers or consistent hard-problem theorists who explicitly reject empirical adequacy as sufficient. It targets the much larger group who:
- Acknowledge the hard problem in their introductions
- Cite Chalmers respectfully 
- Then proceed to evaluate theories of consciousness (including supposedly anti-functionalist ones like HOT or certain predictive processing accounts) entirely by their empirical predictions
- Treat lack of empirical distinguishability as a legitimate criticism even of theories claiming to address phenomenal consciousness

**This is the smuggling the thesis identifies.** These theorists think they're being good Chalmersians by acknowledging the hard problem exists, while simultaneously operating with methodological standards that presuppose it doesn't—or at least that theories failing to address it empirically aren't worth pursuing. The thesis reveals that merely **acknowledging** the hard problem while **methodologically ignoring** it is not a stable position.

**The novelty lies in the dialectical diagnosis, not the metaphysical claim.** Chalmers tells us empirical methods have limits. The thesis tells us that people who claim to accept this nonetheless enforce methodological norms that deny it. That's the difference between a philosophical argument and an observation about how a field actually operates despite its stated commitments.

Consider: A researcher might say "Of course there's a hard problem that empirical methods can't solve" (Chalmersian acknowledgment) and then immediately add "but Theory X is better than Theory Y because it makes more testable predictions about consciousness" (functionalist methodology). The thesis identifies this as incoherent. Chalmers' argument is a resource for making this diagnosis, not the diagnosis itself.

## VERDICT

The defence fails, but reveals the thesis needs substantial reformulation to survive.

The problem is that **the thesis as stated doesn't clearly identify this sociological-dialectical target**. It claims to target "methodological consensus across the consciousness science community, including critics of functionalism," but doesn't specify the particular incoherence the defence now identifies: people who **acknowledge** the hard problem while **methodologically presupposing** it's soluble.

**The defence's reformulation would be a different (and better) thesis:** "Consciousness researchers who acknowledge the hard problem nonetheless evaluate theories using criteria that presuppose it doesn't exist, creating a performative contradiction in the field's practice." This identifies a genuine inconsistency in how the community operates.

But this reformulated thesis faces an immediate problem: **Is the inconsistency real or apparent?** A sophisticated Chalmersian could coherently say:
- "Empirical methods can't solve the hard problem (metaphysical claim)
- But they CAN distinguish better from worse functional theories (methodological claim)  
- And we should prefer theories that get the functional story right, even if that doesn't exhaust consciousness (pragmatic claim)"

This position accepts empirical distinguishability as a criterion for comparing theories **within the domain where empirical methods apply** while denying that domain is exhaustive. There's no contradiction here—just a division of labor between empirical investigation (for functional profiles) and philosophical argument (for phenomenal properties).

**The thesis dies because it cannot show this division of labor is illegitimate rather than sensible.** The supposed "smuggling" evaporates once we distinguish:
- Using empirical testability to evaluate functional theories of consciousness (legitimate)
- Claiming empirical testability exhausts what matters about consciousness (functionalist overreach)

The consciousness science community can consistently do the first while denying the second. Chalmers himself does this: he evaluates empirical theories of awareness, attention, and reportability by their predictions while insisting they don't capture phenomenal consciousness. That's not smuggling functionalism—it's explicitly restricting empirical methods to their proper domain.

The thesis needed to show that accepting empirical criteria for functional theories commits you to functionalism about consciousness as such. But the Chalmersian framework—far from being "already taken" in a way that undermines the thesis—actually provides the resources to **defeat** the thesis by showing how to use empirical methods without functionalist metaphysics. The thesis is not redundant with Chalmers; it's **refuted** by the coherence of Chalmers' position.

</details>

### U1. The search for consciousness's neural correlates systematically conflates the scientific question 'What realizes consciousness?' with the metaphysical question 'What grounds consciousness?', and this conflation makes empirical findings appear more metaphysically significant than they are.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The implicit assumption shared across consciousness science that discovering NCCs will reveal consciousness's nature, held by both empirical researchers and theorists
- **Sketch:** When neuroscientists identify neural correlates of consciousness (NCCs), they typically move between two questions: the scientific question of what physical systems instantiate consciousness in creatures like us, and the metaphysical question of what makes any system conscious at all. But these come apart. Finding that C-fibers realize pain in humans doesn't tell us whether pain is identical to C-fiber firing, whether C-fibers ground pain, or whether C-fibers merely happen to be what implements pain in our particular architecture. Similarly, finding that recurrent processing correlates with consciousness doesn't reveal whether recurrence is essential to consciousness, is identical to consciousness, or just happens to be how our brains implement it. Empirical NCC research answers realization questions; metaphysical theories of consciousness (IIT, GWT, HOT, etc.) make grounding claims. The distinction shows why finding NCCs never settles theoretical debates—the evidence is addressing a different question.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Realization vs. grounding distinction is important but the conflation is widely recognized in the literature.

### U2. The reason no proposed test settles which consciousness theory is correct is that consciousness theories are individuated by their bridge principles, not by their empirical predictions, and bridge principles cannot be empirically tested.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The shared assumption that consciousness theories are primarily empirical hypotheses that make different predictions, held across the debate
- **Sketch:** Current discussions assume theories differ in their empirical predictions about behavior, reports, or neural activity. But the real difference between theories lies in their bridge principles—their claims about what neural/functional structures constitute or realize consciousness. IIT and GWT might make identical predictions about all observable behavior and even neural activity, differing only in what they say that activity amounts to phenomenally. The key premise is that bridge principles (claims about constitution/realization) are metaphysical posits that can't be confirmed by observing their relata separately. We can test whether integrated information correlates with reports, but not whether it constitutes experience. This isn't a problem unique to consciousness—it affects all special science bridge principles—but it's especially acute here.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Bridge principles being untestable is correct but this is a standard point about theoretical terms.

---

### U3. Treating first-person reports as evidence commits you to either treating consciousness as having vague boundaries (which all major theories deny) or to rejecting the reliability of reports at precisely the cases that matter for theory testing.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Scientific consciousness researchers who rely on report-based methodology while maintaining that consciousness has determinate boundaries
- **Sketch:** All empirical tests of consciousness theories rely on subjects reporting their experiences. But reports are unreliable precisely in the cases that distinguish theories: reports about rapid masked stimuli (GWT vs. local recurrence), about gradual neural changes (testing thresholds), about unusual states (anesthesia, locked-in syndrome). Either we trust reports, in which case we must accept that consciousness has vague or fuzzy boundaries (since reports are equivocal in these cases), or we dismiss borderline reports as unreliable, in which case we lose the data that would distinguish theories. The key premise is that the cases where theories make divergent predictions are systematically the cases where reportability breaks down. Current theorists want sharp boundaries to consciousness (you're conscious or not) but also want to use report-based evidence, which pushes toward vague boundaries.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Report reliability at borderline cases is a real problem but the dilemma is fairly obvious.

---

### U4. First-person methodology for testing consciousness theories is coherent only if we presuppose the falsity of all dissociative theories (theories on which consciousness comes apart from its functional/behavioral effects).

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Theorists who believe first-person report methodology is neutral between competing theories, particularly those running psychophysical experiments on consciousness
- **Sketch:** Any first-person test requires subjects to report on their consciousness, but reporting is a functional/behavioral capacity. If consciousness can dissociate from report-capacity (as in dissociative theories like higher-order thought theory, or certain interpretations of global workspace theory during access-failures), then subjects might have consciousness they cannot report, or report consciousness they lack. This means using reports as evidence already assumes consciousness doesn't dissociate from report-capacity—which is precisely what's at stake between associative and dissociative theories. The methodological move commits you to a substantive theoretical position. The key premise: evidential practices have theoretical presuppositions that can contradict the theories they're meant to test.
- **Appraiser (blind):** That report-based methodology presupposes associative theories is true but the circularity is predictable.

---

### U5. The search for 'the' correct theory of consciousness assumes consciousness has uniform metaphysical joints across all instances, but if consciousness is a Wittgensteinian family resemblance concept, then different theories are correct for different consciousness-clusters with no general fact of the matter.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The assumption shared across the debate that consciousness is a natural kind with a single correct theory
- **Sketch:** We assume phenomenal consciousness in humans, echolocation in bats, potential sentience in octopi, and possible machine consciousness are all instances of one phenomenon requiring one theory. But the key premise is that these might share no common essence—only overlapping features like human/bat phenomenality shares sensory integration, while human/AI consciousness might share information integration without phenomenality. On this view, HOT might be correct for human self-consciousness, IIT for certain unified experiences, and functionalism for potential machine consciousness. The dispute appears substantive but is actually about which consciousness-cluster we're theorizing about. We've been seeking a single theory for what are actually distinct phenomena.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Family resemblance framing is reasonable but applying Wittgenstein to consciousness is not particularly novel.

---

### U6. All proposed methods for adjudicating between consciousness theories rely on an asymmetry between the theorist's access to their own consciousness and their access to others', but this asymmetry vanishes in both directions: we cannot use first-personal certainty as a calibration point because self-knowledge of consciousness is itself theory-laden.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** Both phenomenologists who privilege first-person data and physicalists who use personal consciousness as a test case
- **Sketch:** The standard picture: I'm certain I'm conscious, so test theories against whether they'd attribute consciousness to me; extend from there. The counterexample premise is that introspection doesn't deliver theory-neutral data—whether you report 'raw feels' or 'brain states' depends on your background theory. Meditators report no-self; HOT theorists report higher-order thoughts; naive subjects report qualia. Even the supposedly incorrigible 'I am conscious now' depends on what you count as consciousness. If first-personal access is theory-laden, we can't use it to calibrate theories, but all proposed adjudication methods depend on this calibration. We're left with no Archimedean point.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Theory-ladenness of introspection is well-established; applying it to calibration is competent but incremental.

---

### U7. The consciousness debate treats first-person and third-person evidence asymmetrically—demanding that first-person reports answer to third-person theory, never vice-versa—but this asymmetry cannot be justified without begging the question against phenomenal concepts being autonomous.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The methodological consensus shared across competing theories that third-person neuroscience constrains first-person phenomenology rather than vice-versa
- **Sketch:** The argument draws a distinction between two possible evidential hierarchies: (1) phenomenal reports are treated as raw data that successful theories must systematize and potentially correct, or (2) phenomenal judgments are treated as theory-laden but authoritative, and third-person theories must answer to them. Current practice uniformly assumes (1): when subjects report experiences that don't fit IIT or GWT predictions, we suspect the reports are confused, not the theory. The key premise is that this assumption only makes sense if phenomenal concepts are reducible to or explicable in functional/physical terms—otherwise, the first-person perspective has autonomous epistemic authority. But using evidence that presumes reducibility to test theories of consciousness produces circular reasoning: we're using the success of physicalist theories to justify treating first-person reports as corrigible, while using the corrigibility of first-person reports to claim physicalist theories are successful. The distinction reveals that methodological naturalists and phenomenal concept strategists should be using radically different evidential standards.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Asymmetry between first-person and third-person evidence is real but the circularity charge is standard.

---

### U8. The intractability of consciousness theory selection reveals that our epistemic access to consciousness has the structure of testimonial knowledge, not observational knowledge.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared assumption across theories that introspection provides a 'gold standard' or 'ground truth' about phenomenal character, even if third-person methods face epistemic barriers
- **Sketch:** When I introspect my phenomenal states, I'm not directly observing consciousness itself but receiving a report from a cognitive subsystem whose reliability I cannot independently verify—just as with testimony. This explains why consciousness science faces unique methodology problems that don't plague other hidden-entity sciences: in physics, we can triangulate unobservables through multiple independent measurement methods, but consciousness 'reports' come through a single, potentially unreliable channel (introspection) whose accuracy we cannot check against the phenomenon itself. The key premise is that the asymmetry between first-person and third-person methods isn't about privacy or subjectivity per se, but about the unavailability of calibration: we can never catch consciousness 'in the act' independent of the introspective report to verify the report's accuracy. This reframes the hard problem as fundamentally an epistemic reliability problem rather than an explanatory gap.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Testimonial knowledge framing is interesting but the reliability problem it identifies is familiar from other discussions.

---

### U9. Illusionism is unstable because the error theory it requires—explaining why we're systematically wrong about phenomenal consciousness—ends up having to posit states with exactly the functional profile illusionists deny exists.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Illusionists like Frankish, Dennett, and Graziano who think we can explain away phenomenal consciousness while preserving our cognitive science
- **Sketch:** Illusionists like Frankish and Dennett must explain why humans are so convinced there's something it's like to see red, feel pain, etc. Their explanations invoke metacognitive states, introspective models, or self-representational dispositions that seem informationally rich, persistent, and functionally integrated in exactly the ways that make realists think those states ARE phenomenally conscious. The key premise is that any cognitive mechanism sophisticated enough to generate the powerful, stable illusion of phenomenality will itself meet the functionalist criteria for actually being phenomenal. The illusion-generating mechanism collapses into being the phenomenon.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Illusionism instability is a known objection; this formulation doesn't add substantial new insight.

---

### U10. The Hard Problem functions as an empirical prediction that consciousness theories making third-person predictions will inevitably fail—and it's now falsifiable because some theories make checkable predictions.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** Chalmers and those who think the Hard Problem is a permanent conceptual barrier independent of empirical progress
- **Sketch:** Chalmers argues the Hard Problem shows no amount of functional/structural explanation bridges the explanatory gap. But this entails that theories attempting such explanations should fail their own empirical tests. The key premise is that when IIT predicts phi-0 systems aren't conscious or GWT predicts unconscious processing lacks global broadcasting, these are third-person structural predictions. If experiments validate these predictions consistently and comprehensively—if unconscious processing always lacks predicted features and conscious processing always has them—then consciousness systematically correlates with structure in exactly the way the Hard Problem says it shouldn't. The Hard Problem predicts these correlations should be loose or break down.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Hard Problem as falsifiable prediction is creative but the empirical validation claim is questionable.

---

### U11. The Unconscious Inference Commitment: Any theory that denies consciousness to some neural processes while attributing it to others commits itself to a boundary that makes consciousness explanatorily idle for those processes, thereby requiring that unconscious processes can accomplish everything conscious ones can—which undermines the explanatory motivation for positing consciousness at all.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Partial consciousness theories like GWT, higher-order theories, and attention-based accounts that restrict consciousness to a subset of neural activity
- **Sketch:** Take GWT: it says only globally broadcast information is conscious, but local processing is unconscious. This commits GWT to the claim that local processes perform representation, computation, and behavior guidance without consciousness. But if unconscious processing can do sophisticated cognition, what work does consciousness do? The theory must either say consciousness is epiphenomenal (undermining its claim to explain function) or specify something consciousness adds—but this 'extra' must be compatible with unconscious processes doing the same tasks, making the addition explanatorily suspect. The argument's key premise: drawing any consciousness boundary creates an adequacy proof for unconscious processing that threatens to swallow the conscious domain.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Identifies a real tension for boundary-drawing theories but needs sharper development of the explanatory idleness claim.

---

### U12. Consciousness theories are empirically decidable only when they make incompatible predictions about cases where subjects disagree with themselves about whether they are conscious.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared assumption that neural correlates of consciousness provide the primary empirical test bed for consciousness theories
- **Sketch:** The standard framing treats the hard problem as asking what theories predict about third-person observation of neural correlates. But neural correlates are compatible with multiple theories (mere correlation, constitutive realization, etc.). The decisive cases are temporal: split-brain subjects who simultaneously report contradictory phenomenal states, subjects emerging from anesthesia who give conflicting retrospective reports about the transition period, or binocular rivalry cases where subjects reverse their earlier claims about what they experienced. These cases force theories to take incompatible stands on whether a single physical system can host contradictory consciousness claims, revealing that the real empirical bite comes from diachronic inconsistency in first-person reports rather than synchronic neural patterns.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The focus on diachronic self-disagreement cases is useful but the decisive empirical bite claim needs more support.

---

### U13. The only empirically decidable theories of consciousness are those that predict consciousness can be absent despite perfect functional isomorphism, but defending such theories commits you to rejecting the evidential value of the very experiments you propose.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Those who think consciousness could come apart from functional organization and that this claim is empirically testable through synthetic systems
- **Sketch:** Theories become empirically decidable when they permit dissociations: consciousness without function F, or function F without consciousness. The promising direction is building systems functionally identical to conscious beings but allegedly non-conscious (or vice versa). But here's the trap: if you build a functional isomorph of me and it reports 'I am conscious' in response to all probes, you can only maintain it lacks consciousness by rejecting verbal reports as evidence. Yet verbal reports are your only evidence that humans are conscious in the first place. You're forced to either accept that your created system is conscious (collapsing back into functionalism) or embrace solipsism about the original case. The empirical test thus requires you to reject the evidential basis that motivated the test.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The functional isomorph trap is real but the dilemma between rejecting reports and embracing functionalism is well-trodden.

---

### U14. The standard epistemology of consciousness research—that we need theories to explain the neural correlates—has it backwards: we can empirically decide between theories only if we already know which neural patterns are correlates, but knowing which are correlates requires already knowing which theory is true.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** The neural correlates of consciousness research program's assumption that NCCs provide theory-neutral evidence
- **Sketch:** Researchers assume we can observe that neural pattern N reliably co-occurs with consciousness, then ask which theory best explains this correlation. But determining that N genuinely correlates with consciousness (rather than with attention, wakefulness, or reportability) already requires a theoretical commitment about what consciousness is. When we measure the neural correlates of visual awareness during binocular rivalry, we presuppose that 'awareness' means what subjects report, not what's available for global broadcast, not integrated information, etc. Each theory offers different criteria for when genuine consciousness is present versus merely its cognitive aftermath. The supposed theory-neutral data are actually theory-laden. We cannot bootstrap from 'neutral observations' to theory choice because identifying which observations are relevant requires the theory we're trying to test. This makes the empirical project circular in a way that undermines claims that evidence could decide between theories.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The theory-ladenness of observation is well-established; applying it to NCCs is competent but not novel enough to reframe debates.
