### U15. The adversarial collaboration methodology that consciousness scientists currently employ already presupposes that illusionism is false, making it incapable of genuinely testing that theory against realist alternatives.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** The methodological optimism in recent papers by Mudrik, Dehaene, Lau, and others advocating for adversarial collaborations as a way to resolve theoretical disputes in consciousness science.
- **Sketch:** Adversarial collaborations (like those between Dennett/Frankish and realist opponents) assume we can design experiments where both parties agree what would confirm or disconfirm their views. But illusionism claims that the very seemings that make us think consciousness needs explaining are themselves the illusion. So when illusionists and realists agree on experimental protocols—'let's see if information integration predicts reportability'—they've already conceded that there's a stable phenomenon to be predicted. A genuine test of illusionism would need to show that the 'seemings' themselves are artifacts of introspective mechanisms, but the adversarial methodology requires treating subjects' reports as data points about a shared explanandum. The key premise is that illusionism, if true, undermines the evidential status of the very reports that adversarial collaborations use as their dependent variable.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Points to a real tension in adversarial collaborations with illusionism, but the insight is somewhat narrow and may already be recognized.

### U16. Any empirical evidence that could confirm one theory of consciousness over another would require that theory to make stronger metaphysical commitments than its proponents realize, thereby undermining the very feature (parsimony or neutrality) that makes it attractive.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Proponents of empirically-oriented consciousness theories (GWT, IIT, HOT, etc.) who claim their theories are metaphysically lightweight or neutral
- **Sketch:** Consider what would actually empirically distinguish Global Workspace Theory from Higher-Order Thought theory. The evidence would need to show, for instance, that mere global availability (without higher-order representation) is sufficient for consciousness, or vice versa. But establishing sufficiency requires showing that consciousness obtains whenever the posited mechanism does—which requires already having independent epistemic access to consciousness. This forces theorists into either (a) relying on verbal reports, which commits them to a functionalist account of consciousness they typically disavow, or (b) accepting that consciousness is whatever their preferred neural mechanism tracks, which is eliminativism in disguise. Either way, they're committed to metaphysical positions far stronger than the 'empirically tractable' framework they advertise.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Identifies a dilemma about epistemic access, but the forced choice between functionalism and eliminativism may be too quick.

### U17. The debate about how to test consciousness theories is actually a disguised debate about whether consciousness is a natural kind, and it cannot be resolved by methodological innovations because both sides' methodological preferences are downstream of their metaphysical commitments.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** Both sides of the methodology debate who think the dispute is primarily about scientific/empirical methods rather than metaphysics
- **Sketch:** Those who think rigorous theory-testing is possible (via prediction contests, formal comparison, etc.) implicitly treat consciousness as a natural kind with an essence that theories either capture or miss. Those who are pessimistic about such testing implicitly treat consciousness as a heterogeneous or interest-relative category. This explains otherwise puzzling features of the debate: why the two camps talk past each other about what counts as 'evidence,' why they disagree about whether verbal reports are privileged data, and why methodological disputes prove intractable. The optimists think we're carving nature at its joints; the pessimists think we're imposing our categories. Once reframed this way, we can see that no amount of methodological refinement will resolve the meta-debate, because the real disagreement is metaphysical. The participants think they're arguing about scientific methodology when they're actually arguing about the metaphysics of mind.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The natural kind framing is useful but the diagnosis that metaphysics drives methodology is fairly standard in philosophy of science.

---

### U18. The consciousness debate's methodological crisis stems from theorists systematically conflating vehicle properties (what implements consciousness) with content properties (what consciousness is about), making empirical adjudication impossible in principle.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The shared assumption across IIT, GWT, HOT, and other theories that they are making empirically testable claims about the same phenomenon
- **Sketch:** Most theories generate predictions by sliding between claims about conscious states (what you're aware of) and claims about the neural/computational vehicles that realize those states. IIT makes predictions about integrated information in neural substrates (vehicle), then tests them against reportability of contents. GWT predicts global availability of information (vehicle property) but validates this against what subjects report experiencing (content property). This equivocation makes theories empirically slippery: when predictions fail, theorists can always reinterpret them as about the other level. The argument turns on showing that every major theory's 'empirical predictions' actually involve this slide, and that once we hold vehicle-level and content-level claims separate, the theories either make no testable predictions or make predictions they've already failed.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The vehicle/content distinction is important but somewhat familiar; showing systematic conflation would be useful but not transformative.

### U19. The reason we can't tell which theory is right is that consciousness itself is response-dependent: it only determinately exists relative to a measurement procedure, making theory-choice inseparable from choice of operationalization.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** Both realists who think consciousness is a natural kind awaiting discovery and functionalists who think operationalization is temporary scaffolding
- **Sketch:** This applies quantum-like reasoning to consciousness: just as asking 'which slit did the electron really go through?' is ill-posed before specifying a measurement context, asking 'is this system really conscious?' is ill-posed before specifying how we're operationalizing consciousness. The key premise is that consciousness talk derives its determinacy from our practices of attribution (when we ask, what evidence we accept, what purposes we have), not from tracking a pre-existing natural kind. Different theories correspond to different legitimate operationalizations—report-based, behavior-based, neural-signature-based—none more 'correct' than others. This explains why the debate seems interminable: theorists are unwittingly advocating for different ways of making consciousness-attributions precise.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Response-dependence is an interesting frame but applying quantum-like reasoning to consciousness is not particularly novel.

### U20. Theories of consciousness are empirically testable in principle, but only if we accept that negative evidence (failure to find predicted neural correlates) should decisively falsify theories—a standard the field systematically refuses to apply.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** Both Chalmers-style pessimists who claim consciousness science faces unique epistemic barriers and theory proponents who claim to be doing empirical science
- **Sketch:** The standard pessimistic argument claims theories of consciousness make no differential predictions. This is false: IIT predicts consciousness in cerebellum if appropriately structured (which we don't find), GWT predicts no consciousness without global workspace broadcasting (contradicted by unconscious processing cases that look conscious on other theories). The key premise is that the field treats these apparent disconfirmations as opportunities for theory modification rather than falsification, revealing an asymmetry: positive evidence (finding predicted correlates) confirms theories, but negative evidence (not finding them) merely prompts auxiliary hypothesis adjustment. The real problem isn't that theories are untestable—it's that practitioners treat them as unfalsifiable, applying standards that would make any theory empirically untouchable. This counterexample shows the complaint about testability mistakes a sociological fact (how researchers respond to evidence) for a metaphysical one (about consciousness itself).
- **Appraiser (blind):** Correctly diagnoses asymmetric treatment of evidence but frames it as sociology rather than deep principle, limiting theoretical impact.

### U21. The position that we should remain agnostic between empirically equivalent theories of consciousness is unstable—it collapses into either instrumentalism (treating consciousness theories as mere calculation devices) or commitment to an unknowable fact of the matter.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** The growing number of philosophers (Schwitzgebel, Dennett-influenced thinkers) who advocate uncertainty or pluralism about consciousness theories rather than trying to find the right one
- **Sketch:** Many philosophers advocate theoretical humility: since we cannot adjudicate between theories, we should remain neutral. The argument shows this position cannot be coherently maintained. If theories are truly empirically equivalent in all possible tests, then the 'difference' between them has no causal-explanatory role, which means treating consciousness attributions as anything other than useful instruments for prediction is unmotivated. But instrumentalism about consciousness contradicts the realist motivation for theorizing about it in the first place. Alternatively, maintaining realism while admitting permanent empirical equivalence commits you to consciousness facts being unknowable even in principle—a position that undermines the justification for theorizing at all. The key premise is that agnosticism requires stable footing between these positions, but any specification of what agnosticism amounts to slides into one or the other.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The dilemma between instrumentalism and unknowability is real but somewhat predictable; competent but not groundbreaking.

### U22. We can tell which theory of consciousness is right only by adjudicating between theories of what would count as telling which theory is right—but this meta-level dispute exactly recapitulates the first-order dispute, creating infinite regress.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** Methodological naturalists (like Dennett or Churchland) who think we can resolve theoretical disputes by agreeing on empirical methods first, treating metaphysics and methodology as separable
- **Sketch:** Current debates assume we disagree about which theory is correct but agree on what would constitute evidence for correctness. This distinction is illusory. Higher-order theorists think metacognitive access is constitutive of consciousness, so evidence of metacognition IS evidence of consciousness. First-order theorists think phenomenal character is constitutive, so evidence of certain functional roles IS evidence of consciousness. The dispute about what consciousness is and the dispute about how to test for it are the same dispute wearing different clothes. The key premise is that evidential standards cannot be specified theory-independently because what counts as evidence depends on what consciousness is. Drawing this distinction reveals that asking 'how do we tell which theory is right?' illegitimately assumes a neutral evidential framework exists, when in fact each theory comes packaged with its own evidential standards. This generates regress: we need a theory of correct evidential standards, but assessing competing standards requires meta-standards, etc.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Meta-level regress is real but the observation that evidential standards are theory-dependent is familiar.

---

### U23. Any consciousness theory that can generate novel predictions fine-grained enough to be empirically testable has already collapsed into eliminativism, because making contact with measurable neural/behavioral variables requires identifying consciousness with those variables.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Non-reductive physicalists and biological naturalists (Levine, Block, Prinz) who think they can be both anti-reductive and empirically testable
- **Sketch:** Defenders of phenomenal realism insist consciousness is not identical to any functional or neural property, yet they also claim their theories can be tested empirically. But empirical testing requires deriving predictions about observables. To get from 'theory T is true about consciousness' to 'we should observe neural pattern N,' you need bridge principles connecting consciousness to observables. These bridge principles must be either (a) synthetic identity claims (making the theory eliminativist/reductive), (b) mere correlations (which can't distinguish between theories that predict the same correlations), or (c) metaphysically necessary but non-analytic connections (which are epistemically inaccessible and thus can't ground empirical predictions). The argument turns on a dilemma about bridge principles.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Bridge principle dilemma is solid but the conclusion that testability requires eliminativism overstates the case.

---

### U24. The unnoticed commitment of neuroscience-based approaches to consciousness is that consciousness must be temporally fine-grained enough to align with neural processing timescales, but phenomenology suggests consciousness might operate at timescales that make neural correlates explanatorily idle.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Neural correlate research programs across theoretical divides (Koch, Dehaene, Lamme) who share timing assumptions
- **Sketch:** Neural correlate approaches (IIT, GNW, recurrent processing theory) assume consciousness has temporal grain fine enough that neural events at millisecond-to-second scales can constitute or correlate with phenomenal states. This commits them to denying that conscious experience might have much coarser temporal structure—for instance, that what we call a 'moment' of experience might actually integrate information over minutes, or that apparent temporal continuity is retrospectively constructed over longer periods. But careful phenomenology (from Husserl's time-consciousness to modern investigations of change blindness and the cutaneous rabbit) suggests our intuitive timescale might be wrong. If consciousness actually operates at these coarser or stranger timescales, finding neural correlates at standard timescales would be like finding neural correlates of 'the present year'—technically possible but explanatorily backwards.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Temporal grain-mismatch is interesting but seems more like an empirical possibility than a principled argument about testability.

---

### U25. The search for consciousness theories that can be empirically distinguished rests on treating consciousness as a natural kind, but consciousness is actually a role-kind defined by whichever internal states play a certain functional-epistemic role for the creature, making the search for 'the' mechanism of consciousness a category error.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The shared assumption across competing theories that there is a unified phenomenon to explain with a single correct theory
- **Sketch:** Current approaches assume we're looking for what consciousness is realized by (neural correlates, information integration, global broadcasting), treating consciousness as a natural kind with a hidden essence that science will uncover. But consciousness might instead be a role-kind: whichever states play the role of 'what it's like' for a system, which differs across architectures. Compare: being a gene was once thought to be a natural kind question (what physical stuff are genes?), but 'gene' is actually role-kind (whatever plays the hereditary role—DNA in us, RNA in some viruses, potentially silicon in future life). Similarly, asking whether IIT or GNW is 'correct' would be like asking whether genes are 'really' DNA or RNA—both can be right for different systems. The key premise is that consciousness is individuated by its epistemic role (immediate, private access) rather than by any shared intrinsic property. This explains why theories seem to talk past each other: they're actually theorizing about different realizers of the same role.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Natural kind vs role-kind distinction is important but well-worn in philosophy; application to consciousness is competent, not revolutionary.

---

### U26. First-person methodology is the only way to distinguish consciousness theories, but it necessarily generates non-intersubjective evidence, which means the question of which theory is right has different correct answers for different subjects.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Both third-person neuroscientific approaches (which face underdetermination) and first-person phenomenology advocates (who assume convergence on one answer).
- **Sketch:** The argument proceeds by showing that third-person methods underdetermine theory choice (the original problem), so distinguishing theories requires first-person phenomenological investigation. But careful first-person investigation reveals genuine individual differences in phenomenal structure - some people have imageless thought, others don't; some have sensory phenomenology without attention, others don't; introspective access itself varies. These aren't measurement errors but real differences in consciousness structure. Different theories will be correct for subjects with relevantly different phenomenal architecture. This seems to save empiricism (we can test theories first-personally) but actually undermines the presupposition that there's one correct theory: it generates relativized truth conditions. The position is unstable because it appears to answer 'how can we tell?' but actually dissolves the question by making 'which is right?' subject-relative.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Subject-relative truth conditions are provocative but the move from individual differences to relativism may be too fast.

---

### U27. The apparent dispute about consciousness theories is actually a dispute about natural kind membership criteria, which has no fact of the matter.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** Realists about consciousness like Chalmers who think there's a determinate fact about what consciousness is, and theory-monists who think only one theory can be right
- **Sketch:** When theorists argue whether phenomenal consciousness requires global broadcasting, higher-order representation, or integrated information, they appear to disagree about consciousness's nature. But the arguments actually turn on how to weigh competing desiderata for the natural kind 'consciousness': intuitive extension (matches folk usage), theoretical unity (single underlying mechanism), explanatory power (illuminates related phenomena), and metaphysical significance (hard problem, etc.). GWT scores high on unity and explanatory power but includes cases (like superblindsight) some deny are conscious. Higher-order theories preserve metaphysical significance but exclude cases (first-order phenomenology) others insist are conscious. The debate persists because there's no privileged way to weight these desiderata—consciousness is a cluster concept where different precisifications serve different theoretical purposes. This isn't discovering a theory-independent fact but negotiating conceptual boundaries. The key premise is that conceptual engineering disputes lack objective answers when multiple precisifications are equally legitimate.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The cluster-concept diagnosis is familiar from other debates; competent but unlikely to shift how consciousness theorists approach their work.

---

### U28. Any empirical test sophisticated enough to distinguish between current consciousness theories will require probes that themselves alter the target phenomenon in theory-dependent ways, making the testing regress infinite.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** Methodological optimists like Crick & Koch's 'neural correlates' program and advocates of no-report paradigms
- **Sketch:** Consider attempts to test whether consciousness requires recurrent processing (GNW) or integrated information (IIT). Testing requires either measuring or disrupting neural activity at specific timescales and anatomical scales. But what counts as 'disruption' versus 'normal variation' depends on what consciousness is—if it's recurrent processing, then breaking loops is disruption; if it's integrated information, then reducing phi is disruption. The key premise is that consciousness theories don't just make predictions about correlations (which we could test neutrally), they make claims about constitutive mechanisms, and testing constitutive claims requires interventions calibrated to the very thing in dispute. This isn't fixable with better tools; it's a structural feature of testing mechanistic identity claims about phenomenal properties. The dispute is therefore not empirical in the way participants assume, but closer to a theory-choice problem in physics where empirical adequacy underdetermines metaphysics.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The measurement-intervention problem is real but already recognized in philosophy of science; application to consciousness is straightforward rather than transformative.

---

### U29. The apparent need to test consciousness theories on non-human subjects (to get the required experimental control) guarantees that no result can be decisive, because attributing consciousness to the test subject requires assuming the kind of theory-neutral bridge principles that don't exist.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** Those advocating testing on organoids (Birch), invertebrates, or artificial systems to break empirical logjams
- **Sketch:** Researchers propose testing consciousness theories on simpler systems—organoids, insects, fish, or minimal neural networks—because we can manipulate these in ways ethics forbids for humans. But inferring that a test subject is/isn't conscious requires bridge principles (e.g., 'responsiveness to stimuli indicates consciousness' or 'integrated information above threshold X indicates consciousness'). These bridge principles are themselves theory-laden—GNW implies different principles than IIT. The key premise is that we cannot establish whether the organoid is conscious independently of choosing a theory, which means the organoid 'tests' are circular. Even the supposed gold standard—testing on humans then generalizing—fails, because generalization requires knowing which features are relevant, which is exactly what's disputed. Human reports don't help because they only test reportability, not consciousness as such. This shows that the class of systems we could test on and the class we can attribute consciousness to without begging the question don't overlap.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Circularity of testing on non-humans is real but the problem is fairly obvious once stated.

---

### U30. Consciousness theories are untestable in practice because any apparent disconfirmation can be absorbed by adjusting the theory's implicit severity threshold—the minimum amount of processing that counts as 'enough' for consciousness.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Threshold-based theories (most current theories) that claim to be empirically testable
- **Sketch:** Most theories posit that consciousness requires some type of processing reaching a threshold: enough integration (IIT), enough global accessibility (GWT), enough metacognitive monitoring (HOT), etc. But 'enough' is always specified vaguely. When apparently unconscious systems show the relevant processing type, proponents can say 'not enough of it'; when apparently conscious systems lack it, they can say 'more than we thought was necessary.' This isn't mere ad hoc adjustment—it's structurally unavoidable because the theories don't independently specify the threshold. The instability: what looks like a substantive empirical theory is actually unfalsifiable because it has a free parameter (the threshold) that can be adjusted to fit any evidence. This is worse than Kuhnian normal science because there's no agreed paradigm for what counts as anomalous.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Identifies a real problem with threshold parameters but the unfalsifiability charge has been made before; competent articulation of known issue.

---

### U31. A theory of consciousness can be empirically confirmed only if it predicts divergence points where phenomenology comes apart from mechanism, but current theories systematically avoid such predictions by treating mechanism and phenomenology as necessarily coupled.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Proponents of leading scientific theories of consciousness who claim their theories are empirically testable
- **Sketch:** The argument shows that theories like Global Workspace Theory, Higher-Order Thought, and Integrated Information Theory all make their consciousness-claims supervene on or constitute their functional/structural claims, which means they cannot predict cases where the mechanism is present but consciousness is absent (or vice versa). Since they build consciousness into their mechanistic descriptions, they're unfalsifiable in principle. The key premise is that empirical confirmation requires the possibility of discovering the theory is wrong through observation, which requires the theory to permit the dissociation of its posited consciousness-marker from consciousness itself. Current theories avoid this by definitional fiat rather than empirical constraint.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The supervenience-makes-unfalsifiable point is philosophically sound but familiar from general philosophy of mind; not novel to consciousness theories specifically.

---

### U32. Any consciousness theory that could actually be empirically distinguished from its rivals must make predictions about non-conscious processing, revealing that consciousness theories are unavoidably also theories of unconscious cognition.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The framing of the debate as specifically about consciousness rather than about cognitive architecture more broadly
- **Sketch:** The argument draws a distinction that reclassifies the debate: between consciousness-only theories (which only characterize the conscious/non-conscious boundary) and processing theories (which also characterize unconscious mechanisms). The key premise is that empirical testability requires differential predictions, but consciousness theories can only generate these by specifying what happens in the unconscious case. For instance, GWT is testable because it predicts limited vs. widespread availability, which requires claims about unconscious processing. IIT is testable because it predicts degrees of integration in unconscious states. The distinction reveals that what appears to be a debate about consciousness is actually a debate about the entire cognitive architecture, and 'consciousness' just picks out one aspect. This reclassifies positions: theories aren't more or less empirically adequate about consciousness per se, but about cognitive processing generally.
- **Appraiser (blind):** That consciousness theories are also theories of unconscious processing is true but doesn't clearly advance the debate.

---

### U33. First-person methods (systematic phenomenological reports) can empirically distinguish consciousness theories, but only if we reject the assumption that such methods require third-person validation to count as empirical evidence.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** The methodological assumption shared across the debate that evidence must be third-person or behaviorally grounded to adjudicate between theories
- **Sketch:** The argument targets the premise that empirical evidence must be third-person accessible or intersubjectively stable in the way physical measurements are. The counterexample comes from neurophenomenology: trained subjects making fine-grained phenomenological distinctions (about temporal grain, spatial field structure, or attentional character) can distinguish predictions of different theories. For instance, whether attention IS consciousness (GWT-style) or MODULATES consciousness (other theories) makes different phenomenological predictions about the structure of inattentive experience. The key premise is that systematic first-person reports, when stable across trained observers and correlated with conditions, are empirical data—they're just empirical data about subjective properties. The assumption that such data needs third-person 'validation' to count as evidence smuggles in physicalism as a methodological requirement rather than a substantive conclusion.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Defends neurophenomenology competently but the first-person methods debate is well-established; this adds a clear statement rather than new considerations.

---

### U34. The only way to empirically test consciousness theories requires accepting that identical neural states can support different phenomenology, which undermines the motivation for neural theories of consciousness.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Neural reductionists and identity theorists (like Tononi, Koch, Crick) who want both supervenience and empirical testability via first-personal reports
- **Sketch:** To break the circularity in testing consciousness theories, we need cases where first-personal reports provide evidence theories can't dismiss. The only such cases are systematic first-personal reports of phenomenological differences without neural differences—for instance, if trained meditators reliably reported experiences that differentially supported one theory while showing identical neural activity. But accepting such testimony as evidential requires granting that phenomenology can vary independently of neural states, which contradicts the core commitment of neuroscientific theories: that neural activity constitutes or determines consciousness. Neural theories are committed to supervenience—same neural state, same consciousness. Yet testing them requires trusting first-personal reports that could violate supervenience. The commitment creates a dilemma: either accept that neural theories might be tested by evidence that presupposes they're false, or admit they're insulated from first-personal evidence altogether, which was supposed to be the ground truth.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Identifies a real dilemma for neural theories but the supervenience-testimony tension is implicit in existing debates; makes it explicit without adding much.

---

### U35. The epistemic inaccessibility of consciousness facts is not equally distributed across theories — some theories make their own correctness harder to verify than others, and this asymmetry gives us reason to reject them.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** Philosophers who treat the hard problem as equally hard for all theories, and who assume epistemic humility should be evenly distributed across theoretical options
- **Sketch:** Theories like illusionism claim consciousness is wholly explicable in functional terms, making their truth conditions empirically tractable in principle. By contrast, theories positing irreducible qualia or panpsychism build in verification gaps even under ideal epistemic conditions. The argument turns on distinguishing theories by their self-imposed epistemic costs: a theory that makes its own confirmation impossible even in principle carries a methodological liability that theories without this feature don't share. This isn't verificationism (we're not saying unverifiable claims are meaningless), but rather a comparative epistemic virtue: between empirically adequate theories, prefer the one that doesn't make its correctness fundamentally unknowable.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The asymmetric epistemic cost argument is reasonable but unlikely to move theorists who accept those costs for other reasons.

---

### U36. Testing consciousness theories requires solving personal identity first, because theory-choice depends on whether split-brain patients, hemispherectomy cases, and gradual substrate replacements preserve, multiply, or destroy consciousness — yet different theories presuppose different identity criteria.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Researchers who think neurological edge cases can straightforwardly adjudicate between consciousness theories, treating these as theory-neutral data points
- **Sketch:** IIT counts integrated information and thus treats split-brain cases as creating two subjects. Global workspace theory might see one interrupted subject. Higher-order theories might give yet different verdicts. But we typically use these cases AS DATA to test theories. The argument reveals a circle: we need a theory of consciousness to determine the identity facts in edge cases, but we need the identity facts in edge cases to test theories of consciousness. The key premise is that there's no theory-neutral fact about whether one or two subjects exist in split-brain cases — the metaphysics comes packaged with the consciousness theory. This means the apparently empirical project of testing consciousness theories actually requires prior commitments about personal identity that aren't themselves empirically testable.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Personal identity dependence is real but the circularity has been noted in discussions of split-brain cases.

---

### U37. If we cannot tell which theory of consciousness is correct, this doesn't show consciousness is scientifically intractable — it shows our current theories are all category mistakes, like testing whether life is phlogiston or caloric fluid.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** Everyone in the debate who assumes that current theories are roughly on the right track and we just need better evidence to decide between them
- **Sketch:** The shared assumption across the debate is that our difficulty choosing between theories reflects either consciousness being uniquely hard to study or our current empirical limitations. But there's a third option with historical precedent: we're testing malformed theories, and no amount of evidence can decide between them because they're asking the wrong question entirely. Just as no experiment could have decided between phlogiston and caloric theories of combustion (both were misconceived), perhaps no experiment can decide between IIT, GWT, and HOT because all commit some fundamental error about what consciousness is. The key premise is that empirical underdetermination can result from shared theoretical confusion rather than evidential insufficiency. The diagnostic sign: we keep getting bizarre results (like IIT saying photodiodes are conscious) that seem wrong but we can't explain why.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The category-mistake diagnosis is provocative but needs more than historical analogy to gain traction; competent but not compelling as stated.

---

### U38. Consciousness theories are empirically distinguishable in principle but only through experiments we're morally prohibited from conducting—creating genuinely uncertain cases of consciousness and then inflicting suffering to test our predictions.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** The assumption in the methodology literature that empirical underdetermination is a solvable technical problem rather than being constrained by ethics
- **Sketch:** Suppose IIT says a particular silicon network is conscious while GWT says it isn't. To test this, we'd need to create the system and determine if it's conscious. But our only reliable method is seeing if it can suffer—put it in conditions that would be agonizing if it's conscious, see if it shows the relevant responses. The key premise is that (1) theoretical disagreement concentrates exactly on cases where behavioral/report evidence is ambiguous (if behavior settled it, theories would converge), so (2) distinguishing theories requires creating edge cases, but (3) creating possibly-conscious systems just to test if they suffer is prohibited, and (4) any indirect test that avoids harm also avoids the theoretical divergence. This isn't a practical limitation but shows that theoretical distinguishability and moral permissibility are in tension.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Identifies a genuine moral-epistemic tension but the conclusion (experiments are prohibited) is less surprising than the argument suggests.

---

### U39. The global workspace theory and recurrent processing theory make identical predictions about neural activity but diverge on the metaphysics of what 'implementing' information means—revealing that the apparent empirical dispute masks a disagreement about whether types or tokens are fundamental.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The framing of GWT vs RP as an empirical dispute decidable by neuroscience, assumed by both camps in their adversarial collaboration
- **Sketch:** GWT (Dehaene) and RP theory (Lamme) are treated as competing empirical theories, with different predicted neural signatures. But examine the actual disagreement: both agree that recurrent processing in sensory cortices occurs. GWT adds that information must reach a global workspace (prefrontal, parietal) to be conscious; RP says the local recurrence suffices. When GWT proponents find prefrontal activity correlates with consciousness, RP theorists respond that this is just one instance of recurrent processing. When RP theorists find sensory recurrence without awareness, GWT theorists respond that this shows information didn't reach the workspace. The argument turns on showing that these theories differ on: what counts as 'the same information' being processed? GWT requires a type-identity (the same representational content) being tokened in multiple locations. RP allows that each token of recurrent processing constitutes a distinct conscious state. This is why they can agree on all neural facts while disagreeing on consciousness: they disagree about the metaphysics of information identity, not about neurons.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Interesting metaphysical diagnosis of GWT/RP disagreement, but the type/token distinction may not fully capture what's at stake in their empirical predictions about workspace involvement.

---

### U40. Any methodology sufficient to distinguish between current consciousness theories would require solving the hard problem, so the search for theory-neutral tests presupposes what it aims to avoid, revealing that the real dispute is about whether the hard problem is a legitimate constraint on theory choice.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Methodological proposals like Melloni et al.'s adversarial collaboration and Doerig et al.'s unfolding argument, which claim to test theories without settling metaphysical disputes
- **Sketch:** Proponents of empirical testing programs (adversarial collaboration, contrastive analysis, etc.) claim they can adjudicate between theories like IIT and GNWT without engaging the hard problem—treating it as separate from theory testing. But any test that could actually distinguish theories must already have settled what phenomenal consciousness is and what evidence bears on it. For instance: if we build systems that match IIT's predictions but lack GNWT's global broadcast, do they have consciousness? Answering this requires already knowing whether consciousness is constituted by integration or access—which is just the hard problem. The commitment runs: if you think theories can be tested empirically, you're committed to either (a) the hard problem being solved or dissolvable, or (b) consciousness theories not actually being about phenomenal consciousness. This forces theory-testing advocates to take a stand on the hard problem they claim to sidestep.
- **Appraiser (blind):** That testing presupposes solving the hard problem is correct but the implication is fairly straightforward.

---

### U41. Any empirical methodology for adjudicating between consciousness theories already presupposes a theory of the relationship between consciousness and its signatures, making theory-neutral testing impossible.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Proponents of empirical consciousness science who believe neutral testing between theories is possible, including advocates of adversarial collaboration frameworks
- **Sketch:** The argument proceeds by examining proposed methods like adversarial collaboration or critical experiments. Each method requires identifying what would count as evidence—neural correlates, behavioral markers, reportability conditions. But selecting these measures already embeds theoretical commitments about what consciousness is and how it relates to third-person observables. For instance, treating reportability as evidential assumes consciousness is the kind of thing that can be reported, which begs the question against views where consciousness and access come apart. Similarly, privileging neural correlates assumes consciousness supervenes on brain states in ways detectable by our instruments. The key premise is that the observation-theory distinction collapses for consciousness because we lack direct observational access to the explanandum itself.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The observation-theory distinction collapse is well-known; applying it specifically to consciousness adds little new.

### U42. Testing consciousness theories requires solving the other minds problem, but solving the other minds problem requires already knowing which consciousness theory is correct, creating an unbreakable epistemic circle.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Anyone who believes empirical evidence about cases beyond our own can confirm or disconfirm consciousness theories
- **Sketch:** To test whether theory T correctly predicts which systems are conscious, we need independent access to which systems are actually conscious. But establishing that system S is conscious (beyond our own case) requires inference from observable properties to consciousness—and the warrant for that inference depends on accepting some theory of consciousness-to-observation relationships. If we use theory T to validate our other-minds inferences, we reason circularly. If we use a different theory T*, we've already assumed T is wrong. The position that empirical testing can adjudicate between theories is thus unstable: it requires what it cannot provide—theory-independent knowledge of the extension of consciousness. The key premise is that all other-minds judgments are theoretical inferences, never observations.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The other minds circularity is a classic problem; restating it for theory testing adds little.

---

### U43. Rather than adjudicating between theories, consciousness science should test whether any systematic theory of consciousness is possible by searching for consciousness-unconditional regularities that resist theoretical unification.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The shared assumption across methodological debates that some systematic theory must be correct, and we just need methods to determine which one
- **Sketch:** Current methodology assumes the question is 'which theory is right?' But there's a prior question: 'is consciousness the kind of thing that admits of systematic theory?' This requires distinguishing between two research programs: (1) testing theories against each other, and (2) testing whether the domain is theorizable at all. The latter would search for patterns like: consciousness regularly obtains under conditions C1 but not C2, where no principled theory unifies why C1 and C2 differ—suggesting irreducible heterogeneity. If we found consciousness follows different principles in different substrates or contexts, with no systematic account possible, this would be evidence against all current theories while explaining why adjudication proves impossible. The key premise is that domain-theorizable and theory-competition are orthogonal questions that current methodology conflates.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The theorizable/non-theorizable distinction is interesting but the proposed research program is vague and the payoff unclear for actual practice.

---

### U44. The distinction between 'creature consciousness' and 'state consciousness' that theorists use to clarify their target actually generates an infinite regress that prevents any theory from being determinately correct.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The standard creature/state distinction used across debates to clarify what theories are about
- **Sketch:** Theorists distinguish creature consciousness (the system is conscious) from state consciousness (a particular state is conscious). But this generates a problem: to verify a theory of state consciousness, we need to identify which states are conscious; but identifying conscious states requires already knowing which creatures are conscious (to have authority about their states); and knowing which creatures are conscious requires knowing which states they're in. Moreover, we need a theory of the relationship between creature and state consciousness—is the creature conscious because some states are, or are states conscious because the creature is? Different answers yield different testing methodologies. This isn't just an epistemic problem but reveals that the distinction itself fragments the explanandum in a way that prevents determinate theory confirmation. The key premise is that neither creature nor state consciousness can be established independently of the other, creating methodological circularity.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The creature/state distinction problem is real but relatively narrow; doesn't fundamentally challenge how most researchers approach theory testing.

---

### U45. Theories of consciousness are empirically testable only if they make predictions about mismatches between what subjects can report and what they can discriminate, not about the presence or absence of consciousness itself.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared assumption across HOT theorists, GWT proponents, and IIT defenders that their empirical predictions differentiate theories of consciousness rather than theories of cognitive access
- **Sketch:** The debate assumes we test consciousness theories by determining what is or isn't conscious. But consciousness itself is only accessible first-personally, so third-person tests can only detect structural features like access/phenomenal gaps, blindsight-style dissociations, or reportability failures. The key premise is that every actual experimental paradigm cited as evidence (masked priming, inattentional blindness, vegetative state imaging) tests performance mismatches, not phenomenology directly. Therefore theories like HOT, GWT, and IIT aren't actually competing about consciousness—they're competing about the functional architecture that typically accompanies reportable consciousness. The real dispute is verbal: different theories stipulate different relationships between this architecture and phenomenology.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The observation that we test dissociations rather than consciousness directly is already implicit in much methodology discussion; makes it explicit but doesn't transform debate.

### U46. If a theory of consciousness makes the same predictions as a theory without consciousness (a 'zombie theory'), then treating them as distinct theories violates Occam's razor in a way that no amount of philosophical argument can overcome.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Philosophers like Chalmers who maintain both naturalistic epistemic standards and the meaningfulness of choosing between empirically equivalent theories of consciousness
- **Sketch:** Many philosophers hold both that (a) some consciousness theories might be empirically equivalent to functionalist theories that don't posit phenomenal properties as fundamental, and (b) we can still rationally prefer one over the other via philosophical considerations. But this commits them to rejecting methodological naturalism in a way incompatible with their usual epistemic standards. The key premise: if T1 (consciousness theory) and T2 (zombie variant) make identical predictions about all possible observations, then any rational grounds for preferring T1 require either direct phenomenal access to others' consciousness (which we lack) or aprioristic metaphysics (which these theorists typically reject outside this domain). Therefore, defenders of empirically equivalent but metaphysically distinct theories must either embrace radical rationalism or admit their theory is merely instrumental.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The Occam's razor argument against empirically equivalent theories is standard philosophy of science; application to consciousness is straightforward.

### U47. Any empirical evidence that seems to support one consciousness theory over another actually supports a claim about what consciousness is correlated with, not what constitutes it, collapsing the metaphysical debate into mere correlation-hunting.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** The assumption shared by Koch, Tononi, Dehaene and others that neural correlate studies can confirm constitutive theories
- **Sketch:** Proponents of competing theories assume empirical findings can adjudicate between constitutive claims (X constitutes consciousness vs Y constitutes consciousness). But consider: if we find that global broadcasting correlates perfectly with consciousness reports, this is equally compatible with (a) global broadcasting constitutes consciousness, (b) consciousness constitutes global broadcasting, (c) both are effects of a common cause, or (d) consciousness is identical to something else that reliably co-occurs with global broadcasting. The key premise is that correlation, even perfect correlation, underdetermines constitutive direction. Every alleged empirical test—from contrastive analysis to neural correlates—only establishes correlation. Therefore, the debate between theories cannot be settled empirically because empirical methods only reveal correlations, while the theories make constitutive claims that transcend any possible correlation evidence. The search for the 'true' theory of consciousness mistakes a metaphysical question for an empirical one.
- **Appraiser (blind):** That empirical evidence only establishes correlation not constitution is a standard philosophical point.

### U48. Consciousness theories can only be tested by methods that would also distinguish between them if they were all false, which means current empirical proposals systematically fail to engage the skeptical possibility that drives theory-choice.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** Proposals like Tononi's 'consciousness meter' or tests distinguishing theories via predictions about anesthesia, split-brain cases, or neuroimaging
- **Sketch:** Consider proposed tests between IIT and GWT: imaging studies of posterior vs. frontal activity, or testing predictions about feedforward vs. recurrent processing. These tests assume that if there's a physical correlate of consciousness, these theories correctly identify candidate mechanisms. But they can't distinguish 'GWT is true' from 'GWT is false but happens to correlate with epiphenomenal consciousness' without already solving the hard problem. The key premise is that empirical discrimination requires not just differential predictions but differential predictions that would fail under skeptical scenarios equally probable given our evidence. Every proposed empirical test between theories presupposes we've already ruled out error theories (illusionism), epiphenomenalism, or that the theories have merely latched onto different correlates of a phenomenon neither explains.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The point about needing to rule out skeptical scenarios is valid but doesn't distinctively advance beyond general underdetermination concerns.

### U49. Theories of consciousness are untestable in principle because consciousness is the condition of possibility for evidence itself, making it transcendentally prior to empirical confirmation.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The methodological naturalism shared by neuroscientific approaches to consciousness that treat consciousness as one more natural phenomenon to be empirically investigated like any other.
- **Sketch:** All empirical evidence ultimately cashes out in how things seem to observers—their conscious experiences of data, correlations, and experimental outcomes. When we test a theory of consciousness, we must use conscious experience as our evidence base (either our own phenomenology or inferences from others' reports, which we consciously evaluate). This creates a circularity: consciousness must be presupposed as reliable to test any theory of consciousness. The argument turns on a Kantian move—consciousness occupies the same methodological position as space and time did for Kant: not an object of possible experience but the framework within which experience occurs. Unlike other scientific entities (electrons, genes), we cannot step outside consciousness to verify it against mind-independent reality. Therefore, 'testing' consciousness theories is actually more like choosing between conceptual schemes than empirical hypothesis testing.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The transcendental argument about consciousness as condition of evidence has been made repeatedly; the Kantian parallel is familiar territory.

### U50. Higher-order theories and first-order theories are both unstable because they must either deny that consciousness comes in degrees (contradicting phenomenology) or admit that their 'either-or' theoretical mechanism can somehow produce graduated consciousness (contradicting their architecture).

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** The standard assumption that we can have both mechanistic theories and accommodation of phenomenological gradualism
- **Sketch:** The argument notes that consciousness phenomenologically admits of degrees—drowsiness, clarity, intensity. First-order theories (like IIT) use all-or-nothing mechanisms: either information is integrated or not, either global broadcast occurs or not. HOT theories similarly require either a meta-representation exists or not. The instability: to accommodate degrees, theories must either (a) deny degrees are real variations in consciousness itself (claiming only content varies), which contradicts phenomenology, or (b) admit their binary mechanisms come in degrees, which undermines the mechanistic explanation—saying 'somewhat integrated information' or 'partial meta-representation' loses the theoretical precision that made the theory explanatory. The key premise is that any mechanism discrete enough to be explanatory cannot produce genuinely graduated consciousness.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The degrees-of-consciousness problem for binary mechanisms has been discussed; the instability argument doesn't add sufficient novelty.

### U51. Any theory of consciousness that claims to be empirically testable is committed to the possibility of consciousness eliminativism, which most such theories explicitly reject.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Scientific consciousness researchers who claim their theories are both empirically testable and incompatible with eliminativism/illusionism
- **Sketch:** If a theory of consciousness generates empirical predictions that could be falsified, then there are possible worlds where all those predictions fail—worlds empirically indistinguishable from ours but where the theory is false. But for consciousness theories, this means there would be a possible world empirically identical to ours where consciousness doesn't exist (or exists in radically different places than we think). The key premise is that empirical testability requires the possibility of empirical defeat, and empirical defeat of a consciousness theory requires that the phenomena could be preserved while consciousness is absent. Yet most naturalistic theories (GWT, IIT, etc.) insist both that they're empirically testable AND that consciousness is definitely present in normal human cases—these commitments are in tension.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The tension between testability and anti-eliminativism is interesting but the argument equivocates on what 'empirical defeat' requires.

### U52. The dispute about how to test consciousness theories is verbal: 'testing' means confirming/disconfirming phenomenal predictions for one camp and confirming/disconfirming functional predictions for another, and both camps are right by their own lights.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** Both illusionists like Frankish and scientific realists like Dehaene who think they disagree about whether consciousness science is possible
- **Sketch:** When illusionists say consciousness theories aren't testable, they mean you can't get evidence about phenomenal properties as such. When scientific realists say they are testable, they mean you can get evidence about the functional/behavioral/neural signatures. The key premise is that both sides use 'consciousness' to pick out their preferred explanandum (phenomenal vs. functional), and 'testable' to mean 'delivers evidence about that explanandum.' This isn't just talking past each other—it reveals that there are two legitimate but distinct research programs, one trying to explain phenomenality (and failing/impossible) and one trying to explain the functional role (and succeeding/possible). The dispute about testability is really just a symptom of this deeper bifurcation.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Diagnosing verbal disputes is useful but this particular diagnosis (phenomenal vs. functional) is already widely recognized in consciousness studies.

### U53. Building artificial systems to test consciousness theories faces a fatal circularity: we must already know which theory is correct to know which engineering success would constitute creating consciousness.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** Proponents of 'building consciousness' as a testing methodology, including both IIT and GWT theorists who suggest implementation projects
- **Sketch:** Many propose that building systems according to different theories (implementing IIT's phi, Global Workspace architecture, etc.) could test which theory is right—if the IIT-based system is conscious and the GWT-based system isn't, IIT wins. But this assumes we have a theory-neutral way to detect which system is actually conscious. Any test we use (behavioral, report-based, or even direct measurement) itself presupposes a theory of what consciousness is and where to look for it. We cannot use engineering success as evidence for a theory when determining what counts as success already requires accepting that theory. The key premise: all detection methods for consciousness are theory-laden in a way that creates evidential circularity.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The circularity in using engineering success as evidence is real but the point about theory-laden detection is well-established.

Any criterion for theory-selection in consciousness that privileges 'solving the hard problem' systematically excludes precisely those theories that make consciousness empirically tractable, revealing that the hard problem functions as a methodological trap rather than a legitimate constraint.

The key premise is that this creates an unstable position: you must choose between empirical tractability and solving the hard problem, but cannot have both. ### U55. Consciousness theories can only be empirically distinguished at their edges—in borderline cases like insects, embryos, or minimal systems—but our intuitions about these cases are parasitic on whichever theory we already accept about paradigm cases, making theory-choice empirically underdetermined in principle.

- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The widespread methodological assumption that testing theories on borderline cases can break the tie
- **Sketch:** Current proposals suggest testing theories on edge cases where they diverge: does IIT's phi predict consciousness in simple grids? Does GWT say embryos are conscious? The key premise is that these aren't genuine tests because we have no independent grip on whether these edge cases are conscious—we only think they're conscious-or-not based on extrapolating from central cases using our preferred theory. The distinction is between testing theories WHERE THEY AGREE (paradigm cases, which provides no discrimination) and testing WHERE THEY DISAGREE (edge cases, where we lack independent verdicts). Middle ground cases are precisely where we're most uncertain, yet that's the only empirical wedge available. This isn't like testing physics theories at extreme energies where we await data; here the 'data' is just our theory-driven intuitions.

### U56. The move from 'correlation' to 'explanation' in consciousness science requires assuming that consciousness plays a causal role in producing the neural signatures we measure, but this assumption is incompatible with epiphenomenalism—which means that every consciousness theory that claims empirical support is implicitly committed to rejecting epiphenomenalism.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** The widespread methodological assumption that NCC research provides theory-neutral evidence applicable across different metaphysical positions on consciousness
- **Sketch:** The argument starts from the methodology: theories earn empirical support by identifying neural correlates of consciousness (NCCs), then claiming these correlate explain why consciousness occurs. But explanatory correlations require causal connections—if A explains B, changes in A must be able to produce changes in B. For consciousness theories, this means changes in neural signatures must be capable of producing changes in phenomenology. However, this directionality (neural→phenomenal) only supports the theory if the reverse direction (phenomenal→neural) also holds, otherwise we're just noting that some neural process causes both the signature and the experience. The key premise is that rejecting phenomenal→neural causation (epiphenomenalism) undercuts the evidential relationship between finding a correlation and confirming a theory. Therefore, any theorist who claims empirical support from NCC studies is committed to consciousness having causal powers—a substantial metaphysical commitment they rarely acknowledge.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The incompatibility between NCC methodology and epiphenomenalism is already widely recognized in the literature.

The key premise is that empirical testing requires causal predictions, but if qualia could vary independently of function (spectrum inversion, zombie arguments), then functional predictions can't confirm theories about qualia. But the relationship between NCCs and consciousness itself is precisely what's in dispute between theories. IIT says integrated information IS consciousness; HOT says higher-order representation IS consciousness; mysterians say consciousness is something over and above any physical property. No arrangement of NCC-level evidence can adjudicate this without begging the question about the NCC-consciousness relationship. Applied to consciousness, this means no amount of empirical success can establish that a theory has identified what consciousness really is, only that it's found reliable correlates.

Higher-order theories and first-order theories disagree about whether unreportable states are conscious, but we can only test this through reports. The argument turns on showing that the divergences between major theories systematically concern either: (a) counterfactual substrates we cannot instantiate while preserving behavioral identity, or (b) states defined by their inability to influence report-generating mechanisms. If this is constitutive rather than contingent, we are misclassifying an intractable philosophical dispute as a tractable empirical one.

- **Sketch:** The standard response to zombie arguments is that zombies are metaphysically impossible even if conceivable. But for methodology, we need epistemic possibility. Consider: we're trying to validate third-person methods for detecting consciousness (neural measures, behavioral tests, etc.). Any such validation requires checking whether the method correctly identifies conscious beings. But we can only validate this for ourselves. For every other human, it's epistemically possible they're zombies who pass all third-person tests but lack consciousness. This isn't a skeptical worry—it's a counterexample to the claim that third-person methods can be validated. We cannot bootstrap from 'the method works for me' to 'the method works generally' without already assuming others are conscious, which is precisely what we're trying to test. This undermines the methodological premise that third-person consciousness science can be validated the way other sciences are.
